P001 Physiological effects of high flow nasal cannula oxygen therapy combined with continuous negative extrathoracic pressure in healthy volunteers: a pilot study

SM Colombo1, M Leali2, L Vivona3, F Casano2, G Baldin De Bernard4, P Bottausci4, V Scaravilli4, T Mauri4, A Zanella4, G Grasselli4

1Fondazione IRCCS Ca Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Department of Anesthesia, Critical Care and Emergency, Milan, Italy, 2Universita degli studi di Milano, Dipartimento di Fisiopatologia MedicoChirurgica e dei Trapianti, Milan, Italy, 3IRCCS MultiMedica San Giuseppe Hospital, Dipartimento di Anestesia e Rianimazione, Milan, Italy, 4Fondazione IRCCS Ca Granda Ospedale Maggiore Policlinico, Milan, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P001

Introduction: High flow nasal cannula (HFNC) and continuous negative extrathoracic pressure (CNEP) are non-invasive respiratory supports that can modulate lung volumes, work of breathing and cardiopulmonary interactions through distinct but potentially complementary mechanisms [1,2]. The combined use of HFNC and CNEP has not yet been systematically evaluated in humans, and its physiological impact on ventilation distribution and intrathoracic pressures remains unclear.

Methods: A single-centre, stepwise physiological study was conducted in healthy volunteers. Participants underwent six sequential 20-min conditions, consisting of spontaneous breathing with and without HFNC at 60 L/min (FiO2 0.21), each combined with three levels of CNEP (0, -20, -40 cmH2O) applied through a thoraco-abdominal cuirass, while continuous monitoring included clinical variables, esophageal and gastric pressures, and electrical impedance tomography (EIT).

Results: 8 subjects have been enrolled (male 75%, age 30.0 ± 5.3 y, BMI 22.8 ± 3.4). Across study phases, respiratory rate, oesophageal pressure swings, and pressure–time product did not show significant changes (Figure), thus no measurable reduction in inspiratory effort. In contrast, CNEP, particularly when combined with HFNC, produced a consistent decrease in end-expiratory gastric pressure while marked increase in end-expiratory lung impedance, likely related to higher end-expiratory lung volume with predominant ventral aeration and a redistribution of tidal ventilation towards dorsal lung regions. Hemodynamic parameters and oxygen saturation remained within normal ranges, and comfort scores decreased at higher negative pressures but were overall acceptable.

Conclusions: Combination of HFNC and CNEP is feasible and in healthy volunteers is associated with increased end-expiratory lung volume, ventral aeration and dorsal redistribution of ventilation, without detectable changes in inspiratory effort.

P002 Home bronchoscopy: a real improvement for tracheostomized and tracheoventilated patients

E Colombaroli, C Malandruccolo, M Carlini, F Righetti

UTI San Bonifacio AULSS9 Scaligera, Anesthesiology and Reanimation, San Bonifacio Verona, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P002

Introduction: Tracheostomized and tracheoventilated patients who live at home are very fragile, difficult to move to hospital with discomfort and transport-related risks. We performed home bronchoscopy, to simplify their care, analysing the risk and the benefit, to reduce hospital admissions and clinical risk of infection.

Methods: We performed home bronchoscopy to tracheostomized and tracheoventilated adult patients once a year under local anesthesia with lidocaine 4% with MAD (Mucosal Atomization Device), using oximeter and frequency measurement as monitoring and disposable bronchoscopy Ambu aScope4 ® for the procedures. We performed bronchoscopy through the cannula. In case of anticoagulated patient, it is interrupted few days before in order to avoid bleeding. The evaluation aims to diagnose any type of tracheal lesion related to aspiration, decubitus of the tracheostomy tube or cuff and tracheomalacia, to improve home management by their care-givers.

Results: We performed 258 home bronchoscopy. The procedure was tolerated by everyone without adverse events, as bleeding or desaturation. The patients maintained ventilation or oxygen supplementation if they habitually use them. The bronchoscopy showed 37 patients (14%) with minimal tracheal lesion and decubitus of the tracheostomy tube or cuff and 22 patients (8%) with tracheomalacia; in all of them we recommended reducing the tracheobronchial aspirations in order to avoid mucosal lesions and the correct measurement of cuff pressure. In 6 patients (2%) a granuloma was visualized, with endoscopy indication for removal.

Conclusions: Home bronchoscopy is a safe and well tolerated procedure; it can reduce the discomfort of moving the patients, reducing transportation costs and risks. It helps to manage most patients directly, improving care giver education.

P003 Evaluating the trend of the ROX index to predict failure of nasal high-flow oxygenation

V Lertritdecha, W Thongtaeparak

Phramongkutklao Hospital, Division of Pulmonary and Critical Care Medicine, Bangkok, Thailand

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P003

Introduction: High-flow nasal cannula (HFNC) is recommended for patients who have been extubated in high-risk groups. Patients who did not show clinical improvement in 48 h of utilizing HFNC and the attending physician delayed reintubation; the outcome deteriorated, and the mortality rate increased.

Methods: This prospective observational cohort study was conducted between July 2024 and December 2024. The study included patients admitted to the medical intensive care unit (ICU) at Phramongkutklao Hospital with respiratory failure. Following extubation, all patients were initiated on HFNC and ROX index was monitored. The primary outcome was the diagnostic accuracy of cut-off value for ROX index change to predict HFNC failure. Secondary objectives were to evaluate the ROX index change to predict 24 h and factors associated with HFNC failure.

Results: From a total of 70 patients who received HFNC after extubation, 58 patients were in the HFNC success group, and 12 patients were in the HFNC failure group. Receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve analysis was performed to diagnostic accuracy of cutoff value of ROX index change to predict HFNC failure. ROX index change at 24 h reduced by 0.55 can predict HFNC failure, resulting in sensitivity of 75.0%, specificity of 89.8% and area under the ROC 0.838 (Figure). At 24 h, with the HFNC failure group showing a negative trend compared to the HFNC success group (-1.00 ± 2.64 vs. 2.91 ± 3.73, p = 0.001).

Conclusions: The ROX index change at 24 h reduced by 0.55 can predict HFNC failure and diagnostic accuracy represented by an area under the ROC curve of 0.838. The ROX index change at 24 h shows a statistically significant change between the success and failure groups.

P004 Dynamic assessment of ROX index trajectories predicts high-flow oxygen therapy failure in acute hypoxemic respiratory failure

CJ Chang1, CN Tai2, MS Hung2, CM Lin2, CH Hung.3

1Chiayi Chang Gung Memorial Hospital, Department of Respiratory Therapy, Puzi City, Chiayi County, Taiwan, Republic of China, 2Chiayi Chang Gung Memorial Hospital, Department of Pulmonary and Critical Care Medicine, Puzi City, Chiayi County, Taiwan, Republic of China, 3National Cheng Kung University, Institute of Allied Health Sciences, College of Medicine, Tainan, Taiwan, Republic of China.

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P004

Introduction: This study aimed to determine whether both the absolute level and the temporal rate of change (slope) of the respiratory rate-oxygenation (ROX) index are dynamically associated with the risk of high-flow oxygen therapy (HFOT) failure in adults with acute hypoxemic respiratory failure (AHRF). The ROX index was defined as (SpO₂/FiO₂) divided by the respiratory rate.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective single-center study including adults with AHRF receiving HFOT between January and December 2024. Serial ROX values were recorded at 0, 2, 6, 12, and 24 h. A joint longitudinal-survival model simultaneously described individual ROX trajectories using a linear mixed-effects model and the time to HFOT failure (intubation, transition to noninvasive ventilation, or death) using a Weibull relative-risk model. Both the predicted ROX value and its temporal derivative (slope) were modeled as time-dependent covariates. Integration was performed using adaptive Gauss-Hermite quadrature (five points).

Results: A total of 1793 ROX measurements were analyzed (414 subjects; 226 failures, 54.6%). In the value + slope joint model, higher ROX values were associated with lower failure risk (association coefficient, Assoct = -0.3548; hazard ratio [HR] = 0.70; p < 0.001), whereas faster ROX increase independently indicated higher risk (Assoct.s = + 0.3646; HR = 1.44; p < 0.001) (Table). The Weibull shape parameter (0.63; log[shape] = -0.4624) indicated a decreasing baseline hazard over time, and the model demonstrated a good fit and stable convergence (AIC = 12,241.9). These findings suggest that the prognostic meaning of ROX depends not only on its level but also on its short-term evolution.

Conclusions: Both the instantaneous ROX value and its slope independently predict HFOT failure. While higher ROX levels are protective, a rapid rise may reflect compensatory effort and impending deterioration. Incorporating ROX dynamics into real-time monitoring may improve early identification of patients at risk for HFOT failure.

Table (abstract P004)Association between ROX dynamics and HFOT failure (joint model)

P005 Pre-hospital respiratory rescue: a new breakthrough device

G Landoni1, K Donadello2, L Cabrini3, G Giardina4, E Salurso5, F Ciceri Negri5, GB Fiore5, A Redaelli5, F Consolo6

1VitaSalute San Raffaele University, Dept of Anesthesia ICU, Milan, Italy, 2AOUI University Hospital Integrated Trust of Verona, Anaesthesia and Intensive Care B Unit, Verona, Italy, 3Ospedale di Circolo, ASST Setta Laghi, Unit of General and Neurosurgical Intensive Care, Varese, Italy, 4IRCCS San Raffaele Scientific Institute, Department of Anesthesia and Intensive Care, Milan, Italy, 5Politecnico Di Milano, Department of Electronics, Information and Bioengineering, Milan, Italy, 6VitaSalute San Raffaele University, Department of Electronics, Information and Bioengineering, Milan, Italy.

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P005

Introduction: Acute respiratory failure is a medical emergency that can rapidly become fatal. We developed and preclinically validated a portable CPAP device specifically designed for emergency out-of-hospital use which is intuitive and easy to use, enabling even individuals without specific technical skills or expertise to intervene promptly.

Methods: We designed and developed a new highly compact CPAP system allowing one-handed operation and ensuring ergonomic usability (Figure). We evaluated the device prototype performance using a high-fidelity lung simulator to test the ability to maintain a target pressure of 5 cmH₂O under varying respiratory conditions. 15 volunteers with different levels of experience with respiratory support devices and emergency care tested it.

Results: Under all the pathological simulated scenarios (COPD, asthma, ARDS) the prototype device consistently stabilized PEEP at the target 5cmH2O value. 93% of operators confirmed that the device was intuitive and easy to apply.

Conclusions: The device delivered a PEEP which was stable at ~ 5 cmH₂O. Operators reported that the device is straightforward to use and requires no prior technical or clinical training. These results indicate strong potential for real-world application as an early respiratory support tool to shorten the time to treatment and improve outcomes for acute respiratory failure patients. A clinical validation study is currently in progress.

Acknowledgements: Funded by the European Union-Next Generation EU-NRRP M6C2-Investment 2.1 Enhancement and strengthening of biomedical research in the NHS. Project ID: PNRR-POC-2022-12376704—CUP Master C43C22001390007. Project title: PREVENT—A new portable device for PRE-hospital non-invasive VENTilatory support in acute respiratory failure.

P006 Double trunk mask: a simple open-source device to enhance Fio2 at low oxygen flow

W Nabhan, A Khaldi, S Mashayekhi, C Hickmann, V Durant, F Duprez

EpiCura, USI, Hornu, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P006

Introduction: The Double Trunk Mask (DTM) consists of a standard aerosol mask into which two corrugated tubes are inserted through its lateral openings [1]. When positioned over a conventional oxygen nasal cannula (NC), these tubes act as oxygen reservoirs during expiration, thereby increasing the volume of oxygen available for the subsequent inspiratory phase and enhancing the FiO₂ [2]. The aim of this bench study was to assess the FiO₂ delivered with the DTM + NC compared with the NC alone.

Methods: The FiO₂ delivered by a DTM combined with a nasal cannula (DTM + NC) was compared with that of the NC alone using an adult lung model driven by a mechanical ventilator. The resistive and elastic characteristics of the test lung were set to 5 cmH₂O/L/s and 0.06 L/cmH₂O, respectively. Data acquisition was performed using LabScribe 3™ software (iWorx®), after a stabilization period. Experimental parameters were adjusted as follows: O₂ flow 1–6 L/min, tidal volume 0.5 L, respiratory rate 10–30 cycles/min, and Ti/Ttot ratio 0.33.

Results: The DTM dramatically increases FiO₂ compared with oxygen delivery via NC alone. Linear regression analyses showed a strong correlation between O₂ flow rate and FiO₂. Regressions of ΔFiO₂ confirmed a significant DTM-related increase, particularly at lower flow rates (Figure).

Conclusions: The DTM + NC significantly increases FiO₂ compared with NC alone. The DTM has been used for several years in our hospital, particularly during the COVID-19 pandemic, with its safety, tolerance, and lack of PaCO₂ increase already well documented. These results reinforce the DTM’s physiological effectiveness and support its role as a simple, safe, and efficient complement to conventional oxygen therapy.

P007 Preventive measures for dental injury associated with supraglottic airway use: a scoping review

M Akatsuka1,Y Nakamura2

1Sapporo Medical University School of Medicine, Department of Biochemistry, Sapporo, Japan, 2Sapporo Medical University Hospital, Division of Clinical Engineering, Sapporo, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P007

Introduction: Perioperative dental injury (PDI) remains a significant cause of morbidity. While supraglottic airway (SGA) devices are generally considered less traumatic than endotracheal intubation, specific risks such as insertion trauma and biting during emergence remain clinically relevant. This scoping review aimed to map existing evidence on preventive measures for dental and oral injury specifically associated with SGA use in adult patients.

Methods: This scoping review followed JBI methodology. Electronic databases were searched for randomized controlled trials and observational studies reporting preventive interventions for dental/oral trauma during SGA use. Data on device types, prevention strategies, and injury outcomes were extracted.

Results: Twenty-eight included studies identified preventive measures across five domains: physical protection, insertion technique, device selection, emergence management, and systemic intervention. Regarding physical protection, soft bite blocks significantly reduced mucosal bleeding (0% vs 13%, p < 0.01) compared to rigid airways [1]. For insertion techniques, guided methods using a bougie [2] demonstrated less trauma than blind insertion. In terms of device selection, second-generation SGAs showed more favorable safety profiles than first-generation devices. Pharmacological management using remifentanil infusion significantly improved smooth removal (p = 0.002) [3]. Finally, systemic intervention via standardized preoperative assessment reduced injury incidence from 0.108% to 0.009% [4].

Conclusions: Preventing dental injury during SGA use requires a multimodal approach. Evidence supports guided insertion techniques, avoidance of rigid bite blocks, pharmacological management of emergence, and systemic risk assessment.

P011 Preventing aspiration events in hospital using prophylactic antiemetics: a systematic review and meta-analysis

K.B Tworek1, M Giovannoni1, J Kung2, V Lau3

1University of Alberta, Department of Medicine, Edmonton, Canada, 2University of Alberta, Geoffrey Robyn Sperber Health Sciences Library, Edmonton, Canada, 3University of Alberta, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Edmonton, Canada

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P011

Introduction: Aspiration events in hospitalized adults are common and linked to substantial morbidity and mortality. While antiemetics have been studied for aspiration prevention in specific settings, evidence for their broader preventive use in hospitalized adults is limited. We conducted a systematic review and meta-analysis to evaluate the effect of prophylactic antiemetics on aspiration events in adult inpatients.

Methods: Ovid MEDLINE, Ovid Embase, CINAHL, and Cochrane Library (via Wiley) were searched in May 2025. Results were reviewed independently by two investigators to identify eligible studies. We included randomized controlled trials (RCTs) and observational studies assessing antiemetic use in hospitalized adults, with aspiration events as the primary outcome. Secondary outcomes were pneumonia, hospital length of stay, and mortality.

Results: Three RCTs met inclusion criteria (n = 515; 237 antiemetic, 278 placebo), only two RCTs reported on aspiration events (n = 210; 106 antiemetic, 104 placebo). Pooled analysis demonstrated an 88% relative risk [RR] reduction of aspiration events in those receiving prophylactic antiemetics (3/106 (2.8%) antiemetic vs 29/104 (27.9%) placebo; mean difference 25.1%, RR = 0.12, 95% confidence intervals [CI]: 0.02–0.73, p < 0.0001) (Figure). Although the reduction in pneumonia did not reach statistical significance, the antiemetic group showed a favorable trend compared with placebo (39/237 (16.5%) antiemetic, 90/278 (32.4%) placebo; mean difference 15.9%, RR = 0.44, 95% CI: 0.15–1.28, p = 0.15). Mortality rates were comparable between groups (83/237 (35.0%) antiemetic, 106/278 (38.1%) placebo; mean difference 3.1%, RR = 1.02, 95% CI: 0.84–1.24, p = 0.85).

Conclusions: Prophylactic antiemetics were associated with a significant reduction in aspiration events and a non-significant but favorable trend towards reduction of pneumonia among hospitalized adults. Larger, high-quality RCTs are needed to clarify their role in aspiration prevention.

P012 The modified Evan’s blue dye test has high positive predictive value for diagnosing aspiration in tracheostomized critically ill patients: a systematic review and meta-analysis

E Szoke1, C Turan2

1Semmelweis University, Centre for Translational Medicine, Budapest, Hungary, 2Semmelweis University, Intensive Care Unit, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P012

Introduction: Dysphagia and aspiration are common complications in tracheostomized critically ill patients. The Modified Evan’s Blue Dye Test (MEBDT) is a non-invasive, easy-to-perform dysphagia screening tool, but its accuracy compared to the gold standard Fiberoptic Endoscopic Evaluation of Swallowing (FEES) in these patients remains unclear.

Methods: Study protocol was prospectively registered on PROSPERO (CRD: 42023479920). A systematic search was conducted across three databases (PubMed, Cochrane Central, and Embase), followed by a systematic screening against predetermined selection criteria. The included studies reported the sensitivity and specificity of MEBDT versus FEES. The risk of bias and the level of evidence certainty in the included papers were assessed by the QUADAS-2 tool and GRADE approach respectively.

Results: Six out of 2227 screened studies were included. We found that MEBDT had a high specificity 95.42% (95% CI [67.38%, 99.53%]) and positive predictive value 95% (95% CI [81, 100]) (Figure).

Conclusions: In conclusion, MEBDT is a reliable tool for assessing aspiration in tracheostomized patients, especially when positive. Nonetheless, a negative MEBDT still requires further investigations with FEES to confirm the absence of dysphagia and aspiration.

Figure (abstract P012) Random-effects bivariate diagnostic meta-analysis of negative predictive value and positive predictive value of the Modified Evan’s Blue Dye Test compared to the Fiberoscopic Evaluation of Swallowing in the diagnosis of aspiration

P013 Impairment of deglutition in tracheostomized critical care patients

E Sertaridou1, P Bempeletsi2, M Nakou1, K Nikolaou1, C Alexopoulou1, V Papaioannou1

1University Hospital of Alexandroupolis, Intensive Care Unit, Alexandroupolis, Greece, 2University Hospital of Alexandroupolis, Physiotherapy and Rehabilitation Department, Alexandroupolis, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P013

Introduction: Invasive airway access for assisted ventilation has been implicated in swallowing impairment. This study aims to evaluate the deglutition function in tracheostomized ICU patients.

Methods: This prospective study has been included adult ICU patients who underwent percutaneous tracheostomy. Exclusion criteria were altered state of consciousness (GCS < 11) due to disease or sedative/analgesic treatment, mental dysfunction (RASS score < -2 or > + 2 and/or possitive CAM-ICU) and any underlying disease or trauma precluding assessment of the neuromuscular function. After cuff deflation, enrolled patients were clinically evaluated for laryngeal elevation, hypopharyngeal and laryngeal sensation, vocal cord function and cough reflex. Dysphagia was recognized with Gugging Swallowing Screen (GUSS) and the blue dye test, while selected subjects underwent Flexible Endoscopic Evaluation of Swallowing (FEES). Moreover, ICU myopathy was assessed using the Medical Research Council (MRC) Scale for Muscle Strength.

Results: Among 350 patients admitted to the ICU in 10-month period, 88 were tracheostomized, among who 28 were eligible for the study. The tracheostomy was performed after median 9 days since ICU admission (range 4–20 days) and dysphagia was assessed in median 11 days (range 5–33 days). The median GCS score at evaluation time was 15/15 (range: 11–15/15) and RASS score 0 (range: -1 to + 1). The patients presented significant myopathy (median MRC score: 36/60, range: 12–48/60) and notable swallowing disorders (median GUSS score: 7/10, range: 3–10/10). Of note, 12/28 patients (39.3%) with GUSS score < 7/10 were assessed by FEES, which revealed a high risk of aspiration. There was no significant correlation between MRC and GUSS score (Spearman’s r 0.12, p 0.6).

Conclusions: This study demonstrated a noteworthy prevalence of post-tracheostomy dysphagia and a high risk of aspiration. Thus, systematic evaluation of deglutition could allow early identification of the disorder and appropriate treatment modifications.

P015 Pleurodesis with fresh frozen plasma (FFP) for recurrent pneumothorax in patients with pulmonary contusions

K Tsikritsaki, S Antonopoulou, A Mantzioros, R Koliogeorgi, A Vilaeti, E Triantafillidou, M Anyfanti, V Zarkada, A Kalogeromitros, G Koukoulitsios

General Hospital of Athens G. Gennimatas, ICU, Athens, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P015

Introduction: Intrapleural instillation of fresh frozen plasma (FFP) is a minimally invasive alternative for managing postoperative air leaks, potentially avoiding the adverse effects of chemical pleurodesis. This study evaluated the efficacy and safety of FFP pleurodesis in patients with recurrent pneumothorax due to pulmonary contusions.

Methods: From January 2018 to September 2025, 12 patients with recurrent pneumothorax secondary to pulmonary contusions underwent FFP pleurodesis. Two chest tubes were placed, one basally and one apically. One unit of ABO-compatible FFP was instilled through the basal tube, which was then sealed and elevated approximately 60 cm to retain the plasma within the pleural space for 24 h, while the apical tube maintained air evacuation. Tubes were removed sequentially following cessation of air leak and radiographic lung re-expansion.

Results: Of the 12 patients treated, 10 (83%) achieved successful outcomes. One patient required a subsequent procedure, while another underwent surgical decortication for an unresolved air leak. No complications or adverse events were noted.

Conclusions: Intrapleural FFP instillation is a safe, effective, and technically simple option for recurrent pneumothorax associated with pulmonary contusions. The absence of complications suggests it may serve as a promising alternative to chemical pleurodesis. Larger studies are needed to further define its role and optimal indications.

P016 Non-aerated lung is proportional to the transpulmonary pressure when positive pressures are reduced in patients weaning from mechanical ventilation

H Widing1, M Gudmundsson1, C Rylander2, G Perchiazzi2, P Persson1

1University of Gothenburg, The Institute of Clinical Sciences, Gothenburg, Sweden, 2Uppsala University, Department of Surgical Sciences, Uppsala, Sweden

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P016

Introduction: The extent of atelectasis formation in response to the gradual reduction of positive pressures during assisted spontaneous breathing is unknown. Whether the formation of atelectasis is proportional to the end-expiratory transpulmonary pressure (PLEE) and whether the PLEE may be used to guide the weaning process has not been explored in clinical settings. We aimed to test the correlation between the volume of atelectasis and the PLEE in patients being weaned from mechanical ventilation in an intensive care unit.

Methods: Ten mechanically ventilated patients were included in the study. All patients were ventilated using pressure support ventilation with positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP). Repeated computed tomography scans and measurements of esophageal pressure by high-resolution manometry were acquired during a gradual reduction of PEEP and level of pressure support. The volume of atelectasis was calculated and correlated to the PLEE using linear mixed models for repeated measures.

Results: The median (IQR) amount of end-expiratory atelectasis increased significantly with reduced ventilator support from 6.79 (4.12) ml to 8.51 (4.48) ml (p < 0.01). The volume of atelectasis was linearly correlated to the end-expiratory transpulmonary pressure (p < 0.05) (Figure).

Conclusions: The atelectasis was linearly correlated to the transpulmonary pressure at end-expiration and no transpulmonary pressure threshold was observed for the accelerated formation of atelectasis. Our findings indicate that esophageal and transpulmonary pressure monitoring may contribute to the individualization of the weaning process.

P017 Retrospective cohort study of flow index trajectories: application of explainable machine learning models to predict extubation failure in mechanically ventilated ICU patients

J Shen1, S Yang2, J Liu3, C Liu2, X Fu2, M Xu1, F Yan2, X Huang2, Y Li3, C Pan2

1School of Clinical Medicine, Qinghai University, Xining, China, 2Department of Intensive Care Medicine, Sichuan Academy of Medical Sciences Sichuan Provincial People’s Hospital Affiliated Hospital of University of Electronic Science and Technology of China, Chengdu, China, 3Office of Medical Information and Data, Medical Support Center, The General Hospital of Western Theater Command, PLA, Chengdu, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P017

Introduction: Among patients who successfully pass a spontaneous breathing trial (SBT), approximately 10%–20% still experience extubation failure, suggesting that conventional SBT indices may not adequately assess respiratory reserve. Monitoring inspiratory effort is a key component in evaluating respiratory reserve, and the Flow index has been shown to noninvasively reflect inspiratory effort.

Methods: This retrospective cohort study first used a latent class trajectory model (LCTM) to classify Flow index patterns, and then applied the least absolute shrinkage and selection operator (LASSO) for feature selection. Five machine learning models were constructed based on the selected variables. Model performance was evaluated in an independent test set, and Shapley additive explanations (SHAP) analysis was performed for the best-performing model.

Results: A total of 432 intensive care unit patients were screened, and 123 were finally included, among whom 27 (21.95%) experienced extubation failure. LCTM identified three trajectories: Traj1, moderately high levels that slowly decreased and stabilized at a moderate level (61.8%); Traj2, moderate levels that continuously declined to a low level (21.95%); and Traj3, moderately high levels that continuously increased to a high level (16.26%). Multivariable logistic regression showed that, compared with Traj2, Traj1 was associated with a significantly lower risk of extubation failure (OR = 0.06, 95% CI 0.01–0.32). The logistic regression model (LRM) had the highest discriminative performance in the test set (AUROC = 0.933, 95% CI 0.852–1.000). SHAP analysis suggested that dynamic lung compliance and Flow index trajectory classification were the most important predictive factors.

Conclusions: An explainable machine learning model constructed using Flow index dynamic trajectories during SBT can, on the basis of conventional weaning indices, significantly improve the identification of patients at high risk of extubation failure.

P018 Dynamic prediction of weaning outcomes in mechanically ventilated patients with acute brain injury

R Zhang, L Liu

Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P018

Introduction: Weaning from mechanical ventilation remains particularly challenging in patients with acute brain injury (ABI). We aimed to develop a dynamic prediction model capable of providing individualized, time-updated forecasts of weaning outcomes in this population.

Methods: We conducted an observational study using a multicenter prospective cohort and a retrospective cohort from Chinese Database in Intensive Care (CDIC). Intubated adult ABI patients underwent mechanical ventilation for at least 24 h were eligible, while those without a separation attempt were excluded. Data collected included demographics, Sequential Organ Failure Assessment (SOFA), and repeated measurements of Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) and respiratory parameters during the first 7 days. The primary outcome was weaning failure. The prospective cohort was randomly divided into training (70%) and internal validation (30%) sets; the CDIC cohort was used for external validation. A Bayesian joint model was developed to generate dynamic predictions. Model performance was evaluated using the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC).

Results: A total of 1585 patients were included (481 prospective, 1104 CDIC), with a median age of 62 [52, 72] years, and 64.6% were males. Intracranial hemorrhage (37.9%) and traumatic brain injury (20.9%) were the most common etiologies. The incidence of weaning failure was 9.8% in the prospective and 7.4% in the CDIC cohort. Age, admission SOFA score, and longitudinal trajectories of GCS motor score, GCS eye score and PaO2/FiO2 were independently associated with weaning failure. The Bayesian joint model demonstrated moderate discrimination in internal validation (overall AUC 0.784; 95% CI: 0.746–0.854), which was corroborated in external validation (overall AUC 0.696; 95% CI: 0.693–0.701) (Figure). The daily AUC showed a gradual improvement over follow-up.

Conclusions: The newly developed model exhibited moderate performance for dynamic prediction of weaning failure in brain injuried patients.

Discrimination performance of the dynamic prediction model in the (A) internal and (B) external validation.

P019 Effectiveness of a weaning protocol for mechanically ventilated patients in the intensive care unit: a before-and-after study.

A Braz Pereira, G Dias Silva Dutra Macedo, A Caroline Pivovar, R De Geus Koerber, L Alba de Matias, D Delvan, V Hugo Silva Pastorello, A Clara Budal, M Marcela Dadam, G Adrieno Westphal

Hospital Municipal São José, Intensive Care Unit, Joinville SC, Brazil

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P019

Introduction: The implementation of a structured weaning protocol can reduce extubation failure within 7 days among patients who have undergone more than 72 h of invasive mechanical ventilation (MV).

Methods: This quasi-experimental before-and-after study was conducted in the ICUs of a tertiary hospital in southern Brazil. Phase I involved retrospective collection of baseline data, and Phase II consisted of prospective data collection after implementation of a structured weaning protocol (checklist to assess eligibility for weaning and spontaneous breathing trial (SBT) [1], cough peak flow assessment [2], evaluation and management of post-extubation laryngeal edema risk [3], SBT on T-piece [4], reconnection to MV for one hour after a successful SBT [5], and prophylactic use of noninvasive ventilation in high-risk patients [6]).

Results: A total of 95 patients were included (50 in Phase I and 45 in Phase II). Extubation failure occurred in 17/50 patients (34%) in Phase I and 8/45 (17%) in Phase II (risk difference –17%, 95% CI –0.7 to 33.1; p = 0.06). The hazard of extubation failure was lower in Phase II (HR 0.41, 95% CI 0.15–1.15), consistent with a potential benefit of the protocol (Figure). In multivariable logistic regression, Phase I patients had higher odds of extubation failure compared with Phase II (OR 2.88, 95% CI 1.05–7.87), while each additional risk factor increased failure odds by 22% (OR 1.22, 95% CI 0.99–1.50).

Conclusions: Despite its exploratory design, this study suggests that implementing a structured weaning protocol may reduce extubation failure, independent of baseline risk factors. Larger randomized trials are needed to confirm these results.

P020 No intervention safely and reliably reduces the incidence of self-extubation in the ICU: a systematic review and meta-analysis

CT Turan, Z Molnár

Semmelweis University, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P020

Introduction: Critically ill patients are often intubated and ventilated. Up to 10% of ICU patients self-extubate [1], leading to a potentially life-threatening situation. Despite advancements in overall ICU care, the risk of self-extubation remains high, and this may lead to patients being physically restrained or oversedated, prolonging the duration of mechanical ventilation or potentially causing harm [2]. Our objective was to investigate whether any patient-care measure in the ICU reliably reduced the incidence of self-extubation.

Methods: A systematic search with citation chasing was conducted on PubMed, Embase, and Cochrane CENTRAL on November 16, 2025, with no restrictions. Trial protocols were sought for the corresponding publications. Randomized controlled trials investigating the effects of different interventions against the incidence of self-extubation attempts in ICUs were eligible for analysis. Risk of bias was assessed with RoB2 tool, and the level of evidence certainty was assessed using GRADE. A meta-analysis was conducted using a random-effects model with inverse variance and Paule-Mandel estimation, with subgroups based on interventions. The pre-study protocol was registered on PROSPERO.

Results: 24 trials involving 5,562 mechanically ventilated patients were identified. Trials were further divided into subgroups based on the measure used versus standard care. Overall, no group of measures provides a significant benefit in reducing the incidence of self-extubation (RR: 1.01, 95% CI: 0.78–1.31, τ≈0.03, Q(23) = 24.68, p > 0.05, I2: 6.8%) (Figure). Overall, there are some concerns with risk of bias due to blinding and adherence, and the evidence certainty is very weak due to indirectness and inconsistency.

Conclusions: No intervention in the ICU seems to reliably reduce the incidence of self-extubation. These results underscore the importance of further research in patient management to ensure optimal safety.

P021 High accuracy of machine learning models in predicting extubation success in mechanically ventilated patients—a systematic review and meta-analysis

P Bakos, B Szabó, D Laczkó, C Turan, S Galin, P Hegyi, L Zubek, A Lovas, Z Molnár

Semmelweis University, Centre for Translational Medicine, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P021

Introduction: Optimal timing of extubation in mechanically ventilated patients remains a challenge in intensive care. Machine learning (ML) models show promise in outcome prediction, but their predictive performance in extubation success is still unclear. The aim of this study was to systematically review and quantitatively evaluate the accuracy of ML models in predicting extubation success.

Methods: A systematic search was performed in PubMed, Embase and CENTRAL up until November 5th, 2024. Studies including mechanically ventilated critically ill adult patients undergoing planned extubation were eligible for this review. The index test was any ML model predicting extubation outcome. Models reporting an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) were meta-analyzed, and subgroup analyses were conducted. Risk of bias was assessed using a modified version of the Quality Assessment of Diagnostic Accuracy Studies-Comparative (QUADAS-C) tool, adapted for ML studies.

Results: Twenty-six studies were eligible for systematic review, and 47 ML models from 14 studies (n = 34,322 patients) were included in the meta-analysis. The AUC of all predictive models ranged from 0.59 to 0.98. Pooled AUCs by model type were 0.88 (95% CI: 0.78–0.94) for traditional ML models and 0.85 (95% CI:0.68–0.94) for deep learning models. The best performing ML models of each study had a pooled AUC of 0.90 (95% CI: 0.82–0.95). Study heterogeneity was high, driven by differences in predictor selection and model design, potentially reducing the generalizability of the synthesized evidence.

Conclusions: Machine learning models demonstrate strong accuracy in predicting extubation success and could form an integral part of future weaning strategies. Prospective clinical trials are needed to standardize and validate ML-based prediction models.

P022 Cardiopulmonary adaptation during spontaneous breathing and its association with weaning outcomes: a prospective physiologic study

JT Park1, J Kim2, YJ Kim3, JH Kim4, S Kim2

1Uijeongbu Saint Mary’s Hospital, Emergency Medicine, Uijeongbusi, Gyeonggido, South Korea, 2Korea University Ansan Hospital, Critical Care Medicine, Ansan, South Korea, 3Korea University Ansan Hospital, Division of Pulmonology, Department of Internal Medicine, Ansan, South Korea, 4 Korea University Ansan Hospital, Pumonology and Critical Care Medicine, Ansan, South Korea

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P022

Introduction: The hemodynamic consequences of transition from positive-pressure ventilation to spontaneous breathing in patients weaning mechanical ventilation are incompletely characterized. We aimed to evaluate the cardiovascular response associated spontaneous breathing trial (SBT) and to determine whether specific hemodynamic parameters are associated with weaning success or failure.

Methods: This single-center prospective observation study included mechanically ventilated adult who met predefined readiness criteria for SBT. Hemodynamic variables, transthoracic echocardiographic parameters, arterial gas analysis results were collected before and during SBT. Successful weaning was defined as maintenance of spontaneous breathing without mechanical ventilation for 72 h.

Results: A total of 186 patients were included, and 130 (69.9%) succeeded weaning. During SBT, respiratory rate increased but PaO2/FiO2 ratio decreased (290 ± 84 vs 254 ± 97, p < 0.001). Hemodynamically, LVEDV (77.5 ± 41.3 vs. 80.2 ± 40.6, p = 0.003) and heart rate (91.0 ± 19.3 vs. 93.1 ± 18.6, p = 0.006) increase, whereas SV was unchanged (p = 0.708). SBT did not influence LV systolic and diastolic function. When compared by weaning outcome, failed weaning was associated with lower PaO2/FiO2 ratio, higher HR, and smaller LVEDV and SV before SBT. Logistic regression identified PaO2/FiO2 ratio, HR and SV as independent predictors of weaning failure (0.992, 0.985–0.999, 0.031; 1.052, 10.21–1.084, 0.001; 0.948, 95% 0.912–0.986, p = 0.008; OR, 95% CI and p value of PaO2/FiO2 ratio, HR, and SV, respectively), but there was no interaction of hemodynamic change and weaning result in linear mixed model.

Conclusions: SBT induces a characteristic hemodynamic pattern of increased preload, maintained stroke volume, and heart-rate–dependent preservation of cardiac output without overt systolic or diastolic cardiac dysfunction. More integrated cardiopulmonary assessment beyond traditional indices should be considered during SBT.

P023 Evaluating bedside clinical variables and signs to assess extubation failure in ICU patients

BA Andom1, CNL Groenland2, EJ Wils3, EA Dubois2, E Ter Bals4, TCD Rettig5, D Gommers2, L Heunks6, H Endeman2, VJM Baggen2

1Myungsung Comprehensive Specialized Hospital, General Practice, Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, 2Erasmus MC, Intensive Care Medicine, Rotterdam, Netherlands, 3Franciscus Gasthuis Vlietland, Intensive Care Medicine, Rotterdam, Netherlands, 4Groene Hart Ziekenhuis, Intensive Care Medicine, Gouda, Netherlands, 5Amphia Ziekenhuis, Intensive Care Medicine, Breda, Netherlands, 6Radboud University Medical Center, Intensive Care Medicine, Nijmegen, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P023

Introduction: Extubation failure occurs in 10–20% of ICU patients despite a successful spontaneous breathing trial (SBT), reflecting the limitations of SBT as a stand-alone readiness assessment. The clinical judgement of a nurse or intensivist likely offers complementary insight into extubation readiness, but it is not a standard component of readiness-to-wean criteria. This study aimed to investigate the prognostic value of readily available clinical variables, nurse-assessed extubation risk and bedside clinical signs in relation to extubation failure.

Methods: Adult ICU patients ventilated ≥ 48 h who underwent an SBT followed by extubation were included in this multicentre cohort. The primary outcome was extubation failure (reintubation ≤ 7 days). Clinical variables (Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS), cough strength and swallowing ability), nurse-assessed extubation risk (5-likert scale) and bedside clinical signs were assessed before SBT or extubation. Bedside clinical signs included; thumbs-up sign (patient gives a thumbs-up after researcher gives a thumb-up), visual pursuit sign (patient follows researcher in the room), crossed-leg sign (patient lies with crossed legs in bed). Univariable logistic regression and multivariable logistic regression analyses were performed.

Results: Of 150 patients, 33 (22%) failed extubation. Mean age was 63 ± 13 years and 61% were male. In univariable analyses, stronger cough strength, intact swallowing ability, and a positive thumbs-up sign were associated with lower odds of extubation failure, while higher nurse-assessed extubation risk was associated with increased odds of failure (Table). GCS and the other bedside signs showed no significant association.

Conclusions: Systematic bedside evaluation of clinical variables and signs offers readily available prognostic information in assessing extubation outcomes. Future studies should further investigate these clinical signs and clarify how they can be integrated into structured extubation assessments.

P024 Correlations between biomarkers, cardiac and lung ultrasound during spontaneous breathing trials

CNL Groenland1, EJ Wils2, EA Dubois1, E Ter Bals3, TCD Rettig4, D Gommers1, L Heunks5, H Endeman6, VJM Baggen1

1Erasmus MC, Intensive Care Medicine, Rotterdam, Netherlands, 2Franciscus Gasthuis Vlietland, Intensive Care Medicine, Rotterdam, Netherlands, 3Groene Hart Ziekenhuis, Intensive Care Medicine, Gouda, Netherlands, 4Amphia Ziekenhuis, Intensive Care Medicine, Breda, Netherlands, 5Radboud University Medical Center, Intensive Care Medicine, Nijmegen, Netherlands, 6OLVG, Intensive Care Medicine, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P024

Introduction: Cardiac dysfunction, respiratory disease and increased inflammation are potential contributors to extubation failure. Biomarkers, cardiac and lung ultrasound (LUS) can help to identify abnormalities, whereby the correlation between biomarkers and ultrasound may help to further understand the pathophysiological pathways. This study aimed to investigate the correlation of biomarkers with cardiac and lung ultrasound measurements during spontaneous breathing trials (SBT).

Methods: We included patients ventilated ≥ 48 h, undergoing an SBT and extubation in a multicenter cohort study. Blood samples and ultrasound measurements were obtained at start and end of the SBT. Biomarkers included NT-proBNP, hs-TnT, CK-MB, ST-2, galectin-3, Cystatin-C, GDF-15, CA-125, albumin, procalcitonin, IL-6, CRP. Cardiac ultrasound assessed systolic and diastolic function. LUS was performed according to the BLUE protocol, with a maximum LUS score of 18 (max. anterior score 12). Correlations were assessed using Spearman’s rank correlation.

Results: We included 93 patients (mean age 62 ± 14 years, 61% male). Extubation failure occurred in 22%, of whom 76% had extubation failure due to respiratory or cardiac failure. NT-proBNP, hs-TnT, Galectin-3, Cystatin-C had moderate/good significant correlations with LV systolic dysfunction, E/e’, and left atrial area (Figure). NT-proBNP and LUS score showed a moderate significant correlation. Poor correlations were found between ultrasound measurements and inflammatory biomarkers (procalcitonin, CRP, IL-6). Correlations at the end of the SBT were similar.

Conclusions: Several cardiac biomarkers correlated with echocardiographic measurements of systolic and diastolic dysfunction, supporting their shared pathophysiological origin. NT-proBNP showed a modest correlation with LUS score. Cardiac biomarkers and ultrasound measurements may provide complementary information during an SBT, which may facilitate a balanced pathophysiological assessment used to improve extubation failure prediction.

P025 Effect of sedation on respiratory drive and effort in patients with acute hypoxemic respiratory failure during the early phases of assisted mechanical ventilation

S Giovanazzi

University of Brescia, Department of Medical and Surgical Specialties, Radiological Sciences and Public Health, Brescia, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P025

Introduction: Patients with acute hypoxemic respiratory failure (AHRF) may have difficulty maintaining optimal inspiratory effort while on assisted mechanical ventilation (AMV). While sedatives can help regulate respiratory drive and effort, deep sedation may negatively affect short and long-term outcomes, making an optimal sedation strategy uncertain. This study aims to investigate the effect of sedation on respiratory drive, effort, transpulmonary driving pressure, and asynchrony rate.

Methods: This is an observational study on AHRF patients in the first 48 h of receiving AMV, with opiod-free sedation. Sedation and respiratory support level were choosed based on clinical practice. We considered 5-min patient segments every 6 h in which sedation and respiratory data were recorded: Patient State Index (PSI) and Richmond Agitation-Sedation Scale (RASS), airway pressure in the first 100 ms (P0.1) and the rate of increase in Pmus over inspiratory time (Pmus, rate = Pmus/∆t), esophageal pressure swing (∆Pes) and esophageal pressure time product (PTPPes). Depending on the outcome distribution, linear and generalized linear mixed effects regression models were applied, adjusted for patient respiratory mechanical characteristics, drug used and ventilator settings.

Results: We analyzed 2809 respiratory breaths of 11 AHRF patients, sedated with either propofol (66.9%) or dexmedetomidine (33.1%).When the PSI increases, P0.1 (p < 0.001), Pmus, rate (p = 0.033), ∆Pes (p = 0.041), PTPPes (p < 0.036) and ∆PLdyn (p < 0.001) increase as well while a non-significant decrease in AI was observed (Figure). When considering RASS, there was no evidence of sedation’s effect on drive and effort.

Conclusions: In patients with AHRF, light-moderate sedation can help maintain a low respiratory drive and effort during the early phases of AMV. Compared with RASS, sedation guided by processed EEG appears more effective, as it helps avoid overly deep sedation and supports achieving the appropriate sedation level.

P026 Adaptive ventilation modes reduce length of mechanical ventilation: a systematic review and meta-analysis

PR Tóth1, Z Weber1, R Aimene2, L Dávid3, C Turan1, P Hegyi4, Z Sipos2, Z Molnár1, A Lorx1

1Semmelweis University, Intensive Therapy, Budapest, Hungary, 2Semmelweis University, Centre of Translational Medicine, Budapest, Hungary, 3Semmelweis University, Interventional Radiology, Budapest, Hungary, 4Semmelweis University, Institute of Pancreatic Disease, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P026

Introduction: Adaptive modes aim to optimize patient-ventilator synchrony, reduce work of breathing, and potentially decrease staff workload. Despite increasing use, their clinical advantages over conventional protocolized ventilation remain uncertain. Therefore, we investigated whether adaptive modes reduce the duration of ventilation compared to conventional modes.

Methods: We systematically searched MEDLINE, EMBASE, CENTRAL for articles from inception until November of 2024. Randomized and observational studies comparing adaptive and conventional ventilation methods reporting duration of mechanical ventilation (DMV), invasive ventilation duration, weaning and extubation time and success, ventilator-free days, ICU and hospital length of stay (LOS-ICU, LOS-H), and 28-day mortality were eligible for inclusion. Meta-analyses were performed using META and DMETAR packages in R with a random effects model. Heterogeneity was assessed according to the Cochrane Handbook using I2 and tau2. Risk of bias was assessed using ROBINS for observational studies and Rob2 for RCTs. The study was registered to PROSPERO (ID: CRD42024605368).

Results: 46 studies with 4231 patients were included. DMV showed 1.21 days shortening in case of adaptive modes in the pooled result (MD: -1.21 days; 95% CI: -1.80; -0.63) (Figure). Adaptive support ventilation (ASV) shortens the total mechanical ventilation duration (MD: –1.4 days; 95% CI: –2.14; –0.66) and PAV (proportional assist ventilation) shortens it by 1.8 days (MD: -1.81; 95% CI: -2.50; -1.11). In the NAVA (neurally adjust ventilation assist) subgroup we found that it has 2.11 higher odds for successful weaning compared to conventional (OR: 2.11; 95% CI: 1.04; 4.29). Adaptive modes shorten LOS-H (MD: -1.14 days; 95% CI: -1.70; -0.59) and PAV shortens both LOS-ICU (MD: -1.27 days; 95% CI: -2.01; -0.52) and LOS-H (MD: -1.81days; 95% CI: -2.36 to -1.27).

Conclusions: Adaptive ventilation modes, including PAV and ASV, significantly reduce the total duration of mechanical ventilation.

P027 Assessing diaphragm dysfunction: concordance between the gold standard bilateral anterior magnetic phrenic nerve stimulation, diaphragm ultrasound and maximal inspiratory pressure—a pilot study

R Zangari, R Contarino, S Landini, L Ceresoli, A Bonvini, S Piva, F Rasulo, N Latronico, M Bertoni

Department of Anesthesia, Intensive Care and Emergency, ASST Spedali Civili University Hospital, Università degli Studi di Brescia, Brescia, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P027

Introduction: Diaphragmatic dysfunction (DD) contributes to ventilatory impairment and prolonged weaning. Bilateral Anterior Magnetic Phrenic Nerve Stimulation (BAMPS) with Ptr,stim is the diagnostic gold standard, but its use is limited by technical complexity. Simpler bedside measures such as maximal inspiratory pressure (MIP) and ultrasound-derived diaphragmatic thickening fraction (TFdi) assess different physiological domains—global inspiratory strength and diaphragm contractility—but their concordance with BAMPS remains unclear. We aimed to evaluate the concordance of Ptr,stim, MIP and TFdi for diagnosing DD and their ability to predict spontaneous breathing trial (SBT) failure.

Methods: We conducted a prospective study in high-risk adult ICU patients, as defined by Hernández et al. [1], mechanically ventilated ≥ 48 h and fulfilling SBT readiness criteria according to Boles et al. [2]. SBTs were performed with minimal pressure support. During the SBT, each patient underwent three BAMPS-derived Ptr,stim measurements, three TFdi assessments and three MIP maneuvers. Concordance with BAMPS was quantified using percentage concordance, Cohen’s kappa and McNemar’s test. Prediction of SBT failure was evaluated using ROC curves (AUC, 95% CI, Youden index).

Results: 50 patients were included. Concordance with BAMPS was limited: MIP showed 64% concordance (κ = 0.12), while TFdi showed 44% (κ = –0.06) with significant under-detection (McNemar p =0.03) (Figure). Predictive performance for SBT failure was modest, with AUCs of 0.611 (Ptr,stim), 0.629 (MIP) and 0.686 (TFdi), the latter being statistically significant.

Conclusions: Neither MIP nor TFdi showed sufficient concordance with BAMPS to independently diagnose DD and all measures demonstrated limited ability to predict SBT failure. However, because MIP and TFdi reflect distinct physiological domains, their combined use may provide complementary bedside information and support a multimodal approach to diaphragm assessment in critically ill patients.

Both MIP and TFdi show limited concordance with the gold standard. The McNemar test indicates that TFdi has a poor ability to detect diaphragm dysfunction, leading to more false negatives. Right: ROC curves for SBT failure prediction, analyzed using Ptr, stim at BAMPS (blue), MIP (green) or Tfdi (orange)

P029 Low accuracy of detecting ventilatory dyssynchrony using artificial intelligence

V Zvonicek1, V Zvonicek Jr.2, J Pažout3, P Waldauf3, M Nemy4, M Macik4, L Vyslouzilova4

1University Hospital Královské Vinohrady, Department of Anaesthesia and Intensive Care Medicine, Prague, Czech Republic, 2ETH, Zürich, Switzerland, 3University Hospital Královské Vinohrady, Prague, Czech Republic, 4Czech Institute of Informatics, Robotics and Cybernetics, Czech Technical University, Prague, Czech Republic

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P029

Introduction: The aim of the study was to evaluate the precision of the SmartAlert classifiers in detecting ventilatory dyssynchrony. SmartAlert system is a machine learning-based tool designed to enhance the detection of patient-ventilator dyssynchronies (VD) in the intensive care unit (ICU) settings [1]. The system uses state-of-the-art signal processing techniques and deep learning models to analyse data from ventilator screens, capturing and classifying VD, and to predict alarm level. When the alarm threshold is reached, ventilator screen images, along with an analysis report, are sent to physicians’ mobile phones (Figure).

Methods: The study validated the SmartAlert classifier following its implementation into ICU. To evaluate the classifier’s accuracy, 500 ventilator screen images were randomly selected as a sample. The physicians then rated the correctness of the AI-detected assessment of dyssynchronies and ventilation parameters on the selected images. The classifier’s accuracy was calculated for three types of dyssynchronies — double cycling (DC), ineffective effort (IE), and high inspiratory effort (hIE) — and for ventilatory parameters — tidal volume (VTE), respiratory rate) (RR) and peak inspiratory pressure (Ppeak).

Results: The accuracy of SmartAlert in detecting DC, hIE and IE was 62%, 53% and 87%, respectively. The accuracy evaluating VTE, RR and Ppeak was 92%, 94% and 99%, respectively. All dyssynchronies on individual reports were correctly identified in 23% of the reports.

Conclusions: The accuracy of the AI-based assessment of ventilator dyssynchronies is too low to be used in clinical practice.

Acknowledgement: Supported by Grant Ministry of Health, Czech Republic, AZV NU22-06–00625.

P030 The LoVe score: development and validation of a bedside clinical index to identify patients at risk for prolonged invasive mechanical ventilation

PA Hilders1, L Lijovic2, M Otten1, L Biesheuvel1, A Jagesar1, O Sigling1, PWG Elbers1

1Amsterdam UMC, Amsterdam, Netherlands, 2University Hospital Center Sestre Milosrdnice, Zagreb, Croatia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P030

Introduction: Early management decisions after intubation, such as humidification strategy or initiation of prevention bundles for ventilator-associated pneumonia and delirium, often depend on the expected duration of mechanical ventilation. However, no simple, standardised method exists to support such estimates at the bedside. We aimed to develop and validate the Length of mechanical Ventilation (LoVe) index (Figure), a bedside score to predict prolonged invasive mechanical ventilation beyond 48 h using peri-intubation data.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective cohort study using two large, publicly available ICU databases: AmsterdamUMCdb for model development and internal validation, and MIMIC-IV for external validation. The primary outcome was prolonged IMV, defined as support beyond 48 h or death within 48 h. Two LoVe variants were derived: a ventilator-inclusive version incorporating ventilator settings, and a ventilator-agnostic version without ventilator settings. Predictors identified via logistic regression were discretised into clinically interpretable ranges and summed into an additive score. Clinical utility was assessed using decision curve analysis.

Results: In the cardiac surgery and non-cardiac surgery cohorts, the ventilator-inclusive LoVe index achieved AUROC scores of 0.82 and 0.75, respectively. The ventilator-agnostic version reached 0.72 in the non-cardiac surgery cohort. Validation in MIMIC-IV yielded 0.71 and 0.68 for the ventilator-inclusive version, and 0.71 and 0.65 for the ventilator-agnostic index. Decision curve analysis demonstrated that both LoVe indices provided meaningful clinical utility.

Conclusions: The LoVe index provides a simple, interpretable bedside tool to estimate the likelihood of prolonged IMV early after intubation, showing consistent external validity and potential to support early ICU decision-making.

Higher scores reflect higher predicted risk of prolonged invasive mechanical ventilation (IMV). (B) Calibration curve mapping LoVe scores to the estimated probability of prolonged IMV

P031 Pulmonary melioidosis and lung ultrasound: a diagnostic challenge

PY Gonzalez Noris

ISSSTE, ICU, La Paz, Mexico

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P031

Introduction: Pulmonary melioidosis, which is caused by Burkholderia pseudomallei, presents significant diagnostic challenges due to its different clinical findings, often mimicking other infections or malignancies. [1]. The present case study attempts to enhance the efficient use of lung ultrasound as a diagnostic tool while also emphasizing the limitations of less comprehensive imaging methods [2].

Methods: This is a case report with detailed clinical data, including lung ultrasound and microbiological culture. Informed consent was obtained.

Results: A 23-year-old male exhibited a one-month history of cough, fever, and dyspnea. His past medical history included diabetes mellitus, which is a known risk factor for severe melioidosis [3]. A bronchial secretion culture was obtained; Burkholderia pseudomallei grew. The first examination with a 12-zone lung ultrasound revealed diffuse B-lines, subpleural consolidations, and dynamic air bronchograms, indicative of significant inflammatory alterations and inadequate alveolar ventilation. Peripheral nodules were also visible [Figure]. Additionally, we are finding hypoechoic images in the spleen.

Conclusions: This case points out the essential role of lung ultrasound in the diagnosis of pulmonary melioidosis, especially in resource-limited settings where advanced imaging may be unavailable. Likewise, lung ultrasound offers a noninvasive, radiation-free, and cost-effective method for assessing disease progression and treatment effectiveness in these complex cases. This technique significantly enhances early detection and facilitates timely initiation of treatment, resulting in improved patient outcomes in regions where melioidosis is prevalent.

P032 Ultrasound evaluation of the parasternal intercostal muscles and diaphragm activity post-operative mediastinal tumor with unilateral diaphragmatic dysfunction

WEI Wu, FEI Han

Zhongshan hospital, Fudan University, Department of Critical Care, Shanghai, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P032

Introduction: Few studies focus on the compensatory role of the intercostal muscles in respiratory effort in the patients with post-operative unilateral diaphragmatic dysfunction using bedside ultrasound due to phrenic nerve injury.

Methods: This prospective observational study included patients who underwent extended resection of mediastinal tumors and were subsequently admitted to the SICU. Prompted completion of baseline ultrasound assessments within 12 h of ICU admission post-operation to evaluate bilateral diaphragm excursion and thickening fraction, as well as intercostal muscle thickening fraction. For patients during their ICU stay, kindly record diaphragm and intercostal muscle ultrasound data upon discharged the ICU. The primary outcome of this study is the occurrence of postoperative respiratory failure requiring respiratory support, which includes high-flow oxygen, non-invasive ventilation, and intubation.

Results: A total of 40 patients (23 males, average age 48.6 years) were ultimately included in the final analysis. Within this cohort, 21 received respiratory support and 19 received conventional oxygen therapy (COT). At ICU admission, the respiratory support group exhibited higher TFic, RR, and TFic/TFdi, which decreased significantly at ICU discharge. In contrast, the COT group had slight increases in TFic and RR at ICU discharge compared to ICU admission, with both groups showing similar TFic, RR, and TFic/TFdi at discharge. Both groups showed an increasing trend in bilateral TFdi.

Conclusions: This study uncovered that 50% of patients with diaphragm dysfunction following surgery for mediastinal tumors required postoperative respiratory support treatment (RS). Upon admission, the respiratory treatment group displayed higher TFic, RR, and TFic/TFdi, but these indicators appeared to converge between the two groups at discharge. The trend for TFdi was similar in both groups, showing an increase from baseline to discharge, with the respiratory treatment group having a higher baseline TFdi.

P033 Polysulfide-mediated lung protection in ARDS: effects and mechanisms in cell and mouse models

E Kanemaru, K Takeyama, A Sato, K Tojo, T Goto

Yokohama City University School of Medicine, Anesthesiology and Critical Care Medicine, Yokohama, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P033

Introduction: Acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) lacks effective pharmacological therapy, and new therapeutic strategies are urgently needed. Sulfur metabolism is involved in oxidative stress and inflammatory responses. Polysulfides, which contain sulfane sulfur (S0), exhibit versatile reactivity as nucleophiles and electrophiles [1]; however, their potential effects in ARDS remain unclear. This study aimed to evaluate the lung-protective effects of polysulfides and elucidate their underlying mechanisms.

Methods: In an in vitro alveolar epithelial cell injury model by co-culture of isolated neutrophils and LPS, the effects of sodium trisulfide (Na2S3) and sodium tetrasulfide (Na2S4) on cell viability, S0 release quantified by SSP4 fluorescence, expression of GDF15, Slc7a11, and inflammatory cytokines quantified by qPCR, and maximal mitochondrial respiration measured by Seahorse were evaluated. In vivo, Na2S3 was administered intraperitoneally to LPS-induced ARDS mice, and inflammatory cell counts in the bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) were analyzed.

Results: Na2S3 improved cell viability in a dose-dependent manner, with Na2S4 demonstrating similar effects (Figure, panels A, B). Both agents showed greater cytoprotective effects than sodium sulfide (Na2S), lacking S0 (Figure, panel B). The level of S0 inside MLE12 cells increased with increasing concentration of Na2S3 and Na2S4 (Figure, panel C). Na2S3 increased GDF15 expression and significantly reduced Slc7a11 and inflammatory cytokine expression (IL-1β, IL-6, TNF-α). Maximal mitochondrial respiratory capacity was dose-dependently restored (Figure, panel D). In ARDS mice, Na2S3 significantly reduced total inflammatory cell counts in BAL (Figure, panel E).

Conclusions: Polysulfides exerted lung-protective effects in ARDS through protection of mitochondrial function, anti-inflammatory effects partially mediated by GDF15, and suppression of ferroptosis pathways. These findings suggest polysulfides as promising therapeutic candidates for ARDS.

Results. BAL, bronchoalveolar lavage; LPS, lipopolysaccharide; MLE12, a murine alveolar epithelial cell; N, neutrophils; Na2S, sodium sulfide; Na2S3, sodium trisulfide; Na2S4, sodium tetrasulfide; OCR, oxygen consumption rate; PBS, phosphate-buffered saline

P034 Age and immune status modify the harm of high arterial oxygen partial pressure level in ARDS: a causal forest analysis of the ARDSnet cohort

S Hu, Y Yang

Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P034

Introduction: The relationship between arterial oxygen partial pressure (PaO₂) and mortality in ARDS is unclear. Heterogeneity in the association between high PaO₂ and mortality across patient subgroups remains uncertain. The relationship between arterial oxygen partial pressure (PaO₂) and mortality in ARDS is unclear. Heterogeneity in the association between high PaO₂ and mortality across patient subgroups remains uncertain.

Methods: A cohort of 4,540 ARDS patients from the ARDSnet database was analyzed. Restricted cubic splines identified 98.5 mmHg as a potential threshold in the association between day-1 PaO₂ and 28-day mortality. The conditional average treatment effect (CATE) of high PaO₂ (≥ 98.5 mmHg on day 1) on mortality was estimated using causal forest models, adjusting for FiO₂, PEEP, illness severity, comorbidities, and physiological variables, with overlap weighting to ensure valid inference.

Results: High PaO₂ was associated with a 3.5% higher absolute risk of 28-day mortality. The effect varied by age and immune status: CATE increased with age, reaching 0.045 (95% CI: 0.035–0.055) in patients > 65 years (Figure). Immunosuppressed individuals showed greater risk, particularly when elderly (interaction p = 0.049). Older immunosuppressed patients had nearly twice the risk difference compared with younger, immune-competent counterparts.

Conclusions: The mortality risk associated with high PaO₂ in ARDS is modified by age and immune status. Older immunosuppressed patients are especially vulnerable, supporting individualized oxygen targets in this high-risk group.

P035 Power, duration and compliance: reframing the risk of ventilatory induced lung injury with the RAMP score

L Lijovic1, P Hilders2, T Radocaj1, M Siranovic1, PW Elbers3

1Clinical Hospital Centre Sestre Milosrdnice, Intensive Care, Zagreb, Croatia, 2Amsterdam UMC, Intensive Care, Amsterdam, Netherlands, 3 Amsterdam UMC, Adult Intensive Care, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P035

Introduction: Static thresholds for mechanical power may not prevent ventilator-induced lung injury because risk depends on exposure duration and the underlying lung compliance. We wanted to quantify how mechanical power intensity and exposure duration interact with lung compliance to predict oxygenation changes consistent with acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) worsening or 14-day mortality.

Methods: A retrospective analysis of mechanically ventilated adults with moderate to severe ARDS in two large intensive care databases was performed. Time-dependent Cox proportional hazards models stratified by lung compliance estimated the hour-specific associations of immediate exceedance and cumulative time above mechanical power thresholds with the primary outcome. Estimated effects were integrated into a risk-adjusted mechanical power (RAMP) score, which was then evaluated as a predictor of ARDS worsening or 14-day mortality using XGBoost.

Results: Among 2,150 ARDS patients risk, from mechanical power exposure was dictated by lung compliance: in higher-compliance lungs, risk followed a dose–response pattern, with immediate hazard beginning at 10 J/min (HR≈1.04) and cumulative harm amplifying significantly over time (Figure). Conversely, for low-compliance patients, risk was confined to a narrow power band (11–20 J/min) without evidence of cumulative harm. With RAMP score as a predictor of outcome XGBoost yielded an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 0.874 (precision 0.807, recall 0.494; F1 0.612); PaO2/FiO2 remained the dominant predictor.

Conclusions: A single ‘safe’ mechanical power threshold is insufficient for guiding ventilation; the risk of lung injury is governed by a dynamic interplay of power intensity, duration, and the patient’s lung compliance. The risk-adjusted mechanical power score unifies these factors into a time-varying, clinically interpretable metric that warrants prospective validation for personalized ventilator management.

Each panel shows threshold on the y axis and cumulative hours spent above that threshold on the x axis. Dotted line represents cutoff of less than 100 cases assumed necessary for a reliable analysis

P036 Pulmonary effects of two lung-protective ventilation strategies in an experimental swine model of ARDS

F Rodríguez, M Pécora, R Beltramelli, A Cattivelli, A Guerrero, MJ García, AL Suárez, C Morais, P Alzugaray, M Angulo

Hospital de Clinicas, Laboratorio de Exploración Funcional Respiratoria, Montevideo, Uruguay

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P036

Introduction: Mechanical ventilation (MV) can worsen lung injury in acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS), and which ventilation strategy better reduces this damage still remains nuclear.

Methods: Lung injury was induced in pigs using a two-hit model consisting of bronchoalveolar lavages followed by injurious MV. Animals were randomized to either the ARDS Network strategy (ARDSNet, n = 8) or the Open Lung Approach (OLA, n = 8). In the ARDSNet group, PEEP was set according to the low PEEP/FiO₂ table. In the OLA group, PEEP was guided by electrical impedance tomography (EIT) after a recruitment maneuver, selecting the level that minimized both alveolar collapse and overdistension. Ventilation continued for 6 h (T0-1–2-3). A Control group (n = 6) without injury received 6 h of protective ventilation with EIT-guided PEEP. Ventilatory mechanics, EIT (ventilation and perfusion, V/Q), and blood gases were recorded, followed by lung histology. Data (mean ± SEM) were analyzed using repeated-measures ANOVA.

Results: The OLA group maintained higher PaO₂/FiO₂, lower driving pressure, and lower shunt fraction (p < 0.05), approaching Controls (Figure, panels A–D). Optimal V/Q distribution was 63% in Controls, 50% in OLA, and 36% in ARDSNet (p < 0.05). ARDSNet showed dorsal ventilation loss with ventral shift, whereas OLA preserved more homogeneous ventilation. Perfusion maps revealed reduced dorsal perfusion and ventral inhomogeneity in ARDSNet, while OLA maintained greater dorsal perfusion and overall homogeneity. In V/Q maps, OLA showed better matching and less shunt and dead space than ARDSNet (Figure, panel E). Wet-to-dry ratio and septal thickness were lower in Controls (p < 0.05), with no differences between OLA and ARDSNet. Air-to-tissue ratio was higher in Controls and OLA than in ARDSNet (p = 0.06) (Figure, panel F).

Conclusions: OLA was associated with better gas exchange, improved V/Q matching, more favorable ventilatory mechanics, and reduced histological lung injury.

P038 A deep learning approach using chest X-rays for assessing oxygenation status in mechanically ventilated patients

YC Jeong1, C Kang1, LR Lim2, HN Lee2, HG Ryu1

1Seoul National University Hospital, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Seoul, South Korea, 2Seoul National University Hospital, Department of Anesthesiology and Pain Medicine, Seoul, South Korea

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P038

Introduction: Most of deep learning algorithm available are based on standardized images. Chest X-ray (CXR) images in critically ill patients diversely vary and thus challenging to apply general deep learning programs for clinical aid. This study aims to design deep learning algorithm task to predict change in clinical status of intubated intensive care unit (ICU) patients from CXRs.

Methods: In this retrospective model development study, we first trained a publicly available deep learning model including a pooled dataset of CXRs (n = 373,343) from 11,438 ICU patients. After self-supervised learning, each X-ray was paired with patients’ laboratory data and was trained to differentiate between normal (PaO2/FiO2 > 300) and abnormal (PaO2/FiO2 < 300) CXRs. The model was tested in fully-supervised learning in three groups: for training (n = 3,879), for validation (n = 453), and for internal testing (n = 423). The downstream task was to predict whether the CXR improved or deteriorated between two consecutive CXR images. We conducted the downstream tasks in three distinct phases of increasing complexity. Model performance was evaluated using accuracy, sensitivity, specificity, F1 score, and generated a receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve with its corresponding area under the curve (AUC) to assess its ability to capture changes across the entire P/F ratio distribution.

Results: The ability of the X-ray model to detect change in four distinct categories of P/F ratio resulted in an AUROC of 0.948, accuracy of 0.957, sensitivity of 95.7%, specificity of 83.3%, and F1 score of 0.898. The model’s performance exhibited an inverse relationship with task complexity, AUROC and accuracy was 0.711, 0.635 for task 2, and 0.658, 0.621 for task 3, respectively.

Conclusions: A deep learning model can be trained to predict X-ray changes correlated with P/F ratio. Further research and external validation and fine-tuning is needed to support clinical practice in predicting oxygenation of mechanical ventilated patient in ICU.

P039 Association between organ perfusion pressure and mortality in older patients with ARDS

K Gkirgkiris1, G Konstantinou1, D Tsilivarakis1, S Theodosis1, AM Manousaki1, I Siempos2

1National and Kapodistrian University of Athens Medical School, Athens, Greece, 2National and Kapodistrian University of Athens Medical School, First Department of Critical Care Medicine and Pulmonary Services, Athens, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P039

Introduction: Organ perfusion pressure, defined as the difference between mean arterial pressure and central venous pressure, may be used for personalized hemodynamic management of critically ill patients, including those with acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) [1]. We explored whether the association between baseline organ perfusion pressure and mortality depends on age.

Methods: We performed a secondary analysis of individual patient-level data from six ARDS Network and PETAL Network randomized controlled trials [2–7]. We conducted splines regression analyses that included an interaction term between organ perfusion pressure and age. Both organ perfusion pressure and age were treated as continuous variables.

Results: 3046 patients with ARDS were included. Organ perfusion pressure was independently associated with 90-day all-cause mortality (p = 0.022). Their association was non-linear, specifically U-shaped. The association between organ perfusion pressure and mortality depended on age (p = 0.018). As age increased, the range of organ perfusion pressure values associated with lower mortality (i.e., the U-shaped “valley”) appeared narrower (Figure).

Conclusions: As age increases, the range of organ perfusion pressure values associated with lower mortality becomes narrower. This finding suggests that advanced hemodynamic monitoring may be required especially for older patients.

P040 Bedside measurement of exhaled nitric oxide in mechanically ventilated patients: feasibility, reproducibility, and association with lung injury phenotypes

H Wang, MING Zhong

Zhongshan Hospital, Fudan University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Shanghai, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P040

Introduction: Exhaled nitric oxide (FeNO) is a noninvasive biomarker of airway and alveolar inflammation, yet its performance in mechanically ventilated adults and its ability to differentiate early aspiration pneumonitis (AP) from acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) remain unclear.

Methods: We prospectively measured FeNO using online chemiluminescence in mechanically ventilated ICU patients. Measurement reproducibility was assessed using paired FeNO recordings obtained 3–5 min apart. FeNO levels were compared among postoperative controls, early AP, and ARDS. Diagnostic performance for distinguishing AP from ARDS was evaluated using receiver operating characteristic (ROC) analysis.

Results: Fifty mechanically ventilated patients underwent repeated FeNO assessment, demonstrating excellent reproducibility (bias − 0.055 ppb; limits of agreement − 0.91 to 0.80 ppb; ICC 0.978) (Figure). FeNO levels differed significantly across clinical groups: postoperative controls showed low baseline FeNO, AP patients exhibited markedly elevated levels, while ARDS patients had the lowest concentrations (p < 0.0001). FeNO showed outstanding discrimination between AP and ARDS, yielding an AUC of 0.975. The optimal cutoff of 3.17 ppb provided sensitivity 0.886 and specificity 0.971.

Conclusions: FeNO can be measured reliably in mechanically ventilated adults and demonstrates distinct patterns across postoperative controls, early aspiration pneumonitis, and ARDS. A FeNO threshold of 3.17 ppb accurately differentiates AP from ARDS, suggesting potential clinical utility for early phenotype identification and respiratory injury stratification.
