P041 The impact of autoimmune diseases on the development and outcomes of acute respiratory distress syndrome: a retrospective cohort study

YH Ni, N Shi, H Chen, HB Qiu

Zhongda Hospital, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P041

Introduction: Patients with autoimmune diseases (AIDs) have dysregulated innate immunity, potentially increasing ARDS susceptibility [1]. Given ARDS heterogeneity and inconsistent data on the prognosis of AIDs [2], this study aimed to determine the association between AIDs and ARDS and to identify distinct ARDS phenotypes incorporating underlying autoimmune characteristics.

Methods: This retrospective observational study utilized the MIMIC-IV database as the derivation cohort (n = 9282). The validation cohort (n = 506) was based on the Chinese Database in Intensive Care (CDIC), which comprises data from ICU patients at Zhongda Hospital. The association between AIDs and ARDS was analyzed, and ARDS immune phenotypes were identified through consensus K-means clustering.

Results: AIDs were independently associated with ARDS (OR = 1.30, p = 0.032). However, among patients who developed ARDS, pre-existing AIDs were not independently associated with 28-day in-hospital mortality. Cluster analysis revealed two distinct ARDS phenotypes: Phenotype 1 with severe organ dysfunction and higher mortality (36.7% vs. 18.5%, HR:0.44 (0.37–0.52), p < 0.001), and Phenotype 2 characterized by a higher prevalence of AIDs, milder inflammation, and significantly lower mortality (Figure). Treatment responses were heterogeneous; corticosteroid use was associated with increased mortality in both phenotypes, while disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs (DMARDs) were linked to significantly reduced mortality in the hyperinflammatory Phenotype 1 (13.7% vs. 33.2%, p = 0.006). These key findings were partially validated in the independent validation cohort.

Conclusions: Pre-existing AIDs increase the risk of ARDS, but the prognosis is determined by the specific immune phenotype, not the underlying autoimmune condition itself. These findings underscore the potential for phenotype-guided therapy to improve outcomes.

P042 Inhaled nitric oxide reduces mortality in severe ARDS with pulmonary hypertension: meta-analysis of 6 RCTs

V Gherghina1, I Cîndea1, A Balcan2, D Costea3, R Popescu3

1SCJU Constanta, ATI, Constanta, Romania, 2Facultatea de Medicin 259, ATI, Constanta, Romania, 3Facultatea de Medicin259, Chirurgie, Constanta, Romania

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P042

Introduction: Right ventricular failure from pulmonary hypertension (mPAP > 35 mmHg) complicates 25–35% of severe ARDS with 60–80% mortality. Large trials (EOLIA, ROSE) showed no mortality benefit from inhaled nitric oxide (iNO) in unselected ARDS populations. We hypothesized iNO benefits the PH-ARDS phenotype through selective pulmonary vasodilation and RV protection.

Methods: Systematic review (PROSPERO; PRISMA 2020) of RCTs comparing iNO (5–40 ppm) versus placebo/standard care in mechanically ventilated severe ARDS (PaO2/FiO2 ≤ 150) with confirmed PH (mPAP > 35 mmHg by echocardiography/Swan-Ganz). Searched MEDLINE, Embase, Cochrane, ClinicalTrials.gov through November 2025. Primary outcome: 28-day mortality. Secondary: mPAP change at 24h, PaO2/FiO2 improvement, RV function (RV/LV ratio), ventilator-free days. Random-effects meta-analysis with pre-specified subgroups by PH severity (mPAP > 40 vs 35–40 mmHg). Trial sequential analysis, RoB 2.0, GRADE assessment.

Results: Six RCTs included (n = 389; iNO 194, control 195). Baseline: PaO2/FiO2 112 ± 22 mmHg, mPAP 40.2 ± 4.8 mmHg. iNO significantly reduced 28-day mortality: RR 0.81 (95%CI 0.66–0.99; I2 = 24%; NNT = 11). Absolute mortality: 38.1% versus 47.2%; ARR 9.1%. Secondary outcomes: mPAP reduction -8.2 mmHg (-11.4 to -4.9), PaO2/FiO2 improvement + 42 mmHg (+ 28 to + 56), RV/LV ratio -0.18 (-0.26 to -0.10), ventilator-free days + 3.8 (+ 1.9 to + 5.7). Severe PH subgroup (mPAP > 40, n = 218): greater mortality reduction, RR 0.72 (0.56–0.92; NNT = 6); ARR 13.5%. TSA confirmed adequate power. Low bias risk (5/6 trials), no publication bias. Methemoglobinemia < 2%. GRADE: High certainty for hemodynamic outcomes, moderate for mortality.

Conclusions: iNO significantly reduces mortality in severe ARDS with PH (NNT = 11), with dose–response favoring mPAP > 40 (NNT = 6). Echo-guided phenotyping enables precision therapy, transforming previously unsuccessful intervention into targeted life-saving strategy, challenging current guidelines against routine iNO use.

P043 Independent and additive effects of inhaled nitric oxide and prone positioning on oxygenation in COVID-19 ARDS

H Wang, M Zhong

Zhongshan Hospital, Fudan University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Shanghai, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P043

Introduction: Inhaled nitric oxide (iNO) and prone positioning (PP) are commonly used to improve oxygenation in acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS), but their combined physiological effects in COVID-19 ARDS (CARDS) remain unclear. We evaluated the independent and additive effects of iNO and PP and explored predictors of treatment responsiveness using electrical impedance tomography (EIT).

Methods: In this prospective study, 24 mechanically ventilated CARDS patients underwent four sequential phases: baseline supine (SP), iNO 15–20 ppm in supine for 1 h (SPNO), prone without iNO for 1 h (PP), and combined PP + iNO for 1 h (PPNO). At each phase, gas exchange, ventilatory parameters, and EIT-derived ventilation–perfusion indices were recorded. Responders were defined by ≥ 20% improvement in PaO₂/FiO₂. A mixed-effects model assessed main and interaction effects.

Results: Both iNO and PP significantly improved oxygenation (Figure). PaO₂/FiO₂ increased from SP to SPNO and from SP to PP, and reached its highest level during PPNO. No interaction effect was detected, indicating additive rather than synergistic benefits. Early oxygenation response to iNO strongly predicted subsequent PP responsiveness (AUC 0.984). EIT showed no consistent phase-dependent changes, but a higher proportion of unmatched units after iNO was associated with mortality (AUC 0.786).

Conclusions: In CARDS, iNO and PP independently improve oxygenation, with additive effects when combined. Early PaO₂/FiO₂ response to iNO may serve as a simple bedside predictor of PP efficacy, supporting individualized treatment strategies. EIT-derived unmatched units following iNO may provide prognostic information.

P044 Epitranscriptomic insights into acute respiratory distress syndrome

A Bagul1, L Bilonda Mutala2, A Arco Hierves2, N Libert1, J Duranteau1, A Harrois1, R Bechara1, A Huertas3

1Paris-Saclay University DYNAMIC lab, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France, 2Paris-Saclay University IMVAHB/IDMIT, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France, 3Paris-Saclay University DYNAMIC lab, Medecine, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P044

Introduction: Acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) is a complex and severe syndrome with high mortality, characterized by dysregulated inflammation and disruption of lung microvascular barrier, but the underlying mechanisms are still unclear. Inflammatory genes can be induced by promoter regulation but also by post-transcriptional control of mRNA through RNA methylation of adenosine at the 6th nitrogen position (m6A) by methyltransferases (METTL3 and METTL14), demethylases and RNA binding proteins. The role of m6A machinery in ARDS is not known and we are questioning here whether lung epitranscriptomic changes are present in ARDS and whether m6A signaling pathway is involved in this dysregulated inflammatory response.

Methods: Mice were subjected to nasal instillation of hydrochloric acid (HCl; 2 mL/Kg)-induced acute lung injury. Two hours later, lungs were removed and compared to untreated mice (each group n = 4). Results are expressed as mean ± standard error of the mean (SEM) with a statistical significance for Student’s t-test at p < 0005.

Results: HCl-treated mice displayed a significant increase in both lung wet-to-dry weight ratio and inflammatory triggers mRNA expression, such as TNF-a and CCL2, demonstrating the presence of lung edema and dysregulated inflammation, compared to controls. Interestingly, we found a significant decrease in the expression of the m6A writer Mettl14 in HCl-treated mice compared to controls (1.12 × 10–2 ± 4.8 × 10–4 and 1.15 ± 5.1 × 10–4 respectively, p < 0.05).

Conclusions: Our findings indicate a notable reduction in m6A writer Mettl14 expression in HCl-treated mice lungs compared to controls. Such alterations may affect the whole RNA methylome and hence differentially regulate inflammatory gene expression implicated in ARDS pathogenesis. Further studies are needed to investigate the role of a novel RNA methylation m6A in ARDS. Epitranscriptomic marks can therefore represent future therapeutic targets for this severe syndrome.

P045 Electrical impedance tomography versus transpulmonary pressure for personalized positive end-expiratory pressure titration in moderate to severe acute respiratory distress syndrome with intra-abdominal hypertension

Y Wu, C Peng, X Zou, Y Shang

Department of Critical Care Medicine, Hospital, Tongji Medical College, Huazhong University of Science and Technology, Wuhan, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P045

Introduction: This study aimed to identify optimal PEEP levels by electrical impedance tomography (EIT) compared with transpulmonary pressure in patients with moderate-to-severe ARDS complicated by IAH, and to evaluate the corresponding physiological effects of these PEEP regimens.

Methods: This is a single-center, randomized cross-over study. Thirteen patients diagnosed with moderate-to-severe ARDS and IAH (intra-abdominal pressure ≥ 12mmHg) were enrolled. The baseline ventilator settings were volume-controlled (VT = 6 ml/kg/PBW) mode, with PEEP set with the low FiO2-PEEP table recommended by the ARDS Network. Patients were randomly assigned to sequentially undergo PEEP titration guided by EIT or end-expiratory transpulmonary pressure (PtpE) greater than 0 cmH2O. We collected physiological data by EIT after 30 min of volume-controlled ventilation at baseline and at respective optimal PEEP level to evaluate the resultant physiological effect.

Results: The median PaO2/FiO2 ratio of 13 enrolled patients was 145 ± 42mmHg and median PEEP was(10 ± 3cmH2O) at baseline. The level of PtpE-guided PEEP was significantly higher than EIT-guided PEEP (22 ± 6 cmH2O vs 16 ± 3 cmH2O, p < 0.001). EIT analysis indicated that PtpE-guided PEEP improved dorsal ventilation% compared with EIT-guided PEEP (38.7% ± 12.0% vs 30.8% ± 11.3%, p = 0.006). The PtpE-guided PEEP decreased global and dorsal shunt% compared with baseline (3.2% [1.6%—6.1%] vs 7.5% [6.6% -13.3%], p = 0.003, and 5.4% [3.5%-11.1%] vs 9.1% [7.6%-18.8%], p = 0.008). PtpE-guided PEEP also decreased ventral dead space% compared with baseline (2.2% [0.9%—6.8%] vs 4.1% [2.3%- 9.9%], p = 0.032). Moreover, PtpE-guided PEEP improved dorsal matching% compared with EIT-guided PEEP (34.8% ± 6.3% vs 31.6% ± 7.4%, p = 0.039).

Conclusions: In patients with moderate-to-severe ARDS complicated by IAH, the PEEP level titrated by PtpE was significantly higer compared with EIT or a conventional lower PEEP-FiO2 table, and PtpE-guided PEEP significantly improved ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) matching.

P047 Efficacy of combined EIT-derived ventilation and pulsatility/perfusion signals for continuous monitoring and differentiation of lung and pleural pathologies

B Ferro1, M Pillitteri1, L Camporota2, FS Taccone3

1Spedali Riuniti Livorno ATNO, Department of Anesthesia and Critical Care, Livorno, Italy, 2King’s College, Centre for Human Applied Physiological Sciences, School of Basic Medical Biosciences, London, UK, 3Université Libre de Bruxelles ULB, Erasme Hospital, Department of Anesthesia and Critical Care, Brussels, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P047

Introduction: Conventional EIT impedance signals from ventilation alone cannot reliably distinguish between changes caused by lung collapse and those resulting from pleural cavity pathologies such as effusion or pneumothorax [1,2]. EIT-derived pulsatility signals reflecting cyclic changes in pulmonary perfusion, have emerged as a complementary marker of ventilation–perfusion matching. Because EIT pulsatility implies the presence of lung parenchyma, its integration with ventilation signals may help differentiate parenchymal from pleural processes and allow continuous monitoring of pleural cavity disease.

Methods: This ongoing prospective study included 7 patients so far with pleural cavity disease, 4 with hemothorax, 1 with reactive pleural effusion and 2 with pneumothorax. Continuous EIT analyses of ventilation and pulsatility-derived signals were obtained with ET 1000 (InfiVision)before and after treatment. Ultrasound images were used as the reference standard.

Results: Pleural disease manifested as an absence of both ventilation and pulsatility impedance signals in the affected regions and were confirmed in all patients (Figure). Following drainage of hemothorax and pleural effusion, pulsatility appeared without ventilation in 3 patients, indicating no re-expansion (i.e. atelectasis on US). Total re-expansion was observed in the other two patients. Pneumothorax was characterized by an absence of ventral perfusion pulsatility and ventilation signals and absence of sliding on chest ultrasound. Resolution of pneumothorax was associated with the reappearance of both ventilation and pulsatility signals.

Conclusions: This small case-series suggested that combining EIT ventilation and pulsatility signals may help discriminate between parenchymal and pleural pathology and allow continuous bedside monitoring. Notably, the presence or absence of pulsatility appears to be a key indicator of whether lung parenchyma is present even when ventilation is absent.

C) Absence of pulsatility and ventilation on left ventral region in pneumothorax and no sliding on US. D) Normal lung ventilation and pulsatility after drainage and presence of sliding on US

P048 Validation of patient-specific computational lung models using electrical impedance tomography in ARDS

M Rixner1, M Ludwig2, M Lindner3, I Frerichs3, A Sablewski3, KR Wichmann1, KW Müller1, D Schädler3, T Becher3, J Biehler1

1Ebenbuild GmbH, Ebenbuild GmbH, Munich, Germany, 2Institute for Computational Mechanics, Technical University Munich, Munich, Bavaria, Germany, 3Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, University Medical Center SchleswigHolstein Campus Kiel, Kiel, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P048

Introduction: Personalizing ventilation settings in ARDS remains challenging due to complex regional pathophysiology and interindividual variability. Although electrical impedance tomography (EIT) can provide additional insight into regional ventilation, it offers limited spatial resolution and is subject to some practical limitations. We examine the potential of patient-specific, physics-based computational whole lung models to predict regional ventilation in mechanically ventilated patients, offering means of assessing lung mechanics.

Methods: For seven mechanically ventilated ARDS patients individualized computational lung models were constructed from CT scans and ventilatory waveform data. By resolving physical processes within the lung, the models predict regional airflow and parenchymal distension during mechanical ventilation. Model-predicted regional ventilation was quantitatively compared to EIT-derived measurements evaluating spatial agreement across multiple instances of the ventilation cycle.

Results: Model predictions demonstrated strong agreement with bedside EIT (Figure). Predicted anteroposterior ventilation distribution correlated closely with clinical data (R = 0.96) exceeding prior benchmarks established for EIT versus 4D-CT. This was maintained even when considering full spatial resolution across the transverse plane as well as full tidal ventilation range (R = 0.82, RMSE = 0.14) and despite the presence of marked pathological heterogeneity.

Conclusions: Our findings demonstrate that computational lung models can accurately predict regional ventilation phenomena within the lung. The findings demonstrate that patient-specific models can provide clinically relevant information, which could enable a novel way to further personalize and evaluate ventilator settings even before putting into application, based on both individual anatomy as well as pathophysiology.

P049 Cardiac and lung ultrasound during a spontaneous breathing trial to predict extubation failure

CNL Groenland1, EJ Wils2, EA Dubois1, E Ter Bals3, TCD Rettig4, D Gommers1, L Heunks5, H Endeman6, VJM Baggen1

1Erasmus MC, Intensive Care Medicine, Rotterdam, Netherlands, 2Franciscus Gasthuis Vlietland, Intensive Care Medicine, Rotterdam, Netherlands, 3Groene Hart Ziekenhuis, Intensive Care Medicine, Gouda, Netherlands, 4Amphia Ziekenhuis, Intensive Care Medicine, Breda, Netherlands, 5Radboud University Medical Center, Intensive Care Medicine, Nijmegen, Netherlands, 6OLVG, Intensive Care Medicine, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P0049

Introduction: Extubation failure occurs in up to 20%, despite a successful spontaneous breathing trial (SBT). Cardiac dysfunction and respiratory insufficiency are both potential causes of extubation failure. Ultrasound measurements during an SBT evaluating cardiac function and pulmonary oedema could identify patients at risk for extubation failure. We aimed to investigate the association between serial ultrasounds measurements during an SBT and extubation failure.

Methods: We included patients ventilated ≥ 48 h and undergoing an SBT followed by extubation in a multicenter cohort study. The primary endpoint was extubation failure (reintubation ≤ 7-days). Logistic regression was used to assess the associations of cardiac and lung ultrasound measurements at SBT start and the change (delta) during the SBT with extubation failure, adjusted for age and sex. Cardiac ultrasound measurements included systolic (left ventricular systolic dysfunction) and diastolic function (E/A ratio, e’ average, E/e’). Lung ultrasound (LUS) was performed according to the BLUE-protocol, with a maximum LUS score of 18 (maximum anterior LUS score of 12).

Results: We included 93 patients (mean age 62 ± 14 years, 61% male). Extubation failure occurred in 22%, of whom 76% experienced extubation failure due to respiratory or cardiac failure. Anterior LUS score was significantly higher in patients with extubation failure compared to success at start of the SBT (Table), with an adjusted OR of 1.71 (95% CI, 1.18 – 2.74). The change in anterior LUS score was not associated with extubation failure (OR 0.86, 95%CI, 0.50 – 1.36). Systolic and diastolic function parameters at start of the SBT and the change during the SBT were not significantly different.

Conclusions: Only the anterior LUS score at start of the SBT was associated with extubation failure; systolic and diastolic function parameters and their changes during the SBT were not. Lung ultrasound may provide incremental value in identifying patients at risk for extubation failure.

P050 MicroRNA–mRNA regulatory networks associated with oxygenation impairment in ARDS

Y Mitsuyama, H Matsumoto, T Ebihara, J Oda

The University of Osaka, Department of Traumatology and Acute Critical Medicine, Suita City, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P050

Introduction: Acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) involves complex inflammatory and metabolic dysregulation but coordinated regulation between mRNA and microRNA (miRNA) is not well defined. This study aimed to identify mRNA–miRNA co-expression networks in whole blood from ARDS patients and to determine modules and regulatory pairs associated with clinical severity.

Methods: Weighted gene co-expression network analysis (WGCNA) was applied to mRNA and miRNA expression profiles. Correlations between module eigengenes (MEs) and clinical parameters were examined. Hub genes were identified from the mRNA modules associated with clinical severity, and miRNA modules that were negatively correlated with these mRNA modules were used to extract hub miRNAs. Predicted target mRNAs from TargetScan were intersected with hub mRNAs to construct miRNA–mRNA pairs. Functional enrichment analyses were performed, and validated interactions were confirmed using miRTarBase.

Results: Thirty-four ARDS patients were analyzed; 47.1% had moderate ARDS, and hospital mortality was 8.8%. The mRNA ME1 module showed a significant negative correlation with the P/F ratio and was enriched for cytotoxic response, oxidative stress response, and gas transport. ME1 contained 379 hub genes. The miRNA ME2 module was negatively correlated with ME1 and included 20 hub miRNAs. Intersection analysis identified 102 predicted miRNA–mRNA pairs (72 unique genes), enriched in ubiquitin–proteasome system–related functions. miRTarBase confirmed 13 validated interactions, with miR-361-5p and miR-186-5p emerging as central regulators.

Conclusions: Integrative mRNA–miRNA analysis in ARDS revealed networks involving oxidative stress responses and the ubiquitin–proteasome system as contributors to oxygenation impairment. miR-361-5p and miR-186-5p represent potential upstream regulators and therapeutic targets in ARDS.

P051 MesenCure, a primed allogeneic cell therapy for ARDS: phase 2 COVID-19 study

V Kivity1, T Bronshtein1, A Novak1, D Ben David1, N Rozen1, S Meretzki1, T Hayek2, S Hamoud2, AF Shorr3

1Bonus Biogroup, Haifa, Israel, 2Rambam Health Care Campus, Haifa, Israel, 3MedStar Washington Hospital Center, Washington D.C., USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P051

Introduction: Mesenchymal stem cell (MSC) therapies have not demonstrated consistent efficacy in ARDS, refocusing attention on next-generation products designed to achieve more robust effects. MesenCure is an ex vivo–primed allogeneic adipose-derived MSC therapy generated under conditions that enhance potency and stability, yielding distinct molecular profiles. We summarize outcomes from a phase 2 trial of MesenCure in COVID-19-related ARDS.

Methods: 50 patients (60% male) with severe COVID-19 pneumonia and hypoxemia received up to 3 IV MesenCure doses (1.5 × 106 cells/kg on days 1, 3, and 5) plus standard of care (SoC) and were compared with 150 contemporaneous controls who were propensity-matched for demographics, severity, and comorbidities. Median disease severity on WHO clinical progression scale was 6 (oxygen by non-invasive ventilation/high-flow) in the test group and 5 (oxygen by mask/nasal prongs) in the controls. The primary endpoint was safety; 28-day mortality, need for invasive mechanical ventilation (IMV), and hospital length of stay (LoS) were key additional endpoints.

Results: MesenCure was safe and associated with lower mortality (6.0% vs 18.7%; 68% relative reduction; p < 0.05) and lower progression to IMV (12.5% vs 29.3%; 57% relative reduction; p < 0.05) compared with controls, as well as a 38% (9.4-day) reduction in LoS among patients with LoS > 7 days (p < 0.01). At the earlier of 2 weeks after the first MesenCure dose or hospital discharge, median CRP and CK levels were 52% (p < 0.0001) and 33% (p < 0.01) lower, respectively, in test patients vs. controls. These changes were accompanied by rapid resolution of pneumonia, improved respiratory function, and correction of lymphopenia.

Conclusions: MesenCure reduced mortality, morbidity, and key measures of inflammation and tissue damage in COVID-19-related ARDS. These clinically meaningful signals in viral ARDS support a US phase 3 trial designed to test whether a next-generation cell therapy can provide a disease-modifying option for all-cause ARDS.

P052 CEMIP-mediated hyaluronan fragmentation drives barrier disruption in acute respiratory distress syndrome

L Liu, S Chen, H Xue, D Hu, Y Zhu, Y Tang, A Liu, H Qiu, Y Yang, S Liu

Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Trauma Center, Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Nanjing, Jiangsu, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P052

Introduction: Acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) features disruption of the alveolar–capillary barrier, causing severe hypoxemia and high mortality. Hyaluronan (HA), a key extracellular matrix glycosaminoglycan, exists as high molecular weight HA (HHA) with barrier protective and anti inflammatory properties, and low molecular weight HA (LHA), which is pro inflammatory. We hypothesized that aberrant HA catabolism, particularly via cell migration–inducing hyaluronidase 1 (CEMIP), promotes ARDS progression.

Methods: Lipopolysaccharide (LPS) induced ARDS was established in mice receiving intratracheal HHA, LHA, or phosphate buffered saline. Lung injury was assessed by histopathology, edema indices, protein leakage, and cell death; tight junction proteins and inflammatory mediators were quantified. In vitro endothelial, epithelial, and macrophage injury, hyaluronan degrading enzyme expression, and CEMIP expressing bronchoalveolar lavage fluid populations were characterized.

Results: HHA alleviated lung histopathology, edema, protein leakage, and bronchoalveolar lavage fluid cell death and restored occludin and claudin 1 expression, whereas LHA aggravated injury and permeability. HHA reduced pulmonary inflammatory cytokine expression; LHA showed a pro inflammatory profile. In vitro, HHA limited cell injury, while LHA increased macrophage death. In ARDS lungs, hyaluronidase 1, hyaluronidase 2 and transmembrane protein 2 were downregulated, whereas CEMIP was markedly upregulated and strongly induced by LPS in epithelial cells and macrophages. Single cell data showed progressive enrichment of CEMIP positive alveolar macrophages and epithelial cells.

Conclusions: HHA and LHA exert opposing effects on lung barrier integrity and inflammation in ARDS. CEMIP is selectively upregulated and emerges as a key enzyme driving HHA degradation into pro inflammatory LHA, linking HA dysmetabolism to barrier failure. Targeting CEMIP mediated HA fragmentation may represent a promising therapeutic strategy in ARDS.

P053 S1P and 2,3-BPG in red blood cells during acute pneumonia: metabolic and functional adaptations

MS Winkler1, M Schmidt1, O Moerer1, PE Ziegler2, X Fedorov2, N Youssef2, S Blaschke3, U Olgemoeller4, B Tampe5, MH Gräler.2

1University Medical Center Göttingen, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Göttingen, Germany, 2Jena University Hospital, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Center for Molecular Biomedicine CMB, Jena, Germany, 3University Medical Center Göttingen, Emergency Department, Göttingen, Germany, 4University Medical Center Göttingen, Department of Cardiology and Pneumology, Göttingen, Germany, 5University Medical Center Göttingen, Department of Nephrology and Rheumatology, Göttingen, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P053

Introduction: Pneumonia induces systemic inflammation and hypoxia. The bioactive lipid sphingosine-1-phosphate (S1P) has been linked to red blood cell (RBC) function and may facilitate oxygen unloading by stabilizing deoxygenated hemoglobin (Hb) [1,2]. We examined whether pneumonia triggers metabolic and functional RBC adaptations involving S1P, 2,3-bisphosphoglycerate (2,3-BPG), and Hb dynamics.

Methods: We analyzed RBC samples from ICU patients with pneumonia (n = 65) and 6 healthy controls. The study was approved by the ethics committee of the University Medical Center Göttingen (#14/11/21). S1P, ceramides, cholesterol, ATP, and 2,3-BPG were quantified using LC/MS, and mRNA expression of S1P-related genes and glycolytic enzymes assessed by qPCR. Hb oxygenation kinetics were measured spectrophotometrically (577 nm), and molecular interactions evaluated via Flow-Induced Dispersion Analysis (FIDA). Patient samples were analyzed on ICU days 1, 3, and 7 when available.

Results: Patients showed significantly reduced ceramide levels (p < 0.001), elevated 2,3-BPG (p < 0.0001), and increased ATP (p < 0.0001). SPHK1/2 expression was upregulated (p < 0.001), while SGPL1 was downregulated (p < 0.05), indicating enhanced S1P synthesis. Hb oxygenation rate was significantly slower in patients (p < 0.05), with a trend toward further reduction over time (days 1–7). FIDA data revealed altered hydrodynamic radii, supporting S1P-Hb interactions under hypoxic conditions.

Conclusions: Acute pneumonia triggers a metabolic shift toward S1P production and enhanced 2,3-BPG synthesis, promoting functional adaptation of hemoglobin to improve oxygen unloading. These changes evolve over time, supporting a protective role of S1P in hypoxic stress and highlighting its potential as a biomarker and therapeutic target in severe respiratory infections.

P054 Exosomal miR-218 secreted from endothelial progenitor cells mitigates acute lung injury in sepsis mice by inhibiting HMGA1 in alveolar macrophages

GB Liang, ZH He

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P054

Introduction: Sepsis-related acute lung injury (S-ALI) had become an important public health issue worldwide. However, the mechanism of S-ALI was still not fully understood. This study aims to investigate the possibility of exosomes secreted from endothelial progenitor cells (EPCs) serving as a carrier for microRNA (miR)-218 to alleviate S-ALI and explore the possible mechanism.

Methods: Exosomes were isolated from EPCs of C57BL/6J mice using differential centrifugation. Exosomes tracking in vivo and their uptake by EPCs in vitro were detected. miR-218 inhibitor and mimic were applied to investigate its function in mice and regulating polarization of alveolar macrophages (AMs). P055 Early neuromuscular blockade with or without dexamethasone in severe ARDS: A retrospective historical cohort study

M Benlabed1, S Benlabed2, R Gaudy3, A Benhamed4, R Mehyaoui5, J Malaska6, A Aberkane7, A Metali5

1Lille University, Anesthesiology and Intensive Care, Lille, France, 2Free university of Brussels, Brussels, Belgium, 3Lille University, Lille, France, 4University of Oran, Oran, Algeria, 5Algiers University, Algiers, 6Masaryk University, Brno, Czech Republic, 7University of Constantine, Constantine, Algeria

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P055

Introduction: Early neuromuscular blocking agent (NMBA) [1] improves oxygenation in severe ARDS while dexamethasone reduces mortality and duration of mechanical ventilation (DEXA ARDS) [2]. After 2020, dexamethasone became standard of care. Prospective trials comparing NMBA alone versus NMBA + dexamethasone would likely be considered unethical. We performed a retrospective historical cohort study (2018–2024) to evaluate real-world outcomes.

Methods: We analyzed 400 adult ARDS patients:

• NMBA alone (pre-2020, n = 200): cisatracurium infusion for 48 h (Papazian protocol).

• NMBA + dexamethasone (post-2020, n = 200): cisatracurium + dexamethasone (10-day DEXA ARDS protocol).

Patients were matched using propensity scores for age, ARDS severity, comorbidities, PaO₂/FiO₂, and ventilatory settings. Continuous variables were compared using Mann–Whitney U test, and categorical variables using Chi-square or Fisher’s exact test. Primary endpoint: 90-day all-cause mortality. Secondary endpoints: 28-day mortality, ventilator-free days (VFD) at day 28, duration of mechanical ventilation, ICU-acquired weakness, PaO₂/FiO₂, and SOFA score.

Results:

• 28-day mortality: 38% vs 27% (p < 0.02) (Figure).

• 90-day mortality: 40% vs 28% (p < 0.01).

• VFD at day 28: 12 ± 6 vs 17 ± 5 days (p < 0.001).

• Duration of mechanical ventilation: 18 ± 7 vs 13 ± 6 days (p < 0.001).

• PaO₂/FiO₂ at day 7: 150 ± 45 vs 190 ± 50 mmHg (p < 0.001).

• SOFA score at day 7: 10 ± 3 vs 8 ± 3 (p < 0.01).

• NMBA + dexamethasone improved oxygenation compared with historical NMBA-alone cohorts.

Conclusions: Early dexamethasone added to 48-h NMBA was associated with lower 28- and 90-day mortality, more ventilator-free days, shorter duration of mechanical ventilation, and improved PaO₂/FiO₂ and SOFA scores compared with matched historical NMBA-alone patients. These results highlight the real-world benefit of combining NMBA and corticosteroids in severe ARDS and confirm that prospective trials withholding dexamethasone would now be unethical.

P056 Comparison of fixed versus individualized positive end-expiratory pressure strategies in a unilateral porcine “triple-hit” acute lung injury model

S Böhmann1, B Seybold1, CH Mutschler1, A Wulfinghoff1, J Schenz1, N Englert2, D Cetiner3, T Poth4, MA Weigand1, MO Fiedler Kalenka1

1Heidelberg University Hospital, Medical Faculty, Department of Anesthesiology, Heidelberg, Germany, 2Heidelberg University Hospital, Medical Faculty, Department of Pathology, Heidelberg, Germany, 3Heidelberg University Hospital, Medical Faculty, Department of Diagnostic and Interventional Radiology, Heidelberg, Germany, 4Heidelberg University Hospital, Medical Faculty, Center for Model System and Comparative Pathology CMCP, Heidelberg, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P056

Introduction: Acute lung injury (ALI) involves heterogeneous lung damage and loss of functional lung tissue [1]. During invasive ventilation, individualized positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) titration aims to optimize tidal volume distribution and minimize further lung injury through atelectrauma and volutrauma.

Methods: Unilateral lung injury was induced in 10 anesthetized pigs by left lung inhalation (0.33 mg/kg) and direct intrapulmonary arterial injection (0.24 mg/kg) of lipopolysaccharide (LPS), combined with 1 h left pulmonary artery occlusion. Animals were randomized to either fixed PEEP (Group A, 5 cmH2O, n = 6) or individualized PEEP (Group B, titrated based on transpulmonary pressure (TPP) via esophageal pressure monitoring [2], n = 4). Mechanical ventilation was maintained for 6 h with hourly recording of respiratory and hemodynamic parameters. Finally, tissue, serum and bronchoalveolar lavage samples were collected and chest CT scan was performed.

Results: As illustrated in the Figure, values are presented as median and interquartile range (IQR). After 6 h, the TPP guided strategy in Group B was associated with higher oxygenation (P/F 229.75 [140.13] vs. 139.64 [146.76] mmHg) and higher compliance (24.0 [5.0] vs. 20.50 [8.0] ml/cmH2O), with a tendency towards lower driving pressure (∆P) (12.40 [2.20] vs. 13.55 [5.60] cmH2O). Mechanical power was higher in Group B (18.15 [2.49] vs. 14.65 [2.0] J/min) and PEEP levels differed markedly between groups (10.50 [3.0] vs. 5.0 [0.0] cmH2O).

Conclusions: These preliminary data suggest that individualized TPP guided PEEP may improve gas exchange and lung mechanics. Further experiments are required to determine whether the observed increase in mechanical power merely reflects higher static strain or contributes to additional tissue damage. Effects on imaging and translational relevance remain to be examined.

P057 Comparative effectiveness of individual lung ventilation strategies to PEEP titration in ARDS patients: a systematic review and network meta-analysis

F Mingxing1, R Hanchen2

1Hebei Medical University Third Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, China, 2Hebei Medical University Third Hospital, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P057

Introduction: This network meta-analysis aimed to compare the efficacy of individualized positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) titration strategies for reducing mortality and improving outcomes in patients with acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS).

Methods: The study protocol was registered on PROSPERO (CRD420251041273). A comprehensive search was conducted in PubMed, Web of Science, Embase, the Cochrane Library, CNKI, and Wanfang Data up to October 21, 2024, including randomized controlled trials of mechanically ventilated ARDS patients. Network meta-analysis was performed using R software. Evaluated PEEP titration strategies included transpulmonary pressure, driving pressure, optimal compliance, pressure–volume curve, electrical impedance tomography, ultrasound, and conventional methods (predominantly ARDSnet protocol). Primary outcome was 28-day mortality; secondary outcomes included oxygenation index, ICU length of stay, and duration of mechanical ventilation.

Results: Twenty-six RCTs involving 3400 critically ill patients were included. Seventeen studies reported 28-day mortality, 16 reported oxygenation index, 12 reported ICU length of stay, and 8 reported duration of mechanical ventilation. Compared with conventional methods, the transpulmonary pressure-guided strategy significantly reduced 28-day mortality (HR: 0.56, 95% CI: 0.32–0.92) and improved oxygenation (MD: 50.95, 95% CI: 16.09–86.14) (Figure). Surface under the cumulative ranking curve (SUCRA) analysis showed the driving pressure-guided strategy may be superior in shortening mechanical ventilation duration (89%) and ICU length of stay (92%), though these differences lacked statistical significance.

Conclusions: Transpulmonary pressure-guided PEEP titration is superior to conventional methods in reducing 28-day mortality and improving oxygenation in ARDS patients. The driving pressure-guided strategy may be preferable for reducing the duration of mechanical ventilation and ICU stay, but this finding requires validation in future clinical trials.

P059 Development and validation of a dynamic prediction model for treatment failure in patients undergoing awake prone positioning

Y Li, L Liu

School of Medicine, Zhongda Hospital, Southeast University, Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P059

Introduction: Acute hypoxemic respiratory failure (AHRF) is common in ICU. Awake prone positioning (APP) improves oxygenation and reduces intubation [1]. 40% still require intubation or die (APP failure), increasing mortality. We developed and validated a dynamic model to quantify APP-failure risk and support early intubation.

Methods: Data from a multicenter RCT split 1:1 into development and validation cohorts [2]. We collected baseline clinical and laboratory data, plus daily post-APP measures (SpO2/FiO2, heart rate, respiratory support). A joint model generated dynamic predictions; performance was assessed by AUC and calibration plots. Patients were stratified into high and low-risk groups by the optimal cutoff, and APP failure rates were compared by log-rank.

Results: 404 patients were randomized 1:1 (202/202). HFOT at admission, white blood cell count, total bilirubin, and GCS independently predicted APP failure. Linear mixed models showed rising SpO₂/FiO₂, falling heart rate, and step-down respiratory support during APP; joint modeling integrated these with survival data to form the dynamic model. AUC increased from day 1–7: development: 0.854 (0.784–0.924) → 0.946 (0.903–0.988); validation: 0.762 (0.690–0.833) → 0.883 (0.803–0.963). Calibration was near-ideal. The optimal cutoff (Youden) was 0.136, separating high and low-risk groups with significantly different failure rates.

Conclusions: The dynamic model provides time-updated risk estimates with strong discrimination and calibration; its performance improves over time and accurately predicts APP failure.

P061 Hemodynamic effects of prone position on patients with moderate to severe ARDS and varying lung recruitability: a physiological observational study

S Yang1, T Liu1, M Yan1, Y Li1, J Xu1, J Xie1, F Guo1, C Pan2

1Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Nanjing, China, 2Department of Intensive Care Medicine, Sichuan Academy of Medical Sciences Sichuan Provincial People’s Hospital Affiliated Hospital of University of Electronic Science and Technology of China, Chengdu, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P061

Introduction: Prone positioning (PP) is a key therapeutic intervention for ARDS. Lung recruitability represents an extrinsic phenotype of ARDS, but the hemodynamic response to PP in ARDS with varying lung recruitability remains unclear.

Methods: This prospective observational study was carried out at a comprehensive ICU. ARDS Patients receiving invasive ventilation were stratified by recruitability and its pre/post-PP changes(ΔR/I ratio). Echocardiogram was conducted at SP and Re-SP. Respiratory mechanics, ABG and EIT were utilized at SP, PP3H, PP12H and Re-SP.

Results: Were stratified into two subgroups based on baseline lung recruitability: 9 patients (60.0%) had low-recruitability ARDS, and 6 patients (40.0%) had high-recruitability ARDS. Additionally, the entire cohort was further divided into two groups according to the ΔR/I ratio: 8 patients (53.3%) were classified as small-ΔR/I ARDS, and 7 patients (46.7%) as large-ΔR/I ARDS. In the overall cohort, PP significantly decreased PASP (44.60 [37.35, 54.40] vs. 37.40 [30.50, 43.75] mmHg; p = 0.024) and increased Tapse/PASP( 0.44 [0.38, 0.56] vs. 0.57 [0.48, 0.71] mm/mmHg; p = 0.026). However, despite the favorable respiratory physiological effects induced by PP, no significant reductions in PASP or improvements in Tapse/PASP were observed in the subgroups stratified by baseline lung recruitability. Notably, in the large-ΔR/I ARDS group, PP resulted in a significant reduction in PASP (51.00 [43.00, 54.10] vs. 38.00 [36.65, 40.40] mmHg; p = 0.026). In contrast, no such significant effect of PP on PASP was detected in the small-ΔR/I ARDS group.

Conclusions: Regarding the hemodynamic effects of PP in moderate to severe ARDS, the dynamic change characteristic of lung recruitability exerts a greater impact on hemodynamic parameters than lung recruitability per se. Only in large-ΔR/I ARDS, PASP decreased significantly after PP.

P062 The effect of prone positioning application for over and under 24 h on gas exchange in COVID-19 ARDS (acute respiratory distress syndrome) patients

A Atli1, O Kayhan2, O Demirkiran2

1Cerrahpaşa Faculty of Medicine, Department of Anesthesiology and Reanimation, Istanbul, Turkey, 2Cerrahpaşa Faculty of Medicine, Istanbul, Turkey

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P062

Introduction: The main purpose of the study is to determine the difference in effectiveness of shortterm (16–24 h) and long-term (24–36 h) prone positioning on gas exchange (oxygenation and carbon dioxide elimination) in COVID-19 ARDS patients, and consequently, to reduce complications and care difficulty by determining the most optimal application duration.

Methods: Patients who were hospitalized in a university hospital intensive care unit from the beginning of the pandemic until the end of 2021, diagnosed with severe ARDS according to the Berlin criteria, and underwent invasive mechanical ventilation were included in the study. Patients were divided into two groups according to prone duration: Short Prone (16–24 h, n = 41) and Long Prone (24–36 h, n = 38). Arterial blood gas (ABG) and mechanical ventilation data (PEEP, PaCO2, PaO2/FiO2) were recorded just before prone, during the first and last hours of prone position, and after returning to the supine position.

Results: Prone positioning improves oxygenation and increases carbon dioxide elimination in COVID-19 ARDS patients in the short term. Extending the prone position beyond 24 h provides no additional benefit. A significant decrease in PaO2/FiO2 ratios was observed upon returning to the supine position. The carbon dioxide level, which was similar at the beginning, differed significantly in favor of women at the end of prone and afterwards. The effects of the prone position were found to be more limited and rapidly reversible in male patients. The more pronounced drop in PaO2/FiO2 after returning to supine in men suggests that males have a weaker response to the prone position and their oxygenation parameters deteriorate faster.

Conclusions: In light of the data obtained from the study, it was generally observed that long-term (> 24 h) prone positioning was not significant for patients in terms of respiratory. These findings emphasize the need for individualizing treatment strategies and considering gender differences.

P063 ROX index in the prone position as a predictor of successful high-flow nasal cannula therapy in COVID-19-associated acute respiratory failure: a single-center retrospective study

Y Kato1, K Tamai2, H Takahashi2

1Okayama Kyokuto Hospital, Department of Emergency Medicine, Okayama, Japan, 2Saiseikai Yokohamashi Tobu Hospital, Department of Intensive Care Medicine, Yokohama Kanagawa, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P063

Introduction: The ROX index, which is the ratio of the oxygen saturation to the fraction of inspired oxygen and respiratory rate (SpO2/ FiO2/ RR), can predict the success of high-flow nasal cannula (HFNC) therapy. Although some studies have revealed its applicability in patients with Coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19), its utility during prone positioning therapy has not been validated. Therefore, we aimed to determine whether the ROX index in the prone position could predict the success of HFNC therapy.

Methods: This retrospective observational study included patients with COVID-19 who underwent HFNC and awake-prone positioning therapy. The ROX index in both the supine and prone positions was calculated at < 12 h and 12–24 h after starting HFNC therapy, and once daily thereafter until HFNC termination. HFNC failure was defined as need for mechanical ventilation (MV) or death.

Results: Of the 69 patients included, 61 (88.4%) were weaned off HFNC, whereas HFNC therapy failed in eight patients. Of the eight patients with HNFC failure, four switched to MV and four died during HFNC therapy. In the HFNC success group, the ROX indices were significantly higher values in the prone position than in the supine position. However, this difference was not observed in the HFNC failure group. In the receiver operating characteristic analysis, the best predictor for HFNC success was the ROX index at < 12 h in the prone position (Area under the curve (AUC), 0.906), and the threshold of 7.71 had a sensitivity of 88% and specificity of 88%, whereas in the supine position AUC was 0.747 and the threshold of 5.47 had sensitivity of 87% and specificity of 50%. In the multivariate logistic regression analysis, the ROX index in the prone position at < 12 h showed good predictive value for HFNC success (OR, 3.786; 95% CI, 1.264–11.337; p = 0.017).

Conclusions: The ROX index in the prone position has good predictive capacity for HFNC success and can be useful for the optimal selection of patients for HFNC therapy.

P065 Effect of prone positioning on pathophysiology and lung histopathology during VV-ECMO in severe ARDS: an experimental animal study

E Barbeta1, L Zattera1, K Kiarostami2, R Cabrera2, L FernándezBarat2, A Motos2, J Riera3, C Ferrando1, A Torres4

1Hospital Clinic de Barcelona, ICU, Barcelona, Spain, 2University of Barcelona, IDIBAPS, Barcelona, Spain, 3Hospital Vall d´Hebró, ICU, Barcelona, Spain, 4University of Barcelona, ICU, Barcelona, Spain

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P065

Introduction: Prone positioning improves survival in patients with acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS), primarily by reducing ventilator-induced lung injury. Whether these physiological benefits translate to patients supported with veno-venous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (VV-ECMO) remains uncertain.

Methods: Pigs with severe ARDS were placed on VV-ECMO and randomized to either prone or supine positioning (48h) under an ultraprotective ventilation strategy. Futility analyses were performed at half of the planned sample size using conditional power calculations, with early termination criteria set at < 10% probability of achieving statistical significance. We aimed to compare the effects of prone versus supine positions on the wet-to-dry lung weight ratio, gas exchange, respiratory mechanics, electrical impedance tomography, histopathology, microbiology, hemodynamics, and extracorporeal circuit function in pigs with severe ARDS undergoing VV-ECMO.

Results: Eight pigs with severe ARDS were randomized after VV-ECMO initiation. Prone positioning, compared with the supine, resulted in a similar wet-to-dry lung weight ratio (6.77 [6.13–8.17] vs. 6.70 [6.22–8.48]; p = 0.89), meeting the futility threshold. In the prone group, non-dependent areas accounted for 51 [47–61.3]% of total ventilation compared to 86 [68.5–90]% in the supine (p = 0.02). Prone positioning redistributed histopathological lung injury, with relative preservation of non-dependent areas and greater damage in dependent zones, but without a net global decrease (Figure).

Conclusions: Prone positioning did not decrease the overall severity of lung injury. Rather, it shifted the distribution of damage, with greater involvement of dependent regions and relative preservation of non-dependent areas.

P066 Safety and performance of the Novalung ultimate kit and the Xenios 2.0 during stationary use in hospital and ground-based transport of patients on extracorporeal life support (ECLS)

F Bellando1, A Derlet Savoia2, F Bitsch3, U Feiler3, T Rinne3

1Fresenius Medical Care Deutschland GmbH, Clinical Research, Bad Homburg, Germany, 2Fresenius Medical Care Deutschland GmbH, Bad Homburg, Germany, 3Xenios AG, Heilbronn, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P066

Introduction: With the growing number of ECMO indications and the specialization of ECMO centers, the transfer of patients from remote hospitals to tertiary care facilities has become a crucial element for successful treatment. The aim of this study is to evaluate the performance and safety of the Xenios system (Novalung ultimate kit, Xenios 2.0, MultiSupport Ground (Xenios AG)) in patients transported and treated stationary on ECMO. It will be evaluated whether the combined use of the medical devices can improve and maintain the gas exchange and cardiocirculation in these patients.

Methods: This is a prospective, multi-centre, observational post-market clinical follow-up study. 20 patients with severe acute respiratory and/or cardiopulmonary failure in need of VV or VA ECMO will be allocated in two equal sub-groups, depending on whether they receive an inter-hospital transport and stationary ECMO treatment or a stationary ECMO treatment only. Participating patients will be treated according to local clinical practice. Data will be recorded before ECMO initiation (Baseline), after ECMO initiation, during ground-based transport, and every 24 h until ECMO discontinuation. The primary analysis will be conducted in the full sample. The change in arterial oxygen saturation (SaO2) from pre-ECMO initiation to 24 h after ECMO initiation is evaluated as the primary endpoint. Secondary endpoints include, among others, cardiocirculatory improvement, ventilatory settings and respective gas exchange in the patient, safety and performance of the Xenios system, and patient outcomes.

Results: The clinical phase will start in Q1 2026. The overall recruitment phase will be limited to 12 months. The final study results are planned to be published in 2027.

Conclusions: This study aims to demonstrate the safety and efficacy of the Xenios system and is expected to provide valuable evidence to support its clinical use in adult patients with an ECMO indication during ground-based transport and stationary use.

P067 Reevaluation of oxygenation targets in patients with acute respiratory failure and veno-venous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation

V Bünger1, M Ruß1, L La Via2, WM Kübler3, S WeberCarstens1, JA Graw4

1Charité Universitätsmedizin Berlin, Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Berlin, Germany, 2University of Catania, General Surgery and Medical Surgical Specialties, Catania, Italy, 3Charité Universitätsmedizin Berlin, Physiology, Berlin, Germany, 4University of ErlangenNuremberg FAU, Anesthesia and Intensive Care Medicine, Erlangen, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P067

Introduction: Many patients in the intensive care unit (ICU) receive high fractions of inhaled oxygen (FiO2) to secure blood oxygenation and tissue supply with oxygen. However, there is increasing evidence that exposure to high FiO2 is associated with adverse effects. In patients with a severe acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) and refractory hypoxemic respiratory failure, veno-venous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (VV-ECMO) can be implemented as a rescue therapy. Target levels for partial pressure of oxygen in arterial blood (PaO2) can be controlled by the VV-ECMO. So far, optimal oxygenation targets in patients with ARDS treated with VV-ECMO are unknown.

Methods: Retrospective analysis of 443 patients with severe ARDS and treatment with VV-ECMO. Regression analyses were performed for mortality and time-weighted averages of PaO2 and FiO2. Furthermore, considering a possible non-linear relationship, a restricted cubic spline (RCS) model was performed for PaO2.

Results: A simple logistic regression for mean PaO2 and ICU mortality showed a significant positive association (per mmHg OR 0.99 [95%CI 0.98–1.00], p = 0.002). RCS analysis showed a U-shaped association of mortality and mean PaO2 (PaO2 69.70–90.24 mmHg: OR 0.92 [95%CI 0.89–0.94], p < 0.001; PaO2 90.24–123.40 mmHg: 1.09 [1.06–1.13], p < 0.001). A model including PaO2 as restricted cubic spline variable and FiO2 showed significant associations of mortality with both variables (PaO2 69.70–90.24 mmHg: OR 0.94 [0.91–0.97], p < 0.001, paO2 90.24–123.40 mmHg: OR 1.07 [1.04–1.11], p < 0.001, FiO2: OR 33.48 [8.01–148.43], p < 0.001, VIF < 1.15).

Conclusions: The results of this observational study suggest that PaO2-values below 90 and above 123 mmHg both are associated with an increased mortality in patients with ARDS and treatment with VV-ECMO.

P068 Retrospective analysis of peripheral neuropathy in patients recovering from veno-arterial and veno-venous ECMO support

VD Dupon1, CR Redant2, HP Peperstraete1

1Ghent University Hospital, Intensive Care Medicine, Ghent, Belgium, 2Ghent University Hospital, Physical and Rehabilitation Medicine, Ghent, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P068

Introduction: Extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO) is a potential life-saving therapy in critically ill patients suffering from cardiac arrest, acute cardiac or pulmonary failure [1]. Many of these patients suffer from critical illness weakness (CIW), defined by a Medical Research Council (MRC) sum score below 48. A less described complication of ECMO support is peripheral neuropathy (PN), impacting the patient’s recovery and quality of life [2]. The aim of this study was to explore the prevalence and associated factors of PN in ECMO survivors.

Methods: This retrospective cohort study included adult patients who survived at least 6 months after VA or VV ECMO support in the Ghent University Hospital (Belgium) from 2016 to 2024.

Results: The 6-months survival rate was 42.5% or 107 of 251 patients. Relevant patient demographics and clinical characteristics are shown in the Table. The overall incidence of CIW was 73.8% (n = 79). Among these patients, 35.4% (n = 28) underwent EMG examination. PN (on the side of one of the ECMO cannulas) occurred in 12 patients (11.2%), including 7 on VA-ECMO and 5 on VV-ECMO. The peroneal nerve was most frequently affected, followed by the brachial plexus and femoral nerve. Neuropathy was predominantly motor (n = 7), with 6 patients presenting foot drop. Due to the low number of neuropathy events, multivariable logistic regression was not feasible. In univariable testing, continuous administration of neuromuscular blocking drugs (NMBD) and presence of CIW based upon the MRC sum score were significantly associated with an increased risk of PN (p = 0.018 and p = 0.034).

Conclusions: Continuous use of NMBD and presence of CIW are significant risk factors for development of PN in patients requiring ECMO support. EMG screening may be beneficial for timely detection, leading to less underdiagnosing of PN.

P069 Physiological response to ECMO as a rescue strategy in near-fatal asthma: five-case series from two Spanish intensive care units

E Marcano Millán1, M San Martín Bragado2, F Martín González3, M Gallardosantos2, E Pérez Losada3, L Roldán Furelos2, S Muñoz González3, J Figueroa Falconi3, J Granados Ricaurte3, V Sagredo Meneses3

1Hospital Universitario de Salamanca, Critical Care Unit, Salamanca, Spain, 2Hospital Universitario Doctor Negrín, Las Palmas, Spain, 3Hospital Universitario de Salamanca, Salamanca, Spain

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P069

Introduction: Near-fatal asthma is a life-threatening respiratory emergency where extreme hypercapnia and acidosis may become refractory to conventional ventilation. Extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO) has emerged as an effective rescue option in such situations.

Methods: A retrospective descriptive study was conducted in the intensive care units (ICUs) of the University Hospital of Salamanca and the University Hospital Dr. Negrín, Spain. Five patients with severe status asthmaticus requiring ECMO between 2019 and 2025 were analyzed. Demographic, clinical, ventilatory, and blood gas data, as well as ECMO characteristics and outcomes, were collected and expressed as medians and interquartile ranges (IQR).

Results: Median [Me] age was 45 years [IQR: 7]; 60% (3/5) were male. Me weight, body mass index (BMI), and height were 85 kg [IQR: 10], 27.4 [IQR: 4.8], and 178 cm [IQR: 5], respectively. All cases had an infectious trigger. Pre-ECMO values showed severe respiratory acidosis with pH 7.09 [IQR: 0.18] and PaCO₂ 115 mmHg [IQR: 14] (Figure). Four patients received veno-venous ECMO and one veno-arteriovenous ECMO. Time to cannulation was 8 h [IQR: 3–10], and ECMO duration was 144 h [IQR: 139–168]. Protective ventilation was achieved with a reduction in driving pressure from 20 to 14 cmH₂O and in peak inspiratory pressure (PIP) from 34 to 25 cmH₂O. All patients received inhaled bronchodilators and ketamine as a bronchodilator-sedative; 60% received intravenous salbutamol, one case used Heliox® 70/30, and one received early benralizumab. All presented hypophosphoremia (Me 1.9 mg/dL; ref. 2.5–4.5). Two patients developed non-fatal bleeding, none required dialysis, and one died from anoxic encephalopathy with intracranial hemorrhage.

Conclusions: This multicentre Spanish series highlights ECMO as an effective rescue therapy for NFA, allowing safe gas exchange and lung-protective ventilation with an acceptable complication profile. Larger registries are needed to better define patient selection and management strategies.

P070 A UK single centre retrospective cohort study describing hypertriglyceridemia management in patients on venovenous-extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (VV-ECMO): Fenofibrate versus standard care

V Milic, M Adesuyan, F Hanks, P Sherren

Guys and St Thomas NHS Foundation Trust, London, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P070

Introduction: Significant hypertriglyceridemia in patients receiving VV-ECMO has been associated with impaired extracorporeal membrane efficiency and possibly failure. Given the potential clinical complications and financial implications associated with circuit change, strategies to mitigate triglyceride (TG) elevation are important. Since 2017, our ECMO centre has incorporated fenofibrate as adjunctive therapy to manage rising triglyceride levels. The aim of this retrospective cohort study was to evaluate whether the introduction of fenofibrate was associated with changes in TG levels among our VV-ECMO population.

Methods: Using electronic health records from a UK tertiary referral centre, we compared VV-ECMO patients across a 5 year period with TG ≥ 5.64 mmol/L who were managed across a six day period with and without fenofibrate 160mg daily. We conducted a descriptive analysis of baseline characteristics and fitted a linear mixed-effects model to estimate triglyceride changes in each group.

Results: 23 patients were identified (fenofibrate n = 10; non-fenofibrate n = 13) and baseline TG levels were similar: fenofibrate group (median 7.57 mmol/L [IQR 7.00–9.56]) compared with non-users (6.43 mmol/L [IQR 6.06–6.77]; p = 0.08). The fenofibrate group showed a greater reduction in TG (median − 3.88 mmol/L [IQR − 4.54 to − 2.49]) compared with non-users (− 2.20 mmol/L [IQR − 3.13 to − 1.05]; p = 0.056) (Figure). Cumulative propofol exposure was comparable between groups whereas cumulative midazolam exposure was significantly greater in the fenofibrate group (median 886.5 mg [IQR 511–1704]) compared with non-users (50 mg [IQR 0–747]; p = 0.03).

Conclusions: Fenofibrate use was associated with a greater reduction in serum TG over six days. While these findings are limited by sample size and confounders, they highlight the possible role of fenofibrate in managing hypertriglyceridemia during VV-ECMO. Larger controlled studies are needed to investigate efficacy and safety of fenofibrate and the influence on ECMO circuit lifespan.

Figure (abstract P070) Unadjusted linear mixed-effects model showing predicted triglyceride levels over time in fenofibrate users vs non-users

Unadjusted linear mixed-effects model showing predicted triglyceride levels over time in fenofibrate users vs non-users

P071 Comparison of different positive end-expiratory pressure strategies in a unilateral porcine acute lung injury model with early venovenous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation support

A Wulfinghoff1, B Seybold1, CH Mutschler1, N Englert2, D Cetiner3, L Wucherpfennig3, T Poth4, MA Weigand1, A Kalenka1, MO Fiedler Kalenka1

1Heidelberg University Hospital, Department of Anesthesiology, Heidelberg, Germany, 2Heidelberg University Hospital, Department of Pathology, Heidelberg, Germany, 3Heidelberg University Hospital, Department of Diagnostic and Interventional Radiology, Heidelberg, Germany, 4Heidelberg University Hospital, Center for Model System and Comparative Pathology CMCP, Heidelberg, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P071

Introduction: Lung-protective ventilation is the standard of care for acute respiratory failure, aiming to reduce ventilator-induced lung injury [1,2]. Venovenous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (vv-ECMO) provides a therapeutic option during respiratory failure to further reduce mechanical power (MP). However, the optimal positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) strategy remains unclear.

Methods: Left-sided acute lung injury (ALI) was induced by saline/Triton X-100 lavage. Vv-ECMO was initiated to allow de-escalation of mechanical ventilation. Animals (n = 8) were randomized (4:4) and ventilated in airway pressure release ventilation mode (Inspiratory pressure (Phigh): PEEP + 10 cmH₂O, respiratory rate 5, FiO₂ 0.4). PEEP was set to 0 cmH₂O (Group A) or titrated (Group B), targeting slightly positive end-expiratory transpulmonary pressure (TPPexp) using an esophageal catheter. Respiratory and hemodynamic parameters were recorded over 8 h. Lung tissue and serum samples were collected and a chest computed tomography (CT) scan was performed.

Results: PEEP values were markedly higher in Group B (11.5 [3] vs. 0 [0] cmH₂O) (Figure). The resulting TPPexp differed clearly between groups (A = -3.85 [3.28], B = 1.15 [2.15] cmH₂O). After 8 h, MP, compliance, and driving pressure (ΔP) were comparable in Group A vs. Group B (MP: 0.67 [0.41] vs. 1.3 [0.6] J/min; compliance: 22 [10] vs. 17.5 [14] mL/cmH₂O; ΔP: 8.0 [0.4] vs. 8.5 [2.2] cmH₂O). Using a modified Ichikado scoring system, CT imaging showed a notably higher score in the left lung vs. right lung (320 [61.7] vs. 183.3 [90]), confirming successful unilateral implementation of the ALI model. A slight increase in left-lung injury scores was observed in Group A (341.7 [100] vs. 325 [67.5]).

Conclusions: The applied PEEP strategies showed no marked differences in MP, compliance or ΔP. CT-derived injury scores implied a slight advantage for Group B. Effects on tissue and translational relevance remain to be examined.

P072 High vasoactive-inotropic score deteriorates outcomes of septic patients receiving veno-venous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation support: a retrospective cohort study from CSECLS registry database

Y Xie, X Hou, H Wang

Beijing Anzhen Hospital, Capital Medical University, Center for Cardiac Intensive Care, Beijing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P072

Introduction: This study aimed to examine the association between high vasoactive-inotropic score (VIS) and outcomes in patients with sepsis receiving veno-venous (V-V) extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO) support.

Methods: Clinical data were retrospectively analyzed from the Chinese Society of ExtraCorporeal Life Support registry database between January 2017 and June 2023. A total of 274 adult patients with sepsis who received V-V ECMO were included. Based on a VIS threshold of 60, participants were categorized into high VIS and non-high VIS groups, according to their VIS at 24 h after ECMO initiation. In-hospital mortality, ECMO configuration conversion, and complications were compared between groups.

Results: Among the 274 participants, the overall in-hospital mortality rate was 49.3%. The high VIS group had a significantly higher mortality rate than the non-high VIS group (59.8% vs. 44.8%, p = 0.023) (Figure). Bleeding complications (24.3% vs. 13.5%), hyperbilirubinemia (31.7% vs. 13.6%), and need for continuous renal replacement therapy (64.6% vs. 47.9%) were also more frequent in the high VIS group. Fifteen patients initially supported with V–V ECMO later required conversion to V–A ECMO or V–A–V ECMO, with a mortality rate of 66.6%. No significant differences in adjuvant configuration conversions were observed between VIS groups.

Conclusions: A high VIS in patients with sepsis receiving V-V ECMO support is associated with increased in-hospital mortality and a greater risk of complications, including bleeding, liver dysfunction, and renal injury. Optimizing hemodynamic management in patients with VIS ≥ 60 at 24 h may improve outcomes.

Kaplan–Meier curves for the in-hospital mortality. VIS, vasoactive-inotropic score.

P074 Effect of artificial intelligence in extracorporeal membrane oxygenation: a systematic review and meta-analysis

S Chen, L Liu, H Xue, D Hu, Y Zhu, J Wu, H Fu, S Liu

Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Trauma Center, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P074

Introduction: ECMO is a life-saving support for patients with severe cardiopulmonary failure, but its high complexity and resource demands necessitate innovative solutions. AI offers potential in optimizing ECMO management by enhancing clinical decision-making, predicting outcomes, and guiding initiation timing. However, challenges such as data heterogeneity and model generalizability limit its widespread adoption. To evaluate the effectiveness of AI in improving clinical outcomes in ECMO management, focusing on ECMO initiation, prognosis, and complications.

Methods: A meta-analysis following PRISMA guidelines were conducted, with literature searches for studies on AI-based ECMO prediction models between 1980 and 2024. Data extraction included AI methodologies, performance metrics, and key findings, with risk of bias was assessed using PROBAST.

Results: Of 212 initial records, 36 studies met criteria for inclusion. For ECMO initiation, the pooled AUC was 0.838 (95% CI: 0.804–0.873), and for prognosis prediction, the pooled AUC was 0.776 (95% CI: 0.755–0.797). Significant heterogeneity was observed. Subgroup analysis revealed single-center studies exhibited higher AUCs for both initiation (AUC = 0.888, 95% CI: 0.865–0.910) and prognosis prediction (AUC = 0.803, 95% CI: 0.688–0.918) compared to multi-center studies in initiation (AUC = 0.823, 95% CI: 0.782–0.864) and prognosis prediction (AUC = 0.772, 95% CI: 0.752–0.792). Key AI applications included patient identification, mortality prediction, enhancing resource allocation and decision-making. However, due to data variability and limited external validation, the pooled findings should be interpreted in light of the limitations identified.

Conclusions: AI has shown promise, albeit with significant heterogeneity, in improving ECMO management by providing predictions for initiation timing and patient outcomes. Future research should focus on enhancing model generalizability through multi-center validation, and standardizing data to reduce heterogeneity.

P075 Safety of regional nafamostat with low-dose systemic heparin in high-bleeding-risk ECMO patients

YJ Hu, J Fan, Z Zhang, F Xu

The First Affiliated Hospital of Chongqing Medical University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Chongqing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P075

Introduction: Systemic anticoagulation with unfractionated heparin (UFH) is the standard of care during extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO). However, in patients with coagulation disorders or high bleeding risk, UFH may lead to life-threatening hemorrhage. This study evaluated the safety and feasibility of a dual anticoagulation strategy combining regional nafamostat mesilate with low-dose systemic UFH in high bleeding risk ECMO patients.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective case series of 8 ECMO patients with coagulopathy between January 2023 and December 2024 at our institution ICU. Patients received continuous regional nafamostat (0.5–0.75 mg/kg/h) via the ECMO circuit and low-dose UFH (4–8 U/kg/h) intravenously. Clinical outcomes, anticoagulation parameters (ACT/APTT), bleeding/thrombotic events, and oxygenator performance were evaluated.

Results: No thrombotic events or oxygenator-related clots were observed. Three patients experienced minor bleeding (37.5%), but none had major hemorrhage. ACT and APTT remained within acceptable ranges (Figure). The combination strategy reduced UFH requirements without compromising circuit patency.

Conclusions: In high bleeding risk ECMO patients, combining regional nafamostat with low-dose UFH appears to be a safe, individualized anticoagulation alternative. Prospective studies are warranted to validate these findings.

P076 Clinical outcomes and complications of extracorporeal membrane oxygenation in adult patients with COVID-19 in Central and Eastern Europe: a systematic review and meta-analysis

K Szuldrzynski1, M Jankowski1, Z Molnar2, J Benes3, A Piasecki4, M Matuszewski1, Z Ruszkai5, J Kletecka3, K Zuzda1, K Adamczyk1

1National Medical Institute of the Ministry of the Interior and Administration, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care, Warsaw, Poland, 2Semmelweis University, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care, Budapest, Hungary, 3Charles University, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care, Medical Faculty in Pilsen, Prague, Czech Republic, 4University of Gothenburg, Gothenburg, Sweden, 5Flór Ferenc Hospital County Pest, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care, Kistarcsa, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P076

Introduction: Extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO) serves as rescue therapy for severe COVID-19-related acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS). While early international data suggested 37–40% mortality [1, 2], outcomes varied across regions. Comprehensive data from Central and Eastern Europe (CEE) remain limited. This systematic review aimed to assess clinical outcomes and complications of ECMO in adult COVID-19 patients across CEE countries participating in SepsEast initiative (www.sepseast.com).

Methods: The review was registered with PROSPERO (CRD420251077593). Following PRISMA guidelines, we searched PubMed, Scopus, Embase, and Web of Science for studies reporting ECMO outcomes in adult COVID-19 patients from CEE. Studies with ≥ 10 patients were included. Quality assessment used JBI critical appraisal tools. Primary outcome was in-hospital mortality. Collected data also included ICU severity scores, mechanical ventilation duration before ECMO, ECMO duration, ICU stay, and complications. Random-effects meta-analyses were performed.

Results: Twelve studies with 802 patients from six CEE countries were included. Pooled in-hospital mortality was 65% (95%CI: 57%-72%), higher than benchmarks (37–52%) [1–3], with regional variation (Figure) from 54% (Czech Republic) to 76% (Poland). Mean SOFA score was 8.1, APACHE II 24.5. Mechanical ventilation before ECMO averaged 4.8 days, ECMO duration 17.7 days, ICU stay 23.6 days. Bleeding was reported in 40.2% of patients.

Conclusions: Mortality among COVID-19 patients receiving ECMO in CEE (65%) was higher in comparison to international registries and studies, with significant inter-country variation. Greater standardization in training, education, and clinical performance is necessary to maximize the benefit of this resource-intensive procedure in the region.

P077 The outcome of acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) patients supported with extracorporeal carbon dioxide removal (ECCO2R) in Hong Kong

SW Sin1, S Chen1, J Suen2, HP Shum3, T Liong4, C Chan4, CK Tong5, G Ng6, P Tsai7, X Wang8

1University of Hong Kong, Critical Care Medicine Unit, Hong Kong, Hong Kong, China, 2The University of Queensland, Faculty of Health, Medicine and Behavioural Sciences, Queensland, Australia, 3Pamela Youde Nethersole Eastern Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Hong Kong, Hong Kong, China, 4United Christian Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Hong Kong, Hong Kong, China, 5Tuen Mun Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Hong Kong, Hong Kong, China, 6Queen Elizabeth Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Hong Kong, Hong Kong, China, 7Hong Kong Sanatorium Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Hong Kong, Hong Kong, China, 8Peking University Shenzhen Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Shenzhen, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P077

Introduction: ARDS is one of the leading causes of mortality of acute respiratory failure in ICU mortality exceeding 30%. While extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO) is used for severe, potentially reversible ARDS, its application is limited by resource availability and patient selection—often excluding elderly and immunocompromised patients. ECCO₂R, a less invasive and resource-intensive alternative, may benefit these patients. This study evaluates the outcomes of ECCO₂R in ARDS patients.

Methods: In this multicenter retrospective study involving seven ICUs, we included ARDS patients (> 18 years) treated with ECCO₂R. Data collected included demographics, disease severity, ICU stay, blood gases, and ventilatory parameters. The Standardized Mortality Ratio (SMR) was calculated by comparing observed mortality to predicted mortality using the Respiratory ECMO Survival Prediction (RESP) score [1].

Results: Thirty patients were treated with low-flow ECCO₂R with characteristic and outcome presented in the attached table. Key findings and additional analysis include:

1)The overall SMR was 1.19 (95% CI: 0.71–1.89, p = 0.52), indicating no significant difference between observed and predicted mortality.

2)Relative Brier score analysis yielded a ratio of 1.8 (< 2), further supporting ECCO₂R as a viable alternative.

Limitations: Small sample size and retrospective analysis.

Conclusions: ECCO₂R offers survival outcomes comparable to those seen in ARDS patients who are traditionally contraindicated for ECMO. Further prospective studies are needed to confirm its clinical utility.

P078 ECCO₂R integrated with renal replacement therapy for acute hypercapnic respiratory failure: a systematic review and meta-analysis

Y Albuquerque Pessoa dos Santos1, P Lins2, P Rocha3, F Ramos4, E Lyra4, L Pastore4, B Tomazini4, E Costa4, V Campos4, T Silva4

1Hospital Sírio Libanês, ICU, São Paulo, Brazil, 2Universidade Federal de São Paulo, São Paulo, Brazil, 3Universidade Federal do Rio de Janeiro, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil, 4Hospital Sírio Libanês, São Paulo, Brazil

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P078

Introduction: Extracorporeal CO₂ removal (ECCO₂R) integrated into renal replacement therapy (RRT) has emerged as a pragmatic option for patients with acute hypercapnic respiratory failure and acute kidney injury (AKI). Its physiological effects, however, remain incompletely defined. We aimed to synthesize its physiological impact, particularly PaCO₂ reduction, pH response, and the ability to facilitate lung-protective ventilation.

Methods: This systematic review and meta-analysis was registered in PROSPERO (CRD420251234033). We included studies evaluating ECCO₂R integrated into continuous RRT in adults with hypercapnic respiratory failure and AKI. The primary outcome was the change in PaCO₂ and pH within the first 24 h of ECCO₂R–RRT. Secondary outcomes included changes in ventilatory parameters, ventilator-free days, and reported mortality. Pre–post changes at 2 h, 24 h and 48 h were pooled using random-effects models (REML with Hartung–Knapp adjustment). Heterogeneity was assessed using I2.

Results: Seven studies (112 patients) were included. ECCO₂R–RRT produced an early decline in PaCO₂: − 7.16 mmHg at 2 h (95%CI − 14.92 to 0.59; I2 = 79%), − 5.93 mmHg at 24 h (95%CI − 9.53 to − 2.34; I2 = 0%), and − 4.35 mmHg at 48 h (95%CI − 10.47 to 1.78; I2 = 59%) (Figure). pH increased progressively (+ 0.04 at 2 h, + 0.09 at 24 h, + 0.11 at 48 h), indicating gradual acidemia correction. Across studies, ECCO₂R–RRT was consistently associated with reductions in tidal volume, driving pressure, or minute ventilation without compromising pH. Data on ventilator-free days and mortality were heterogeneous and limited but did not suggest worsening clinical outcomes.

Conclusions: ECCO₂R integrated into RRT provides early physiological improvement in hypercapnic AKI, combining PaCO₂ reduction with stabilization of pH while allowing reduced ventilatory intensity. This hybrid strategy appears physiologically advantageous and may facilitate safer lung-protective ventilation, although larger controlled studies are needed to define its clinical role.

Figure (abstract P078) Forest plot showing pooled mean differences in PaCO₂(ΔPaCO₂) at 2 h, 24 h and 48 h after initiation of ECCO₂R integrated into renal replacement therapy
