P079 Extracorporeal membrane carbon dioxide removal in patients with acute respiratory distress syndrome: impact of blood flow rate in ECCO2R on ventilator tidal volume reduction

D Ngoc Son, B Van Cuong, V Xuan Toan, N Van Trong, H Minh Hoan, P The Thach, V Quang Trung, N Tu Anh, N Cong Tan

Bach Mai Hospital, Center for Critical Care Medicine, Ha Noi, Viet Nam

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P079

Introduction: ECCO2R (extracorporeal carbon dioxide removal) is a technique designed to facilitate the removal of carbon dioxide from the body, which is allowed for ultra-protective ventilation strategies with lower tidal volumes (Vt) in ARDS patients [1]. Higher blood flow improves CO2 removal, allowing for lower tidal volumes (Vt) on mechanical ventilators, reducing ventilator-induced lung injury (VILI) while maintaining adequate ventilation.

Methods: A prospective, intervention study was conducted in severe and moderate ARDS patients supported with ECCO2R in the Center for Critical Care Medicine, Bach Mai hospital from August 2023 to March 2025. We used the Prismax machine and PrismaLung + with area 0.8 m2.

Results: A total of 15 patients were included in our study with an average age of 61.0 ± 11.9 years (min 34, max 77), males accounted for 73.3%. Patients were divided into 3 groups according to blood flow (BF): 7 patients in the < 350 mL/min group, 3 patients in the 350–399 mL/min group, and 5 patients in the ≥ 400 mL/min group. Before ECCO2R, mean tidal volume was 6.5 ± 0.8 mL/kg. After 2 days of ECCO2R, Vt decreased significantly in all groups (Figure). In the < 350 mL/min group, Vt decreased from 6.5 ± 0.8 to 5.6 ± 1.0 mL/kg. In the 350–399 mL/min group, Vt decreased from 6.8 ± 0.7 to 5.5 ± 0.7 mL/kg. In the ≥ 400 mL/min group, Vt decreased from 6.6 ± 1.0 to 5.5 ± 1.3 mL/kg. At 24 h, the reduction in Vt was 0.4 mL/kg in the < 350 mL/min group, 1.3 mL/kg in the 350–399 mL/min group, and 0.9 mL/kg in the ≥ 400 mL/min group.

Conclusions: During ECCO2R, higher ECCO2R blood flow rates were associated with the reduction of ventilator tidal volume. We suggest that blood flow rates in ECCO₂R should be maintained above 350 mL/min to ensure effective carbon dioxide removal in ARDS patients.

P080 Comparing 60 mEq/L bicarbonate CRRT with 40 mEq/L CRRT + ECCO₂R during progressive tidal-volume reduction in severe AKI

Y Albuquerque Pessoa dos Santos1, B Besen2, L Cardozo3, P Mendes3, M Park3

1Hospital Sírio Libanês, ICU, São Paulo, Brazil, 2Universidade Federal de São Paulo, ICU, São Paulo, Brazil, 3Hospital das Clínicas da Faculdade de Medicina da Universidade de São Paulo, ICU, São Paulo, Brazil

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P080

Introduction: Severe acute kidney injury (AKI) frequently coexists with acute respiratory distress syndrome, where combined metabolic and respiratory acidosis may limit protective ventilation. Bicarbonate-enriched continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT) and extracorporeal CO₂ removal (ECCO₂R) are potential strategies to optimize pH, but their comparative physiological effects remain uncertain. We evaluated these two approaches in an experimental model of severe AKI undergoing protocolized hypoventilation with stepwise tidal-volume reduction.

Methods: Twelve pigs with surgically induced AKI were randomized to receive either very-high–bicarbonate CRRT (60 mEq/L dialysate) or high–bicarbonate CRRT (40 mEq/L) combined with low-flow ECCO₂R. Ventilation was reduced every two hours targeting pH ≥ 7.2 over 12 h. Primary outcomes were lowest tidal volume (Vt) achieved and time to vasopressor initiation. Hemodynamic, respiratory, and metabolic variables were serially collected.

Results: Both strategies maintained pH ≥ 7.2 and enabled ~ 50% Vt reduction (3.7 [3.5–5.1] vs 4.8 [4.0–5.5] mL/kg; p = 0.756). Time to vasopressor initiation was similar (HR 0.76; 95%CI 0.21–2.71; Figure, panel A). Survival during the 12-h protocol also did not differ (HR 5.65; 95%CI 0.62–51.21; Figure, panel B). Both groups developed progressive hypotension requiring norepinephrine, while cardiac output remained stable. CO₂ accumulation was more pronounced with the 60 mEq/L CRRT strategy, whereas ECCO₂R attenuated PaCO₂ and bicarbonate rise.

Conclusions: CRRT with 60 mEq/L bicarbonate and CRRT + ECCO₂R using 40 mEq/L bicarbonate demonstrated comparable short-term pH control, ventilatory feasibility, hemodynamic behavior, and survival. High-bicarbonate CRRT may offer a simpler alternative when ECCO₂R is unavailable, although longer-term effects warrant further investigation.

Figure (abstract P080) Kaplan–Meier curves. Panel A: Probability of remaining alive and free from norepinephrine use. Panel B: Overall survival probability, irrespective of vasopressor requirement.

Kaplan–Meier curves. Panel A: Probability of remaining alive and free from norepinephrine use. Panel B: Overall survival probability, irrespective of vasopressor requirement.

P081 Thoracic fascial plane blocks in cardiac anesthesia—systematic review and meta-analysis

SF Tünde, Á Csomós2, Z Molnár3, K Csöke-Kabai2, D Laczkó2, C Turan2, P Hegyi2

1Hospital of the Hungarian Defence Forces, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care, Bupapest, Hungary, 2Semmelweis University, Centre for Translational Medicine, Budapest, Hungary, 3Semmelweis University, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P081

Introduction: The increasing number of cardiac surgeries requires new anesthetic approaches to ensure enhanced patient recovery. Thoracic fascial plane blocks (TPBs) are safe and effective additions to conventional analgesia; the evidence for their role in cardiac analgesia is lacking. This systematic review and meta-analysis aim to find the possible role of additional TPBs in cardiac analgesia.

Methods: We conducted our systematic search in three databases (PubMed, Embase, Cochrane) on November 4, 2024 (PROSPERO ID CRD42024601988). We included 70 randomized controlled studies investigating adults undergoing cardiac surgery and receiving TPB in addition to conventional analgesia, in a total of 4632 patients (2318 intervention, 2314 control group). Other study types, studies comparing two types or combining TPBs, and pediatric patients were excluded. Data were pooled using a random-effects model, and the pooled mean difference (MD) and odds ratio (OR) with 95% confidence interval (CI) were calculated.

Results: TPBs administered before the surgical incision significantly reduced intraoperative fentanyl requirements by 271.38 µg (CI 95% (-370.99) – (-171.76); p < 0.001) [Figure]. Data showed lower VAS scores in the TPB group at the 12th postoperative hour, especially during coughing (-1.34; CI 95% (-2.01)–(-0.67); p = 0.001). TPBs were associated with faster extubation (-1.56 h; CI 95% (-2.28) – (-0.94); p < 0.001) and shorter ICU stay (-0,38 days; CI 95% (-0.54) – (-0.23); p < 0.001).

Conclusions: Our results suggest that additional TBPs are safe and effective in enhancing recovery in cardiac surgery. However, analgesia of minimally invasive approaches needs to be further investigated.

P082 Exploring a multi-parameter technology for pain assessment in adult intensive care: the nociception level index (NOL)

C Gélinas1, R Kagie2, S Shahiri1, HT Wang3, MC Gallani4, W Oulehri5, D Laporta6, P Richebé7

1McGill University, Ingram School of Nursing, Montreal, Canada, 2Jewish General Hospital, Centre for Nursing Research, Montreal, Canada, 3CHUM, Division of Intensive Care, Montreal, Canada, 4Université Laval, Faculty of Nursing, Quebec City, Canada, 5University Hospital of Strasbourg, Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Strasbourg, France, 6McGill University, Medicine Respiratory Division, Montreal, Canada, 7Polyclinic Bordeaux Nord Aquitaine, Anesthesia and Resuscitation, Bordeaux, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P082

Introduction: Pain is complex to assess in the adult intensive care unit (ICU). Many ICU patients cannot communicate their pain through self-report or behaviors, highlighting the need for alternative measures. Initially developed for anesthesia, the Nociception Level Index (NOL) is a multi-parameter technology capturing several parameters and their variability (i.e., heart rate, plethysmography, skin conductance and temperature). This study aimed to describe the validity of the NOL for ICU pain assessment.

Methods: An observational design was conducted in 3 ICUs in Montreal, Canada. ICU patients were assigned to one group according to their communication ability: a) Able to self-report (Group A), b) Able to express behaviors (Group B), and c) Unable to self-report and to express behaviors (Group C). NOL values were obtained at baseline (rest), during non-noxious (soft touch, and non-invasive blood pressure [NIBP]) and noxious stimulation (e.g., suctioning or turning). Group A was asked to provide their self-report of pain intensity (0–10 Pain Thermometer), and behaviors were rated using the 0–8 Critical-Care Pain Observation Tool (CPOT) in Group A and B during the noxious stimulation.

Results: A total of 91 ICU patients were enrolled (Group A: 42; Group B: 35; Group C: 14) with 33% female, mean age of 61, and mostly from North America or Europe (73%). Admissions were medical (50%) or surgical (42%) with a median ICU length of stay of 15 days (interquartile range or IQR = 4–36); half of them received opioids during data collection. Across all groups, median NOL values increased from 7.5 (IQR = 3–14) at baseline, to 8 (IQR = 2–16) at soft touch, 11 (IQR = 4–20) at NIBP and 28 (IQR = 18–40) during noxious stimulation (Friedman test, p < 0.001). NOL values were correlated with self-reported pain intensity (rho = 0.66, p = 0.002)) and CPOT scores (rho = 0.35, p = 0.005) but only during suctioning.

Conclusions: The NOL could detect noxious stimulation and was associated with pain scores in ICU patients.

P085 Efficacy and safety of remimazolam tosylate versus propofol for sedation of mechanically ventilated patients in intensive care units: a multicenter, randomized, single-blind, non-inferiority, phase 3 trial

N Liu1, M Chen2, X Guan2, F Lin3, J Yu4, M Yang5, Q Zhou6, K Chen7, H Zhao8, L Tang8

1The First Affiliated Hospital of Sun Yatsen University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Guangzhou, China, 2The First Affiliated Hospital of Sun Yatsen University, Guangzhou, China, 3Fujian Provincial Hospital, Fuzhou, China, 4Tianjin Nankai Hospital, Tianjin Medical University, Tianjin, China, 5Qujing No.1 Hospital, Qujing, China, 6Peking University Shougang Hospital, Beijing, China, 7Jinhua Central Hospital, Jinhua, China, 8Jiangsu Hengrui Pharmaceuticals Co., Ltd., Shanghai, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P085

Introduction: Remimazolam tosylate, a novel short-acting benzodiazepine, has shown effective and safe sedation in mechanically ventilated patients in intensive care units (ICU) in a phase 2 trial. We conducted a multicenter, randomized, single-blind, actively controlled, non-inferiority phase 3 trial (NCT06222294) for further evaluation.

Methods: Mechanically ventilated patients, requiring sedation for 6–24 h with a target Richmond Agitation–Sedation Scale (RASS) of -2 to 1, were randomized (1:1) to receive intravenous remimazolam tosylate (loading dose, 0.08 mg/kg; maintenance, 0–2.0 mg/kg/h) or propofol (loading dose, 0.3–0.5 mg/kg; maintenance, 0.3–4.0 mg/kg/h). Both allowed for adjusted infusion rates or given additional doses to maintain target RASS. Primary endpoint was sedation success rate, defined as the percentage of time maintaining target sedation range ≥ 70% of study drug administration time without rescue sedation. Non-inferiority margin was -8%.

Results: 211 randomized patients completed the trial (remimazolam tosylate, n = 106; propofol, n = 105). Sedation success rate was 98.1% with remimazolam tosylate and 96.2% with propofol (difference 1.9%, 95% CI -3.3% to 7.8%). Percentage of time in RASS target range and proportion of patients receiving rescue sedation were comparable. Mean (SD) number of additional doses required was 0.03 (0.17) for remimazolam tosylate and 0.18 (0.77) for propofol. Incidence of AE was 76.4% with remimazolam tosylate and 73.3% with propofol; all cases were mild to moderate, except one severe with propofol. Hypotension led to dose interruption in 1 patient in each group; bradycardia incidence was similar. The mean terminal half-life of remimazolam was generally consistent with that observed in a healthy human pharmacology study (1.97 ± 1.62 h vs. 1.76 ± 0.76 h), which involved a 2h remimazolam tosylate infusion.

Conclusions: Remimazolam tosylate demonstrated non-inferiority to propofol with good tolerance and efficacy for sedation of mechanically ventilated patients in ICU.

P087 Evaluating the use and safety of intravenous olanzapine at a level I pediatric trauma center emergency department and pediatric ICU

AS Mujeeb, ML Albrecht, JL Jantzer, JT Jancik

Hennepin Healthcare, Pharmacy, Minneapolis, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P087

Introduction: Intravenous olanzapine (OLZ) is not FDA approved for use in children. Pediatric-specific evidence for the safety of IV OLZ is limited, especially in children < 9 years of age. One review found that IV and IM OLZ were effective and well-tolerated when used in the ED for acute agitation in patients ages 9–18 years [1]. Another case series highlighted safe administration of IV OLZ in an 8-year-old patient, with minimal adverse events and no serious complications [2]. This study aims to characterize the use and safety of IV OLZ among pediatric patients ≤ 8 years of age.

Methods: This single-center retrospective observational cohort study included patients admitted from 1/7/2021 to 1/7/2025. The primary outcome was the incidence of IV OLZ administration in patients ≤ 8 years of age. Secondary outcomes included the incidence of bradycardia, hypotension, hypoxia, intubation, and QTc prolongation.

Results: We included 14 patients who ranged in age from 1–8 years. There were 33 IV OLZ administrations. The most common dose was 1.25 mg. Bradycardia was the most common adverse event occurring in 71.4% of patients, but no interventions were required. Hypotension occurred in 2% of patients, but vasopressors were not required. Prior to IV OLZ administration, 64.3% of patients were intubated. No patients experienced hypoxia or required intubation as a result of IV OLZ. Baseline EKGs were obtained in 43% of patients; 14.3% received a repeat EKG, and no patients had a prolonged QTc interval.

Conclusions: In children ≤ 8 years old, IV OLZ seemed to be well tolerated with bradycardia being the most common adverse effect.

P088 Safety of olanzapine doses greater than 30 mg within 24 h in the medical intensive care unit

JA Lloyd, KA Considine, JT Jancik

Hennepin Healthcare, Pharmacy, Minneapolis, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P088

Introduction: Intravenous olanzapine (OLZ) is not FDA-approved for agitation or delirium but is often used for this indication [1]. Studies have not shown an improvement in outcomes with antipsychotics, but most providers support their use [2]. The safety of total daily doses > 30 mg from all routes and IV OLZ doses > 20 mg within 24 h have not been evaluated. The maximum FDA-approved daily dose is 20 mg IM or enteral. The purpose of this study was to examine the incidence of cumulative OLZ doses > 30 mg (enteral, IV/IM) in MICU patients who received > 20 mg IV OLZ within 24 h and assess potential side effects.

Methods: This was a single-center retrospective observational cohort study from 1/7/2023 to 30/6/2025. The primary outcome was the incidence of OLZ doses > 30 mg within 24 h. Patients were included if they received > 20 mg IV OLZ within 24 h with at least 1 dose of ≥ 10 mg in the MICU. Secondary outcomes included average OLZ dosing and the incidence of hypotension, bradycardia, hypoxia, and QTc prolongation.

Results: There were 67 patient encounters with 44 having cumulative daily doses > 30 mg from all routes, of which 41 solely received IV OLZ. The average cumulative IV OLZ dose was 35 mg within 24 h. Hypotension occurred in 24% of patients with 4% requiring vasopressors. Hypoxia occurred in 12% of patients. Of all encounters, 84% were intubated prior to receiving > 20 mg IV OLZ within 24 h, 6% were subsequently intubated, and 10% were never intubated. There were no incidences of bradycardia requiring intervention. Prolonged QTc post-OLZ was seen in 4% of encounters with no episodes of torsades de pointes.

Conclusions: In a MICU population, cumulative OLZ doses > 30 mg were common among those who received > 20 mg IV OLZ within 24 h. Hypotension was the most common adverse effect.

P090 Multi-center prospective observational study of current analgo-sedation practices and the incidence of delirium in Indian ICUs

S Bhosale1, A Kulkarni2

1ACTREC, 22 Owe Village Kharghar, Critical Care Medicine, Mumbai, India, 2ACTREC, 22 Owe Village Kharghar, Critical Care Medicine, Tata Memorial Centre, HBNI, Mumbai, India

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P090

Introduction: Delirium is a neuropsychiatric syndrome shown to prolong mechanical ventilation, duration of ICU and hospital length of stay and in some studies, even mortality [1,2]. At present, the knowledge regarding current sedation practices and the incidence of delirium in the Indian ICUs is lacking.

Methods: Patients more than 18 years with an anticipated duration of ventilation of more than 48 h, need for sedation for more than 48 h were recruited in the study. These patients were followed up for 30 days (or until discharge from the hospital or death). The patients eligible for the study had a CAM-ICU score evaluated every 8 h to detect delirium.

Results: A total of 885 patients were included, out of which 538 (61%) patients had delirium. Mortality was higher in patients with delirium compared to patients who did not ( 21% vs 13%). The median Apache score was higher in patients with delirium (16 (IQR 12, 22) vs 14 (IQR 11, 20) The percentage of patients who remained in the desired -2 to 0 range was less in the delirium group (58% vs 66%) The median length of stay was higher in delirium group 6.0 (IQR 4.0, 10.0) vs 5.0 (IQR 3.0, 9.0)There was no difference in the duration of mechanical ventilation in patients with delirium compared to patients who did not have delirium. In multivariable risk regression analysis, age (odds ratio 1.66 (1.20–2.30, p = 0.002), male sex ( OR 1.48 (CI 1.06 -2.07 p = 0.022), use of prescription meds (2.33. 1.29–4.44 p = 0.007), use of vasopressors (1.81, 1.13, 2.95 p = 0.015), Sofa score (1.55, 1.11, 2.16 p = 0.01) were independent risk factors for delirium.

Conclusions: Delirium remains a significant occurrence in the ICU and is associated with poor outcomes. Several risk factors for delirium are linked to the development of delirium in the intensive care unit.

P091 Continuation versus discontinuation of renin–angiotensin–aldosterone system inhibitors in patients undergoing major non-cardiac surgery-systematic review and meta-analysis

ZW Weber1, C Turan1, SF Tünde2, L Zubek1, Z Molnár1, E Zima1

1Semmelweis University, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Budapest, Hungary, 2Hospital of the Hungarian Defence Forces, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P091

Introduction: Many patients undergoing major non-cardiac surgery are chronically treated with angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors (ACEIs) or angiotensin receptor blockers (ARBs) for hypertension or chronic heart failure. Perioperative management of these drugs remains controversial, as both continuation and discontinuation may lead to adverse outcomes. This study aimed to assess which strategy—continuation or discontinuation—is safer.

Methods: A systematic literature search was performed in CENTRAL, PubMed, and Embase on November 10, 2024, according to a registered PROSPERO (ID: CRD42024607744) protocol. Eligible studies included randomized controlled trials (RCTs) and observational studies of adult patients chronically treated with ACEIs or ARBs and undergoing major non-cardiac surgery. Studies comparing continuation versus discontinuation and reporting at least one predefined outcome were included. The risk of bias was assessed using the ROB2 tool for RCTs and ROBINS-I for observational studies.

Results: Sixteen studies with a total of 74,032 patients met the inclusion criteria, including seven observational studies, seven RCTs, and two post-hoc analyses. Data from six RCTs showed a significantly higher incidence of intraoperative episodes of hypotension (OR: 1.33, 95% CI: 1.20–1.47, p < 0.0001) in the continuation group (Figure). Other outcomes, such as myocardial injury after non-cardiac surgery (MINS) (OR:1.09, 95%CI: 0.95–1.24, p = 0.276) and mortality (OR: 0.91, 95% CI: 0.8–1.03, p = 0.104), were not statistically significant but tended to be higher in the continuation group.

Conclusions: Continuation of ACEIs or ARBs before major non-cardiac surgery is associated with a higher risk of intraoperative hypotension. Further studies are needed to determine optimal discontinuation timing and identify subgroups that may benefit from continuation.

P092 Epidemiology, outcomes and risk factors for mortality or severe postoperative complications in cardiac surgery patients admitted to intensive care units in Brazil: a multicenter observational study

R Carneiro de Freitas Chaves, N Naiara Barros de Vasconcelos, A José Pereira, T Domingos Corrêa, JM Silva Júnior

Hospital Israelita Albert Einstein, Critical Care Medicine, São Paulo, Brazil

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P092

Introduction: Cardiac surgery carries substantial risks of perioperative complications and mortality, yet outcome data from low- and middle-income countries are scarce. This study evaluated the incidence of early mortality and major postoperative complications in patients undergoing cardiac surgery in Brazil.

Methods: The study was registered at ClinicalTrials.gov (NCT06154473) and the protocol with the statistical analysis plan was published before completion of patient enrolment [1]. This multicenter prospective study included adults (≥ 18 years) undergoing cardiac surgery who require postoperative care in ICU. The primary outcome was the risk factors and incidence of mortality or severe postoperative complications within the first 3 postoperative days or until ICU discharge (whichever occurs first). Risk factors were assessed using generalized linear mixed-effects models with a binomial distribution and an identity link function, accounting for the study center as a random intercept to adjust for clustering, reported as odds ratios (OR) with 95% confidence intervals (CI).

Results: Between September 2023 and March 2025, 528 patients were enrolled across 11 hospitals in Brazil. Among 528 patients (91% open-heart and 9% percutaneous procedures, mainly CABG and valve replacements), 170 (32%) experienced early mortality or severe postoperative complications, most within the first 24 h (132 of 170, 78%). The most frequent complications were severe hemodynamic instability (126, 24%) ARDS (71, 13%), and severe bleeding (33, 6%). Risk factors included prior stroke, preoperative thrombocytopenia, open-heart surgery, biological valve use, higher SOFA scores, lower arterial pH, and postoperative vasoplegia (Figure).

Conclusions: In this large multicenter cohort of cardiac surgery patients in Brazil, one in three experienced early mortality or severe postoperative complications, mostly within the first 24 h.

P094 EA-230 attenuates the duration of clinically relevant postoperative complications in on-pump cardiac surgery patients: a post hoc analysis of a phase 2b study

S Van Doren1, M Kox1, G Wensvoort2, P Pickkers1

1Radboudumc, Intensive Care, Nijmegen, Netherlands, 2Exponential Biotherapies, Inc. EBI, The Hague, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P094

Introduction: In the phase 2b EASI study [1], administration of the PAMP/DAMP signaling attenuator EA-230 during on-pump cardiac surgery improved hemodynamic stability and attenuated organ dysfunction. Additionally, EA-230 significantly reduced hospital and ICU length of stay (LoS). We investigated whether or not these findings could be explained by a shorter duration of clinically relevant Post-Operative Complications (POCs).

Methods: By double-blinded post hoc analysis of the EASI study, adverse events were adjudicated according to the European Perioperative Clinical Outcome (EPCO) definitions [2] to assess POCs. Following EPCO definitions, POCs are determined as clinically relevant when they require specific clinical treatment. POC incidence, severity and duration were compared to LoS and between EA-230 and placebo groups.

Results: Of 179 patients, 110 developed ≥ 1 POC; predominantly arrhythmias, delirium, and infections. Although reduced incidence and severity-scores of POCs were found in the active versus placebo group, these did not reach statistical significance. EA-230 compared to placebo significantly attenuated the duration of clinically relevant POCs (median [IQR] 17.2 [9.4–32.0] vs 44.3 [10.1–171.0] hrs; p = 0.003). Patients with ≥1 POC had a significantly longer hospital LoS compared to those without POCs (median [IQR] 24 [20.0-146.0] vs 220 [189-282] hrs; p = 2e-16). In linear regression of log-transformed hospital LoS in patients with defined POC duration (R2 0.336), longer POC duration was associated with longer hospital LoS (β [95% CI] 1.05 [1.03-1.07] per 24 hr increase; p<0.001), whereas treatment with EA-230 was associated with reduced LoS (β [95% CI] 0.73 [0.60-0.88]; p = 0.002). Accordingly, in a Cox proportional hazard model including cumulative POC duration as a time dependent covariate for the population with no POCs and POCs of defined duration, longer POC duration was associated with a lower hazard of discharge (HR [95% CI] 0.87 [0.79-0.96] per 24 hr increase; p = 0.005), while treatment with EA-230 was linked to an increased hazard of discharge (HR [95% CI] 1.46 [1.03-2.09]; p = 0.036).

Conclusions: Intraoperative treatment with EA-230 significantly attenuates the duration of clinically relevant POCs. This plausibly accounts for the previously reported shorter hospital LoS.

References van Groenendael R et al. Crit Care Med 2021;49:790–803Jammer I et al. Eur J Anaesthesiol 2015;32:88-105

van Groenendael R et al. Crit Care Med 2021;49:790–803

P096 Perioperative oxygenation, cardiac rhythm disturbances, and hypotension in nonagenarian surgical patients

G Madrid, AL Delgadillo, A Romero, A Ojeda

Hospital Universitario Fundacion Santa Fe de Bogota, Anestesiologia, Bogotá, Colombia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P096

Introduction: Nonagenarians have markedly reduced physiological reserve, making them highly susceptible to intraoperative cardiopulmonary instability [1]. Oxygen desaturation (SpO₂ < 90%), new-onset arrhythmias, and hypotension may signal early respiratory or cardiovascular failure, yet their prognostic value in patients ≥ 90 years is unclear. This study examined whether these events were associated with postoperative complications, mortality, and recovery.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective cohort study of nonagenarian surgical patients in Colombia. Oxygenation deterioration was defined as any SpO₂ < 90% requiring intervention; arrhythmias as new rhythm disturbances requiring evaluation; and hypotension as systolic blood pressure requiring vasopressors or fluids. Primary outcomes were postoperative complications and in-hospital mortality. Secondary outcomes included vasopressor use, length of stay, delirium, and discharge disposition. Descriptive analyses were performed due to low event frequencies.

Results: Among 206 patients, oxygenation deterioration occurred in 8 (4%), arrhythmias in 14 (6.8%), and hypotension in a larger subset (Figure). All events were associated with higher postoperative complications (50–63% vs ≈22% without instability) and longer hospitalization (6–7 vs 4 days). Patients with instability required more vasopressors and had more delirium, reflecting reduced physiologic reserve. Mortality was low but proportionally higher in those with oxygenation deterioration or arrhythmias.

Conclusions: Intraoperative oxygen desaturation, arrhythmias, and hypotension, though infrequent, were strongly associated with postoperative complications, delayed recovery, and increased hemodynamic support needs in nonagenarians. Even brief instability appears to reflect profound vulnerability in this population. Enhanced monitoring and proactive management may help mitigate deterioration, highlighting the need for larger studies to guide perioperative care.

P098 Metabolism-centered TyG and TG/HDL models for early recognition of sepsis-induced cardiomyopathy: a multi-cohort machine-learning study

ZZ Zuo, JD Dai, QH Huang, WX Xu, LZ Zhang, ZQ Qian

Xiangya Hospital, Central South University, Critical Care Medicine, Changsha, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P098

Introduction: Sepsis-induced cardiomyopathy (SICM) is associated with high morbidity and mortality. However, effective biomarkers for predicting prognosis remain lacking.

Methods: We retrospectively assembled 2,588 adults with sepsis from MIMIC-IV and eICU. Variables obtained within 24 h of ICU admission were screened. Boruta (500 iterations) selected predictors. Five classifiers were benchmarked, and both light gradient boosting machine (LGBM) and random forest (RF) showed superior discrimination. Considering stability and interpretability, RF was selected to build three collinearity-aware pipelines: TyG-RF, TG/HDL-RF, and combined TyG + TG/HDL-RF. The final RF model underwent nested fivefold cross-validation, calibration and decision-curve analysis, and SHAP interpretation. Discrimination was assessed on a held-out internal test set with 95% CIs. External validation was performed in the Xiangya Immuno-Infection Biobank and Data Platform (n = 504). Restricted-cubic-spline (RCS) analyses explored non-linear associations and data-driven thresholds (Figure).

Results: Boruta retained 21 predictors. In the final RF pipeline, SHAP highlighted SOFA, creatinine, age, hemoglobin, TyG, TG/HDL as the most influential features (mean |SHAP|> 0.05). On the internal test set, the combined TyG + TG/HDL-RF model achieved the highest discrimination (AUC = 0.725 [95% CI 0.673–0.777]) with good calibration (intercept = 0.02; slope = 0.96), outperforming TyG-RF (AUC = 0.722) and TG/HDL-RF (AUC = 0.717). Multivariable logistic regression confirmed independent associations for TyG (OR 1.18, p = 0.001) and TG/HDL (OR 1.10, p = 0.045). RCS identified a TyG inflection around 10.21 for increased SICM risk. External validation in the XYIDB cohort yielded an AUC = 0.619, demonstrating acceptable generalizability.

Conclusions: The TyG + TG/HDL-based RF model provided the best internal discrimination and external robustness, while SHAP interpretation confirmed its biological plausibility, indicating TyG + TG/HDL-based RF model a promising tool in SICM.

P099 Differential cardiac microRNA expression in gram-negative and gram-positive sepsis: insights into septic cardiomyopathy

H Säkkinen1, P Lehto1, H Syrjälä1, R Kerkelä2, S Skarp2, K Porvari3, T AlaKokko1

1Oulu University Hospital, Research Group of Intensive Care Medicine, Intensive Care Center, Oulu, Finland, 2University of Oulu, Research Unit of Biomedicine and Internal Medicine Medical Research Center Oulu, Biocenter Oulu, Oulu, Finland, 3University of Oulu, Department of Forensic Medicine, Research Unit of Internal Medicine, Medical Research Center Oulu, Oulu, Finland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P099

Introduction: Septic cardiomyopathy (SCM) occurs in both gram-negative (GN) and gram-positive (GP) sepsis, despite distinct cytokine profiles. MicroRNAs (miRNAs) are key regulators of inflammation. To investigate SCM, we compared cardiac miRNA expression between GN and GP sepsis.

Methods: In our previous work, we identified differential expression of 32 miRNAs in cardiac autopsy samples from adult ICU patients with sepsis compared to non-septic medicolegal autopsy hearts. In this study, we analyzed miRNA expression in 4 GP sepsis hearts, 5 GN sepsis hearts, and 5 negative blood culture (NBC) hearts.

Results: GP sepsis patients had the highest APACHE II scores, procalcitonin (PCT) levels, and vasoactive-inotropic scores during the first 24 h in the ICU. miR-652-3p, which attenuates myocyte death, was upregulated in GP sepsis compared to NBC (1.4-fold change, p < 0.01). C-reactive protein (CRP) levels were highest in GN sepsis. Inflammatory promoters miR-199b-5p and miR-654-3p were upregulated in GN sepsis compared to GP sepsis (1.9-fold change, p < 0.05 for both). In GP sepsis, miR-199b-5p was downregulated compared to NBC (46% reduction in read counts, p < 0.05), along with miR-181c-3p, an inhibitor of mitochondrial COX1 (19% reduction, p < 0.05). Furthermore, miR-181a-3p (restricts vascular inflammation), miR-21-3p (promotes cardiac failure), and miR-3196 (unknown cardiac role) were upregulated in patients receiving levosimendan compared to non-receivers, though expression patterns did not differ by blood culture status.

Conclusions: GN sepsis appears to induce more intense cardiac inflammation than GP sepsis. In contrast, GP sepsis was associated with miRNA expression patterns suggesting reduced inflammation and enhanced myocyte protection, despite patients presenting with the highest risk of death at ICU admission. Myocardial miR-181a-3p, miR-21-3p, and miR-3196 were upregulated in cases of low cardiac output, regardless of the underlying pathogen.

P101 Left ventricular diastolic dysfunction in sepsis and its prognostic significance: a prospective observational study

N Wang, F Guo

Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Nanjing, Jiangsu, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P101

Introduction: Sepsis is frequently associated with cardiac dysfunction, among which left ventricular diastolic dysfunction (LVDD) plays a critical role in a significant morbidity and mortality. This study was aimed to characterize LVDD in patients with sepsis and assess its prognostic significance.

Methods: This prospective, single-center, observational study enrolled adult patients diagnosed with sepsis (Sepsis-3 definition). Patients were echocardiographically assessed within 12 h after admission, independently performed by an expert in critical care echocardiography who was unaware of patients’ outcome. LVDD was defined by the presence of a septal e’ maximal velocity < 8 cm/s and categorized by severity according to the septal E/e’ ratio. The primary outcome was 28-day mortality. Survival curves were generated using the Kaplan–Meier method.

Results: A total of 162 patients (age: 64 [53, 74] year; 57.4% male; APACHE II: 19 [14, 25]; SOFA: 8 [5, 11]) were enrolled. LVDD was observed in 50.6% of patients. 28-day mortality was statistically different between patients with and without LVDD (23/82 [28.0%] vs. 11/80 [13.8%]; HR: 2.25 [1.10–4.62]; p = 0.023). Further stratification of LVDD into mild, moderate, and severe grades revealed that patients with moderate and severe LVDD each accounted for 45.1%. Kaplan–Meier survival analysis revealed significant differences in 28-day cumulative survival among patients with different LVDD grades (Figure). Increasing LVDD severity was associated with higher 28-day mortality (X2 = 9.035, p = 0.027) and a higher incidence of new-onset atrial fibrillation within 72 h (X2 = 10.929, p = 0.012).

Conclusions: LVDD is significantly associated with 28-day mortality in patients with sepsis. The higher degree of diastolic dysfunction, the higher 28-day mortality and the higher risk of new-onset atrial fibrillation within 72 h.

Figure (abstract P101) Kaplan–Meier survival curves for 28-day mortality according to LVDD grades. Patients with severe LVDD had significantly lower 28-day survival than those with none or mild–moderate LVDD

Kaplan–Meier survival curves for 28-day mortality according to LVDD grades. Patients with severe LVDD had significantly lower 28-day survival than those with none or mild–moderate LVDD

P102 Assessment of septic cardiomyopathy prevalence in ICU patients with sepsis and septic shock using echocardiography-based tools.

L Puodziukaite1, U Parchutikaite1, D Kersnauskaite2, V Vicka1, J Trzebicka3, O Doroba3, M Zawadka3, S Glaveckaite2, G Davidavicius2, T Jovaisa1

1Vilnius University, Clinic of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care, Institute of Clinical Medicine, Vilnius, Lithuania, 2Vilnius University, Clinic of Cardiovascular Diseases, Institute of Clinical Medicine, Vilnius, Lithuania, 3Medical University of Warsaw, 2nd Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Warsaw, Poland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P102

Introduction: Septic cardiomyopathy (SCM) is a reversible cardiac dysfunction that occurs in patients with sepsis or septic shock and is associated with increased morbidity and mortality. Early recognition of SCM using echocardiography-based parameters is crucial for optimizing patient management.

Methods: A multicenter, prospective observational study was conducted at tertiary reference hospitals in Lithuania and Poland between March 2024 and October 2025. Transthoracic echocardiography and hemodynamic assessment were performed within 48 h of enrolment. SCM was diagnosed using left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF < 50% or ≥ 10% reduction from baseline), cardiac power output (CPO < 0.6W), and afterload-related cardiac performance (ACP < 80%). Statistical analysis was conducted using SPSS (version 31.0.1.0).

Results: A total of 104 patients were included. The median age was 64 [52–72] years. The median SOFA score was 10 [9–13], the mean APACHE II score was 21.8 ± 6.9, the mean SAPS II score was 48.9 ± 15.5. The median of lactate concentration was 2.96 mmol/L [1.9–5.5], and the median of maximal norepinephrine dose was 0.35 µg/kg/min [0.2–0.5]. Septic shock was diagnosed in 89 patients (85.6%). SCM was identified using these criteria: a reduced LVEF in 21 patients (20.2%), decreased CPO in 33 patients (33.3%), and impaired ACP in 29 patients (49.5%). LVEF served as the reference standard. Pearson’s Chi-square test showed no significant correlation between LVEF and CPO (p = 0.215) or between LVEF and ACP (p = 0.960).

Conclusions: SCM was detected in a substantial proportion of patients, with CPO and ACP identifying additional cases beyond LVEF. However, no significant correlation was observed between LVEF and the other criteria, suggesting that CPO and ACP may detect different aspects of cardiac dysfunction not captured by LVEF alone.

P103 Diastolic dysfunction in acute and critical illness: from acute pathophysiology to chronic HFpEF

A Binuraj, O ReevesChen, M De Silva Morgan, T Veenith, S Thilak, C Chacko, T Fisher, M Abbawy, J Cotton

New Cross Hospital, Intensive Care, Wolverhampton, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P103

Introduction: Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF) is a multisystem inflammatory syndrome linked to comorbidities such as obesity, diabetes, hypertension and ageing [1]. In critical illness, especially sepsis, acute diastolic dysfunction is common, but its long-term impact remain unclear [2]. This review examines evidence on mechanisms, diagnosis and clinical trajectory across acute and chronic settings.

Methods: This was a narrative review of mechanistic, echocardiographic and clinical outcome studies. Our review incorporated HFpEF pharmacotherapy trials, ICU cardiac imaging literature, and emerging diagnostic techniques e.g. AI-assisted echocardiography.

Results: HFpEF arises from comorbidity-driven inflammation causing endothelial dysfunction, microvascular disease and myocardial stiffening. Critical illness adds further insult: cytokine storm, vasoplegia and mitochondrial dysfunction impair relaxation and trigger acute diastolic dysfunction. Large cohorts show high prevalence in sepsis, many of whom develop ongoing dysfunction and elevated long-term cardiovascular risk. ICU diagnostic accuracy is limited by ventilation, fluid shifts and vasoactive therapy, with lateral e’ (a doppler tissue imaging measure) emerging as the most reliable parameter. Newer therapies: SGLT2 inhibitors, mineralocorticoid antagonists and GLP-1 receptor agonists, coupled with AI-enhanced imaging present diagnostic and therapeutic opportunities.

Conclusions: Diastolic dysfunction in critical illness reflects interplay between inflammation and cardiac vulnerability (Figure). Though often reversible, persistent dysfunction may lead to chronic HFpEF. Advances in imaging, phenotyping and targeted therapy offer promising avenues for intervention and follow-up.

P104 The HEAR score in ruling out acute coronary syndrome

Y Walha, W Demni, E Ben Mhamed, D Hamdi, F Lachtar, W Saidi, W Bahria, N Nouira

Monji Slim Hospital, Emergency Department, Tunis, Tunisia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P104

Introduction: Chest pain is a common symptoms in patients visiting the emergency department. Acute coronary syndrome (ACS) poses a diagnostic challenge for physicians. The HEAR score is a clinical tool developed to identify patients at low risk of ACS. the aim of this study was to assess the performance of the HEAR score in ruling out the diagnosis of ACS and major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) at 6 weeks.

Methods: A prospective, observational and analytical study was conducted at the emergency department of Mongi Slim Hospital from January 2022, to September 2023, involving patients presenting with acute non-traumatic chest pain. The HEAR score was calculated for all patients, and a follow-up telephone contact was made after 6 weeks to assess for MACE.

Results: A total of 277 patients were included. The average age was 63 ± 16.5 years. The sex-ratio was 1.9. ACS was diagnosed with 27.8% of patients( 0.9% in the low-risk group). The HEAR score was predictive of ACS (p = 0.01; adjusted OR = 1.7; CI [1.1–2.6]). AUC = 0.85. The threshold for the HEAR score to exclude ACS was < 4, with a sensitivity (98.7%), specificity (52.8%), PPV (45.6%), NPV (98.2%) and LR- (0.02). At 6 weeks, 21% of patients experienced MACE. The HEAR score was predictive of MACE (p < 0.01; OR = 1.7; CI [1.2–2.3]). AUC = 0.74. The threshold for the HEAR score to exclude MACE at 6 weeks was < 3, with sensitivity (98.2%), specificity (33.6%), PPV (27.9%), NPV (98.7%), and LR- (0.05).

Conclusions: The HEAR score is a simple and valid clinical tool with strong discriminative power, allowing for the exclusion of ACS diagnosis in low-risk patients.

P105 Epidemiological profile and risk factors for cardiovascular related admissions to a coronary intensive care unit over a 10-year period

G Silva1, J Fidelis1, M Camargo1, H Valente2, P Morelhão3, CE Bosso1

1Universidade do Oeste Paulista UNOESTE, Medical College, Presidente Prudente, Brazil, 2Universidade Estadual Paulista “Júlio de Mesquita Filho” UNESP, Department Physical Therapy, Presidente Prudente, Brazil, 3Associação Fundo de Incentivo à Pesquisa AFIP, Institute, São Paulo, Brazil

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P105

Introduction: The increasing demand for care in coronary intensive care units (CICU) among patients with cardiovascular diagnoses is a growing reality [1].The aim was to evaluate the epidemiological profile and risk factors associated with cardiovascular related admissions to a CICU over a 10-year period.

Methods: An epidemiological study was conducted using logistic regression analysis of data from 2014 to 2025, obtained from electronic medical records of the CICU at the Regional Hospital of Presidente Prudente, São Paulo, Brazil. The study was duly approved by the institution’s Medical Ethics Committee.

Results: There were 8,617 admissions, of which 3,595 involved a cardiovascular diagnosis. The mean age was 62 years, with a predominance of male patients (60.9%), and mortality ranged from 5.2% to 13.7%. The cohort presented a low-risk Charlson Comorbidity Index (1.2), a pre-frail Modified Frailty Index (MFI-5) (2.02), and a Simplified Acute Physiology Score (SAPS 3) of 44.33. The average duration of mechanical ventilation was 2 days (SD 2.49), and the mean length of stay was 4 days (SD 4.32).Deep vein thrombosis (DVT) (OR = 3.42; 95%CI = 1.57–7.40; p < 0.001), dialysis-dependent chronic kidney disease (DD-CKD) (OR = 2.95; 95%CI = 1.77–4.42; p < 0.001), and the use of vasopressors (OR = 2.38; 95%CI = 1.93–2.94; p < 0.001) were associated with a higher likelihood of cardiovascular related admission. The use of non-invasive ventilation (NIV) (OR = 0.655; 95%CI = 0.540–0.794; p < 0.001) were associated with reduced probability.

Conclusions: The severity scores indicate an intermediate morbidity and mortality profile, with generally favorable prognoses despite the predominance of older adults. Modifiable clinical factors that protect against progression to cardiovascular complications were identified, such as reducing vasopressor exposure and limiting the need for NIV. Recognizing underlying conditions such DVT and DD-CKD that increase risk is essential for optimal management.

P106 Temporal trends in the presentation and management of ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction (STEMI)

E Vanstaen1, R Lauwers1, A Demolder2, R Herman2, A Rafajdus3

1AZORG, ED, Aalst, Belgium, 2AZORG, Cardiology, Aalst, Belgium, 3AZORG, PM Cardio, Aalst, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P106

Introduction: Temporal variations in the presentation rates and treatment of ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction (STEMI) are not fully understood. Identifying patterns of STEMI occurrence may reveal underlying factors altering emergency cardiac care delivery and resource planning.

Methods: We retrospectively analyzed all patients with acute chest pain who underwent cardiac catheterization between January 2023 and December 2024 at a tertiary referral center. Demographics, clinical and procedural characteristics, ECG findings and management metrics were collected. Temporal STEMI incidence was analyzed across days, weeks and months using time-series analyses. Door-to-cathlab time and in-hospital mortality were compared.

Results: Among 562 chest pain patients (mean age 68.2 ± 13.4 years, 69.6% male), 165 patients (29.4%) were diagnosed with STEMI. Distribution throughout the year was non-uniform: peaking in April (late winter, early spring), while the lowest incidence was observed in October (autumn) (p = 0.003, chi-square goodness-of-fit test). Monthly patterns were consistent across both years (p = 0.30). Weekly variation revealed peaks on Mondays and Fridays and fewer admissions on weekends (p = 0.016, chi square goodness-of-fit test). The weekly variation was consistent across all months (p = 0.329). Median door-to-cathlab time improved from 2023 to 2024 (15.2 h [1.5–45.5] vs. 7.6 h [0.9–22.6], p < 0.001). The shortest median door-to-cathlab time was noted in April (0.4 h [0.3–1.1]), suggesting higher vigilance during clustered presentations though there was no statistical significance (p = 0.158).

Conclusions: In this single-center registry, STEMI presentations showed seasonal variation, peaking in late winter/early spring and reaching a low in autumn, alongside consistent weekly patterns. Continuous temporal monitoring may support more responsive resource planning in acute cardiac care.

P107 Myocardial infarction in young adults < 45: risk profile and clinical course

G Silva1, J Fidelis1, M Camargo1, H Valente2, P Morelhão3, CE Bosso1

1Universidade do Oeste Paulista UNOESTE, Medical College, Presidente Prudente, Brazil, 2Universidade Estadual Paulista “Júlio de Mesquita Filho” UNESP, Department Physical Therapy, Presidente Prudente, Brazil, 3Associação Fundo de Incentivo à Pesquisa AFIP, Institute, São Paulo, Brazil

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P107

Introduction: The first coronary event has been occurring at progressively younger ages [1]. Yet evidence on the risk factors and clinical outcomes of myocardial infarction (MI) in young adults in Brazil remains limited. Understanding this population is essential due to the long term health and socioeconomic implications of early onset MI. This study evaluated the risk factors and clinical outcomes associated with MI in individuals under 45 years of age.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective cohort study of 1,845 patients. Pathological data were obtained through a systematic review of electronic medical records from the Coronary Intensive Care Unit at the Regional Hospital of Presidente Prudente, São Paulo, Brazil. The study was approved by the institutional Ethics Committee. Logistic regression models were employed to evaluate the associations of interest.

Results: The use of psychoactive substances (OR = 13.36; 95%CI = 1.87–95.62; p = 0.01), alcoholism (OR = 2.28; 95%CI = 1.26–4.14; p = 0.007), smoking (OR = 1.60; 95%CI = 1.12–2.29; p = 0.010) and male sex (OR = 1.55; 95%CI = 1.03–2.38; p = 0.035) were significantly associated with an increased likelihood of MI in young people. Diabetes (OR = 0.27; 95%CI = 0.16–0.45; p < 0.001), hypertension (OR = 0.25; 95%CI = 0.17–0.36; p < 0.001), hypothyroidism (OR = 0.13; 95%CI = 0.02–0.95; p = 0.045), NYHA functional class II-III (OR = 0.32; 95%CI = 0.12–0.87; p = 0.025), and a previous history of MI (OR = 0.49; 95%CI = 0.27–0.88; p = 0.017) were associated with a lower probability of MI in young people. Younger patients had lower odds of requiring a bladder catheter (OR = 0.315; p < 0.001) and mechanical ventilation (OR = 0.401; p = 0.021).

Conclusions: Modifiable lifestyle factors, including smoking, alcohol consumption, and the use of psychoactive substances were associated with MI in young adults. Younger patients also required fewer invasive procedures, indicating a more favorable short-term clinical course.

P108 The CAMI score: a new prognostic tool

Y Walha, W Demni, D Hamdi, K Jemai, K Ouerhani, F Mansour, W Bahria, N Nouira

Monji Slim Hospital, Emergency Department, Tunis, Tunisia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P108

Introduction: ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction (STEMI) represents a critical diagnostic and therapeutic emergency. Risk stratification is strongly recommended to guide management. The objective was to evaluate the performance of the CAMI-STEMI score in predicting in-hospital mortality as well as the occurrence of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) during hospitalization and at 30 days.

Methods: A prospective, observational, and longitudinal study conducted from February 1, 2022, to September 30, 2023, including patients admitted for the management of STEMI. The CAMI-STEMI score was evaluated for predicting in-hospital and 30-day mortality.

Results: During the study period, 163 patients were included. The mean age was 61 ± 13 years, with a gender ratio of 4.6. Hypertension was the main cardiovascular risk factor (41.7%), followed by smoking (40.5%) and diabetes (38%). The primary reperfusion methods were thrombolysis (79 patients) and primary percutaneous coronary intervention (69 patients). In-hospital mortality was 14.1%, in-hospital MACE was 23%, and MACE at 30 days post-discharge was 19%. Univariate analysis of the CAMI-STEMI score showed it to be a predictor of in-hospital mortality, in-hospital MACE, 30-day MACE, and cumulative 30-day mortality, with p values of 0.01, < 0.01, 0.019, and < 0.01, respectively. ROC curve analysis demonstrated that the score was significant for in-hospital mortality, in-hospital MACE, and cumulative 30-day mortality, with AUCs of 0.88, 0.8, and 0.87, respectively.

Conclusions: The CAMI-STEMI score in our study not only predicts in-hospital mortality and early in-hospital MACE but also mortality at 30 days post-episode.

P109 Real-world diagnostic and prognostic performance of an AI-enhanced electrocardiogram for acute myocardial infarction in the emergency department

YJ Seong, SH Lee, EJ Jung, SJ Ryu, YS Cho

Chonnam National University Hospital, Emergency Medicine, Gwangju, South Korea

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P109

Introduction: Artificial intelligence–based ECG interpretation shows potential for detecting acute myocardial infarction (AMI), but its real-world clinical utility remains unclear. This study evaluated the diagnostic and prognostic performance of an AI-enhanced ECG model in emergency department (ED) patients with suspected AMI.

Methods: The ROMIAE project was a prospective, multicentre cohort study across 18 university hospitals in Korea (March 2022–October 2023). Adults presenting within 24 h of AMI-suggestive symptoms were enrolled. Key predictors included the AI-ECG score, HEART score, GRACE 2.0 score, high-sensitivity troponin, and a physician-derived AMI score. The primary outcome was confirmed AMI during index hospitalization; the secondary outcome was 30-day major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE).

Results: Among 8,493 participants, 1,586 (18.6%) were diagnosed with AMI. AI-ECG demonstrated strong diagnostic performance (AUROC 0.878; 95% CI 0.868–0.888), comparable to the HEART score (0.877) and superior to GRACE 2.0, troponin, and physician assessment. For 30-day MACE prediction, AI-ECG maintained high discrimination (AUROC 0.866; 95% CI 0.856–0.877), similar to the HEART score (0.858). Adding AI-ECG to risk stratification significantly improved classification, yielding a net reclassification index of 19.6% and increasing the C-index to 0.926, compared with the HEART score alone.

Conclusions: In routine, multicentre ED practice, the AI-ECG provided robust diagnostic accuracy for AMI and strong short-term prognostic performance. Its effectiveness matched or exceeded established clinical scores and physician judgment, supporting the value of integrating AI-ECG into AMI assessment and risk stratification.

P110 Non-ST-elevation acute coronary syndrome in the emergency department: predictive factors of acute heart failure

W Saidi, W Bahria, Y Walha, D Wiem, D Hamdi, I Aloui, F Lachtar, N Nouira

Monji Slim Hospital, Emergency Department, Tunis, Tunisia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P110

Introduction: Non-ST elevation acute coronary syndromes (NSTE-ACS) represent a major public health issue in Tunisia. They are associated with a significant risk of complications, particularly acute heart failure (AHF), with a high mortality rate. The aim of our study was to determine the predictive factors of AHF in patients admitted to the emergency department (ED) for NSTE-ACS.

Methods: This observational, descriptive, prospective, and single-center study was conducted in the ED of Mongi Slim Hospital over a period of 17 months. We included patients over 18 years old admitted to the ED for NSTE-ACS. Patients with sepsis or hemorrhagic shock were excluded. The primary outcome was the occurrence of short-term (in the ED) and medium-term (at 6 months) AHF.

Results: A total of 245 patients were included in the study. The mean age of the patients was 63 ± 0.54 years, with a range from 27 to 93 years. A male predominance was observed, with a sex ratio of 1.69. Among the patients, 87% had at least one cardiovascular risk factor. AHF was observed in 16% of patients at admission and in 27% of patients at 6 months. In multivariate analysis, the independent predictive factors for short-term AHF were a history of atrial fibrillation, renal failure, CRP ≥ 10 mg/dl, and LVEF < 45%, with (p = 0.001; adjusted OR = 5.32; 95% CI [1.48–19.18]), (p = 0.006; adjusted OR = 5.51; 95% CI [1.61–18.76]), and (p ≤ 0.001; adjusted OR = 4.20; 95% CI [1.61–10.97]), respectively. The independent predictive factors at 6 months were a history of diabetes and treatment with loop diuretics, with (p = 0.002; adjusted OR = 4.04; 95% CI [1.64–9.98]) and (p = 0.001; adjusted OR = 6.5; 95% CI [2.39–17.96]), respectively.

Conclusions: These results highlight the importance of early and appropriate management of patients at risk for AHF, especially those with cardiovascular history and unfavorable biological markers, in order to improve their short- and medium-term prognosis.

P112 Prognostic value of the leuko-glycemic index in ST-segment elevation myocardial infraction in the emergency department

D Hamdi, F Sbei, Y Walha, W Demni, W Bahria, N Nouira

Monji Slim Hospital, Emergency Department, Tunis, Tunisia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P112

Introduction: The Leuko-Glycemic Index (LGI) is an index that combines white blood cell count and venous blood glucose level. It has been described in the literature as a marker of systemic inflammatory response and as a prognostic factor in myocardial infraction. The aim of this study was to determine the prognostic value of the LGI in ST-Segment Elevation Myocardial infarction (STEMI) in the emergency department.

Methods: This was a prospective analytical study, conducted in the emergency department over a 24-month period, including patients over 18 years old admitted to the emergency department with STEMI. The LGI was measured at admission for all patients.

Results: A total of 168 patients were included. The mean age was 61 ± 13 years, with a male predominance (76%). Hypertension was the most common cardiovascular risk factor (41%), and 37% were known diabetics. The mean LGI in known diabetic and non-diabetic patients was respectively 9579 ± 6297 mg/dL·mm3 and 6449 ± 6386 mg/dL·mm3, (p = 0.026). The most frequent complication was acute pulmonary edema (12.5%), followed by arrhythmias (11.3%) and cardiogenic shock (9.5%). Patients with arrhythmia had a higher LGI compared to those without arrhythmia (12,899 mg/dL·mm3 versus 7,258 mg/dL·mm3, p < 0.001). The LGI measured at admission was significantly associated with the occurrence of acute pulmonary edema (AUC = 0.724; p = 0.004; 95% CI = [0.58–0.868]), cardiogenic shock (AUC = 0.756; p = 0.016; 95% CI = [0.54–0.97]), and in-hospital mortality (AUC = 0.801; p < 0.001; 95% CI = [0.665–0.936]).

Conclusions: The LGI was associated with the occurrence of complications and mortality in patients with STEMI. The LGI could be used for prognostic assessment. Larger-scale studies with patient follow-up are needed to better determine the prognostic value of the LGI.

P114 How should new-onset atrial fibrillation (NOAF) on critical care be managed at discharge?

H Dial, M Spivey, A Garland, S Thomas, CC Research Team

Royal Cornwall Hospital, Critical Care, Truro, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P114

Introduction: Atrial fibrillation (AF) is exceedingly common in the critically unwell. Emerging data suggests that NOAF in ICU survivors is associated with poor outcomes. There is increased risk of short and long term complications following NOAF [1]. Individuals who develop NOAF during their ICU admission represent a unique population and there are currently no accepted risk stratification tools to guide follow up.

Methods: Analysis of 561 patients who developed NOAF during their ICU admission from 2015 to 2025. Electrolyte disturbance and the use of vasopressors were recorded. Initiation of anticoagulation at the time of NOAF and whether echocardiography was utilised. We recorded whether NOAF had resolved at the time of discharge and whether NOAF was included in the discharge summary.

Results: Of the 561 patients included in this study, 47% underwent echocardiogram during their inpatient stay. 430 patients survived until discharge. Of these patients, 21% were still in AF on discharge from ICU. 42% had documentation of NOAF in their discharge summary. Interestingly, only 70% of patients still in AF at discharge had NOAF mentioned in their discharge summary.

Conclusions: Follow up after discharge could improve outcomes for these patients. Evidence from this large DGH shows that while AF is a common occurrence during ICU admission, echocardiogram is rarely used in these patients. The minority of patients with NOAF have any documentation after discharge.

P116 New-onset atrial fibrillation (NOAF) in ICU patients: An audit of clinical characteristics, management strategies, and outcome in a large DGH critical care unit

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P116

Introduction: NOAF is common in critically ill patients and associated with increased morbidity and mortality [1]. There is no consensus for management strategies of ICU acquired NOAF.

Methods: Using Philips ICCA, patients who developed NOAF during their ICU admission from 2015 to 2025 were identified. Records were examined for pre-AF electrolytes, vasopressor requirements, management strategies, and discharge status.

Increased vasopressor dosing identified a critically ill subgroup with elevated mortality.There was no difference in rhythm control between amiodarone or β-blockade, but DCCV was the most effective, yet rarely used.

P117 Effect of landiolol compared to amiodarone in treatment of supraventricular cardiac arrhythmias in septic shock patients

A Hordyna1, M Domagala2, M Szukala1, D Siwicka1, W Dabrowski3

1Medical University of Lublin, Lublin, Poland, 2Medical Division of Jan Kochanowski University, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Kielce, Poland, 3Medical University of Lublin, Anaesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Lublin, Poland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P117

Introduction: Supraventricular cardiac arrhythmias (SA) in septic shock patients (SS) can lead to haemodynamic instability and hypotension, requiring increased doses of vasopressors. Landiolol, the ultra-selective β1 blocker, or amiodarone are commonly used to treat such disorders. Some studies documented a beneficial effect of landiolol in critically ill patients [1,2]. The aim of this study was to analyse the effects of landiolol and amiodarone on the dose of vasopressors, some haemodynamic variables and time required to restore normocardia.

Methods: Adult patients treated with landiolol or amiodarone for SA following SS were studied. Patients with a previous history of recurrent SA were excluded. Haemodynamic variables including: heart rate (HR), cardiac index (CI) and stroke volume variation (SVV) were measured using EV1000 platform (Edwards, USA). Continuous norepinephrine infusion was used to correct the mean blood pressure and systemic vascular resistance index. Both landiolol and amiodarone were administered continuously: Landiolol at 20 μg/kg/min and amiodarone at 7 μg/kg/min (approximately 600 mg/day). The effects of landiolol and amiodarone were analysed within 96 h.

Results: 120 patients were included. Five were excluded due to a lack of data or withdrew their agreement. Finally, 60 patients were treated with landiolol and 55 with amiodarone. The mean time to restoration of normocardia was 12 ± 5.5 h in patients treated with landiolol and 42 ± 16 h in patients treated with amiodarone. Landiolol corrected CI and SVV within 24 h, whereas amiodarone did not affect either parameter for 72 h. Treatment with landiolol allowed for a reduction a dose of norepinephrine during the studied period (Figure).

Conclusions: Landiolol restored normal heart rhythm significantly faster than amiodarone. Use of Landiolol improves cardiac output, probably by SVV correction.

P118 Early versus late beta-blocker initiation in critically ill patients: a retrospective observational study

WL Song1, WN Zhu1, XX Ma1, YL Lu2, JF Wu1, XD Guan1, X Si1, R Shi1

1The First Affiliated Hospital of Sun Yatsen University, Critical Care Medicine, Guang Zhou, China, 2Longgang Central Hospital, Critical Care Medicine, Shen Zhen, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P118

Introduction: The optimal timing for initiating beta-blocker therapy in critically ill patients remains unclear. This study investigated the impact of early versus late administration of short-acting beta-blockers on heart rate control, hemodynamic stability, organ support duration, and clinical outcomes.

Methods: In this single-center, retrospective observational study, ICU patients who received beta-blockers between June 2016 and December 2022 were analyzed. Propensity score matching (PSM) was used to balance baseline characteristics between groups. Early initiation was defined as within 24 h of ICU admission; late initiation occurred thereafter. Heart rate (HR) was assessed using generalized estimating equations. Kaplan–Meier and Cox regression analyses were employed to evaluate 28-day mortality. The primary outcome was HR control; secondary outcomes included hemodynamic parameters, ICU and hospital length of stay (ICULOS, HLOS), vasopressor duration, and mortality.

Results: Of 3,963 screened patients, 466 were included after PSM (233 per group). Median BB initiation time was 10 h (early) vs 45.7 h (late), using a 24h cutoff. Early BB use significantly reduced HR (− 5.9 bpm, p < 0.01; Figure, panel a), ICU-LOS and HLOS (21 vs 28 days, p < 0.001), without affecting mortality (Figure, panel d). Despite higher initial NE doses, vasopressors were weaned faster in the early group (32 vs 46 h, p = 0.024; Figure, panel c). In subgroup analyses, early BB shortened ICU-LOS/HLOS in non-septic patients (21 vs 29 days, p < 0.001) and ventilation time in septic patients (56 vs 126 h, p = 0.046). Among those on NE before BB, early use further improved HR (− 8.9 bpm, p < 0.001; Figure, panel e) and reduced vasopressor duration (82 vs 131h, p = 0.015).

Conclusions: Early initiation of beta-blockers in ICU patients was associated with better heart rate control, reduced ICU/hospital stay, and faster vasopressor weaning—particularly among non-septic patients and those receiving early vasopressor support.

P119 Some factors affecting the ineffective treatment of cardiac arrhythmias with landiolol in septic shock patients

W Dabrowski1, M Domagala2, M Szukala1, D Siwicka1

1Medical University of Lublin, Anaesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Lublin, Poland, 2Medical Division of Jan Kochanowski University of Kielce, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Kielce, Poland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P119

Introduction: Supraventricular arrhythmias (SA) are frequently noted in critically ill septic shock (SS) patients. Landiolol, the ultra-selective β1 blocker, is used to treat SA. However, the Landi-SEP study challenged its efficacy in SS patients [1]. SS causes electrolyte disorders and associated inflammation predispose to SA [2,3]. Therefore, disorders in blood magnesium concentration and massive inflammatory response can affect landiolol efficacy. The aim of this study was to analyze the relationship between landiolol efficacy, degree of inflammation, and plasma ionized magnesium (iMg) concentration in SS.

Methods: Adult patients treated for SA following SS were studied. Patients with a previous history of recurrent SA were excluded. PCT, CRP, IL-6 concentrations, and neutrophil-to-lymphocyte and platelet-to-lymphocyte ratios reflected the level of inflammation. A blood sample was collected just before landiolol treatment. Landiolol was administered continuously at the dose of 20 μg/kg/min. The effect of landiolol was analysed within 24 h.

Results: 73 patients were included. Treatment with landiolol was ineffective in 13 patients (17.8%). iMg was lower, and IL-6 and PCT were higher in patients treated with landiolol unsuccessfully (iMg: 0.77 ± 0.21 vs. 0.54 ± 0.22 mmol/L, IL-6: 2959.2 ± 1378.2 vs. 1591.7 ± 2033.1 pg/mL, PCT: 23.9 ± 17 vs. 17 ± 16.8 ng/mL) (Figure). Multivariate analysis showed that low iMg and high IL-6 concentrations were associated with ineffective treatment with landiolol (p < 0.001 and p < 0.05, respectively).

Conclusions: An inflammatory response accompanied by low iMg reduces the effectiveness of landiolol. Low iMg concentrations appear to be the most critical factor affecting landiolol efficacy in septic shock patients.

P120 Effects of preoperative antiarrhythmic drugs in heart recipients on the occurrence of primary graft dysfunction

G Thery1, K Ouellette2, Y Lamarche2, A Ducharme2, G Giraldeau2, MC Parent2, M Tremblay-Gravel2, J Boulet2, PE Noly2

1.Reims University Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Reims CEDEX, France, 2 Montreal Heart Institute, Montreal, Canada

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P120

Introduction: The use of medications—especially antiarrythmic drugs administered to the recipient before heart transplantation (HT)—and their association with primary graft dysfunction (PGD) is a matter of concern [1]. In particular, amiodarone may exert negative chronotropic and inotropic effects on the cardiac transplant. Therefore, we sought to investigate the association between pre-transplant antiarrythmic therapy in patients with end-stage heart failure undergoing HT and the incidence of PGD.

Methods: Retrospective, monocentric cohort study, including all primary HT in adult patients between 2010 and 2024 at the Montreal Heart Institute (Québec, CA). Exclusion criteria were multiorgan transplantations and re-transplantation. Inverse probability weights were estimated as the inverse of patients’ estimated probability of developing a PGD. In the propensity score models, the group (absence of PGD or presence of PGD) was the dependent variable, and all confounders were independent variables.

Results: We included 194 patients. One hundred and thirty-six were male (70%), with a mean age of 50.2 years-old and mostly transplanted for a dilated cardiomyopathy (44.8%). Among them, 69 experienced mild to severe PGD with an increased risk of in-hospital mortality (20.3% vs 4.8%, p = 0.002). Ninety-two patients received amiodarone in the 48 h before HT. Preoperative amiodarone was associated with an increased risk of developing PGD (odds ratio (OR) = 3.30 [1.00–10.89], p = 0.049). Amiodarone associated with one beta-blocker or two anti-arrythmic medications was also associated with a risk of PGD (respectively OR = 2.82 (1.46–5.45), p = 0.002, and OR = 2.37 (1.26–4.46), p = 0.007]. The adjusted probability per group of PGD occurrence are represented on the Figure.

Conclusions: Pre-HT use of amiodarone is significantly associated with PGD, whether it is associated with another antiarrhythmic medication or not.

P121 Comparison of esmolol versus landiolol on mortality in adult patients with sepsis: a systematic review and network meta-analysis

ZY Tang1, Q Sun2, J.Y Xu2, Y Yang2, F Peng2

1Southeast University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China, 2Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P121

Introduction: The clinical efficacy of short-acting β-blockers in the management of sepsis remains uncertain. In particular, the comparative effects of two commonly used agents—esmolol and landiolol—have not been clearly established. This network meta-analysis aims to systematically evaluate and compare the effects of esmolol, landiolol and standard of care (SOC) on mortality in patients with sepsis.

Methods: A systematic search of PubMed, Web of Science, Embase, MEDLINE, CENTRAL, ClinicalTrials.gov, preprints and citation searching was conducted before April 15, 2025 (CRD420251033640). A network meta-analysis based on Bayesian (R v4.4.3) and frequentist approaches (Stata v18.0).

Results: A total of 1,165 records were identified through searches of five databases, registries and relevant references up to April 15, 2025. Ten studies involving 1,035 patients were included, after screening and eligibility assessment. Compared to esmolol, landiolol was associated with increased 28-day mortality (RR, 1.57; 95% CI, 1.08–2.30; low certainty) and higher norepinephrine requirements (MD, 0.17 μg/kg/min; 95% CI, 0.02–0.32; low certainty) (Figure). Esmolol significantly reduced 28-day mortality (RR, 0.69; 95% CI, 0.56–0.85; moderate certainty) and 24-h heart rate (MD, –16.92 bpm; 95% CI, –23.49 to –10.36; moderate certainty) compared to SOC. In contrast, landiolol increased norepinephrine use compared to SOC (MD, 0.09 μg/kg/min; 95% CI, 0.01–0.18; moderate certainty).

Conclusions: Moderate to very low certainty evidence suggests that, compared with SOC and landiolol, esmolol may reduce sepsis mortality while maintaining hemodynamic stability without increasing norepinephrine requirements. However, landiolol’s effect remains uncertain. Esmolol indicates potential clinical advantages over landiolol, but direct comparative trials are needed to confirm these results.

P122 Improvement in cardiovascular performance with landiolol infusion in cardiogenic shock patients with tachyarrhythmias

G Paissan, M Tosi, S Finetto, M Pasetto, L Peluso, M Ceola Graziadei, A Russo, L Gottin

Hospital and University Trust of Verona, Cardiothoracic and Vascular Intensive Care Unit, Verona, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P122

Introduction: Atrial tachyarrhythmias often coexist with worsening heart failure (HF) and can precipitate cardiogenic shock (CS). Adressing rate, rhythm and synchrony is meant to improve the hemodynamic profile. Landiolol, a short-acting β1-superselective blocker, is guideline-recommended for rhythm and rate control of atrial fibrillation (AF) and has demonstrated effectiveness in HF or CS patients. Cardiac power output (CPO) represents the cardiovascular system energy output per unit time.

Methods: Prospective observational single-center cohort study, started 03/25. Adult patients with acute HF/CS and tachyarrhythmias. Hemodynamic profile evaluation before (T0), at 1 h (T1) and 6 h (T2) after landiolol infusion start; pulse-contour monitoring and echocardiography.

Results: In this ongoing study we enrolled 7 patients treated with landiolol for CS due to or aggravated by AF; median age was 68 [57–72] years. Four patients were cardiac surgery cases, 3 were CS evolved from secondary cardiomyopathies. Two patients died in the ICU and another 1 in hospital (all were withdrawal of life support therapies); 4 were discharged home. Five cases were new onset AF. In 3 patients (43%) landiolol was effective in restoring sinus rhythm; 2 of 5 that remained in AF already had permanent AF. CPO improved from a median 0.6 [0.3–0.7] W before landiolol infusion to 0.7 [0.5–0.8] W at 1 h and 0.7 [0.6–1.0] W at 6 h, in a statistically significant manner (p = 0.006, Friedman test); effect size (Kendall’s W) of 0.7 (Figure). Considering RAP in the CPO computation, median increases were from a starting median 0.5 [0.2–0.5] W to 0.5 [0.4–0.7] W at 1 h and to 0.6 [0.5–0.9] W at 6 h (p = 0.006); effect size (Kendall’s W) of 0.7. CI improved from median 1.9 [1.4–2.2] L/min/m2 to 2.0 [1.9–2.9] L/min/m2 at 1 h and to 2.2 [1.9–3.1] L/min/m2 at 6 h.

Conclusions: These preliminary data show that landiolol was effective in ameliorating cardiac mechanic and energetic performance in CS with AF.

P123 AI-driven echocardiographic model for early identification of stress cardiomyopathy

J Ryu1, C Harris1, C Zhang1, R Stevens2

1Johns Hopkins University, Whiting School of Engineering, Baltimore, USA, 2Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine, Department of Anesthesiology and Critical Care Medicine, Baltimore, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P123

Introduction: This study aimed to develop a view-agnostic, multimodal deep learning model using echocardiography and clinical features to differentiate stress cardiomyopathy (SCM) from acute myocardial infarction (AMI), addressing the limitations of prior work restricted to apical views and lacking EHR integration. SCM closely resembles AMI in presentation, leading to frequent misdiagnoses and inaccurate treatment decisions [1,2].

Methods: Within the MIMIC-IV echocardiography dataset, 33 SCM and 561 AMI patients were identified. A ResNet-18 model with attention generated 512-dimensional embeddings from all available views. Three XGBoost classifiers were trained: an Echo model using video embeddings, an EHR model using 45 clinical variables, and a Fusion model combining both. Five-fold cross-validation was performed, and metrics are reported as mean ± standard deviation.

Results: The Echo model achieved an AUROC of 0.73 ± 0.03 and AUPRC of 0.27 ± 0.07, outperforming the baseline AUPRC of 0.06. The Fusion model showed the highest performance (AUROC 0.78 ± 0.03, AUPRC 0.25 ± 0.04), while the EHR model also provided discriminative value (AUROC 0.63 ± 0.04, AUPRC 0.14 ± 0.06).

Conclusions: Using all echocardiographic views, not only apical views, enabled robust SCM identification even from short, non-standardized clips. This is important as real-world echocardiograms vary widely in view type, quality, and length. The strong performance of the Echo model highlights that echocardiography alone carries a distinct diagnostic signature for early SCM detection.

P124 Invasive hemodynamic monitoring guided resuscitation improves survival in shock: a systematic review and meta-analysis.

L Nagy1, C Turan1, D Laczkó2, PR Tóth1, L Légár3, B Danisan3, Z Sipos3, P Hegyi3, Z Molnár1, L Zubek1

1Semmelweis University, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Budapest, Hungary, 2Semmelweis University, Department of Interventional Radiology, Heart and Vascular Centre, Budapest, Hungary, 3Semmelweis University, Centre for Translational Medicine, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P124

Introduction: Mortality in shock may reach 60%, hence, immediate, adequate resuscitation has a crucial role in improving outcomes. Detailed hemodynamic monitoring is desirable, but evidence concerning its outcome benefits is limited. Therefore, this study aimed to compare advanced hemodynamic monitoring (AHDM) guided to conventional treatment on outcomes in shock.

Methods: A systematic search was performed on PubMed, EMBASE, and Cochrane Library until November 9, 2024. RCTs, non-randomised and observational studies about adult shock patients were eligible. Main outcomes were in-hospital and 30-day mortality, with length of stay, need and duration of organ support, and amount of fluid administered as secondary outcomes. Meta-analyses were performed using a random-effects model, with assessment of heterogeneity and risk of bias.

Results: 34 studies, with seven RCTs and a total of 636,441 shock patients comparing AHDM versus conventional monitoring, were analysed. The use of any type of AHDM in any type of shock was associated with a significantly lower in-hospital mortality (OR: 0.66; 95% CI: [0.48;0.91]; p = 0.014), especially in cardiogenic shock patients, managed by pulmonary artery catheter-guided therapy (OR: 0.68; CI: [0.60;0.78]; p < 0.001) (Figure). This contrasts with the significantly higher odds of need for organ support treatment, including inotropes (OR: 2.32; CI: [1.29;4.19]; p = 0.002), vasopressors (OR: 1.46; CI: [1.05;2.04]; p = 0.030), mechanical circulatory support (OR: 2.85; CI: [1.62;5.02]; p = 0.002), renal replacement therapy or mechanical ventilation, while the the duration of mechanical ventilation was shorter.

Conclusions: AHDM use is associated with a significant reduction in mortality among shock patients, with the greatest benefit observed in cardiogenic shock. The higher need for organ support reflects a more precise identification of the underlying cause and a more targeted therapeutic approach, enabled by AHDM, which improves outcomes. These results support its early application in severe or refractory cases.
