P125 Prognostic performance of lactate, ΔPv–aCO₂ and central ScvO₂ for 28-day mortality in septic and cardiogenic shock: a retrospective comparative analysis

M Benlabed1, S Benlabed2, R Gaudy3, V Sobkova4, R Mehyaoui5, J Malaska6

1Lille University, Anesthesiology and Intensive Care, Lille, France, 2Free University of Brussels, Brussels, Belgium, 3Lille University, Lille, France, 4University of Prague, Prague, Czech Republic, 5Algiers University, Algiers, Algeria, 6Masaryk University, Brno, Czech Republic

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P125

Introduction: Lactate, ΔPv–aCO₂, and central ScvO₂ are routinely used to assess tissue perfusion in circulatory shock, yet their comparative prognostic performance remains debated. We aimed to evaluate and compare the ability of these three markers, measured at ICU admission (T0) and 6 h later (T6h), to predict 28-day mortality in septic and cardiogenic shock.

Methods: This retrospective study included 300 adult ICU patients, 150 with septic shock and 150 with cardiogenic shock (2018–2024). Lactate, ΔPv–aCO₂, and ScvO₂ were systematically collected at T0 and T6h. The primary endpoint was 28-day mortality. ROC curves were generated using DeLong’s method. Pronostic performance for predicting 28 day mortality used AUC values for comparison of biomarkers within each shock phenotype.

Results: Overall 28-day mortality was 34% in septic shock (51/150) and 42% in cardiogenic shock (63/150), consistent with European cohorts. In septic shock, lactate [1] demonstrated the best prognostic accuracy (AUC 0.90), outperforming ΔPv–aCO₂ (AUC 0.83) and ScvO₂ (AUC 0.78) (Figure). In cardiogenic shock, lactate again showed superior prognostic performance (AUC 0.94) vs ΔPv–aCO₂ (AUC 0.89) and ScvO₂ (AUC 0.82). Across both shock phenotypes, T6h values moderately improved AUCs compared with T0, especially for ΔPv–aCO₂, supporting a dynamic assessment strategy.

Conclusions: In this large retrospective cohort including 300 patients, lactate consistently outperformed ΔPv–aCO₂ and ScvO₂ for predicting 28-day mortality in both septic and cardiogenic shock. ΔPv–aCO₂ provided intermediate prognostic value, whereas ScvO₂ showed limited discrimination. Measuring these biomarkers at T0 and at 6 h enhances prognostic accuracy, reinforcing their role in early hemodynamic assessment. These findings support lactate [1,2] as the most robust single perfusion marker across shock phenotypes and provide a strong rationale for prospective validation.

P127 Invasive cannula placement: impact on training opportunities of an advanced critical care practitioner (ACCP) and subsequent independent service and teaching provision

M Vaz Guedes1, R Heij1, E Young2, M Osborne1, J Oliveira1, H Santos1, P Young1

1QEH, Critical Care, Kings Lynn, UK, 2University of Cambridge, Department of Mathematical Statistics, Cambridge, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P127

Introduction: Advanced critical care practitioners (ACCPs) require supervised experience in central venous catheterisation and arterial line placement. During this initial period, ACCPs may appear to reduce procedural opportunities for resident doctors. Once competent, however, they provide independent service and deliver procedural teaching, potentially generating a substantial net gain. The objective of this study is to quantify the invasive line training required for a new ACCP, describe the timeline to independent practice, and estimate the long-term service and teaching benefit relative to the initial supervised training period.

Methods: Prospectively collected data were analysed for a newly appointed ACCP from September 2022 to September 2025 in a mixed medical-surgical ICU. Cumulative counts were recorded for supervised procedures during training, independent procedures after competency sign-off, and episodes where the ACCP taught resident doctors. A linear 30-year projection estimated long-term impact.

Results: The ACCP required 38 supervised invasive procedures for competency. Over the subsequent three years, the ACCP performed 92 independent procedures and provided 58 supervised teaching episodes (Figure). This represents a + 240% service benefit and + 150% teaching benefit relative to the training "cost", achieving an early approximately + 395% net gain. A 30-year projection indicates that training procedures would constitute less than 1% of lifetime procedural activity, with approximately 61% representing independent service and 38% supervised teaching.

Conclusions: Training an ACCP in invasive cannulation requires a modest supervised procedural investment, yet independent practice begins early and rapidly generates a substantial surplus of both service provision and resident teaching opportunities. These findings support ACCP development as an efficient strategy for sustaining invasive procedural capacity and enhancing ICU training.

P129 SCORED: ScvO₂-centered optimisation of red blood cell transfusions – protocol of a randomised controlled trial

N Kiss, C Turan, DM Karim, M Papp, L Zubek, Z Molnar

Semmelweis University, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P129

Introduction: In our multicentre randomised controlled trial, we aim to investigate the efficacy and safety of central venous saturation (ScvO2)-guided transfusion triggers versus transfusion based on clinicians’ decisions in the critically ill. International guidelines recommend restrictive transfusion policies based on haemoglobin (Hb) levels; however, evidence regarding physiological transfusion triggers is lacking [1,2].

Methods: In Hungarian medical and surgical ICUs, adults who are haemodynamically and respiratorily stable with a Hb level between 7 g/dl and 9 g/dl will be randomised in a 1:1 ratio. In the treatment arm, 1 unit of red blood cells (RBC) will be transfused if the ScvO2 is below 70%. Additional units will be given until the central venous saturation reaches 70%. In the control arm, the decision to transfuse will be made based on clinicians’ judgement, without knowledge of ScvO2. After enrolment, patients will continue to receive RBC transfusions in their respective arms. A two-sided overall significance level of 5% and 80% power were targeted to detect a mean difference of 1 RBC unit between the two treatment groups. The planned sample size resulted in 619 patients in total. An unblinded interim analysis is planned after 30% of patients (~ 186) have been enrolled.

Results: Our primary outcome will be the number of units of red blood cells transfused. The secondary outcome will be the change in central venous saturation. As exploratory outcomes, we will report on mechanical ventilation duration and vasopressor requirements, changes in the carbon dioxide gap, incidence of end-organ failure, infectious complications, renal replacement therapy, organ support-free days, and ICU and hospital lengths of stay.

Conclusions: We aim to demonstrate that incorporating a physiological transfusion trigger is an effective and safe approach to reduce the overall transfusion rate in the critically ill.

P131 Impact of red blood cell transfusion on oxygen metabolism in patients receiving venovenous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation

L Liu, J Wu, S Chen, H Xue, D Hu, Y Zhu, Y Tang, A Liu, H Qiu, S Liu

Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Trauma Center, Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Nanjing, Jiangsu, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P131

Introduction: This study evaluated the effects of red blood cell (RBC) transfusion strategies on oxygen metabolism and clinical outcomes in venovenous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (VV ECMO) patients, addressing controversies on hemoglobin thresholds, oxygen delivery, and tissue utilization.

Methods: A retrospective study included adults on VV ECMO at Zhongda Hospital (Jan 2017–Mar 2025). Demographic, clinical, ECMO-specific, and transfusion parameters were analyzed. Primary endpoints were pre/post-transfusion oxygen metabolism changes; secondary endpoints included hemoglobin trends, transfusion rates, and clinical outcomes. Spearman’s correlation assessed hemoglobin–oxygen metabolism relationships. Mortality was analyzed via Kaplan–Meier methods and covariate-adjusted models; locally weighted scatterplot smoothing regression explored nonlinear hemoglobin–mortality associations.

Results: Among 115 patients, 76.5% received ≥ 1 RBC transfusion within 2 weeks of ECMO initiation. Transfused patients had higher sequential organ failure assessment scores (p = 0.037) but similar demographics/diagnoses. Median transfusion volume was 1200 mL, with no association to blood type or survival ( 85.2% vs. 88.6%, p = 0.886). Transfusion increased systemic oxygen delivery (pre 588.13 vs. post 689.53 mL/min/m2, p < 0.001) without improving oxygen consumption, oxygen extraction ratio, central venous oxygen saturation or lactate levels. Post-transfusion ECMO blood flow and oxygen concentration reductions were feasible without compromising stability. Survival did not differ between transfused and non-transfused groups (log-rank p = 0.37), and pre-transfusion hemoglobin concentration below 7 g/dL was associated with higher mortality risk.

Conclusions: RBC transfusion improves systemic oxygen delivery but not tissue oxygen utilization or survival in VV ECMO patients with hemoglobin > 7 g/dL. Restrictive transfusion strategies appear safe in non-severe anemia, though critical thresholds for tissue hypoxia warrant further investigation.

P132 An untargeted exploratory metabolomic analysis in severe trauma patients: the potential role of transfusion

Y Louraoui1, A Bagul1, N Kseibati1, C Chollet2, M Werner1, N Libert1, J Duranteau1, F Castelli2, A Harrois1, A Huertas3

1Paris-Saclay University DYNAMIC Lab, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France, 2Paris-Saclay University MetaboHUB Paris, Gif-sur-Yvette, France, 3Paris-Saclay University DYNAMIC lab, Medecine, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P132

Introduction: Post-trauma multiorgan failure (MOF) mortality is still very high and the underlying mechanisms are not fully understood. Multiple components from injured tissues, altered systemic responses and resuscitation therapies such as transfusion may contribute to MOF onset and/or progression. Identifying predictive phenotypes may help elucidating the pathophysiological mechanisms of post-trauma MOF after transfusion. This study aims to characterize metabolic signatures linked to transfusion using untargeted metabolomics in patients with severe trauma.

Methods: Untargeted exploratory metabolomic analysis was performed in severe trauma patients (n = 19). Blood samples were collected at admission (H0) and after 24 h (H24) in transfused (n = 10) and in non-transfused patients (n = 9), then processed to isolate plasma for storage. Metabolite profiling employed liquid chromatography coupled with high-resolution mass spectrometry (LC-HRMS) utilizing dual chromatography columns to enhance metabolite detection and separation. Univariate and multivariate statistical approaches were applied to validate results and identify distinct metabolic signatures, ensuring analytical robustness. Wilcoxon test was used with a statistical significance for p < 0.05.

Results: Significant differences in metabolomic profiles were found between H0 and H24 timepoints across the overall cohort. Marked alterations were observed in critical pathways, including mitochondrial function, inflammation and oxidative stress at H24 compared to H0. Interestingly, we found different metabolomic signatures in the two subgroups at H24 (Figure).

Conclusions: The identification of a metabolomic signature associated with transfusion after severe trauma holds significant clinical relevance. Further studies are needed to better understand the pathophysiological role of transfusion in MOF onset and/or progression and are critical for advancing therapeutic strategies to improve clinical outcomes.

P133 Patient blood management based coagulation management for massive postpartum hemorrhage: a randomized controlled pilot trial

AA Perez-Calatayud1, K Görlinger2, LF Giraldo-Cadavid3, J Perez-Uriza4, K Aguilar-Vidales3, D Torres-Valenzuela3, ED Anica-Malagon1

1Hospital General de México Dr Eduardo Liceaga, Critical Care, Mexico City, Mexico, 2University Hospital Essen, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Essen, Germany, 3Hospital del Niño y la Mujer Dr. Felipe Nuñez Lara, Department of Anesthesiology, Queretaro, Mexico, 4Faculty of Chemical Sciences Universidad Autónoma de San Luis Potosí, Pharmacology Laboratory, San Luis Potosi, Mexico

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P133

Introduction: Postpartum hemorrhage (PPH) is the leading cause of maternal morbidity and mortality worldwide, particularly in middle- and low-income countries [1–3].

Methods: In this single-center randomized controlled pilot trial, two coagulation management algorithms for massive PPH were compared: one guided by thromboelastometry using fibrinogen concentrate and prothrombin complex concentrate (ROTEM-FC) versus one guided by standard coagulation tests with blood products (SCT-BP). The primary outcome was the number of allogeneic blood products transfused within 24 h. Secondary outcomes included incidence of ≥ 1, 5, and 10 U RBC transfusion, estimated blood loss, time to bleeding control, ICU and hospital length of stay, coagulopathy, hysterectomy, complications, composite adverse events, and in-hospital mortality.

Results: Of 52 randomized subjects, 17/18 (ROTEM-FC) and 32/34 (SCT-BP) were analyzed. At inclusion, both groups had severe PPH, with mean blood loss of 1,256 mL vs. 1,400 mL, respectively. Compared with SCT-BP, the ROTEM-FC protocol significantly reduced total blood components transfused (median [IQR]: 1 [0–2] vs. 2 [1–4]; p = 0.0040), the need for ≥ 1 unit transfused at 24 h (8/17 [47.1%] vs. 25/32 [78.1%]; p = 0.0316), and time to bleeding control (34.1 [10.0] vs. 76.1 [55.2] min; p = 0.0033) (Table). Composite adverse events were lower in the ROTEM-FC group (2/17 [11.8%] vs. 26/32 [81.3%]; RR 0.1758 [0.0482–0.6412]; p = 0.0085). Relative risk for hysterectomy and ICU admission was 0.5882 [0.2607–11.3274]; p = 0.2013 and 0.7240 [0.4704–1.1142]; p = 0.1420, respectively. ICU and hospital stays were significantly reduced. No mortality was observed.

Conclusions: A thromboelastometry and factor concentrate-based algorithm was more effective than an SCT-BP-guided strategy in controlling massive PPH and reducing transfusion requirements, with a marked decrease in composite adverse events.

P134 Early prophylactic anticoagulation with heparin alleviates short-term mortality in critically ill patients with acute pancreatitis patients: a target trial emulation

X Luo, FR Lin, T Huang, JZ Zhang, AQ Lao, ZF Yang

Sun Yatsen Memorial Hospital, Sun Yatsen University, Intensive Care Unit, Guangzhou, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P134

Introduction: Minimal data exist on anticoagulation use and timing of heparin in critically ill patients with acute pancreatitis, and whether early heparin use improves ancreatitis survival remains controversial. We aim to emulate a target trial evaluating the effect of early prophylactic anticoagulation with heparin with severe acute pancreatitis patients.

Methods: This target trial emulation used the MIMIC-IV v3.1 database and included adult ICU patients with severe acute pancreatitis. The exposure was defined as prophylactic heparin initiation within 24 h of ICU admission, with dose–response relationships examined. Primary and secondary outcomes were 28-day and 60-day all-cause mortality, respectively. The primary analysis was conducted using the parametric g-formula. To assess robustness, sensitivity analyses were conducted, including evaluations of delayed initiation and dose–response relationships. To explore heterogeneity, subgroup analyses were performed.

Results: In the emulated trial, early prophylactic anticoagulation was associated with significantly lower mortality. The estimated 28-day mortality was 4.3% with heparin compared with 21.3% without (relative risk [RR], 0.20; 95% CI, 0.04–0.45) (Figure). The 60-day mortality was 6.9% with heparin compared with 33.7% without (RR, 0.21; 95% CI, 0.05–0.45). This survival benefit was most pronounced when heparin was initiated within the first day and at a dose of 15,000 IU. The association was consistent across most prespecified subgroups.

Conclusions: Early prophylactic anticoagulation within 24 h of ICU admission was associated with significantly reduced 28-day and 60-day mortality in patients with severe acute pancreatitis. These findings suggest potential benefits of early anticoagulation in this high-risk population and warrant further prospective validation.

Figure (abstract P134) Estimated 28-day (A) and 60-day (B) potential mortality risk under a treatment strategy that allows heparin sulfate use on the first day versus an intervention that never allows heparin use

Estimated 28-day (A) and 60-day (B) potential mortality risk under a treatment strategy that allows heparin sulfate use on the first day versus an intervention that never allows heparin use

P135 Determinants and outcomes of delayed etoposide administration in adult secondary hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis

J Schmidt1, C Gautier1, N Bigé2, C Bloch-Queyrat3, F Pène4, S Gaudry1

1Hôpital Avicenne, Intensive Care Unit, Bobigny, France, 2Institut Gustave Roussy, Intensive Care Unit, Villejuif, France, 3Hôpital Avicenne, Clinical Research Unit, Bobigny, France, 4Hôpital Cochin, Intensive Care Unit, Paris, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P135

Introduction: Secondary hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis (HLH) in adults is a rare but severe hyperinflammatory syndrome often leading to multiorgan failure and ICU admission. Etoposide is recommended in severe forms, yet its real-life use and impact in critically ill adults remain poorly characterized. This study aimed to describe etoposide administration patterns and assess the effect of timing on outcomes.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective bicentric cohort study in two French ICUs (2014–2024). Adults with a first episode of severe secondary HLH, defined by ≥ 5 Henter criteria or H-score > 168 plus ≥ 1 organ failure, were included. Clinical, biological, etiological, and therapeutic variables were collected, with particular focus on etoposide use, reasons for delayed or absent administration, and 30-day survival.

Results: Thirty-three patients were included; 17 (51.5%) received etoposide (Figure). Among patients receiving etoposide, median time from diagnosis to administration was 3 days [1–8]. Main reasons for withholding treatment were moribund presentation (n = 5), sepsis (n = 5), renal failure (n = 2), prioritization of etiological therapy (n = 2), or use of another immunomodulator (n = 1). Among treated patients, 8 received early etoposide (≤ 24 h) and 9 late etoposide (> 24 h). Patients receiving etoposide exhibited more organ failures at admission. No survival difference was observed between etoposide and non-etoposide groups (p = 0.76), nor between early and late administration (p = 0.83). Overall ICU mortality was 54.5%.

Conclusions: In this cohort of critically ill adults with secondary HLH, etoposide was administered in only half of cases and often with delay. Diagnostic uncertainty, sepsis, renal dysfunction, and perceived futility were major barriers. No survival benefit was associated with etoposide use or earlier treatment. These findings highlight the challenges of applying current recommendations in ICU practice and the need for prospective studies to define optimal therapeutic strategies in adult HLH.

P136 Elastic-net regression using tromboelastographic and perioperative parameters to predict early haemoglobin and fibrinogen changes after bilateral lung transplantation (BLTx)

E Marcano Millán1, M Bueno Bueno1, S Muñoz González1, C Obando Martínez1, G Jiménez Tejera1, T Recio1, G Siesta López2, C Rivas Duarte3, G García Carrera4, V Sagredo Meneses1

1Hospital Universitario de Salamanca, Critical Care Unit, Salamanca, Spain, 2Hospital Universitario de Salamanca, Respiratory/Pneumology Department, Salamanca, Spain, 3Hospital Universitario de Salamanca, Thoracic Surgery Department, Salamanca, Spain, 4Hospital Universitario de Salamanca, Anaesthetics Department, Salamanca, Spain

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P136

Introduction: Major blood loss and coagulopathy remain common after BLTx. Point-of-care viscoelastic testing (POCVT) dynamically assesses clot formation and lysis, enabling early recognition of bleeding risk. We evaluated whether baseline TEG parameters at ICU admission, combined with operative time and intraoperative ECMO duration, predict early postoperative declines in haemoglobin, platelets, fibrinogen, and chest drainage volume.

Methods: Eighteen consecutive bilateral lung transplants performed in 2025 were analysed. Sixteen baseline TEG6s variables were collected at ICU admission, along with operative time and ECMO duration. Outcomes included changes in haemoglobin, platelets, and fibrinogen from 0 to 24 h postoperatively, and chest drainage at 24 h. Predictors were standardised, and elastic-net regression with fivefold cross-validation determined model hyperparameters. Median coefficients and 95% confidence intervals were obtained from 1,000 bootstrap resamples.

Results: Cross-validation selected mild penalisation for the haemoglobin model, identifying ECMO duration, operative time, CK_K, and heparinase alpha angle as predictors. For fibrinogen change, stronger lasso-like penalisation retained ECMO duration, CK_ANG, and CKH_MA as main predictors. Models for platelet change and drainage volume were heavily penalised, with near-zero coefficients. Overall, longer ECMO and operative times, along with TEG-derived clot kinetics and strength measures, were linked to greater haemoglobin decreases and fibrinogen variation, while platelet and drainage predictions were weak (Figure).

Conclusions: Combining baseline TEG parameters with perioperative durations using elastic-net regression could help to identify patients at risk of early hemoglobin and fibrinogen decline after BLTx. ECMO and operative times, along with CK-derived indices, were consistent predictors. These findings suggest that integrating POCVT with surgical variables may guide personalized transfusion strategies, pending confirmation in larger cohorts.

Figure (abstract P136) Elastic-net median coefficients for hemoglobin (panel A) and fibrinogen (panel B) models after lung transplantation. Each bar represents the relative contribution of selected clinical and coagulation parameters, with color gradients indicating the magnitude of coefficients. The hemoglobin model (blue scale) highlights ECMO duration and surgery time as key predictors, while the fibrinogen model (orange scale) emphasizes ECMO duration and CK-derived parameters

P137 Early haemostatic phenotypes after bilateral lung transplantation identified by thromboelastography and unsupervised learning

E Marcano Millán1, M Bueno Bueno1, N Núñez Blanco1, N Albalá Martínez1, V Pérez González2, M Jiménez López3, I Trejo González4, S Martín Oliva1, M Ochoa Calero1, V Sagredo Meneses1

1Hospital Universitario de Salamanca, Critical Care Unit, Salamanca, Spain, 2Hospital Universitario de Salamanca, Respiratory/Pneumology Department, Salamanca, Spain, 3Hospital Universitario de Salamanca, Thoracic Surgery Department, Salamanca, Spain, 4Hospital Universitario de Salamanca, Anaesthetics Department, Salamanca, Spain

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P137

Introduction: Postoperative coagulopathy is common after bilateral lung transplantation (BLTx) and contributes to early bleeding and transfusion requirements. Thromboelastography (TEG) provides a dynamic assessment of haemostasis, but its integration with conventional markers using artificial intelligence (AI) remains unexplored. This study aimed to identify early haemostatic phenotypes through principal component analysis (PCA) and unsupervised clustering.

Methods: Eighteen consecutive BLTx patients admitted to the intensive care unit between March and October 2025 were included. TEG parameters (CK/CKH/RT: R,K, Angle, MA), haemoglobin, platelets and fibrinogen were recorded at 0, 6 and 24 h, together with chest-drain output. Variables were standardized (Z-scores) and analysed by PCA to reduce dimensionality. The first two components (explaining 56% of total variance) were entered into a k-means model (k = 2–3). Cluster separation was assessed using the Silhouette index.

Results: The model with k = 2 showed the best performance (Silhouette = 0.38), identifying two distinct phenotypes. Cluster 1 (n = 9) showed lower maximum amplitude (MA; CK_MA 53.6 mm, CKH_MA 54.5 mm) and fibrinogen levels (182 mg/dL at 0 h; 184 mg/dL at 6 h), with a trend toward higher early bleeding (chest drainage 428 mL at 6 h) (Figure). Cluster 0 (n = 9) presented higher MA (CK_MA 60.5 mm, CKH_MA 56.7 mm) and fibrinogen (267 mg/dL and 330 mg/dL at 0 and 6 h) with lower early drainage (365 mL). Total 24-h drainage was similar (~ 1000 mL).

Conclusions: Combining TEG with conventional coagulation tests identified two early haemostatic phenotypes after BLTx. The group with lower MA and fibrinogen showed a hypocoagulable profile associated with greater early bleeding. Despite the small sample, this exploratory approach demonstrates the feasibility of PCA-based clustering to reveal clinically relevant phenotypes and may support individualized transfusion strategies in complex thoracic surgery.

Figure (abstract P137) Summary heatmap of key haemostatic parameters by cluster. The figure displays mean values of selected thromboelastography (TEG) and conventional haemostatic variables for each cluster. Cluster 0 corresponds to a normocoagulable phenotype and Cluster 1 to a hypocoagulable phenotype

Summary heatmap of key haemostatic parameters by cluster. The figure displays mean values of selected thromboelastography (TEG) and conventional haemostatic variables for each cluster. Cluster 0 corresponds to a normocoagulable phenotype and Cluster 1 to a hypocoagulable phenotype

P138 Utility of anti-FXa guided enoxaparin dose adjustments in critically ill COVID-19 patients

Z Athanasa1, A Dimoula1, S Manioudaki1, A Sakagianni2

1Sismanogleio General Hospital, Athens, Greece, 2Sismanogleio General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Athens, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P138

Introduction: Critically ill COVID-19 patients are at high risk of thrombotic complications, often requiring intermediate-dose enoxaparin. Anti-FXa activity is measured to guide dosing, but its effectiveness in preventing bleeding or thrombotic events is unclear [1]. This study evaluated the impact of anti-FXa guided dose adjustments in invasively ventilated ICU patients.

Methods: We prospectively studied 100 ICU patients with PCR-confirmed SARS-CoV-2. All received weight-based intermediate-dose enoxaparin, adjusted according to anti-FXa activity after the fourth dose (target 0.3–0.7 IU/mL). Bleeding (ISTH criteria), transfusions, thromboembolic events, and ICU outcomes including mortality, ventilation duration, and ICU stay were recorded. Multivariable regression identified independent predictors of bleeding and ICU mortality.

Results: Dose modifications were required in 32%. Bleeding occurred in 47% (14% major), VTE in 7%. Anti-FXa levels did not differ between bleeding and non-bleeding patients. Multivariable analysis identified CrCl < 60 ml/min as the only independent predictor of bleeding, while bleeding, APACHE II ≥ 15, L/N ratio ≥ 0.07, and MV ≥ 8 days were independently associated with ICU mortality (Table). Patients with bleeding had higher mortality (60% vs 17%), longer ventilation, and ICU stay.

Conclusions: Anti-FXa guided dose adjustments alone may be insufficient to prevent bleeding in critically ill COVID-19 patients receiving intermediate-dose enoxaparin. While systematic monitoring allows dose optimization, bleeding remains frequent, especially in patients with impaired renal function. Clinicians should integrate anti-FXa monitoring with individualized risk assessment to balance thrombotic prevention and hemorrhagic risk.

P139 Fibrinogen concentrate versus cryoprecipitate for bleeding in cardiac surgery patients stratified by surgical urgency in the phase 3 FIBRES study

J Callum1, K Karkouti2, C Solomon3, S Werner4, M Gareis5, S Knaub3

1Queens University and Kingston Health Sciences Centre, Kingston, Canada, 2University of Toronto and University Health Network, Toronto, Canada, 3Octapharma AG, Lachen, Switzerland, 4Octapharma USA, Paramus, New Jersey, USA, 5Octapharma Pharmazeutika Produktionsges.m.b.H, Vienna, Austria

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P139

Introduction: This post-hoc analysis explored efficacy of fibrinogen concentrate (FC) vs cryoprecipitate (cryo) for treatment of coagulopathic bleeding related to acquired fibrinogen deficiency in cardiac surgery patients from the Phase 3 FIBRES study, stratified by surgical urgency. FIBRES previously demonstrated non-inferiority of FC vs cryo in the population overall [1].

Methods: FIBRES (NCT03037424) enrolled adult cardiac surgery patients on cardiopulmonary bypass (CPB) requiring fibrinogen replacement for clinically significant bleeding. Patients were randomized (1:1) to receive 4 g FC or 10 U cryo for each order within 24 h post-CPB. Patients were stratified by elective/non-elective surgery status. The primary endpoint was allogeneic blood products (ABP) administered up to 24 h after CPB end. Some patients experienced catastrophic bleeding with high, variable transfusion requirements, likely reflecting severe coagulopathies and requiring surgical re-exploration. To eliminate the disproportional impact of these outliers, an exploratory analysis excluded those receiving ≥ 35 U ABPs within 24 h post-CPB (> 90th percentile).

Results: The primary analysis included 735 patients (N = 372 FC, N = 363 cryo). The majority (N = 466; 63%) underwent elective surgery, with 269 (37%) undergoing non-elective surgery. In patients receiving < 35 U ABPs (N = 658, representing 90% of the primary analysis population), and in all patients undergoing elective surgery, FC was non-inferior and superior to cryo (Figure). Non-inferiority, but not superiority, was also demonstrated in patients undergoing non-elective surgery when excluding those receiving ≥ 35 U ABPs (Figure).

Conclusions: FC was superior to cryo after excluding the 10% most extreme or variable surgical cases and in patients undergoing elective surgery. These results support using FC as the preferred treatment for bleeding in cardiac surgery patients with acquired hypofibrinogenemia.

P140 Four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate is superior to frozen plasma in bleeding adult cardiac surgery patients with coagulopathy—results from a phase 3 study

K Karkouti1, J Callum2, C Solomon3, S Knaub3, S Werner4, K Tanaka5, JH Levy6

1University of Toronto and University Health work, Anesthesia and Pain Management, Toronto, Canada, 2Queen´s University and Kingston Health Sciences Centre, Kingston, Ontario, Canada, 3Octapharma AG, Lachen, Switzerland, 4Octapharma USA, Paramus, New Jersey, USA, 5University of Oklahoma Health Sciences Center, Oklahoma City, Oklahoma, USA, 6Duke University School of Medicine, Durham, North Carolina, USA.

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P140

Introduction: This trial compared the efficacy and safety of four-factor prothrombin complex (PCC; Octaplex/BALFAXAR, Octapharma) with frozen plasma (FP) in cardiac surgery. Cardiac surgery with cardiopulmonary bypass (CPB) is often complicated by coagulopathic bleeding, leading to morbidity and mortality. Although guidelines recommend using either PCC or FP for bleeding management, the mainstay therapy in North America is FP.

Methods: LEX-211 (FARES-II; NCT05523297) included patients aged ≥ 18 years undergoing cardiac surgery with CPB. After protamine administration, patients with coagulopathic bleeding and INR ≥ 1.5 were randomized 1:1 to receive PCC (1500 IU if ≤ 60 kg; 2000 IU if > 60 kg) or FP (3 U if ≤ 60 kg; 4 U if > 60 kg). Clinical teams were blinded to group allocation until treatment start. The primary endpoint was hemostatic response (effective if no additional hemostatic interventions were administered from 60 min to 24 h after treatment start). Safety endpoints included 30-day treatment-emergent thromboembolic events (TEEs) and death.

Results: Of 538 enrolled patients at 12 sites in Canada and the US, 420 were randomized, treated, consented, and included in the analysis (PCC = 213; FP = 207). Baseline characteristics were comparable between groups. Effective hemostasis was achieved in 77.9% (n = 166) of PCC vs 60.4% (n = 125) of FP patients (diff. 17.6%; 95% CI 8.7, 26.4; p < 0.001 for superiority). Overall, the mean (95% CI) number of allogeneic blood product units transfused within 24 h post-CPB, excluding intervention FP, was 6.6 (5.7, 7.7) in PCC and 9.3 (8.0, 10.8) in FP patients (ratio 0.71; 95% CI 0.57, 0.88; p = 0.002). Treatment-emergent TEEs and mortality occurred in 8.5% (n = 18) and 3.3% (n = 7) of PCC patients and 7.2% (n = 15) and 3.9% (n = 8) of FP patients, respectively.

Conclusions: In cardiac surgery patients, PCC was more efficacious than FP in controlling coagulopathic bleeding without increasing thromboembolic risk. These results support the use of PCC over FP for bleeding management in cardiac surgery.

P141 Comparison of coagulation profiles between treatment with fibrinogen concentrate and cryoprecipitate in patients undergoing major abdominal surgery

A Roy1, N Preston1, J Rowley1, P Porter1, S Stanford1, S Aigner2, H Boehm2, S Abraha2, J Schuettrumpf3, N Rahe-Meyer4

1Hampshire Hospitals NHS Foundation Trust, Basingstoke, UK, 2Biotest AG, Dreieich, Germany, 3Grifols, Barcelona, Spain, 4Clinic for Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Franziskus Hospital, Bielefeld, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P141

Introduction: The AdFIrst trial (NCT03444324) showed that the new human fibrinogen concentrate (FC, BT524, Biotest) was non-inferior to cryoprecipitate (Cryo) for managing severe bleeding in patients undergoing complex surgeries with a lower incidence of thromboembolic events. This subgroup analysis evaluated a preemptive hemostatic strategy using FC vs Cryo to manage severe bleeding in patients undergoing cytoreductive surgery for pseudomyxoma peritonei, referred to abdominal surgery, comparing the changes in coagulation parameters between groups.

Methods: In this prospective, phase 3, randomized, partially blinded, active-controlled trial, adult patients undergoing abdominal surgery were randomized 1:1 to receive FC (4 g) or Cryo (2 pools, 10 units, equivalent to 4g), with additional doses as needed. Change in coagulation markers (thrombin-antithrombin III complex [TAT], prothrombin fragments 1 + 2 [F1 + 2]), were measured intraoperatively (prior to first dose, after 15 min, 90 min post dose, end of surgery) and postoperatively on days 2 through 8. Factor VIII [FVIII] and von Willebrand factor [VWF] were evaluated intraoperatively, and at the end of surgery.

Results: During surgery, all patients showed a hypercoagulable state, indicated by elevated levels of TAT and F1 + 2. Patients treated with Cryo showed higher levels of both markers, at 90 min post-administration and at the end of surgery, indicating enhanced systemic thrombin activation. In contrast, patients receiving FC exhibited comparatively lower TAT and F1 + 2 levels. FVIII and VWF levels remained within physiologically regulated ranges in the FC group, whereas higher levels were observed in the Cryo group at 90 min post dose (Figure). At the end of surgery, no relevant differences were observed between groups.

Conclusions: During major abdominal surgery, the prothrombotic tendency appeared to be more pronounced in patients treated with Cryo, while patients receiving FC demonstrated a more controlled coagulation response.

P142 Evaluation of the performance of qLabs®Fib at admission in severe trauma patients at risk of bleeding

M Holleville1, A Gallet1, PA Baron1, C Bernard1, C Raulet-Bussian2, E De Raucourt2, E Weiss1

1Hôpital Beaujon APHP Nord, Department of Anaesthesiology and Critical Care, Clichy, France, 2Hôpital Beaujon APHP Nord, Service Hématologie Biologique, Clichy, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P142

Introduction: Post-traumatic coagulopathy remain leading causes of potentially preventable death. Hypofibrinogenemia is associated with greater blood loss and increased transfusion requirements [1]. The qLabs®Fib is a recent point-of-care tool developed to measure fibrinogen concentration (Fg) using a single drop of blood. Validated during postpartum haemorrage [2], no data are available in severe trauma. Our study aims to evaluate the performance of qLabs®Fib at admission of severe trauma patients at risk of bleeding (Score REDFLAG ≥ 2) [3].

Methods: For 20 months, in a level1 French traumacenter, Fg was systematically measured at patients admission with REDFLAG ≥ 2 according to pre-hospital informations, using both the qLabs®Fib and the standard Clauss method. We compared Fg results between both methods. Agreement between methods and diagnostic performance of qLabs®Fib for detecting hypofibrinogenemia were evaluated.

Results: On 73 paired tests, agreement between qLabs®Fib and Clauss fibrinogen assays (Figure) is good: 0.94 (0.84–1.04). Using Clauss < 1.5 g/L as the reference standard, qLabs®Fib < 1.5 g/L achieved a high negative predictive value of 95%, indicating that patients classified as non-hypofibrinogenemic by qLabs®Fib were almost never missed. Sensitivity reached 77%, meaning that most clinically relevant cases of low fibrinogen were correctly detected. Specificity was also strong at 89%, supporting the overall reliability of the assay in ruling out unnecessary interventions. Taken together, these findings suggest that qLabs®Fib provides a reassuring level of safety for frontline screening, with a very low risk of failing to identify patients who require fibrinogen replacement.

Conclusions: In severe trauma patients with massive bleeding risk, the use of qLabs®Fib showed good agreement of Fg results with the Clauss method.

P143 The effect of recombinant human thrombopoietin on platelet levels in critically ill patients with pneumonia-associated thrombocytopenia: a multicenter, randomized controlled trial

Z Zeng1, HU Hu1, HL Hou2, ZS Zhang3, LZ Lin4, SY Sun5, LEI Huang6, ZW Zheng7

1Nanfang Hospital, Southern Medical University, Critical Care Medicine, Guangzhou, China, 2Zhongshan People´s Hospital, Critical Care Medicine, Zhongshan, China, 3Jiangmen Central Hospital, Critical Care Medicine, Jiangmen, China, 4Panyu Central Hospital, Guangzhou Medical University, Critical Care Medicine, Guangzhou, China, 5Yuebei People´s Hospital, Critical Care Medicine, Shaoguan, China, 6Peking University Shenzhen Hospital, Critical Care Medicine, Shenzhen, China, 7Zhuhai People´s Hospital, Critical Care Medicine, Zhuhai, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P143

Introduction: Thrombocytopenia is a common and prognostically adverse complication in critically ill patients with pneumonia. Recombinant human thrombopoietin (rhTPO) stimulates endogenous platelet production, but robust prospective evidence supporting its use specifically in ICU patients with pneumonia-associated thrombocytopenia is lacking.

Methods: This multicenter, open-label, randomized controlled trial enrolled 178 patients with pneumonia and thrombocytopenia across 8 ICUs in Guangdong Province, China. Participants were randomized (1:1) to receive subcutaneous rhTPO (15,000 U/day; n = 89) or placebo (n = 89). The primary outcome was time to platelet recovery (defined as platelet count ≥ 100 × 10⁹/L or sustained ≥ 50 × 10⁹/L increase for ≥ 2 days). Secondary outcomes included transfusion requirements, bleeding/thrombotic events, 28-day mortality, and ICU/hospital length of stay (LOS). Analysis followed modified intention-to-treat principles (rhTPO = 77, placebo = 82) (Figure).

Results: Among 159 patients, receiving rhTPO significantly shortened the median time to platelet recovery (6.0d vs 7.0 d, p = 0.032). The rhTPO group exhibited a lower incidence of deep vein thrombosis (DVT) at day 7 (11.6% vs 34.2%; p = 0.003). No significant differences were observed in 28-day mortality (32.5% vs 29.3%; p = 0.79), ICU LOS (10.0 vs 12.0 days; p = 0.48), hospital LOS, or bleeding events. Subgroup analysis revealed a greater reduction in platelet recovery time with rhTPO in patients at high thrombotic risk (5.0 vs 9.0 days to recovery; p = 0.001). Safety profiles were comparable between groups.

Conclusions: rhTPO accelerates platelet recovery and reduces thrombotic complications in critically ill patients with pneumonia-related thrombocytopenia, offering a promising therapeutic alternative with a favorable safety profile.

P144 Use of erythropoiesis stimulating agents for anemia correction in critically ill lung transplant candidates

S Chaudhary1, A Chaudhary2, SB Bhusal2, AC Chaudhary2, P Balasubramanian2, PK Guru2, MB Baz2, K Quiza2, R Bag2

1Mayo Clinic, Critical Care, Jacksonville, USA, 2Mayo Clinic, Jacksonville, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P144

Introduction: Anemia is a common finding among lung transplant candidates. However, whether chronic pre-transplant anemia independently predicts perioperative red blood cell (RBC) transfusion burden is unclear. This study aims to determine the impact of pre-transplant anemia severity and anemia-directed therapy on perioperative RBC transfusion burden in lung transplant recipients.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective cohort study of 952 adult lung transplant recipients. Anemia severity was defined by the lowest hemoglobin within 6 months before transplant and categorized as ≥ 10 g/dL, 8– < 10 g/dL, or < 8 g/dL. RBC burden was defined as the sum of preoperative and intraoperative transfusions, and high burden as ≥ 4 units. Multivariable linear and logistic regression models adjusted for age, sex, body mass index, donor age, and pre-transplant creatinine.

Results: In our study, hemoglobin level was < 10 g/dL in 120 patients (12.6%), including 42 (4.4%) with Hb < 8 g/dL. Mean RBC transfusion was 0.04 ± 0.35 units preoperatively, 0.75 ± 1.81 units intraoperatively, and 2.22 ± 5.77 units postoperatively. Transfusion burden increased progressively with worsening anemia in unadjusted analyses. Iron therapy was used in 27 (2.8%) patients and erythropoiesis-stimulating agents in 20 (2.1%; 5 epoetin, 15 darbepoetin) with higher use among patients with severe anemia. Among patients with hemoglobin < 10 g/dL and < 8 g/dL, IV iron was associated with higher unadjusted transfusion, while ESA showed no association with PRBC use. In adjusted models, neither anemia severity nor anemia-directed therapy independently predicted transfusion burden. Pre-transplant creatinine was the only independent predictor (OR 2.00, 95% CI 1.27–3.07; p = 0.002) (Figure).

Conclusions: In our cohort, RBC transfusion occurred mainly intraoperatively and postoperatively. Anemia-directed therapy was infrequently used and did not independently reduce transfusion, even in patients with hemoglobin < 10 g/dL or < 8 g/dL.

P146 Observational real-life study of the use of octaplasLG® in France

Y Benhamou1, M Gareis2, P Poullin3

1Hôpital Charles-Nicolle, Rouen, France, 2Octapharma Pharmazeutika Produktionsges.m.b.H, Vienna, Austria, 3Aphérèse, APHM – Hôpital de la Conception, Marseille, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P146

Introduction: This study aimed to describe the conditions of use of octaplasLG®, a pooled solvent/detergent-treated and filtered human plasma product. Although octaplasLG® is indicated for a variety of uses, real-world clinical data are limited.

Methods: This was a prospective, non-interventional, post-marketing study of patients receiving plasma infusions with octaplasLG® (Octapharma) at 14 hospitals/clinics in France. Primary endpoints were the indication for transfusion, degree of urgency, ordering department and infusion characteristics. Secondary endpoints were adverse reactions (AEs) and time to platelet count (PC) normalisation in patients with thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura (TTP).

Results: In total, 243 patients received at least 1 infusion of octaplasLG®. Indications for use were therapeutic plasma exchange (TPE) procedures (56.8%), complex deficiency of coagulation factors (CFs; 40.7%) and substitution therapy in congenital CF deficiencies (2.5%). OctaplasLG® posology (mean ± SD) was 25.7 ± 21.9 mL/kg, and was higher for TPE procedures (39.0 ± 20.4 mL/kg) than for complex deficiency of CFs (7.7 ± 4.1 mL/kg) or substitution therapy in CF deficiencies (14.3 ± 5.8 mL/kg). The Figure shows usage by diagnosis and hospitalisation emergency level; overall, 54.7% of patients who received octaplasLG® were hospitalized in emergency. Overall, 27 patients (11.1%) had 70 AEs. AEs occurred within 24 h post-infusion in 3 patients (1.5%): 1 with coagulopathy due to complex deficiency of CFs experienced ischaemic stroke; 1 with thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA) experienced vomiting, paraesthesia and visual impairment; 1 with another diagnosis experienced erythema, hot flush and malaise. For 29 patients with TTP in whom treatment was deemed effective, the median time to PC normalisation was 5 days.

Conclusions: This study described real-life use of octaplasLG® and confirmed its safety and efficacy in the whole population across a range of diagnoses, including its use for TPE treatment in patients with TMA such as TTP.

P148 Gelofusine administration in critically ill trauma patients is not associated with adverse renal or coagulation outcomes

J Boesch1, J Gebetsberger1, P Tscholl2, P Lichtenberger1, D Niederbrunner1, D Fries1, M Bachler1

1Medical University Innsbruck, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Innsbruck, Austria, 2Data Lab Hell GmbH, Zirl, Austria

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P148

Introduction: This study evaluated the safety of Gelofusine use in trauma patients admitted to the ICU, focusing on its potential association with acute kidney injury (AKI), coagulation impairment, and mortality. Gelatin-based volume therapy remains controversial, particularly regarding renal effects. The European trauma guideline advises restricted use of gelatin solutions: they should not be applied as standard-of-care therapy. Initial resuscitation should rely on crystalloids despite their negligible volume-expanding effect, while gelatin may be considered only in selected situations and with great caution [1].

Methods: We conducted a retrospective single-center cohort study of 220 adult trauma patients treated in the ICU at Innsbruck University Hospital. Data from the prehospital phase, emergency room, operating room, and ICU days 1–7 were analyzed. AKI was assessed using KDIGO and AKIN criteria; coagulation via FIBTEM; and outcomes included mortality, need for renal replacement therapy (RRT), fluid overload, and hemodynamic instability. Multivariable mixed-effects models controlled for relevant confounders.

Results: The overall in-house mortality was 16.4%, which was lower than predicted by national trauma registry data (Figure). Although higher Gelofusine doses were administered to sicker patients (e.g., those with higher lactate, shock, or AKI at admission), Gelofusine was not independently associated with worsening renal function, RRT, or coagulation impairment. Instead, renal outcomes were predominantly driven by early AKI, fluid overload, and baseline creatinine.

Conclusions: Gelofusine use was safe in this ICU trauma population and not linked to renal or coagulation-related harm. Its administration reflected illness severity rather than causing worse outcomes. Notably, despite relatively frequent Gelofusine use, overall morbidity and mortality remained below national and international benchmarks.

P150 Albumin structural and conformational conservation in Vialebex® compared with other available therapeutic albumin products: an in vitro study

Z Farhane, M Bilong, M Le Boedec, V Blanchard, A Besle, A Seifert

LFB Biotechnologies, Les Ulis, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P150

Introduction: Human serum albumin (HSA) is an effective plasma expander and antioxidant used as infusion in intensive care unit for critical patients with severe burns or septic shock or hypoalbuminemia or decompensated cirrhosis. These emergencies are characterised by a reduction in total effective HSA concentration and a significant structural and functional alterations. It is important to conserve the structural integrity of albumin to maximise clinical effectiveness by minimising albumin oxidation and maintaining its interaction to neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn) to increase albumin recycling. We investigated potential structural differences among albumin products.

Methods: The study was performed on five albumin commercial products Albutein® 25%/Albunorm® 20%/Alburex® 20%/Flexbumin® 25% compared with Vialebex® 20%. Albumin’s isoforms distribution was determined by ion exchange chromatography and affinity to FcRn receptor was analysed by Surface Plasmon Resonance.

Results: Vialebex® batches contained on average a higher proportion of non-cysteinylated HSA (32.2 ± 1.2 vs range of other albumin products [15.8 ± 2.3;22.2 ± 13.3%]), a lower proportion of oxidised HSA compared with the other albumin products (15.5 ± 0.4 vs [27.4 ± 11.8;31.6 ± 15.7%]) and lower batch-to-batch variability. Vialebex® had a higher affinity for FcRn receptor driven by a faster association rate and a lower dissociation. Mean dissociation rate for Vialebex® was 3.85E-06 M, compared with the other albumins, which ranged from 4.73E-06M to 5.53E-06M (Table).

Conclusions: Vialebex® presented the highest level of non-cysteinylated isoform, the lowest oxidised one and the better affinity to FcRn receptor. The lower proportion of oxidised HSA in Vialebex® may explain its greater antioxidant potential, compared with other albumins. Preservation of structural and conformational integrity and superior antioxidant effect of Vialebex® could be advantageous in the management of critical patients.

P152 Optimal fluid resuscitation patterns in sepsis: a multi-center phenotype discovery and causal inference study

S Gao, B Zhang, Y Zhang, M Deng, X Zhang, L Tang, W Xu, M Luo, Y Tian, T He

Foshan Hospital of Traditional Chinese Medicine, ICU, Foshan, Guangdong Province, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P152

Introduction: While the Surviving Sepsis Campaign recommends a fixed 30 mL/kg initial fluid volume [1], the "one-size-fits-all" approach remains debated. Moving beyond the binary "liberal vs. restrictive" paradigm, we hypothesized that unsupervised machine learning combined with causal inference could deconstruct clinical heterogeneity, identifying a precise therapeutic window associated with better outcomes than current standards.

Methods: We conducted a multi-center retrospective study (MIMIC-IV, n = 3,904; eICU-CRD, n = 6,266). Dynamic Time Warping clustered 48-h fluid patterns. IPTW mitigated confounding. Causal effects on mortality were estimated via subgroup analyses by baseline lactate (≤ 2 vs. > 2 mmol/L). Contour heatmaps visualized fluid-lactate-vasopressor interactions.

Results: DTW distinguished three clinical phenotypes: Restrictive, Moderate (25–40 mL/kg), and Liberal. In IPTW-adjusted analysis, the "Moderate" phenotype exhibited a robust survival advantage compared to the Restrictive baseline in both MIMIC-IV (HR 0.85, 95%CI 0.71–1.01) and eICU-CRD (HR 0.78, 95%CI 0.62–0.98). Subgroup analysis revealed this protective effect was most pronounced in patients with hyperlactatemia (lactate > 2 mmol/L), with odds ratios of 0.52 (95%CI 0.44–0.63, p < 0.001) in MIMIC-IV and 0.44 (95%CI 0.35–0.54, p < 0.001) in eICU-CRD. Contour heatmaps revealed that survival probability was highest in the 25–40 mL/kg range, particularly among patients requiring low-to-moderate vasopressor support, forming a U-shaped mortality curve with elevated risks at both extremes (Figure).

Conclusions: Using data-driven phenotyping, we identified a "Moderate" fluid strategy that is associated with lower mortality compared to restrictive or liberal extremes, particularly in hypoperfused patients. These findings support an optimal-window paradigm, suggesting that personalizing resuscitation within a 25–40 mL/kg window may offer superior outcomes over fixed targets.

P153 Validity of the initial distribution volume of glucose as a preload indicator under cardiac tamponade: an experimental study in pigs

K Noto, E Hashiba, S Uchida, A Ishii, J Saito

Hirosaki University Graduate School of Medicine, Department of Anesthesiology, Hirosaki, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P153

Introduction: The initial distribution volume of glucose (IDVG) reflects the central extracellular fluid volume and is used as an indicator of cardiac preload [1]. Cardiac tamponade (CP) is a typical model of obstructive shock in which venous return is impaired. The study aimed to evaluate the validity of the IDVG as a preload indicator under CP in pigs.

Methods: After approval of the animal ethical committee of Hirosaki University, six anesthetized pigs were used. Cardiac output (CO), intrathoracic blood volume (ITBV), stroke volume variation (SVV), and CVP were measured using the PiCCO (Pulsion Medical Systems, Germany). The IDVG was calculated using a one-compartment model after intravenous injection of glucose (2 g), as previously reported [2]. In the CP protocol, an aortic occlusion balloon (Edwards Lifesciences, Japan) was inflated within the pericardial space to produce CP state which showed approximately a 1.5-fold CVP increase and 40% CO reduction. Measurements were obtained 5 min later. Fluid volume loading (10 mL/kg Hydroxyethyl starch) was performed with or without CP to assess volume responsiveness of hemodynamic indices and IDVG. Data were expressed as the mean ± SD/SEM, and the statistical analysis was performed with RM-ANOVA, followed by Dunnett’s test.

Results: CP significantly decreased CO and ITBV and increased SVV, whereas IDVG remained unchanged. IDVG significantly increased after fluid volume loading regardless of CP status, while CO increased only when fluid loading was performed without CP (Figure).

Conclusions: IDVG did not change in response to CP-induced obstructive shock and increased with fluid loading even under CP. These findings suggest that IDVG reflects central extracellular fluid volume and may serve as a preload indicator similar to stressed volume, which is related to venous return.

P154 Fluid responsiveness and changes in venous congestion and lung water during volume expansion in critically ill patients: a multicentre observational study

X Si

The First Affiliated Hospital, Sun Yatsen University, Critical Care Medicine, Guangzhou, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P154

Introduction: The relationship between fluid responsiveness and right-and left-sided congestion remains underexplored. This study aimed to delineate the dynamic behaviour of venous congestion and extravascular lung water index (EVLWI) during a fluid challenge depending on the concomitant changes in cardiac index (CI).

Methods: In patients from three intensive care units, for whom a 500-mL fluid challenge was administered, we retrospectively analysed CI, CVP, venous excess ultrasound (VExUS), and EVLWI, which had been prospectively recorded. A VExUS congestion point was calculated from 0 to 7 by assigning 1 point to each degree of abnormality for the 4 investigated veins. A subgroup analysis was planned in patients with ARDS.

Results: We analysed 64 patients, of whom 42 (66%) were fluid responders (FR +) defined by a CI increase ≥ 15% with fluid infusion. Before the fluid challenge, CVP was lower in FR + than in fluid non-responders (FR-) (7.3 [2.9–10.3] vs. 10.6 [8.2–13.0] mmHg, respectively, p = 0.002). VExUS grades and congestion points were not different between FR + and FR- (Grade 0: 62% vs. 55%, respectively, p = 0.601; congestion points: 1.0 [0.0–2.3] vs. 2.0 [1.0–3.0], respectively, p = 0.053). EVLWI was also similar between groups. Following fluid administration, VExUS grade deterioration occurred in 5% of FR + versus 73% of FR − (p < 0.001) (Figure). After the fluid challenge, abnormal VExUS grades were more prevalent in FR − than in FR + (91% vs. 43%, respectively, p < 0.001), and congestion points were higher (4.0 [3.0–5.0] vs. 1.5 [1.0–3.0], respectively, p < 0.001). CVP increased by 1.4 [0.4–2.4] mmHg in FR + and 2.0 [1.1–3.5] mmHg in FR- (p = 0.064). Among 25 patients with ARDS, EVLWI increased more than in patients without ARDS, in both FR- (by 1.7 [0.9–3.3] vs. 0.7 [0–1.4] mL/kg, respectively, p = 0.046) and FR + (by 1.0 [-0.6–2.5] vs. 0 [-0.7–0.4] mL/kg, respectively, p = 0.009).

Conclusions: A fluid challenge worsened venous congestion, assessed by VExUS as well as CVP, in fluid non-responders, while it was not in fluid responders.

P155 Can ultrasound-derived jugular venous Doppler waveform reflect fluid intolerance in acute circulatory failure? A prospective, dual-center observational study

WN Zhu1, WL Song1, CW You1, RL Chen2, YL Lu2, JF Wu1, XD Guan1, X Si1, R Shi1

1The First Affiliated Hospital of Sun YatSen University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Guangzhou, China, 2Longgang Central Hospital, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Shenzhen, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P155

Introduction: Fluid resuscitation is a cornerstone of management in acute circulatory failure, yet fluid overload carries significant risks. Accurate assessment of systemic venous congestion is therefore crucial. Venous excess ultrasound (VExUS) has known limitations [1]. Thus this study aimed to evaluate the potential ability of a hands-free, non-invasive jugular Doppler device (CVD) for estimating VExUS.

Methods: This prospective, dual-center observational study enrolled patients with circulatory failure (mean arterial pressure < 65 mmHg or receiving vasopressors) who were scheduled for a fluid challenge (FC) (500 mL Ringer’s lactate in 10–15 min) and monitored with PiCCO. Venous congestion grading by CVD and VExUS was performed before and after FC by two experienced blinded operators. Spearman correlation was used to assess overall agreement. The Wilcoxon test compared fluid responder (FR +) and non-responder (FR -) subgroups, and repeated measures correlation (rrm) evaluated trending ability.

Results: From July 2024 to August 2025, a total of 41 patients (mean age 61 ± 17 y) with 29 males (71%) were included. Among them, 88% were on norepinephrine (0.51 ± 0.59 μg/kg/min), and 85% on mechanical ventilation. The mean SOFA score was 9 ± 4. There was no diffrence regarding fluid balance between two groups (p = 0.85). In FR - patients, VExUS grades increased after fluid challenge, with 54.6% progressing to Grade 2, whereas FR + patients exhibited stable grades. CVD showed consistent trends with VExUS in both groups. Repeated measures correlation between CVD and VExUS was rrm(32) = 0.55, 95%CI [0.265–0.751], p < 0.001; CVD vs. CVP rrm(32) = 0.51 95%CI [0.211–0.725], p = 0.002; CVP vs. VExUS rrm(32) = 0.43, 95%CI [0.141–0.647] p = 0.005 (see Figure).

Conclusions: CVD shows promising concordance with VExUS and may better reflect systemic venous congestion. Though not a full replacement, CVD offers valuable, continuous insight into fluid intolerance in critically ill patients.

P156 Venous excess ultrasound score is a marker of mean circulatory filling pressure in a porcine model of systemic inflammation

BK Lakatos1, BA Barta1, B Lonkai2, K Marosi1, S Spiesshofer1, A Kovacs3, E Németh1, B Merkely1, T Radovits3, Z Túróczi1

1Semmelweis University, Heart and Vascular Center, Budapest, Hungary, 2Semmelweis University, Department of Intensive Therapy, Budapest, Hungary, 3Semmelweis University, Department of Experimental Cardiology and Surgical Techniques, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P156

Introduction: Hypervolemia resulting in venous congestion is detrimental to patients in the intensive care unit, however, accurately assessing or even predicting it with routinely accessible techniques remains a challenge. Venous excess ultrasound (VEXUS) score is a noninvasive method, which aims to achieve this by integrating results from visceral venous flow curves. There have been several studies confirming that higher VEXUS scores are associated with increased morbidity attributed to hypervolemia, but this relationship has never been confirmed. Our aim was to examine the relationship of VEXUS to venous congestion in a porcine model of systemic inflammation and its control group.

Methods: We examined 9 pigs (Zymosan-induced systemic inflammation n = 6, sham controls n = 3, weighing 20–30 kgs). After general anaesthesia, we performed baseline ultrasound measurements for VEXUS scoring, measured cardiac output with transpulmonary thermodilution and determined mean circulatory filling pressures (MCFP) using a validated method based on Guyton’s principle. Later we induced hypervolemia in two stages (giving 60 mL/kg crystalloid/stage) with the reassessment of every parameter.

Results: Baseline MCFP was 14.3 ± 1.1, which increased to 16.5 ± 1.7 and 18.4 ± 1.9 mmHg with each volume load respectively. Vexus score showed similar increase from baseline grade 1.1 ± 0.4 to 1.9 ± 0.8 and 2.4 ± 0.7. Using repeated measure statistics, both parameters displayed a significant increase with loading state (MCFP: p < 0.001, VEXUS: p = 0.002). In case of MCFP, the increments significantly differed from each other (p < 0.001 for every pairwise comparison). MCFP correlated significantly with the VEXUS score (r = 0.88, r2 = 0.78, p < 0.001) (Figure), and regression analysis suggests that for each VEXUS score increment MCFP increases by 2.9 ± 0.4 mm Hg.

Conclusions: In this animal model of systemic inflammation and its sham control, VEXUS score was strongly associated with MCFP, potentially confirming the previous clinical assumption about the method.

P157 Velocity-time integral and stroke volume measured in the ascending aorta for prediction of volume responsiveness in mechanically ventilated adults with septic shock

S Bhattacharjee1, B Kayarat2, AA Kundar2, BR Ray2, S Maitra2

1All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Anaesthesiology, Pain Medicine Critical Care, New Delhi, India, 2All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, India

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P157

Introduction: Velocity time integral (Ao-VTI) and stroke volume (Ao-SV) can be estimated from spectral Doppler analysis of ascending aortic blood flow measured at the suprasternal notch [1]. We hypothesized that change Ao-VTI and Ao-SV will be able to identify volume responsiveness in mechanically ventilated critically ill adults with septic shock and also will be an acceptable alternative of left ventricle- VTI (LV-VTI) and left ventricle SV (LV-SV) measured by transthoracic echocardiography.

Methods: Adult patients with a diagnosis of septic shock were recruited. LVOT-VTI, LVOT- SV, Ao-VTI and Ao-SV were measured before and after a passive leg raising (PLR) maneuver. An increase in LVOT-VTI (ΔLVOT-VTI) > 12% after PLR was considered as volume responsiveness. Predictive ability of change in Ao-VTI (ΔAo-VTI) after PLR was evaluated by area under the receiver operator characteristics curve (AUROC). Agreement between LV-VTI and Ao-VTI was evaluated by mixed- effect Bland- Altman analysis.

Results: ΔAo-VTI was a decent predictor of volume responsiveness [AUROC (95% CI) 0.82 (0.68- 0.95), p < 0.001] when compared against the ΔLVOT-VTI as gold standard. The best cut-off was 8.5% with a sensitivity of 79.2%, specificity of 86.2% and accuracy of 83% to predict fluid responsiveness. There was statistically significant linear correlation between ΔLVOT-VTI and ΔAo-VTI [rho = 0.49, p < 0.0001]. The mixed effect Bland- Altman agreement analysis reported bias of –1.8 with limit of agreement of (-0.31, -3.3) between LVOT-VTI and Ao-VTI (Figure).

Conclusions: The ΔAo-VTI with PLR maneuver had moderate predictive ability for volume responsiveness. It has a decent sensitivity and high specificity and can be used in for assessment of volume responsiveness, particularly when it is difficult to obtain topical four chamber view. The limits of agreement between LVOT-VTI and Ao-VTI needs further evaluation as a large number of data points were outside the limit of agreement.

P159 Optimization of postoperative fluid therapy using biomarkers and bioelectrical impedance analysis integrated in artificial intelligence model

VA Crisan1, CC Cobilinschi1, A Dumitriu1, A Andrei1, L Mirea1, I Darie1, A Wong2

1Emergency Clinical Hospital of Bucharest, Intensive Care Unit, Bucharest, Romania, 2Kings College Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, London, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P159

Introduction: Optimizing postoperative fluid therapy is essential for reducing complications and supporting recovery, yet conventional static parameters may miss early or subclinical imbalance [1,2]. This study evaluates the combined use of the Capillary Leakage Index (CLI), the Vascular Leakage Index (VLI), and bioelectrical impedance analysis (BIA) for individualized postoperative fluid assessment [3].

Methods: Adults (> 18 years) admitted to the ICU after abdominal surgery or multiple trauma were included; pregnancy, severe cardiovascular disease, chronic dialysis, and major limb amputations were exclusion criteria. At ICU admission, CRP, albumin, hematocrit and BIA-derived TBW, ECW and ICW were measured to calculate CLI and VLI, with repeat assessment on day 4. Conclusions: These findings suggest that CLI and VLI capture meaningful postoperative fluid shifts, and further enrollment will clarify their clinical value for individualized postoperative fluid management.

P160 Personalized fluid resuscitation reduces mortality in septic shock as compared with fixed-volume approach – a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials

G Nagy1, J Hollosi2, C Turan2, D Laczko2, Z Sipos2, L Zubek2, Z Molnar2

1Semmelweis University, Department of Emergency Medicine, Budapest, Hungary, 2Semmelweis University, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P160

Introduction: Immediate fluid resuscitation plays a pivotal role in sepsis management. It remains unclear whether a fixed-volume or personalized strategy is more beneficial. Our aim was to compare effectiveness and safety of personalized vs. fixed-volume resuscitation in septic patients in a systematic review and meta-analysis.

Methods: We conducted a systematic literature search on PubMed, EMBASE and CENTRAL on October 30, 2024. The pre-study protocol was registered with PROSPERO (ID: CRD42024601970). Randomized controlled trials investigated septic patients who received fluid therapy in a personalized way during their initial resuscitation and reported the outcomes of our interest. The meta-analysis was performed using META packages in R with a random-effects model. Risk of bias was assessed using RoB2.

Results: The selection yielded 10 eligible randomized controlled trials (RCTs) with 1,011 patients. Data from 9 RCTs (n = 901) revealed significantly reduced odds of 30-day mortality, (odds ratio: 0.62; 95% confidence interval: 0.42–0.93) (Figure); however, there was no significant difference in the requirement for renal replacement therapy (odds ratio: 0.77; 95% confidence interval: 0.23–2.55), duration of mechanical ventilation (mean difference: 0.27; 95% confidence interval: -3.09–3.63), vasopressor therapy (mean difference: -3.70; 95% confidence interval: -32.88–25.48) and length of hospital (mean difference: -0.87; 95% confidence interval: -4.97–3.25) or intensive care unit stay (mean difference: 0.44; 95% confidence interval: -1.90–2.77).

Conclusions: Personalized fluid resuscitation, compared with fixed-volume resuscitation, significantly reduced 30-day mortality in septic patients.

P161 Trendelenburg maneuver to predict fluid responsiveness in patients under mechanical ventilation with spontaneous breathing: a prospective study

N Hemtanon1, N Raykateeraroj1, S Kongsayreepong1, T Yuyen1, P Boorapapon1, Y Hikasa2, S Laoamornphunkul1, N Kitisin1

1Mahidol University, Department of Anesthesiology, Faculty of Medicine Siriraj Hospital, Bangkok, Thailand, 2Austin Hospital, Department of Intensive Care, Victoria, Australia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P161

Introduction: Inappropriate fluid management is associated with adverse outcomes in critically ill patients. The Trendelenburg maneuver (TM), which transiently increases cardiac preload through a head-down tilt and operates on the same principle as passive leg raising for fluid responsiveness assessment [1], may offer a practical adjunct in light-sedated patients under mechanical ventilation (MV) with spontaneous breathing activity (SBA).

Methods: In this single-center, prospective study, the patients with signs of tissue hypoperfusion under MV with SBA in a surgical ICU underwent the TM which consisted of a sequential assessment of hemodynamic parameters at baseline supine position (T0), reverse Trendelenburg + 10° (T1), Trendelenburg − 13° (T2), return to supine before fluid loading (F0), and reassessment after a 4 mL/kg infusion of 5% albumin over 15 min (FL) as illustrated in the Figure. Cardiac index (CI) and its surrogates were measured at each step with FloTrac™/HemoSphere™. Fluid responder (FR) was defined as a ≥ 10% increase in CI from F0 to FL. The diagnostic performance of the TM was evaluated using the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC).

Results: Among 38 patients, 21 were classified as FR. A TM-induced ΔCI > 0.15 L/min/m2 yielded an AUROC of 0.78 (95% confidence interval 0.64–0.93), with 52.4% sensitivity and 82.4% specificity; however, more than 80% of patients lay within indeterminate gray zones. A TM-induced Δ%CI > 6% showed similar performance, while ΔSVV and ΔPPV demonstrated lower but moderate accuracy. In sensitivity analyses, its performance was slightly lower among patients with norepinephrine < 0.1 µg/kg/min.

Conclusions: The TM was feasible and safe but showed limited and inconsistent diagnostic performance, with wide gray zones encompassing most patients. These findings render the test inconclusive for bedside decision-making; at most, it may serve as a supplementary assessment when passive leg raising is not feasible.

P162 Perioperative myocardial injury phenotypes in cardiac surgery: cardiovascular biomarkers as prognostic indicators of death and new disability

MSY Thio1, TCD Rettig1, HJT Ruven2, MLP Langelaan3, MC Post4, PG Noordzij5

1Amphia Hospital, Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care, Breda, Netherlands, 2St. Antonius Hospital, Clinical Chemistry, Nieuwegein, Netherlands, 3Amphia Hospital, Result Laboratorium, Breda, Netherlands, 4St. Antonius Hospital, Cardiology, Nieuwegein, Netherlands, 5St. Antonius Hospital, Anaesthesiology, Intensive Care, and Pain Medicine, Nieuwegein, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P162

Introduction: This study aimed to identify prognostic perioperative phenotypes of myocardial injury, in patients undergoing cardiac surgery using biomarkers for myocardial injury, cardiac dysfunction and inflammation.

Methods: Patients undergoing coronary artery bypass grafting, valve surgery or Bentall procedures were included. NT-proBNP (pg/mL), cTnT and GDF-15 were measured preoperatively, postoperatively and on postoperative day 1–3. Longitudinal latent class mixed effects models were used to identify phenotypes. Relative risk ratios adjusted for EuroSCORE II (RR (95% CI)) were calculated for new-disability and one-year mortality.

Results: In 1,154 patients, four perioperative phenotypes were identified. Phenotype 1 (n = 927) had normal pre-operative biomarker values and minimal postoperative myocardial injury, cardiac dysfunction or inflammation. This group had the lowest risk of new disability (26.1%) and one-year mortality (0.7%). Phenotype 2 (n = 45) had similar baseline biomarker levels, but more comorbidities and pronounced postoperative cardiac dysfunction (peak NT-proBNP 7,704 [6,168–9,418]), with RRs for new-disability of 1.7 (1.2–2.3) and one-year mortality of 7.7 (2.1–22.9). Phenotype 3 (n = 164) presented with elevated pre-operative NT-proBNP (2,032 [1,566–2,973]) and high prevalence of chronic heart failure (30%) and atrial fibrillation (38%). Despite limited additional perioperative deterioration, one-year mortality was higher (RR 4.3 (1.5–11.6)). Phenotype 4 (n = 18) was characterized by elevated preoperative NT-proBNP (4,948 [3,146–7,738]) and severe perioperative myocardial injury, inflammation and cardiac dysfunction. Outcomes were poor, with RRs for new-disability of 1.8 (1.0–2.7) and one-year mortality of 25.1 (9.2–46.4).

Conclusions: A perioperative cardiovascular biomarker panel identified one low-risk and three high-risk phenotypes of myocardial injury, with distinct patterns of cardiac dysfunction and inflammation, prognostic for new-disability and one-year mortality after cardiac surgery.

P163 Randomized comparison of intensive blood pressure control in patients with acute type B aortic dissection (RAID)

P Yu1, P Lan1, C Wu2, H Kang3, Z Liu4, S Hu5, M Yan6, J Sun7, J Zhou8

1Sir Run Run Shaw Hospital, Zhejiang University School of Medicine, Zhejiang, China, 2Ganzhou People´s Hospital, Jiangxi, China, 3West China Hospital, Sichuan, China, 4Second Affiliated Hospital of Zhejiang University School of Medicine, Department of Vascular Surgery, Zhejiang, China, 5Wuxi People´s Hospital, Department of Vascular Surgery, Jiangsu, China, 6Chongqing Daping Hospital, Department of Cardiovascular and Vascular Surgery, Chongqing, China, 7Lishui Central Hospital, Department of Vascular Surgery, Zhejiang, China, 8Sir Run Run Shaw Hospital, Zhejiang University School of Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Zhejiang, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P163

Introduction: The optimal perioperative systolic blood pressure (BP) for patients with acute type Stanford B aortic dissection (ABAD) was currently uncertain. Objective was to clarify whether early intensive BP lowering provides better clinical prognosis for patients with ABAD compared with conventional BP management.

Methods: A multicenter, prospective, randomized trial was conducted from January 2018 to March 2022 at seven hospitals in China. The trial was registered in the website of ClinicalTrial.gov (NCT03001739). Participants were randomly assigned to receive intensive BP management (systolic BP target < 120 mm Hg, n = 163) or conventional BP management (systolic BP target 120–140 mm Hg, n = 169) after enrollment. The primary outcome is a composite in-hospital adverse outcome, including perioperative death, stroke, permanent paraplegia or hemiparesis, and renal failure requiring hemodialysis at hospital discharge.

Results: Among 332 patients randomized, mean age was 55 years and 280 (84.3%) patients were male. Among patients who received intensive BP management, 6.7% had a composite in-hospital adverse outcome, compared with 5.9% of patients who received conventional BP management, a non-significant difference (OR 1.15 [95% CI, 0.48-2.79], p = 0.93). The median hospital length of stay in the intensive BP management group was 16 days (IQR, 10–23 days), and the median hospital length of stay in the conventional group was 16 days (IQR, 10–21 days). The median perioperative maximal creatinine level during hospitalization in the intensive BP management group was 88.0 mmol/L (IQR, 71.4-121.5 mmol/L), and the median perioperative maximal creatinine level in the conventional group was 97.3 mmol/L (IQR, 80.0-128.0). 266 (80.1%) of 332 patients had received thoracic endovascular aortic repair.

Conclusions: In patients with ABAD, intensive BP lowering to within 120 mm Hg did not confer additional reductions in composite in-hospital adverse outcome over conventional BP of lowering of 120-140 mmHg.

P164 Hypotension endotypes during airway-management in critically ill patients: a hybrid deep-learning analysis

VC Kurucz1, CA Den Otter1, J Schuurmans2, JTM Tol1, SR Rellum2, BJP Van der Ster2, APJ Vlaar3, E Kho2, J Schenk4, DP Veelo2

1Amsterdam UMC, Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Amsterdam, Netherlands, 2Amsterdam UMC, Anesthesiology, Amsterdam, Netherlands, 3Amsterdam UMC, Intensive Care, Laboratory of Experimental Intensive Care and Anesthesiology, Amsterdam, Netherlands, 4Amsterdam UMC, Department of Anesthesiology, Department of Intensive Care and Department of Epidemiology and Data Science, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P164

Introduction: Hypotension during airway management (e.g. intubation or tracheostomy) in critically ill patients is common. While monitoring detects hypotension, the cause often remains unclear, leaving treatment reliant on clinicians’ judgment. We aimed to identify hypotension endotypes using a hybrid deep-learning approach applied to high-resolution intensive care unit (ICU) hemodynamic data.

Methods: We analyzed 113 ICU patients with hypotension (MAP < 65 mmHg for ≥ 1 min) during airway management. Dimensionality reduction using a long short-term memory autoencoder was followed by Gaussian mixture modeling to identify clusters based on the following hemodynamic variables: stroke volume variation (SVV); stroke volume index (SVI); pulse rate (PR); systemic vascular resistance index (SVRI); and rolling standard deviation features. Endotypes were identified through unsupervised clustering and labeled by a clinical panel based on hemodynamic profiles. Internal validation used a 20% holdout dataset.

Results: From 265 hypotensive episodes, four endotypes were identified (Figure). The most common (69%) was labeled as “hypovolemia due to vasodilation”, with normal-to-high SVV, low SVI, and low-to-normal SVRI. The “hypovolemic” endotype (13%) showed high SVV, reduced SVI and SVRI, and tachycardia. “Vasodilation” (10%) was marked by low SVRI, high SVI, and low SVV. “Myocardial depression” (8%) involved low cardiac index driven by reduced SVI and a relative drop in PR, alongside elevated SVV, and increased SVRI. Internal validation confirmed accurate data representation and consistent clustering across training and holdout sets, with a median per-episode reconstruction error difference of 0.6 (95% CI: 0.3–1).

Conclusions: Unsupervised deep learning identified four distinct hypotension endotypes during airway management, which may inform mechanism-specific interventions and contribute to improved clinical decision-making.
