P165 Tissue perfusion pressure, not mean arterial pressure, associated with 28-day mortality in septic shock: a cohort study using high-resolution waveform data

K Jin, H Chen, Y Yang

Southeast University, Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P165

Introduction: Despite achieving MAP targets (≥ 65 mmHg), tissue hypoperfusion often persists in septic shock. Flow is physiologically limited by critical closing pressure (Pcc); thus, the effective driver is tissue perfusion pressure (TPP = MAP-Pcc). We hypothesized that TPP predicts mortality better than MAP and sought a clinical threshold.

Methods: This retrospective study analyzed high-resolution (125 Hz) arterial waveforms from adults admitted to the ICU of a large tertiary academic medical center. Patients with sepsis requiring vasopressors within 24h were included. Pcc was estimated minute-by-minute using a validated physiological algorithm derived from beat-to-beat data [1]. The exposure was the 24h time-weighted average TPP. Primary outcome was 28-day mortality. Multivariable logistic/Cox regression and restricted cubic splines (RCS) were used, adjusting for confounders including SOFA, Vasoactive-Inotropic Score.

Results: Among 590 patients, 28-day mortality was 28.3%. Non-survivors had significantly lower TPP (median 32.1 vs. 38.1 mmHg, p < 0.001). In continuous multivariable models, higher TPP independently reduced mortality risk (p < 0.001), whereas MAP showed no independent association (p > 0.05). RCS revealed a non-linear L-shaped relationship (p nonlinear < 0.001) with a sharp risk increase below approximately 39 mmHg (Figure). Patients with TPP < 39 mmHg had higher mortality vs. TPP ≥ 39 mmHg (35.2% vs. 17.5%; adjusted OR 0.45, 95% CI 0.28–0.74; p = 0.002). Higher TPP also correlated with more vasopressor-free and organ-failure-free days (p < 0.001). Interaction analysis suggested stronger protective effects of TPP in patients with higher SOFA scores.

Conclusions: In septic patients requiring vasopressors, TPP, but not MAP, is independently associated with 28-day mortality. A TPP of approximately 39 mmHg may serve as a critical lower limit for maintaining effective organ perfusion. TPP represents a promising physiological target for hemodynamic optimization.

P166 A porcine model of septic shock with fluid accumulation and prolonged resuscitation

HS Ly1, A Hammed1, H Dumargne2, R Lac1, V Louzier1, A Dargent3

1VetAgro Sup, Marcy l´Etoile, France, 2Hospices Civils de Lyon, Oullins, France, 3Hospices Civils de Lyon, LyonSud Hospital Critical Care Department, Oullins, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P166

Introduction: Fluid overload in sepsis, driven by capillary leakage, poses a major challenge in managing septic shock. Capillary leakage is a central mechanism, but experimental data to guide targeted therapeutic approaches remain limited. It is therefore essential to develop robust preclinical models in order to explore strategies aimed at limiting its deleterious effects. We therefore developed a translational porcine model of Pseudomonas aeruginosa-induced septic shock with fluid overload, resuscitated over 24 h.

Methods: Sixteen female pigs (55 kg) were divided into three groups. control (CT, n = 4, no bacteria), the two other groups received an IV infusion of Pseudomonas aeruginosa (5 × 10⁸ CFU/mL) at short dose (SD, n = 6, 0.3 mL/20 kg/min for 90 min), and continuous dose (CD, n = 6, 0.1 mL/kg/h with hourly doubling over 6 h). Upon hypotension (MAP < 65 mmHg), resuscitation followed a standardized protocol (fluids ± norepinephrine). Data were collected over 24 h from the start of bacterial infusion.

Results: No sepsis was observed in the CT group, while all pigs in the SD and CD groups met sepsis criteria based on the porcine-adapted five-domain SOFA score. Septic shock developed in 33.3% of SD and 100% of CD animals. Twenty-four-hour survival was 100% (CT), 83.3% (SD), and 66.7% (CD). Significant differences were observed in fluid balance (1230, 5650, 7808 mL; p < 0.001), lung wet/dry ratio (2.35, 6.57, 5.75; p = 0.0008), kidney wet/dry ratio (2.78, 5.78, 5.09; p = 0.02), and combined pleural/peritoneal fluid accumulation (11, 255, 974 mL; p = 0.003) (Figure).

Conclusions: Continuous P. aeruginosa infusion effectively induced septic shock with significant fluid overload, providing a robust porcine model of sepsis-induced capillary leakage. The resuscitation protocol was efficient and allowed to reproduce the course of fluid accumulation during early septic shock.

P167 The role of ICU nurses in hypotension management: knowledge, practices, and the potential for a nurse-driven protocol

PP Oomes1, M Groot1, J Schuurmans2, S Rellum2, F Paulus1, AP Vlaar1, DP Veelo2, W Stilma1, J Schenk.2

1Amsterdam UMC, Intensive Care, Amsterdam, Netherlands, 2Amsterdam UMC, Anesthesiology, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P167

Introduction: Hypotension is common in the ICU and is associated with increased morbidity and mortality. ICU nurses play a key role in its detection and initial management, but their autonomy may be limited. Insight into their knowledge and perspectives regarding nurse-driven hypotension treatment is currently lacking. The aim of this study is to asses ICU nurses’ theoretical and practical knowledge of hypotension management and to identify perceived barriers to implementing a nurse-driven treatment protocol.

Methods: A single-center, cross-sectional survey was conducted among ICU nurses at Amsterdam UMC. The questionnaire assessed knowledge, clinical practices and attitudes toward nurse-led hypotension management.

Results: The response rate was 99% (N = 142). Nurses encountered hypotension in 55.1% of shifts. Over 80% supported nurse-led interventions, and 68.6% reported independently initiating treatment in 70% of hypotensive events (Figure). Knowledge accuracy exceeded 80% for treatment-related items. However, scores for complex hemodynamic interpretation were lower (36.4–48.1%). The most frequently reported barrier to implementing a nurse-driven protocol was uncertainty regarding patient inclusion and exclusion criteria.

Conclusions: ICU nurses frequently manage hypotension and support nurse-driven protocols, but knowledge gaps in advanced hemodynamic reasoning remain. Clear physiological criteria, structured education and decision-support tools may help ensure safe and effective nurse-led hypotension management in high-acuity settings.

P169 A machine-learning digital twin for predicting vasopressor need in sepsis patients

L Mellhammar1, AM Scott2, E Malmström2, AG Gustafsson2, A Bakochi3, T Mohanty2, L Thelaus2, F Kahn2, J Malmström2, A Linder2

1Faculty of Medicine, Lund University, Department of Clinical Sciences, Lund, Sweden, 2Faculty of Medicine, Lund University, Lund, Sweden, 3Swedish Infrastructure for Biological Mass Spectrometry, Swedish Infrastructure for Biological Mass Spectrometry, Lund, Sweden

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P169

Introduction: We postulated that a machine-learning digital twin model that incorporates clinical data and plasma proteome may predict which patients, with suspected sepsis, will require vasopressor treatment, already on admission to the emergency department, and before fluid challenge.

Methods: We have constructed a digital twin model for organ dysfunction in patients with suspected sepsis (n = 1613) using organ dysfunction-informed supervised projections of the blood plasma proteome at hospital admission. The proteome was analyzed by liquid chromatography mass spectrometry and demonstrate distinct protein profiles indicative of specific organ dysfunction [1]. We tested the digital twin model’s ability to predict vasopressor treatment out of proteome and clinical data available at admission.

Results: We analyzed a validation cohort (n = 1046) of patients with suspected sepsis and an external cohort (n = 350) with mixed emergency department patients. The digital twin model was able to aggregate patients together who received vasopressor treatment (Figure). Patients with a vasopressor risk score of ≥ 0.4 according to the digital twin model are up to 10-times more likely to receive vasopressor treatment and it is a stronger predictor of future vasopressor treatment than systolic blood pressure, AUC for the digital twin model 0.76 and for systolic blood pressure 0.62.

Conclusions: These findings suggest that proteome and clinical data can be used to accurately identify patients in need of vasopressor treatment already at the time-of-admission to the emergency department which could reduce potentially harmful fluid therapy and select patients for sparse beds in intensive care. Further it uncovers other clinically and molecularly similar patients who may benefit from a similar therapy. This general approach could also be applied towards other treatment types, unlocking the potential for precision medicine in sepsis.

P170 Methylene blue as secondary vasopressor in septic shock: a single-center randomized controlled trial

L Li, Y Li, Y Liao, Z Qian, L Zhang

Xiangya Hospital, Central South University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Changsha City, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P170

Introduction: While methylene blue (MB) as a second-line vasopressor effectively reduces vasopressor dosage and duration, its impact on changing organ failure scores in patients with septic shock remains unclear.

Methods: Between Feb. 17, 2023, and Feb. 14, 2025, adult patients with septic shock receiving norepinephrine (NE) ≥ 0.25 μg/kg/min for over 2 h were screened. Participants were randomized using computer-generated numbers. The intervention group received a single intravenous infusion of 2 mg/kg MB in 100 mL of 0.9% normal saline over 20 min upon meeting inclusion criteria, while the control group continued NE monotherapy. If NE requirements reached ≥ 0.5 μg/kg/min for over 2 h, the attending physician decided whether to initiate other vasopressors (excluding MB). The primary outcome was the change in SOFA (Sequential Organ Failure Assessment) score at 72 h after inclusion (ΔSOFA = SOFA score at 72 h—baseline SOFA score). Secondary outcomes included time to shock resolution, ICU mortality, and length of ICU stay.

Results: Of 120 enrolled patients, 119 were included in the final analysis (60 in the MB group, 59 controls). The ΔSOFA score showed tendency of greater reduction in the MB group (–3.5 ± 3.4 vs. –2.4 ± 3.0, p = 0.068). Although NE doses were similar at baseline (0.19 [0.15, 0.17] vs. 0.15 [0.15, 0.17] μg/kg/min, p = 0.345), the MB group had significantly lower NE requirements at 72 h (0.0 [0.0, 0.19] vs. 0.25 [0.0, 0.50] μg/kg/min, p = 0.003). The median time to shock resolution was numerically shorter in the MB group (36.5 [20, 72] hours) than in controls (72 [22, 72] hours), though without significance (p = 0.114). No statistical differences were observed in ICU mortality (41.7% vs. 52.5%, p = 0.235) or ICU length of stay (7 [4, 15] vs. 9 [5, 16] days, p = 0.682).

Conclusions: As adjunctive, a single dose of MB demonstrated a trend toward improved SOFA scores and significantly reduced NE requirements at 72 h in septic shock patients.

P171 Our experience with angiotensin 2: a mixed ICU perspective

I Vuksan, B Barbalic, M Bobinac, K Juricic, A Protic

University Hospital Centre Rijeka, Department of Anesthesiology, Intensive Care and Pain Medicine, Rijeka, Croatia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P171

Introduction: Angiotensin II (AT-II) has been recently discussed as third line therapy for refractory vasodilatory shock in septic patients based on the ATHOS-3 trial. However, its effectiveness in non-septic shock remains unclear. The objective of our study was to investigate AT-II hemodynamic response in different subgroups patients treated in mixed intensive care unit (ICU) [1,2].

Methods: Retrospective analysis of 36 patients in shock, refractory to 2 vasoactive drugs, receiving AT-II (2019–2024). The cause of shock in 30 (30/36, 83.3%) patients was sepsis, while others were hemorrhagic shock patients(6/36, 16.7%). Primary observed outcomes were norepinephrine (NE) dose reduction at 4 and 24 h, mean arterial pressure (MAP), and 28-day mortality. Secondary outcome followed changes in SOFA score at 24 h.

Results: Septic shock patients showed significant NE dose reduction (0.48 ± 0.12 to 0.16 ± 0.08 mcg/kg/min; 67% reduction, p < 0.001) and MAP improvement (58 ± 8 to 76 ± 5 mmHg; + 31%, p < 0.001) while nonseptic shock patients showed minimal NE reduction (0.52 ± 0.14 to 0.48 ± 0.13 mcg/kg/min; 8%, p = 0.521) and insignificant MAP change (62 ± 9 to 65 ± 8 mmHg; + 5%, p = 0.412) (Table). At 24 h, survivors showed lower SOFA score and better lactate levels. Despite hemodynamic improvement, overall 28-day mortality stayed high, 80.6% (septic 76.7%, polytrauma 100%). No thrombogenic complications were observed.

Conclusions: In our experience, AT-II showed different efficacy based on shock aetiology. There was a vasopressor-sparing effect and MAP improvement in septic shock patients, while poor results in polytrauma suggest RAAS dysregulation and disease etiology could be critical treatment determinants [2].

P172 Early identification of responders to angiotensin II in distributive shock: a real-world hemodynamic and endocrine-based approach

IC Cappellini, EC Schirru, LC Campagnola, FL Lai, SM Magazzini, VP Pavoni

Azienda USL Toscana Centro, Emergency and Critical Care, Prato, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P172

Introduction: The clinical response to angiotensin II (ATII) in vasoplegic shock remains heterogeneous [1]. Identifying early responders may optimize timing, reduce catecholamine exposure, and improve outcomes. We retrospectively analyzed hemodynamic, endocrine, and clinical data from patients treated with ATII as a third-line vasopressor in distributive shock, integrating renin levels and dynamic norepinephrine (NA) response.

Methods: Consecutive ICU patients receiving ATII after norepinephrine and vasopressin were included. Baseline renin, APACHE II, and SOFA scores were recorded. Hemodynamic parameters and NA dose were monitored at baseline, 3h, and 6h post-ATII initiation. Responders were defined by ≥ 50% NA reduction at 6h ("LCR model"). An AIMRITE-derived early response index [2] (estimated NA reduction at 1h) was modeled to simulate rapid responders. VLAD and Cox analyses were performed for survival adjustment.

Results: Among 18 evaluable patients, 72% were classified as responders. Median baseline renin was higher in responders (292 [111–818] vs 146 [61–777] pg/mL), with earlier NA reduction (median Δ% t0 → t6 = − 25%). Early estimated NA reduction at 1h correlated with 6h response (p < 0.01). Responders showed a higher rate of vasoactive weaning at 6h (67% vs 28%) and lower mortality trend. AIMRITE-modeled early response was associated with improved survival in Cox analysis (HR 0.42, 95%CI 0.18–0.95, p = 0.04).

Conclusions: Early hemodynamic response to ATII (within 1–3h) can identify true responders, supporting the AIMRITE physiological model. Prompt initiation and dynamic reassessment may enhance clinical outcomes and cost-effectiveness in distributive shock management. A multicenter registry is warranted to validate these findings.

P173 Renin-guided initiation of angiotensin II in catecholamine-refractory septic shock: an open-label randomized controlled trial.

M Benlabed1, S Benlabed2, R Gaudy3, V Sobkova4, R Mehyaoui5, A Benhamed6, A Aberkane7, A Metali5

1Lille University, Anesthesiology and Intensive care, Lille, France, 2Free University of Brussels, Brussels, Belgium, 3Lille University, Lille, France, 4University of Prague, Prague, Czech Republic, 5Algiers University, Algiers, Algeria, 6University of Oran, Anesthesiology, Oran, Algeria, 7University of Constantine, Constantine, Algeria

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P173

Introduction: Renin is a key biomarker of renin–angiotensin–aldosterone system failure in septic shock. Elevated renin levels (> 180 pg/mL, median value) have been associated with impaired vascular responsiveness and increased mortality. Elevated plasma renin identifies septic shock patients with impaired angiotensin I–to–II conversion. Retrospective data [1,2] suggest these patients may benefit from exogenous angiotensin II (AngII). We hypothesized that a renin-guided strategy improves hemodynamics and outcomes in patients with cathecolamines refractory septic shock.

These findings support the development of a renin-guided strategy as a personalized approach to the management of refractory septic shock.

P174 Time-weighted mean vasopressor dose and clinical outcomes in a cohort of patients with septic shock

F Quilty1, M Renshaw2, Z Ilesanmi3, M Smith3, J Bannard-Smith3

1Manchester University NHS Foundation Trust, Manchester Royal Infirmary, Manchester, UK, 2University of Manchester, Manchester, UK, 3Manchester University NHS Foundation Trust, Manchester, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P174.

Introduction: Mortality is the most utilised outcome measure in clinical trials testing interventions for septic shock. An internationally agreed core outcome set has yet to be established. Duration of shock may be an outcome of interest, but previous measures of vasopressor dependence are insensitive to other indications for organ support and confounded by mortality. Time-weighted mean dose has been considered as an alternative measure of vasopressor dependence. We conducted a study describing the distribution of these measures in an observational patient cohort.

Methods: We retrospectively analysed a cohort with septic shock (Sepsis-3 criteria) admitted to an ICU in England over a year. Noradrenaline infusion rate (mcg/kg/min) was calculated hourly. Metaraminol was given a nominal value of 0.1. Days on vasopressors (DOV), days alive and vasopressor-free (DAVF) up to 28 days and time-weighted mean vasopressor dose (TMVD) for the hours on vasopressors were calculated. Values were calculated in the same way for survivors and non-survivors. Descriptive statistics were produced for the cohort. ROC curves were produced to assess each measure’s ability to discriminate ICU mortality and survival.

Results: We extracted data for 197 patients. Mean age was 61 (SD 14), 110/197 (56%) were male, the ICU mortality rate was 81/197 (41%). ICU survivors had 5.5 (SD 5.3) DOV, 22.5 (SD 5.3) DAVF and had a TMVD of 0.117 (SD 0.073) mcg/kg/min. Non-survivors had 5.4 (SD 4.8) DOV, 1.8 (SD 4.0) DAVF and had a TMVD of 0.398 (SD 0.551) mcg/kg/min. In discriminating for mortality, AUROC was 0.521 (95% CI 0.442—0.604) for DOV, 0.991 (0.979—0.998) for DAVF and 0.723 (0.642—0.799) for TMVD (Figure).

Conclusions: Time-weighted mean vasopressor dose may be a suitable secondary outcome in septic shock trials, with a lower mean dose considered favourable. Further validation is needed in a prospective cohort.

P175 Safety-aware reinforcement learning for vasopressor weaning in septic ICU patients

L Lijovic1, G Kim2, P Hilders3, J Han2, PWG Elbers3, WY Kim2

1Clinical Hospital Centre Sestre Milosrdnice, Intensive Care, Zagreb, Croatia, 2Chung-Ang University Hospital, Adult Intensive Care, Seoul, South Korea, 3Amsterdam UMC, Adult Intensive Care, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P175

Introduction: Reinforcement learning (RL) can discover treatment policies that optimise long-term outcomes, but in critical care a purely efficacy-driven reward risks unsafe recommendations. We demonstrate on a task of reducing vasopressor support, that an RL agent learns a strategy to wean faster than a clinician, while incorporating safety considerations, such as maintaining stable hemodynamics.

Methods: Using MIMIC-IV, we identified septic ICU patients receiving norepinephrine (NE) and vasopressin (VP) concurrently and realigned each stay to the hour of peak combined NE-equivalent dose. Vitals, laboratory data, organ support and treatments were aggregated hourly for up to 168 h or until death or discharge. State space consisted of 78 features, and observed clinician NE/VP titrations in 5% steps defined a nine-dimensional action space. A tiered reward combined large penalties for death and relapse after liberation, bonuses for ≥ 24 h off both vasopressors and survival at 7 days, modest rewards for favourable trends in lactate, SOFA and costs for vasopressor use, organ support and mean arterial pressure (MAP) outside 65–85 mmHg. A conservative Q-learning (CQL) agent trained on an 80/20 stay-level split with inverse-frequency action weighting; performance was assessed with fitted Q-evaluation (FQE) and compared with a behaviour-cloned clinician policy.

Results: We analysed 110,639 hourly decisions from 1128 patients. CQL achieved FQE-estimated return − 93.9, outperforming the clinician policy (− 67.5; Δ≈26.4). The agent agreed with observed clinician actions in 68.2% of steps (Figure) and most frequently recommended small down-titrations or maintenance of current dose, rarely up-titrating both NE and VP. Case studies showed maintained MAP within the 65–85 mmHg safety band during earlier and more sustained weaning.

Conclusions: CQL agent learned a weaning-oriented vasopressor policy that achieved higher estimated return than a clinician policy while largely respecting predefined hemodynamic safety targets.

Each cell shows the number of hourly decisions for each joint norepinephrine (NE) / vasopressin (VP) action pair in the 9-action discrete space (↓/ ↔ /↑ NE × ↓/ ↔ /↑ VP). (B) A random case study illustrating a septic ICU stay, with top panel plotting MAP and vasopressor doses, and bottom panel compares hourly clinician and agent actions

Each cell shows the number of hourly decisions for each joint norepinephrine (NE) / vasopressin (VP) action pair in the 9-action discrete space (↓/ ↔ /↑ NE × ↓/ ↔ /↑ VP). (B) A random case study illustrating a septic ICU stay, with top panel plotting MAP and vasopressor doses, and bottom panel compares hourly clinician and agent actions

P176 Vasopressor use does not worsen sublingual microcirculation in a porcine model of non-traumatic hemorrhagic shock

MW Kim1, M Magnin2, T Schuhler3, T Victoni4, B Allaouchiche3, G Hoareau5, C Pouzot Nevoret6

1Agro Sup, SIAMU, Marcy L'Etoile, France, 2VetAgro Sup, Unité de Physiologie, Pharmacodynamie et Thérapeutique, Marcy L'Etoile, France, 3Université de Lyon, Hospices Civils de Lyon, Centre Hospitalier Lyon Sud, Réanimation Médicale, Lyon, France, 4VetAgro Sup, Unité de Pharmacie et de Toxicologie, Marcy L'Etoile, France, 5University of Utah, School of Medicine, Nora Eccles Harrison Cardiovascular Research & Training Institute, Salt Lake City, Utah, USA, 6VetAgro Sup, SIAMU, Marcy L'Etoile, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P176

Introduction: Vasopressors can reduce fluid requirements in hemorrhagic shock resuscitation but may cause ischemic organ injury. We measured the effect of standard resuscitation alone or with either noradrenaline or vasopressin on sublingual microcirculation in a porcine hemorrhagic shock model.

Methods: Ethics approval was obtained (no. 1819). 15 female pigs, 60 – 65 kg, were anesthetized, instrumented, then bled in 2 stages (20 mL/kg in 20 min followed by 15 mL/kg in 15 min) to induce hemorrhagic shock. After a 90 min observation period, pigs were resuscitated with lactated Ringer’s and autologous whole blood (group C, n = 6), plus noradrenaline (0.2 µg/kg/min, group N, n = 5), or plus vasopressin (0.6 mU/kg/min, group V, n = 5), targeting MAP = 65 mmHg. Sublingual microcirculation was recorded at baseline, during the observation period then every hour for 6 h during the resuscitation period using sidestream darkfield microscopy (SDF; Microscan, The Netherlands) by 2 trained operators and analysed using proprietary software following published consensus guidelines [1]. SDF variables were analysed using linear mixed-effects models with time and group as fixed effects.

Results: 1 pig in group C died before resuscitation. The percentage of perfused vessels and small vessels decreased significantly during the 90 min observation period post-blood withdrawal (Figure). The heterogeneity index increased significantly for groups C and V over this observation period. For all pigs, there were almost no between-group differences in SDF variables over the resuscitation period aside from isolated periods for the De Backer score and heterogeneity index.

Conclusions: Vasopressor use did not significantly affect sublingual microcirculation compared to standard resuscitation. Investigation of vasopressor impact on vital organs in hemorrhagic shock should be continued.

P177 Impact of vasopressor administration on peripheral perfusion assessed by temperature gradient during severe hemorrhagic shock

M Magnin

Agro Sup, Physiology/Pharmacology, Marcy L'Etoile, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P177

Introduction: The use of vasopressors in hemorrhagic shock remains debated. They may help limit crystalloids and transfusion needs, but excessive vasoconstriction could impair tissue perfusion. We therefore assessed the impact of vasopressin and norepinephrine on peripheral perfusion, measured by temperature gradients, in a porcine model of severe hemorrhagic shock.

Methods: Hemorrhagic shock was induced by removing 35 mL/kg of blood over 1 h (T1) in 60- 65 kg pigs. After 1.5 h of untreated hypotension (T2), the control (C) group (n = 5) received boluses of whole blood and Ringer’s lactate. The vasopressin (V) group (n = 5) received vasopressin at 0.6 mU/kg/min, and the norepinephrine (N) group (n = 5) received 0.2 µg/kg/min, in addition to whole blood and Ringer’s lactate. Resuscitation targeted a mean arterial pressure of 65 mmHg, with a pre-specified plasma hemoglobin-based algorithm. The tarsus–abdomen temperature gradient (GTmean) was measured every 30 min using a thermal camera, calculated as mean abdominal temperature minus mean tarsal temperature. ΔGTmean was defined as the difference between each time point and the individual T2 value (before resuscitation). Linear mixed models were used for analysis.

Results: One pig in group C died before resuscitation. GTmean increased significantly before resuscitation (+ 3.8 °C, CI [2.9; 4.7]). GTmean profiles showed marked inter-individual variability (Figure, panel A). ΔGTmean decreased over time in the C and V groups (–0.0093 °C/min, CI [–0.0127; –0.0057] and –0.0078 °C/min, CI [–0.0109; –0.0046]), with no significant change in the N group (0.0000 °C/min, CI [–0.0033; 0.0032]) (Figure, panel B).

Conclusions: Vasopressin and control animals showed improving peripheral perfusion, whereas norepinephrine failed to reduce the temperature gradient during resuscitation.

P178 Elevated neutrophil-to-lymphocyte ratio as a biomarker of systemic stress complementing hemodynamic determinants of capillary refill time: a physiologic analysis in critical illness

C Michailides1, B Khaled Mohamed Sallam1, D Velissaris2, R Attou1, C Pierrakos1, MT Talpos1

1Brugmann University Hospital, Department of Intensive Care, Brussels, Belgium, 2University Hospital of Patras, Department of Internal Medicine, Patras, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P178.

Introduction: The interplay between macro-hemodynamic variables and catecholaminergic stress may contribute to CRT prolongation. The neutrophil-to-lymphocyte ratio (NLR) is a simple biomarker of stress and inflammation in critical illness. We conducted a physiological study to assess whether systemic stress (high NLR) influences CRT prolongation across distinct macro-hemodynamic states.

Methods: This prospective physiological study enrolled critically ill adults, for whom fingertip CRT was measured under controlled hemodynamic conditions and analyzed offline from standardized video recordings. Cardiac index (CI) and mean arterial pressure (MAP) were measured concomitantly using transthoracic echocardiography (cut-off 3 L/min/m2) and an intra-arterial catheter (cut-off 70 mmHg). Routine early-morning blood samples provided NLR values (cut-off ≥ 7.2). Macro-hemodynamic phenotypes were defined by the four combinations of low/high CI and MAP. The primary endpoint was the Bayesian posterior probability of prolonged CRT (> 3 s) across NLR strata within each phenotype.

Results: 84 patients were evaluated, of whom 36 (43%) exhibited CRT ≥ 3 s. Compared with those with normal CRT, these patients showed lower CI (3.2 [2.5–3.6] vs 3.3 [2.8–4.1] L/min/m2; SMD 0.26), lower MAP (76 [68–85] vs 86 [72–95] mmHg; SMD 0.41), and higher NLR (10.1 [4.1–17.5] vs 7.1 [4.4–11.3]; SMD 0.21). Bayesian modeling showed that the highest posterior probabilities of CRT > 3 s occurred in the Low CI/Low MAP phenotype (0.77–0.97). The intermediate phenotypes showed variable and overlapping probabilities, whereas the High CI/High MAP group had the lowest and most stable estimates. Within each hemodynamic phenotype, a high NLR produced an upward shifts in posterior probability (Figure).

Conclusions: CRT prolongation was predominantly linked to macro-hemodynamic impairment, whereas systemic stress assessed by NLR had a modest yet consistent influence across hemodynamic phenotypes.

P179 How stable is capillary refill time? Assessing repeatability in critically ill patients

B Khaled Mohamed Sallam1, MT Talpos1, C Michailides1, D Velissaris2, R Attou1, C Pierrakos1

1Brugmann University Hospital, Department of Intensive Care, Brussels, Belgium, 2University Hospital of Patras, Department of Internal Medicine, Patras, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P179

Introduction: Capillary refill time (CRT) is a simple, bedside indicator of peripheral perfusion used to identify hypoperfusion in critically ill patients. Recently, CRT has been suggested as a monitoring tool to assess the effects of interventions aimed at enhancing perfusion. However, its natural variability and consistency in real-world conditions remain poorly established, which limits confidence in its use for continuous monitoring.

Methods: This prospective observational study measured critically ill patients’ fingertip CRT twice, 20 min apart, with no interventions in between. Each measurement was recorded on video and analyzed offline using a standardized digital workflow. The primary endpoint was the agreement between the first and second CRT readings, as evaluated using the two-way random-effects, absolute-agreement, single-measurement intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC[A,1]).

Results: 94 critically ill patients were enrolled; CRT could not be analyzed in 13 (14%) due to technical reasons. No significant difference was observed between baseline (2.7 [1.9–4.2] seconds) and control (3.2 [1.9–4.9] seconds, p = 0.08). Test–retest reliability was good (ICC[A,1] = 0.77; 95% CI, 0.68–0.84; p < 0.001; Figure), indicating consistent repeatability with limited residual variability, likely due to technical or physiological factors. The paired bootstrap-derived least significant change (LSC%) between repeated CRT measurements was 63% (95% CI 52–74%).

Conclusions: Measuring fingertip CRT at different times under pragmatic ICU conditions showed good reproducibility, supporting its use as a bedside monitoring tool. However, the notable variability observed in several cases suggests that significant changes only should be considered clinically meaningful when CRT is used to monitor patients in critical care.

Scatter plot of repeated CRT values. Dashed red line: identity line

P180 Capillary refill time response to norepinephrine and vasopressin: benefit beyond cardiac output

RC Castro1, PM Mercado2, MA Amthauer1, VO Oviedo1, ED Valenzuela1

1Pontificia Universidad Catolica de Chile, Intensive Care Medicine, Santiago Centro, Chile, 2Clinica Alemana, Intensive Care Unit, Santiago, Chile

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P180

Introduction: Vasopressin (AVP) is often used cautiously as a second-line vasopressor because its predominantly vasoconstrictive action may reduce cardiac output (CO) despite increasing mean arterial pressure (MAP) [1]. Whether this hemodynamic profile translates into impaired tissue perfusion remains unclear. We aimed to compare the effects of norepinephrine (NE) and AVP on capillary refill time (CRT) and to relate these changes to CO and ventriculo–arterial (V–A) coupling during a standardized vasopressor test.

Methods: Thirty-two vasodilatory shock patients on NE underwent a vasopressor test (VPT) with either NE (n = 20) or AVP (n = 12) to increase MAP to 85 mmHg to improve perfusion. CRT, CO, heart rate, and indices of V–A coupling were assessed at baseline and after VPT (Figure). In the AVP group, patients were prespecified as afterload-sensitive or afterload-tolerant according to CO changes.

Results: In patients with NE only, V–A coupling and CO remained stable (CO ↑ in ~ 30%), with no group-level CRT change. AVP revealed two phenotypes: a) Afterload-sensitive (n = 4): ↑ Ea/Ees, ↓ CO, no CRT improvement; these patients had higher baseline CVP & mean systemic filling pressure (Pmsf). b) Afterload-tolerant (n = 8) showed stable CO and V–A coupling, with significant CRT shortening; these patients had lower baseline CVP & Pmsf, suggesting flow-independent perfusion improvement probably via restored vascular waterfall [2].

Conclusions: NE increased MAP without disturbing V–A coupling, preserving CO in a majority of patients with limited impact on CRT. AVP did not uniformly impair perfusion. When CO was preserved and CVP was low, CRT improved, likely reflecting microcirculatory benefit via improved pressure gradients (vascular waterfall) rather than increased flow. CRT identified afterload-tolerant vs afterload-sensitive phenotypes and may guide individualized AVP indication and vasopressor selection.

Acknowledgements: FONDECYT 1241463, 1250200

P181 Early transport vs. on-scene resuscitation for refractory shockable rhythms: a nationwide time-dependent propensity matched study

SJ Ryu, YJ Seong, EJ Jung, SH Lee, YS Cho

Chonnam National University Hospital, Emergency Medicine, Gwangju, South Korea

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P181

Introduction: For OHCA patients who remain in shockable rhythms after the first defibrillation, early transport may theoretically expedite access to hospital-based therapies. However, its benefit is uncertain in EMS systems without full ACLS capabilities. This study evaluated whether intra-arrest transport improves survival in refractory shockable OHCA where anti-arrhythmics and advanced defibrillation strategies are unavailable.

Methods: Data were derived from a nationwide OHCA registry between January 2015 and December 2022. Adult, non-traumatic OHCA patients with initial shockable rhythms who did not convert following the first defibrillation were included. Intra-arrest transport was evaluated using time-dependent propensity score matching to minimize resuscitation time bias. The primary endpoint was favorable neurological recovery at discharge. Risk ratios (RRs) with 95% confidence intervals (CIs) were estimated, and stratified assessments were performed by matched intervals after first defibrillation.

Results: Among 10,246 eligible patients, 8,131 received intra-arrest transport. After 1:1 time-dependent matching, 2,332 patients remained in each comparison arm. Neurological recovery rates were similar between the intra-arrest transport and on-scene resuscitation groups (11.7% vs. 11.5%; RR 0.97; 95% CI 0.91–1.07). However, transport initiated within 5 min after the first defibrillation was associated with worse neurological outcomes (RR 0.86; 95% CI 0.77–0.97).

Conclusions: In EMS systems lacking advanced prehospital interventions, intra-arrest transport does not improve neurological outcomes for OHCA patients who remain in refractory shockable rhythms despite initial defibrillation. Very early transport may be harmful, highlighting the importance of maintaining high-quality on-scene resuscitation before transfer. System-level strategies that strengthen both prehospital care and coordinated hospital management warrant further investigation.

P182 Combined insomnia and depression as a risk factor for OHCA: evidence from a multicenter case–control study

EJ Jung, YJ Seong, SH Lee, SJ Ryu, YS Cho

Chonnam National University Hospital, Emergency Medicine, Gwangju, South Korea

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P182

Introduction: Insomnia and depression are well-recognized contributors to chronic disease and cardiovascular morbidity. However, their joint influence on the risk of out-of-hospital cardiac arrest (OHCA) has not been fully elucidated. This study examined whether insomnia is associated with OHCA incidence and whether its effect differs according to the presence of depression.

Methods: A prospective, multicenter case–control design was applied using data from the Phase II Cardiac Arrest Pursuit Trial with Unique Registration and Epidemiology Surveillance (CAPTURES-II) project, which included OHCA patients and matched community controls in Korea. The primary exposure was a documented history of insomnia. Conditional logistic regression models were used to evaluate OHCA risk associated with insomnia, and interaction analyses were performed to determine whether depression modified this relationship. Additionally, subgroup analyses were conducted among individuals with insomnia based on their medication status.

Results: A history of insomnia alone was not associated with an increased risk of OHCA (adjusted odds ratio [AOR] 0.95; 95% CI 0.64–1.40). However, a significant interaction was observed in younger participants: those with both insomnia and depression demonstrated a markedly elevated risk of OHCA (AOR 3.65; 95% CI 1.29–10.33), whereas patients with insomnia without depression did not show increased risk (AOR 0.84; 95% CI 0.59–1.17). In subgroup analyses, depression increased OHCA risk only among patients with insomnia who were not taking sleep-related medications (AOR 3.66; 95% CI 1.15–11.66).

Conclusions: Comorbid insomnia and depression significantly heighten the risk of OHCA in younger individuals, while insomnia alone does not. The elevated risk was confined to patients not receiving pharmacologic treatment for insomnia, suggesting that early recognition and adequate management of sleep disorders may help reduce the likelihood of sudden cardiac arrest.

P185 Hemoperitoneum and cardiac arrest: diagnostic challenges following prolonged resuscitation

AR Branco, P Coelho, I Lima Freitas, L Costeira, A Colmente, R Passos, R Corga Silva

ULSAM, SMI, Viana do Castelo, Portugal

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P185

Introduction: Managing a patient during and after cardiac arrest (CA) presents significant challenges, as a myriad of factors contributes to the overall patient outcome. Post-resuscitation management focuses on identifying and managing multiorgan dysfunction resulting from the ischemic events and/or the cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR) maneuvers. Although abdominal injuries in cardiac arrest are rare, abdominal trauma related to resuscitation maneuvers is recognized as a potential complication needed to be considered in patients with rapidly worsening cardiovascular dysfunction [1].

Methods: We present two cases of CA in which identification of hemoperitoneum was essential for optimizing outcomes.

A) 46 y.o. woman suffered a non-traumatic CA in which rhythm remained shockable, supporting a primary dysrhythmic etiology. Early whole-body computed tomography revealed hemoperitoneum. With progressive cardiovascular decline, an urgent laparotomy identified and controlled hepatic lacerations, resulting in significant cardiovascular improvement.

B) 50 y.o. woman had a non-traumatic CA with persistent pulseless electrical activity. Fibrinolytic therapy for suspected pulmonary embolism was given during 32 min of CPR, restoring circulation. She then developed hemorrhagic shock and imaging showed liver and spleen lacerations with active bleeding. Urgent surgery controlled the hemorrhage and improved her outcome.

Results: Discerning whether the hemoperitoneum resulted from resuscitation efforts or constituted the primary cause of arrest due to hemorrhagic shock, remains challenging.

Conclusions: Patient prognosis following CA is influenced by multiple factors. Resuscitation-related injuries are an accepted risk in emergency settings, prolonged CPR requires active evaluation for potential complications. The two cases are particularly relevant as they show how it’s challenging to determine whether the hemorrhage was the precipitating cause of the arrest or a consequence of CPR.

Acknowledgement: Written consent to publish was received from the patients.

P186 Effects of inspiratory pressures in chest compression synchronized ventilation with arterial blood gases and lung trauma in experimental cardiac arrest

R Östman1, J Kopra1, A Heino2, L Mehtonen3, M Laitinen4, E Litonius5, O Arvola5, JA Heinonen6, MB Skrifvars1, PT Pekkarinen5

1University of Helsinki and Helsinki University Hospital, Department of Emergency Care and Services, Helsinki, Finland, 2University of Helsinki and Helsinki University Hospital, Helsinki, Finland, 3University of Latvia, Riga, Latvia, 4VetCT Teleconsulting – Teleradiology Small Animal Team, Helsinki, Finland, 5University of Helsinki and Helsinki University Hospital, Division of Anaesthesiology, Department of Anaesthesiology, Intensive Care and Pain Medicine, Helsinki, Finland, 6PäijätHäme Central Hospital, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, and Centre for Prehospital Emergency Care and Emergency Medicine, Lahti, Finland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P186

Introduction: Chest compression synchronized ventilation (CCSV) is a novel ventilation strategy, which may improve arterial oxygenation during cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR). Our previous study raised a safety concern due to more pneumothoraces in the CCSV group compared to manual asynchronous ventilation. To study this further, we performed a new study including an additional group with lower inspiratory pressure in CCSV.

Methods: We randomized 36 pigs into 3 groups: CCSV with 40 mbar or 60 mbar inspiratory pressure (CCSV40, CCSV60) and manual asynchronous ventilation (MAV). A MEDUMAT Standard2 ventilator was used to provide CCSV. Chest compressions were performed with a Lucas® 2 device. Arterial samples were drawn every 5 min and monitoring was recorded continuously. Post-mortem chest CT scans were used to assess lung trauma.

In the CCSV60 group PaO2 = 407.4 (291.1 – 498.3) mmHg, PaCO2 = 23.4 (17.5 – 28.1) mmHg and pH = 7.49 (7.44 – 7.52). In the manual ventilation group PaO2 = 120.9 (99.5 – 143.3) mmHg, PaCO2 = 46.6 (41.0 – 50.8) mmHg and pH = 7.26 (7.20 – 7.36). The results showed higher oxygenation (p = 0.0002), lower CO2 (p = 0.0005) and higher pH (p = 0.0486) in the CCSV60 group when compared with a linear mixed model. The number of pneumothoraxes were (none/mild/moderate/severe) MAV = 3/7/2/0, CCSV40 = 5/0/2/5, CCSV60 = 3/0/3/6. Difference in severity was analyzed with Fisher’s exact test (p = 0.0013).

Conclusions: This study shows that PO2, CO2 and pH were superior with CCSV60, CCSV40 did not show benefits compared to MAV. The severity of pneumothorax was greater with increasing inspiratory pressures.

P188 Keeping cool? A reality check on post-ROSC TTM

C Thornton, AR Fennessy, J Bates

University Hospital Galway, Department of Intensive Care, Galway, Ireland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P188

Introduction: Targeted temperature management (TTM) is a core recommendation by the European Resuscitation Council (ERC) for post-return of spontaneous circulation (ROSC) care. ERC guidelines (2021) advise maintaining a core temperature between 32–36 °C for at least 24 h and < 37.7 °C for 72 h post ROSC. Continuous, accurate, and consistent temperature measurement is essential to achieve these recommendations. This audit evaluated adherence to TTM temperature targets, duration and frequency of monitoring, and methods used in patients who suffered an out-of-hospital cardiac arrest (OOHCA) who survived to ICU admission.

Methods: A retrospective audit of temperature management in all patients surviving to ICU admission in University Hospital Galway post OOHCA with ROSC during 2024 was conducted. Data collated included temperature readings, methods of measurement, total monitoring duration, and ICU length of stay. Compliance with ERC 2021 guidelines was assessed.

Results: Twenty-two patients were included. No patient had reliable documentation confirming maintenance of a target temperature of 32–36 °C for ≥ 24 h (Figure). Two patients appeared compliant, although documentation was incomplete. Eleven patients did not exceed 37.7 °C during their monitoring period; however, no patient had monitoring beyond 63 h. Median monitoring duration was 48 h (IQR 33–49.5). No patient received hourly or consistently timed measurements. Data quality was limited by variability in both frequency and modality of measurement.

Conclusions: Delivery and documentation of TTM in this cohort were suboptimal. Key deficiencies included inadequate monitoring duration, lack of sustained temperature control within therapeutic targets, inconsistent measurement frequency, and non-standardised techniques. These findings support the introduction of a new departmental protocol for TTM post-ROSC to include core temperature monitoring, unified modality per patient, and structured 72-h monitoring protocols.

P189 Timing of hypothermic temperature control does not affect neurological outcomes after cardiac arrest: a systematic review and meta-analysis

K Csokekabai1, Z Molnár2, L Zubek2, P Hegyi3, G Nagy4, EH Kovács1, Z Sipos1, D Laczkó5, C Turan2, K Tánczos2

1Semmelweis University, Centre for Translational Medicine, Budapest, Hungary, 2Semmelweis University, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Therapy, Budapest, Hungary, 3Semmelweis University, Institute of Pancreatic Diseases, Budapest, Hungary, 4Semmelweis University, Department of Emergency Medicine, Budapest, Hungary, 5Semmelweis University, Department of Interventional Radiology, Városmajori Heart and Vascular Centre, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P189

Introduction: Out-of-hospital cardiac arrest is associated with a low rate of favourable neurological recovery, estimated at 8%. Lowering the cerebral metabolic rate with hypothermia is hypothesized to improve this outcome. Since in-hospital temperature control is recommended to start as early as possible, we investigated whether initiating earlier hypothermic temperature control in the prehospital setting could further enhance neurological recovery.

Methods: A pre-registered systematic search (PROSPERO: CRD42024601051) was run in PubMed, Embase and Cochrane Central on November 3, 2024, to identify randomised controlled trials that compare prehospital vs. in-hospital initiation of hypothermic temperature control of out-of-hospital cardiac arrest patients. The Cochrane Handbook was followed for data extraction and synthesis. The RoB 2 tool was used for risk of bias assessment. The meta-analyses were conducted using a random-effects model.

Results: A total of 7117 articles were screened, and 7 studies were selected for the analysis containing the data of 3288 patients. The intervention group achieved the target temperature of 33 °C significantly earlier (MD: -97.27 min; 95% CI: -139.46 to -55.08; p = 0.010). In the intervention group, no significant difference was observed in the odds for favourable neurology (OR: 0.98; 95% CI: 0.80–1.22, p = 0.855) (Figure). The odds for recurrent cardiac arrest and pulmonary oedema were significantly higher in the intervention group (OR: 1.33; 95% CI: 1.20–1.47; p = 0.009 and OR: 1.66; 95% CI: 1.24–2.23; p = 0.001).

Conclusions: Despite reaching the target temperature significantly earlier, prehospital initiation of hypothermic temperature control did not improve favourable neurological recovery compared to in-hospital initiation. Furthermore, early initiation also significantly increased the odds for recurrent cardiac arrest and pulmonary oedema. Therefore, these findings do not support the implementation of prehospital hypothermic temperature control in out-of-hospital cardiac arrest.

P190 Application of a frontal electroencephalogram for rapid neurological prognostication by ICU nurses in cardiac arrest patients (FRONT study); an implementation study

I Van de Pol1, AJ Court2, J Van Helden1, A Seeber2, E Scholten1, V Lagerberg3, HS Moeniralam1, L Schoonhoven4, D Stalpers4, L Wieske2

1St. Antonius Ziekenhuis, Intensive Care Unit, Nieuwegein, Netherlands, 2St. Antonius Ziekenhuis, Clinical Neurophysiology, Nieuwegein, Netherlands, 3St. Antonius Ziekenhuis, Medical Physics and Instrumentation, Nieuwegein, Netherlands, 4UMCU, General Practice Nursing Science, Utrecht, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P190

Introduction: Continuous electroencephalography (cEEG) is a valuable tool for neurological prognostication after cardiac arrest. However, limited availability of EEG technicians and time-consuming electrode placement delay early registration. Self-adhesive electrodes in a 6-electrode frontal (fEEG) configuration have been shown to be a reliable alternative and could allow ICU nurses to start registrations early. This study investigated implementation of fEEG applied by nurses within three hours after ICU admission in cardiac arrest patients.

Methods: The FRONT study followed a type 3 hybrid design. Nurses were trained to start an fEEG through a theoretical and practical session and were supported by a Step-by-Step interactive guide. Knowledge, attitude, and practice (KAP) surveys were completed by nurses after the training and after the application of each fEEG. Effectiveness was measured prospectively in 60 patients by assessing whether fEEG was successfully applied.

Results: The response rate was 46% (49/107) after training and 68% (41/60) post-application. Most nurses (84%) indicated that they had sufficient knowledge about fEEG electrodes placement and monitoring. The interactive guide was frequently (98%) used during fEEG initiation; median application time was 20 min [IQR: 15–25]. All nurses found early EEG monitoring to be meaningful; perception improved since before implementation (3.98 + -0.78 vs 4.54 + -0.50, p < 0.001). An fEEG was applied in 88% of patients (53/60), including 73% (44/60) within 3 h. Correct fEEG electrode placement was confirmed by an EEG technician in all patients.

Conclusions: Nurses are able to start fEEG registrations in nearly all patients within three hours after ICU admission, using a short and multifaceted training program in combination with an online Step-by-Step interactive guide. Addressing the value of early EEG monitoring in the training program may help to prevent resistance to this task by ICU nurses. Further research is needed on the interpretability of the fEEG.

P191 Optimising EEG acquisition for neuroprognostication after cardiac arrest: a systematic review

A Binuraj, O Reeves-Chen, M De Silva-Morgan, C Chacko, T Veenith

New Cross Hospital, Intensive Care, Wolverhampton, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P191

Introduction: Accurate neuroprognostication after cardiac arrest requires EEG recordings that are not limited by sedation, artefact, or technical factors. This systematic review assessed approaches that improve EEG quality to support reliable prognostic interpretation [1].

Methods: A structured literature search identified studies describing technical and clinical strategies to optimise EEG acquisition. Evidence relating to sedation washout, artefact reduction, electrode placement, reactivity testing, seizure control, and recording timing was synthesised. Relevant guidelines were included for alignment with current prognostication frameworks [2].

Results: Key practices consistently improved EEG interpretability. Sedation washout, particularly of propofol and long-acting opioids, reduced drug-induced suppression that may mimic malignant patterns [3]. Good electrode placement and low impedance improved background assessment. Early EEG after ROSC aided evaluation of continuity. Muscle artefact frequently obscured cortical activity; short-acting neuromuscular blockers improved signal clarity when needed. Standardised stimulation better identified EEG reactivity, while continuous EEG allowed detection of seizures and evolving abnormalities. Integrating EEG findings with clinical examination and imaging strengthened overall prognostic confidence (Figure).

Conclusions: Structured management of sedation, artefact, electrode technique and stimulation protocols enhances EEG quality after cardiac arrest. These measures support clearer pattern classification and more reliable multimodal neuroprognostication.

P192 Achieving optimal EEG quality for neuroprognostication after cardiac arrest: a structured confounder-elimination protocol

O Reeve-Chen, M De Silva-Morgan, A Binuraj, T Veenith, C Chacko

The Royal Wolverhampton NHS Trust, Intensive Care, Wolverhampton, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P192

Introduction: Electroencephalography (EEG) is key in multimodal neuroprognostication in comatose survivors of cardiac arrest, but accuracy is limited by heterogeneity in EEG timing, inconsistent terminology, and confounders such as sedation or artefact. Standardised ACNS criteria and improved acquisition methods aim to enhance reliability. This review synthesises current evidence to evaluate EEG performance, confounders, and optimal integration into prognostic pathways [1].

Methods: A qualitative synthesis of studies from 2015–2026 was performed, assessing prognostic accuracy of malignant, benign, and reactive EEG patterns, alongside the influence of targeted temperature management, sedation, aetiology, and artefact-limiting strategies on interpretability [1].

Results: Highly malignant EEG patterns—suppression and burst-suppression ≥ 24 h post-ROSC—retain very high specificity (93–100%) but low sensitivity (~ 50%), making them useful “rule-in” markers rather than standalone predictors. Benign patterns, especially early continuous and reactive backgrounds, strongly indicate favourable outcome [3]. Reactivity adds further prognostic value. Sedation and hypothermia can alter appearance but rarely reduce specificity. Artefact remains a major source of error, with neuromuscular blockade improving clarity when EMG interference dominates. Malignant-pattern specificity decreases in non-anoxic coma, underscoring the importance of clinical context (Figure).

Conclusions: EEG provides critical insight into hypoxic–ischaemic brain injury and is a powerful prognostic tool when acquired early, interpreted with ACNS criteria, and integrated within guideline-mandated multimodal frameworks. Optimisation of EEG quality—via sedation washout protocols, standardised reactivity testing, and artefact reduction—substantially enhances diagnostic confidence. Emerging quantitative EEG and machine-learning approaches offer promising avenues to reduce the persistent “indeterminate” prognostic gap.

P194 Evaluating the safety of valproic acid in the traumatic brain injury population

AE Johnson, RM Nelson, TS Lam, JT Jancik

Hennepin Healthcare, Pharmacy, Minneapolis, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P194

Introduction: A 1999 study by Temkin et al. suggested a trend towards higher mortality in patients with traumatic brain injuries (TBI) on prophylactic valproic acid (VPA) compared to phenytoin [1]. Because of this incidental finding, subsequent guidelines discouraged VPA use for seizure prophylaxis in TBI patients [2]. There is currently no guidance for the use of VPA for agitation management in the TBI population. This study aimed to evaluate the safety of VPA in TBI patients when used for agitation.

Methods: This single-center, retrospective study compared mortality at up to 2 years between TBI patients who received at least 48 h of VPA between 1/1/2019 and 22/10/2023 to a historical control of TBI patients admitted between 1/1/2015 and 31/12/2018. Secondary outcomes in the VPA group included incidence of hepatotoxicity, hyperammonemia, thrombocytopenia, and free or total VPA levels above the upper limit of normal.

Results: The 73 encounters in the treatment group were matched by Injury Severity Score, age, and sex to 73 control encounters. A Kaplan–Meier survival analysis showed mortality rates at up to 2 years were not significantly different between VPA treatment and control groups (p = 0.836). The median starting and maximum dose of VPA was 20.2 (IQR 15.8–25.9) and 27.7 (IQR 23.3–34.2) mg/kg/day, respectively. The incidences of VPA-related safety outcomes can be found in the Figure.

Conclusions: Patients with TBIs who were initiated on VPA for agitation management had similar mortality rates at up to 2 years compared to a historical control. Valproic acid was generally well tolerated.

P195 Association between hyperosmolar therapy and ICU mortality in patients with moderate to severe traumatic brain injury without pupillary abnormality: a propensity score-matched cohort study from the TraumaBase® registry

M Lamamri1, JD Moyer2, T Geeraerts3, T Botrel4, M Werner5, M Leone6, E Weiss1, A Codorniu1, Traumabase Group7

1Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris Hôpital Beaujon, Réanimation Chirurgicale, Clichy, France, 2CHU Caen, Réanimation Chirurgicale, Caen, France, 3CHU Toulouse, Réanimation Chirurgicale, Toulouse, France, 4Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris Hôpital Pitié, Réanimation Chirurgicale, Paris, France, 5Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris Hôpital Kremlin-Bicètre, Réanimation Chirurgicale, Paris, France, 6Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Marseille APHM Nord, Réanimation Chirurgicale, Marseille, France, 7Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris Hôpital Beaujon, Clichy, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P195

Introduction: Evidence remains scarce regarding the clinical benefit of hyperosmolar therapy and its effect on outcomes in TBI patients without pupillary abnormality during the prehospital phase [1,2]. The aim of this study is to assess the association between hyperosmolar therapy and ICU mortality in TBI patients without pupillary abnormality.

Methods: We conducted a multicentric retrospective study using data from a national registry across 20 Level 1 Trauma Centers from 2015 to 2025 in France. We performed propensity score matching on selected variables [i.e. age, sex, initial Glasgow, center, Abbreviated Injury Scale head, Simplified Acute Physiology Score II, prehospital systolic blood pressure, CT before hospital admission] with a ratio of 1:2. The primary outcome was ICU mortality. Hemorrhagic shock was defined as receiving at least 4 packed red blood cells transfusions within the first 6 h of management or requiring immediate hemostatic surgery or interventional radiology before whole-body computed tomography scan.

Results: We included 858 patients: after matching, no significant differences were observed between the two groups regarding baseline demographic characteristics and the mechanism of TBI (Table). No significant difference was observed in the need of neurosurgery within 24 h between hyperosmolar therapy group (20%, n = 56) and control group (15.2%, n = 85; p = 0.079). ICU mortality was higher in hyperosmolar therapy group (28.6%, n = 79) compared to control group (22.2%, n = 124; p = 0.049).

Conclusions: In this propensity score-matched cohort, the administration of hyperosmolar therapy during the prehospital phase was associated with a higher ICU mortality in TBI patients without pupillary abnormality.

P196 A protocol for studying consciousness in the intensive care unit

A Addis1, V D´Ambrosio2, P Mangili1, L Stanzani3, P Spiandorello1, M Baggiani1, A Guglielmi4, G Citerio2

1IRCCS Fondazione San Gerardo dei Tintori, Neurointensive Care Unit, Monza, Italy, 2University of MilanoBicocca, School of Medicine, Milan, Italy, 3IRCCS Fondazione San Gerardo dei Tintori, Neurology, Monza, Italy, 4Alma Mater Ticinensis Foundation, Progetto Professionalità, Pavia, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P196

Introduction: Consciousness is thought to depend on differentiation of brain activity and integration of distributed neural populations. EEG-based protocols that quantify signal complexity and large-scale connectivity are being developed with high-density recordings. In the ICU, high-density EEG is rarely available, yet identifying markers of emerging consciousness could improve prognostication and decisions about treatment intensity.

Methods: We aimed to develop an EEG analysis protocol to characterize thalamo-cortical dynamics and cortico-cortical connectivity in subacute disorders of consciousness (DoC) patients at the bedside in the NeuroICU.

Results: Patients with subacute DoC admitted to the NeuroICU underwent EEG recording using a reduced longitudinal montage. Recordings were exported to EDF, visually inspected to remove major artifacts, and contiguous epochs of at least 5 min were manually selected. Data were cleaned with an ASR-ICA pipeline and then processed in Python for quantitative analysis. We assessed thalamo-cortical dynamics by computing power spectra with a multitaper method and visually classifying them according to the ABCD model [1]. Cortico-cortical interactions were evaluated by computing two complexity metrics (Kolmogorov and Lempel–Ziv), permutation entropy in the alpha and theta bands, sample entropy, and three connectivity metrics: Granger causality and weighted Symbolic Mutual Information for unidirectional and bidirectional connectivity, and the Phase Lag Index.

Conclusions: We present a practical, bedside-compatible EEG protocol that integrates ABCD spectral classification with complementary complexity and connectivity measures in patients with subacute disorders of consciousness (Figure). We plan to implement this framework in a prospective data collection protocol to enable future validation studies linking EEG markers to clinical trajectories and outcomes.

P197 Investigation into the role and mechanism of heparanase inhibition in sepsis-associated blood–brain barrier damage

HS Zhang, YX Li, XX Wang, L Zhang

Xiangya Hospital of Central South University, Changsha, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P197

Introduction: Blood–brain barrier (BBB) injury is a primary pathological event in sepsis-associated encephalopathy (SAE). Heparanase (HPSE), the key enzyme degrading heparan sulfate (HS) proteoglycans in the endothelial glycocalyx, is upregulated early in sepsis and exacerbates BBB disruption. This study evaluated whether pharmacological or genetic inhibition of HPSE mitigates SAE by protecting BBB integrity.

Methods: LPS-induced BBB injury models in HUVECs and P-BMECs (cellular) and endotoxemia/CLP mice (animal). Interventions: HPSE inhibitors (heparin, SDX, fucoidan, OGT2115) and HPSE-/- knockout. Assessments: functional BBB damage (live imaging, Evans blue, Transwell); structural damage (CCK-8, LDH, WB/IF); SAE severity (survival, behavior, Nissl staining). Mechanism: pyroptosis agonists and AAV-X1.1-mediated HPSE restoration in BMECs for rescue.

Results: In cellular models and animal models, LPS stimulation significantly increased HPSE expression and HS degradation. HPSE inhibitors could effectively reduced BBB hyperpermeability. Structurally, inhibitors preserved cell viability, reduced LDH release, and maintained tight junction proteins (Claudin-5, ZO-1). In vivo, all inhibitors improved survival post-endotoxemia. Behavioral tests revealed enhanced performance with heparin and fucoidan yielding the most consistent benefits. HPSE knockout (HPSE-/-) mice and derived BMECs confirmed these protective effects. Mechanistically, HPSE inhibition reduced pyroptosis-related proteins (Caspase-4/11, N-GSDMD) in endothelial cells. Pyroptosis agonists reversed BBB protection, indicating HPSE acts via promoting pyroptosis. Endothelial-specific HPSE restoration in HPSE-/- mice partially reinstated BBB dysfunction and pyroptosis, identifying BMECs as a critical source of pathogenic HPSE.

Conclusions: HPSE inhibition alleviates SAE by preserving BBB integrity, largely through suppressing pyroptosis in BMECs, highlighting HPSE as a promising therapeutic target.

P199 Beyond recanalization: early organ-support profile and outcomes after mechanical thrombectomy for proximal ischemic stroke in Bogotá, Colombia

MC Niño de Mejia1, D Cohen1, MF Parada1, A Romero1, JA Mejia2, C Martinez3

1Hospital Universitario Fundacion Santa Fe de Bogota, Anestesiologia, Bogotá, Colombia, 2Hospital Universitario Fundacion Santa Fe de Bogota, Neuroradiologia Intervencionista, Bogotá, Colombia, 3Hospital Universitario Fundacion Santa Fe de Bogota, Neurologia, Bogotá, Colombia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P199

Introduction: Mechanical thrombectomy (MT) achieves high recanalization rates in proximal ischemic stroke (PIS), but many critically ill patients treated in the ICU remain dead or severely disabled. We evaluated whether simple periprocedural variables define a high-risk profile after MT.

Methods: Retrospective single-centre cohort of adults undergoing MT for PIS (2019–2023) in a Joint Commission International–accredited stroke centre in Bogotá Colombia. We collected demographics, NIHSS, vessel territory, TICI, number of passes, anaesthetic technique, haemodynamic monitoring, vasopressor use and postoperative mechanical ventilation (MV). Outcomes were 90-day modified Rankin Scale (mRS), disability (mRS 0–2, 3–5, 6) and mortality.

Results: Fifty-eight patients were included (59% male, mean age 66 years, median NIHSS 16.5); 90% had anterior circulation stroke and 86% achieved TICI 2b–3. Despite this, 53% died by day 90 and only 26% achieved mRS 0–2. Higher baseline NIHSS was associated with worse 90-day mRS (p = 0.001). A greater number of device passes was associated with both disability and mortality (p ≤ 0.03). Postoperative MV was strongly associated with disability and death (p = 0.001), while vasopressor support was associated with greater disability (p = 0.002) but not mortality. No signal was observed for anaesthetic technique or neuromonitoring (BIS, NIRS) on outcome (Table).

Conclusions: In this MT cohort, a very good angiographic reperfusion did not translate into survival or functional independence. A simple early profile combining severe stroke (high NIHSS), difficult thrombectomy (multiple passes) and need for perioperative MV identified a critically ill MT phenotype with dismal prognosis. Identifying this group would highlight the need to intensify compliance with perioperative hemodynamic and MV targets and to select patients for trials aimed at bridging the gap between recanalization and recovery.

P200 Not all bradycardias are the same: trigeminocardiac reflex, brainstem stimulation and anesthetic vagotonia during brain tumor surgery

MC Niño de Mejia, D Cohen, MF Parada, A Romero

Hospital Universitario Fundacion Santa Fe de Bogota, Anestesiologia, Bogotá, Colombia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P200

Introduction: Severe intraoperative bradycardia is a relevant hemodynamic event in brain tumor surgery. It may arise from three main mechanisms: activation of the trigeminocardiac reflex (TCR) during manipulation of trigeminally innervated dura or cortex, direct stimulation of brainstem vagal centers, and vagotonia related to anesthetic drugs. This study describes the incidence, causes and surgical distribution of bradycardia in supratentorial and infratentorial tumor resections.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective observational study of adult patients who underwent brain tumor surgery. 1020 supratentorial and 126 infratentorial procedures were performed. Bradycardia was defined as heart rate < 40 beats per minute requiring intervention. Variables included overall incidence, etiological mechanism, surgical region and approach.

Results: Severe bradycardia occurred in 37 patients. In supratentorial surgery, 80.7% of episodes were related to vagotonia secondary to anesthetic drugs and/or other cardiovascular mechanisms such as TCR. In infratentorial surgery, brainstem-mediated mechanisms caused 54.5%. Parasagittal and temporal craniotomies were most frequently associated with TCR (Table).

Conclusions: Although infrequent, requires close hemodynamic monitoring and mechanism-directed treatment. Supratentorial procedures were mainly associated with TCR and anesthetic-induced vagotonia, whereas infratentorial surgery showed a predominance of brainstem-mediated bradycardia. This pattern may help anesthesiologists anticipate and tailor therapy: TCR- or anesthetic-related bradycardia typically responds to anticholinergic drugs, whereas brainstem-mediated bradycardia should not be treated with anticholinergics and is best managed by immediately alerting the surgeon and stopping the surgical stimulus to preserve brainstem reflex monitoring.

P201 Does temporary aneurysm clipping matter? Association with postoperative cerebral edema in a surgical cohort

MC Niño de Mejia, D Cohen, MF Parada, A Romero

Hospital Universitario Fundacion Santa Fe de Bogota, Anestesiologia, Bogotá, Colombia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P201

Introduction: Temporary arterial clipping (TAC) is a fundamental technique in intracranial aneurysm surgery, used to create a bloodless surgical field and reduce the risk of intraoperative rupture (IOR), decreasing IOR from 26 to 8% in some reports. However, its impact on neurological outcome remains controversial.

Methods: We performed a retrospective study of 101 patients with cerebral aneurysms treated with surgical clipping between 2017 and 2025 at a tertiary center (Figure). Postoperative cerebral edema was assessed intraoperatively. Variables included age, vascular territory, TAC duration and type of anesthesia. Logistic regression assessed TAC–edema associations, and a binomial test examined whether TAC exposure among patients with edema exceeded that expected by chance.

Results: Edema occurred in 31 patients. TAC use was strongly associated with edema (OR 5.17; RR 2.23); 70% of patients in the TAC group developed edema, compared with 31.6% without TAC. Chi-square (p = 0.00024) and Fisher’s exact tests (p = 0.00013) confirmed this association. TAC is mainly linked to ischemia due to flow interruption, while prolonged ischemia and reperfusion may trigger cytotoxic and vasogenic edema. Evidence addresses ischemia, IOR and delayed vasospasm; the relationship with early cerebral edema remains underexplored. Although TAC duration < 20 min is considered low risk, longer periods (e.g. > 30 min) are often accepted when neuroprotective strategies.

Conclusions: TAC is a crucial tool for surgical safety, reducing intraoperative rupture, but our findings suggest a potential association with greater cerebral edema. This supports stricter intraoperative neuroprotection and monitoring, including continuous SSEP, to limit ischemia time and potentially mitigate ischemia–reperfusion-related edema, particularly in patients with pre-existing risk factors.

P202 Association between ventilator-associated pneumonia and escalation of intracranial hypertension therapy in TBI patients

M Lamamri, S Carville, C Jeantrelle, A Gallet, E Weiss

Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris Hôpital Beaujon, Réanimation Chirurgicale, Clichy, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P202

Introduction: Ventilator-associated pneumonia (VAP) is the most common infection in traumatic brain injured (TBI) patients [1]. Although its impact on mortality and neurological outcome is unproven, VAP increases the duration of mechanical ventilation and ICU length of stay [2]. Through hyperthermia, hypercapnia and hypotension, VAP may induce intracranial hypertension (ICH) [3]. To date, the association between VAP and the escalation of intracranial hypertension therapy remains unexplored. The primary objective of this study was to assess whether the first episode of VAP was associated with EVD use, a first-tier treatment for ICH in TBI patients.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective study in a French ICU between April 2021 and December 2024. We included TBI patients requiring invasive mechanical ventilation and intracranial pressure monitoring upon ICU admission. Patients requiring immediate EVD placement or decompressive craniectomy upon admission were excluded. We performed a time-to-event analysis to evaluate whether the first episode of VAP was associated with the likelihood and timing of EVD insertion.

Results: We included 159 patients: most were male (119, 75%) with a median age of 42 [26–57] years, and 133 (84%) had severe TBI. VAP was observed in 82 patients (51%): among these patients, the first episode of VAP occurred within the first 7 days for 77 of them (94%). The incidence of ICH was significantly higher in the VAP group (55/82; 67%) than in the group without VAP (34/77; 44%) (OR = 2.6, 95% CI: 1.3–4.9; p = 0.006). Patients with VAP had a significantly increased risk of EVD placement compared with those without VAP (Log rank, p = 0.014) (Figure).

Conclusions: VAP was associated with higher and earlier EVD placement, underscoring that this infection can impose a significant ICH care burden in TBI patients.

P203 Interaction between admission hemoglobin and sex on ICU mortality in patients with traumatic brain injury: a retrospective bi-centric cohort study

M Lamamri1, M Werner2, S Sigaut3, J Dupont3, A Rodrigues4, A Gallet3, E Weiss3, C Jeantrelle3

1Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris Hôpital Beaujon, Réanimation Chirurgicale, Clichy, France, 2Hôpital Bicêtre, Anesthésie Réanimation Chirurgicale, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France, 3Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris Hôpital Beaujon, Clichy, France, 4Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris, Hôpital Kremlin-Bicètre, Paris, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P203

Introduction: Recent literature has been searching for a transfusion threshold in traumatic brain injury (TBI) patients [1,2]. However, general population has baseline differences in hemoglobin levels based on sex [3]. Given the male-predominant TBI epidemiology, it is essential to determine if this sex-specific physiological difference influences the prognosis.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective observational study using data from a national trauma registry across two Level 1 Trauma Centers in France from November 2010 to July 2025. The primary outcome was ICU mortality. We built multivariable logistic regression models, including a sex × hemoglobin interaction and performed causal inference analyses to estimate standardized risks, adjusting for prognostic variables.

Results: We included 5513 TBI patients. After adjustment, every 1 g/dL increase in admission hemoglobin was associated with a 22% decrease in the odds of ICU mortality (OR: 0.78, 97.5% CI [0.69–0.88], p < 0.0001). The sex × hemoglobin interaction effect was not significantly associated with ICU mortality (OR: 1.04, 97.5% CI [0.91–1.19], p = 0.433). Predicted ICU mortality risk was lower in females across all hemoglobin levels (Wald tests, all p < 0.05 from 9.0 to 13.0 g/dL). However, causal inference methods confirmed the absence of any difference in standardized ICU mortality risk at equivalent hemoglobin levels (adjusted OR: 1.01, 95% CI [0.85–1.19], p = 0.936) (Figure).

Conclusions: No interaction between admission hemoglobin and sex was found regarding ICU mortality following TBI. These findings suggest that setting sex-differentiated hemoglobin transfusion thresholds may not be required for the initial management of TBI patients.

P204 Community-acquired versus health care-associated intracranial empyema and abscess: a 12-year single-centre cohort study

RS Hellwege1, M Gönel2, M Sebök2, J Sarnthein2, D Weller3, SD Brugger3, G Brandi1

1University Hospital Zurich, Institute of Intensive Care Medicine, Zurich, Switzerland, 2University Hospital Zurich, Department of Neurosurgery, Zurich, Switzerland, 3University Hospital Zurich, Department of Infectious Diseases and Hospital Epidemiology, Zurich, Switzerland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P204

Introduction: Intracranial empyema and abscess may occur through contiguous spread from local infections or by haematogenous dissemination from distant foci (community-acquired (CA) or complicate neurosurgery (health care–associated (HCA). Comparative ICU data between these two entities remain limited [1,2].

Methods: We retrospectively analysed adults treated for intracranial empyema or abscess (2012–2023) at a tertiary university hospital. Patients with CA or HCA were compared for clinical presentation, microbiology, ICU course, and neurological outcomes (GOSE). Neurological functional status, use of ICU resources and in-hospital mortality were analysed.

Results: Eighty-four patients were included (CA = 37, HCA = 47). CA infections presented with more severe neurological impairment (lower GCS, more focal deficits, impaired consciousness) and higher sepsis rates (43% vs 21%). ICU admission was more frequent in CA cases (81% vs 32%). CA infections were dominated by Streptococcus anginosus and anaerobes, whereas HCA infections by Staphylococcus aureus, Cutibacterium acnes, and Gram-negative rods, including AmpC producers. In-hospital mortality was 15.5%, comparable between groups. Functional outcomes were favourable and similar between groups at discharge and follow-up (median GOSE 6 vs 7 and 7 vs 7). In multivariable analysis, SAPS II was the only independent predictor of in-hospital mortality (OR 1.20 per point; 95% CI 1.05–1.37; p = 0.009) (Figure).

Conclusions: CA intracranial empyema and abscess present with more severe neurological impairment and require substantially more intensive care. Outcomes remain comparable when early diagnosis, surgical source control, and comprehensive ICU support are ensured. Severity of illness, not infection origin, is the strongest prognostic determinant.
