P205 Brain-derived exosomal miRNA profiles in experimental sepsis-associated encephalopathy and their correlation with cerebrospinal fluid and plasma

X Qi, X Zhu

The Affiliated Hospital of Qingdao University, Qingdao, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P205

Introduction: Sepsis-associated encephalopathy (SAE) is a diffuse brain dysfunction lacking specific diagnostic biomarkers. Exosomal miRNAs may reflect early neuropathological alterations and serve as liquid “brain biopsy” indicators. This study investigated brain-derived exosomal miRNA profiles in experimental SAE rats and examined their concordance with cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) and plasma exosomal miRNAs.

Methods: SAE was induced in rats via intraperitoneal lipopolysaccharide (LPS) injection. Brain injury, blood–brain barrier disruption, and neuronal apoptosis were confirmed histologically. Brain-derived exosomes were isolated using established ultracentrifugation and validated by TEM, NTA, and Western blotting. High-throughput sequencing was performed on exosomal miRNAs from SAE (n = 3) and control (n = 3) brain tissue. Four candidate miRNAs were selected based on differential expression and functional enrichment. Their expression in brain tissue, CSF, and plasma was verified by qRT-PCR. Correlations across compartments were analyzed using Spearman coefficients.

Results: A total of 2156 miRNAs were detected, including 101 differentially expressed miRNAs (85 upregulated, 16 downregulated) in SAE brain-derived exosomes. GO and KEGG analyses indicated enrichment in immune, inflammatory, and neuromodulatory pathways. Four miRNAs—miR-127-3p, miR-423-3p, miR-378b, and miR-106-3p—were significantly altered in SAE brain tissue (p < 0.001) and showed identical directional changes in CSF and plasma. Strong correlations were observed between brain-derived miRNAs and corresponding CSF (rs = 0.638–0.857) and plasma miRNAs (rs = 0.690–0.795).

Conclusions: SAE markedly alters brain-derived exosomal miRNA profiles. The strong concordance between brain, CSF, and plasma miRNAs highlights their translational potential as minimally invasive biomarkers. These findings support exosomal miRNAs as promising candidates for early SAE detection and mechanistic investigation.

P206 Light assessment and systematic trends in pupillometry for ultimate prognosis in late-stage injury (Last–Pupil study)

C Avoni1, P Spiandorello2, A Addis2, P Mangili2, M Baggiani2, A Fornoni2, A Guglielmi3, F Graziano4, G Citerio2

1School of Medicine and Surgery, University of Milano Bicocca, Monza, Italy, 2Neuroscience Department, NeuroIntensive Care Unit, San Gerardo Hospital, ASST Monza, Monza, Italy, 3Alma Mater Ticinensis Foundation, Progetto Professionalità, Pavia, Italy, 4Bicocca Bioinformatics Biostatistics and Bioimaging Center B4, School of Medicine and Surgery, University of Milano Bicocca, Milan, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P206

Introduction: Bedside examination is the gold standard for diagnosing brain death (BD) [1]. Quantitative pupillometry (QP) objectively assess midbrain function through the Neurological Pupil Index (NPi) [2], but its evolution before BD is poorly described. This study analysed NPi and constriction percentage (%CH) trajectories in the 72h before BD and identified a Critical Inflection Point (CIP) defined as the last time point with the best recorded NPi and %CH before a sustained irreversible decline.

Methods: This single-center retrospective study included ICU patients ≥ 18 years meeting BD criteria at IRCCS San Gerardo Hospital (2019–2025). Pupillometry and clinical data were analyzed. Only patients with at least one NPi and %CH measurement ≠ 0 within the observation window (ICU admission or the 72h preceding BD) were included. The lowest NPi per 8h block was used.

Results: Forty-nine patients were included (median age 66 [56–71]); intracerebral hemorrhage was the most frequent admission diagnosis (28.6%). At ICU admission, the median worst NPi was 3.40 [0.00–4.00], while %CH was consistently low, with 90% of patients showing CH < 17.2%. CIP most frequently began between 72–64h before BD, with a median worst NPI of 3.30 [0.25–3.90]. In the whole cohort, the median time to CIP was 48h before BD [40–64] (Table). Both NPi and CH reached zero around 40h before BD (median 40h, [32–56]). NPi = 0 consistently preceded the disappearance of all brainstem reflexes, and a sustained NPi < 3 for 8–16h was followed by complete brainstem areflexia within 48–72h.

Conclusions: NPi and %CH seems to follow a stereotyped and temporally consistent deterioration pattern before BD. Detection of a CIP and the observation that NPi deterioration precedes brainstem areflexia suggest that QP may provide a sensitive and early marker of impending brainstem failure. These findings support the integration of pupillometry as a prognostic adjunct in BD evaluation.

P207 Reliability of transorbital TCCD insonation of the ophthalmic artery: a valid approach for the evaluation of intracranial circulation

MC Laganà1, F Daniele1, S Benedetti1, E D´Agostino1, GM Mazza2, M Pagani2, CN Colombo3, G Tavazzi4, A Guglielmi5

1University of Pavia, Department of Clinical, Surgical, Diagnostic and Pediatric Sciences, Pavia, Italy, 2Intensive Care Department 1, Fondazione IRCCS Policlinico San Matteo, Pavia, Italy, 3Alma Mater Ticinensis Foundation of Pavia under the Progetto Professionalità, call 2024-25, Intensive Care Department 1, Fondazione IRCCS Policlinico San Matteo, Pavia, Italy, 4University of Pavia, Department of Clinical, Surgical, Diagnostic and Pediatric Sciences, Pavia, Italy, 5Alma Mater Ticinensis Foundation of Pavia under the Progetto Professionalità, call 2024-25, Pavia, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P207

Introduction: The ophthalmic artery (OA), the first branch of the internal carotid artery (ICA), is important for the non-invasive assessment of anterior cerebral circulation. Transorbital OA evaluation has been used in preeclamptic women [1] and may be a marker of intracranial circulation, but data are limited [2]. We evaluated the feasibility of transorbital Transcranial Color-Coded Duplex (TCCD) of the OA (Figure), defined normative OA values and compared them with those of the middle cerebral artery (MCA) and the ICA.

Methods: Observational study of 25 volunteers undergoing TCCD of the OA, MCA and ICA with a 2-MHz phased-array probe. Peak systolic velocity (PS), end-diastolic velocity (ED), time-averaged maximum velocity (TAMAX) and pulsatility index (PI) were recorded.

Results: Participants were 13M, 12F. All values are expressed as mean ± standard deviation (SD). The OA showed excellent bilateral symmetry between the right and left: PS 38.52 ± 11.14 vs 38.42 ± 8.64 cm/s, TAMAX 17.20 ± 4.89 vs 17.13 ± 3.38 cm/s and PI 1.68 ± 0.39 vs 1.56 ± 0.48. End-diastolic velocity was slightly higher on the left (11.65 ± 6.74 cm/s) than the right (9.68 ± 3.55 cm/s), without statistical significance (p = 0.55). PS, ED, TAMAX and PI were not different across sexes (p > 0.05). In the MCA, females had higher TAMAX (60.87 ± 13.34 vs 52.52 ± 11.27 cm/s, p = 0.022) and lower PI 0.80 ± 0.14 vs 0.90 ± 0.13 (p = 0.013). In the ICA, females showed higher ED (29.10 ± 5.72 vs 25.19 ± 7.62 cm/s, p = 0.044), higher TAMAX (41.07 ± 9.17 vs 32.70 ± 7.84 cm/s; p = 0.001) and lower PI (0.84 ± 0.17 vs 0.98 ± 0.25; p = 0.016).

Conclusions: TCCD is a feasible and reliable tool for evaluating OA circulation. However, further studies are needed to expand the population and validate normal TCCD values for age. A better understanding of OA hemodynamics in pathological settings is needed to support its use in the non-invasive assessment of intracranial circulation.

P208 Predictors of mechanical ventilation and unfavourable outcome in patients with severe tick-borne encephalitis in Slovenia

N Grasselli1, M Mavric1, D Stupica1, M Veluscek1, S Makarovic2

1University Medical Center Ljubljana, Ljubljana, Slovenia, 2Faculty Of Medicine Ljubljana, Ljubljana, Slovenia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P208

Introduction: Tick-borne encephalitis (TBE) is an endemic zoonotic viral central nervous system infection in Slovenia. In 2006, Jereb et al. reported a 9% mortality among intensive care unit (ICU) patients, with no breakthrough infections [1]. The aim of this retrospective study was to evaluate epidemiological, clinical, and laboratory characteristics of patients with severe TBE requiring ICU treatment in Slovenia from 2014–2024. Secondary objective was to analyse and predict mechanical ventilation (MV) characteristics and predictors of death.

Methods: All adult patients (> 18 years) with TBE, hospitalised in the Infectious Diseases ICU of the University Medical Centre Ljubljana, Slovenia, between 1.1.2014–31.12.2024 were included retrospectively. Data was extracted from medical documentation. Severe TBE was defined as positive serum TBE serology and need for ICU treatment. P values < 0.05 were considered statistically significant.

Results: Among 74 included patients (mean age 64.9 ± 15.9 years; 60.8% males) the mean Charlson comorbidity index was 3.1 ± 2.2). 6 patients (8.1%) were previously fully vaccinated against TBE. 40 patients (54%) needed MV, and 4 patients (5%) died. Mean ICU length of stay was 25.1 ± 55.2 days and mean MV time 403.6 ± 1034.6 h. Important statistically significant predictors of MV or prolonged MV (> 21 days) are presented in the Table. Hazard ratio for mortality (95% CI: 1.048–38.640; p = 0.044) in patients with elevated cerebrospinal (CSF) protein concentration was 6-times higher.

Conclusions: Results of our study show that patients with TBE, who require ICU treatment are older with multiple comorbidities, and the mean length of MV often exceeds 21 days. Compared to the cohort from 2001–2005 [1], our findings show lower mortality, but detection of breakthrough infections. For the first time our study identifies significant predictors of MV (advanced age, MRI changes, peripheral paresis) and death (elevated CSF protein concentration).

P209 Impact of intracranial pressure on low cerebral perfusion pressure tolerability and outcome in traumatic brain injury

F Tokmak1, F Güiza1, B Depreitere2, G Meyfroidt1, On Behalf of the BrainIT Group

1KU Leuven, Laboratory of Intensive Care Medicine, Leuven, Belgium, 2KU Leuven, Research Group Experimental Neurosurgery and Neuroanatomy, Leuven, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P209

Introduction: Increasing intracranial pressure (ICP) reduces the effective cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP) [1] and is often accompanied by reduced brain compliance, which may affect how patients respond to periods of low CPP. However, the effect of ICP on the tolerability for low CPP has not been systemically studied, and in this study, we aimed to visualize the low CPP burden in relation to patient outcomes across different ICP values.

Methods: 161 adult patients with minute-by-minute ICP, CPP data along with Glasgow Outcome Score (GOS) at 6 months from BRAIN-IT dataset (collected between March 2003 and July 2005 across 22 neuro-ICUs in 11 European countries) [2] were included in the analysis. Using the algorithm described by Güiza et al. [3], color-coded insult burden plots for CPP were visualized by correlating frequency of low CPP episodes —defined by both intensity and duration— with neurological outcomes. Five visualizations were generated for CPP episodes occurring while mean ICP exceeded > 12, 14, 16, 18, or 20 mmHg, and the transition curves derived from these were displayed together for comparison.

Results: Our visualizations revealed that as ICP increases, the transition curves separating harmful low CPP doses from non-harmful ones shift to the right, representing a reduced tolerability for low CPP and an increase in CPP dose threshold associated with unfavorable outcomes (see Figure).

Conclusions: Beyond decreasing CPP and compromising brain compliance, increasing ICP appears to further affect clinical outcome by reducing tolerability for low CPP. Further research may focus on the mathematical formulation of the relationship between ICP and the shift in the CPP curve to provide clinically actionable information.

P210 Risk factors of external ventricular drain weaning failure in patients with moderate to severe traumatic brain injury

P Joly1, S Aubert1, A Parrin2, M Faillot3, E Weiss1, M Lamamri1

1Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris Hôpital Beaujon, Critical Care, Clichy, France, 2Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris Hôpital Beaujon, Anesthesiology, Clichy, France, 3Assistance Publique des Hôpitaux de Paris Hôpital Beaujon, Neurosurgery, Clichy, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P210

Introduction: External ventricular drain (EVD) is commonly used in Traumatic brain injured (TBI) patients’ management to control of intracranial pressure through cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) diversion [1]. No study focused on the risk factors of EVD weaning failure in this specific population. The aim of the study was to evaluate the risk factors associated with EVD weaning failure in TBI patients.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective monocentric study in a French Level 1 trauma center between January 2011 and January 2024. We included all patients with TBI who required an EVD. Failure of EVD weaning was defined by either the necessity of unclamping the EVD during the clamping trial, or the reinsertion of a new EVD, or by the necessity for permanent CSF diversion. A multivariate logistic regression model was performed to identify independent risk factors.

Results: During the study period, 128 TBI patients required an EVD. Patients were mostly men (85%), with a median age of 33 years. Failure of EVD weaning was observed in 15 patients (19%). Those patients were older than patients with success of weaning (median age of 35 versus 31, p = 0.015) (Table). The only risk factor associated with EVD weaning was EVD infection (ventriculitis/ meningitis) (OR 7.39, 95% CI 1.45–37.7 p = 0.03). Persistence of intraventricular hemorrhage on the follow-up brain CT scan before EVD weaning was also associated with EVD weaning failure, with an odds ratio of 5.43 (95% CI: 1.48–20.0, p = 0.012).

Conclusions: The risk factors associated with EVD weaning failure were the infection of the EVD and the persistence of intraventricular hemorrhage on the follow-up brain CT scan before EVD weaning in TBI patients.

P211 Prognosis of severely traumatized patients with associated traumatic brain injury: the impact of extra-cranial lesions

K Cheour, W Demni, Y Walha, D Hamdi, K Ouerhani, F Lachtar, N Nouira

Monji Slim Hospital, Emergency Department, Tunis, Tunisia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P211

Introduction: Traumatic brain injury (TBI) represents a major public health concern. When it occurs in the context of polytrauma, extra-cranial injuries (ECIs) may worsen the prognosis. The aim of the study was to evaluate the impact of ECIs on in-hospital mortality and functional outcome in severely injured patients with TBI.

Methods: This was a prospective observational study conducted in the emergency department of Mongi Slim Hospital in Tunisia over a two-year period. We included patients aged 15 years and older, who sustained severe trauma with a TBI confirmed by brain CT findings, with or without ECIs. Outcomes were in-hospital mortality and the presence of motor disability at hospital discharge (assessed using the Glasgow Outcome Scale(GOS). To evaluate the impact of ECIs on both vital and neurological functional outcomes, patients were divided into two groups “isolated TBI” and “TBI with ECIs.”

Results: We included 202 patients. The mean age was 41 ± 18 years. The male-to-female ratio was 4.05. All patients presented with brain CT abnormalities, primarily subarachnoid hemorrhage (40.6%), cerebral edema (39.6%) and epidural or subdural hematoma (23.3%). ECIs were present in 63.9% of cases. In-hospital mortality was 26.7%. Unfavorable outcome (GOS ≤ 3) was noted in 30.7% of patients. ECIs were not significantly associated with in-hospital mortality or unfavorable neurological outcomes. Independent predictors of unfavorable outcome in multivariate analysis were: Oxygen saturation < 90% (p = 0.006), Requirement for mechanical ventilation (p < 0.001), Serum creatinine > 110 µmol/L (p = 0.01), Reverse Shock Index < 1 (p = 0.001) and midline shift on brain CT (p = 0.002). The median survival time was shorter in the group with TBI and associated ECIs (28 vs 40 days) but the difference was not statistically significant (p = 0.7).

Conclusions: ECTs are not predictive factors of in-hospital mortality or functional disability in severely injured patients with traumatic brain injury.

P212 Early vs late nutrition in traumatic brain injury: a secondary analysis of the Center-TBI study

G Mazza1, A Guglielmi2, A Fornoni3, F Graziano4, FS Taccone5, G Citerio6

1Fondazione IRCCS Policlinico San Matteo, Intensive Care Department 1, Pavia, Italy, 2University of Pavia, PhD in Experimental Medicine, Pavia, Italy, 3Neurointensive Care Unit, IRCCS San Gerardo dei Tintori, Monza, Italy, 4Neurointensive Care Unit, Biostatistics and Clinical Epidemiology, Fondazione IRCCS San Gerardo Dei Tintori, Monza, Italy, 5Department of Intensive Care, Erasme Hospital, Université Libre de Bruxelles, 1070, Brussels, Belgium, 6School of Medicine and Surgery, University of MilanoBicocca, Milan, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P212

Introduction: Artificial nutrition in TBI is crucial due to its potential influence on clinical outcomes. Current guidelines recommend early nutrition (EN) as soon as possible, ideally within 48h after injury. Clinical practice remains highly heterogeneous, and a large-scale description is currently lacking. The primary objective of this study is to describe the practice of artificial nutrition in TBI patients.

Methods: Secondary analysis of the CENTER-TBI study, enrolling patients with GCS < 13 admitted to the ICU. Patient’s characteristics, daily nutritional therapy, fluid balance and therapy intensity level (TIL) in the first week were collected. EN was defined within 48h after injury; late nutrition (LN) was defined after 48h from injury. Outcome data were collected.

Results: During the first week in ICU, 746 (75.8%) patients received EN. Among patients initiating LN, 88 (8.9%) received nutrition between 3 and 7 days while 150 (15.2%) didn’t receive any nutritional. No significant difference was found between patients receiving EN or LN regarding previous medical history and severity of trauma (GCS, pupillary reactivity, ISS, and Marshall CT classification). Nevertheless, EN group had more frequent neurological deterioration, received more frequently ICP monitoring, higher TIL and had a positive fluid balance. Patients in the EN group had higher incidence of ventilator associated pneumonia (27% VS 10.9%, p < 0.001), longer duration of ventilation [10 (5–18) vs 7 (2–12) days, p < 0.001], longer time of ICU stay [336 (192–528) vs 216 (96–408) days, p < 0.001) and longer time to obey commands [ 7 (3 -14) vs 3.5 (1 – 10) days, p < 0.001]. GOSE at 6m was lower in patients in the EN group (Figure).

Conclusions: Despite similar baseline characteristics between the two groups, EN-group presented higher severity during the first week in ICU, having a worse outcome.

P213 High-risk spine surgery in older adults: predictors of intraoperative bleeding and implications for hemodynamic optimization

MC Niño de Mejia1, D Cohen1, MF Parada1, A Romero2

1Hospital Universitario Fundacion Santa Fe de Bogota, Anestesiologia, Bogotá, Colombia, 2Hospital Universitario Fundacion Santa Fe de Bogota, Bogotá, Colombia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P213

Introduction: Spine surgery increasingly involves older, multimorbid patients exposed to larger, instrumented procedures. In this context, strict hemodynamic management is crucial, and intraoperative bleeding is a key driver of instability and postoperative complications. We aimed to identify clinical and intraoperative factors associated with significant bleeding.

Methods: Retrospective analysis of 724 consecutive spine surgeries of patients over 60 years. Bleeding was categorized as ≤ 500 or > 500 mL. Bivariate associations were explored between bleeding and surgical position, frailty (MFI-5), cardiovascular comorbidity and other perioperative variables. Chi-square tests were used to estimate p values.

Results: High-volume bleeding (> 500 mL) occurred in 100 patients (13.8%). Prone positioning, the predominant approach for extensive cases, concentrated 92% of all high-bleeding events and showed a strong association with bleeding (supine 4.2% vs prone 16.5%, p = 0.0002) (Table). Frail patients (MFI-5 ≥ 2) showed a slightly higher proportion of bleeding (14.7% vs 13.6% in non-frail, p = 0.85), while cardiovascular comorbidity displayed similar rates (12.7% vs 13.9%, p = 0.93). Overall immediate postoperative complications were infrequent (2.6%) without direct correlation with bleeding.

Conclusions: In a large cohort of older adults undergoing spine surgery, intraoperative bleeding was strongly linked to prone positioning, reflecting the complexity of these operations. Frailty and cardiovascular comorbidity did not reach statistical significance but remained clinically relevant markers of hemodynamic vulnerability. Integrating frailty, comorbidity profiles and procedural complexity into preoperative planning, together with meticulous real-time hemodynamic management, may be essential to reducing bleeding-related instability and improving outcomes in contemporary spine surgery.

P214 Time burden analysis of perfusion metrics following traumatic spinal cord injury

F Tokmak1, L Chibuzor Ilochonwu2, F Güiza1, B Depreitere3, MC Papadopoulos2, S Saadoun2, G Meyfroidt1

1KU Leuven, Laboratory of Intensive Care Medicine, Leuven, Belgium, 2City St. George´s, University of London, Academic Neurosurgery Unit, London, UK, 3KU Leuven, Research Group Experimental Neurosurgery and Neuroanatomy, Leuven, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P214

Introduction: High intraspinal pressure (ISP) may hamper the perfusion by reducing spinal cord perfusion pressure (SCPP) [1]; yet, there are no guidelines on managing these metrics for optimal perfusion after traumatic spinal cord injury (TSCI). In this study, we visualized the insult burden curves for ISP and SCPP to quantify secondary injuries in TSCI patients.

Methods: 91 patients with TSCI were recruited for data collection. Minute-by-minute ISP, mean arterial pressure (MAP), and SCPP data were collected and prospectively analyzed. The frequency of ISP and SCPP episodes (defined by intensity and duration) was correlated with the American Spinal Injury Association Impairment Scale (AIS) grade at admission and follow-up, as well as with AIS grade improvement, defined as the difference between follow-up and admission AIS grades. Using the algorithm described by Güiza et al. [2], results were visualized as color-coded burden plots, with a color map ranging from blue to red, where red indicates the strong negative association with the variable of interest.

Results: High ISP doses were associated with worse follow-up AIS grades and were even more strongly associated with worse admission AIS, but showed no clear relationship with AIS improvement (Figure). This suggests that the link between follow-up status and ISP dose may be confounded by the association between admission AIS and ISP. On the other hand, low SCPP doses were associated with both worse admission and follow-up status, as well as with less AIS grade improvement (Figure).

Conclusions: Time burden analysis of ISP and SCPP revealed that SCPP showed a clearer association with patient prognosis and may represent a better therapeutic target than ISP. In our analysis, ISP appeared to be more related to injury severity and patients’ admission status.

P215 Long-term outcomes after neuroICU care: results from the Lombardia neurointensive care network (LINK) cohort

M Baggiani1, S Piva2, M Amigoni1, A Guglielmi3, P Mangili1, G Citerio4

1Neurointensive Care Unit, IRCCS San Gerardo dei Tintori, Monza, Italy, 2Department of Medical and Surgical Specialties, Radiological Sciences and Public Health, Università di Brescia, and Department of Emergency, Spedali Civili University Hospital, Brescia, Italy, 3University of Pavia, PhD in Experimental Medicine, Pavia, Italy, 4School of Medicine and Surgery, University of MilanoBicocca, Milan, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P215

Introduction: Post-Intensive Care Syndrome (PICS) is common after Neuro-Intensive Care (NIC). Its manifestations often overlap with the acute neurological injury, making ICU-acquired sequelae hard to distinguish. To address this challenge, the LINK network [1] launched a prospective, multicentre follow-up programme at 6, 12, and 24 months across 18 hospitals, aiming to detect and characterise post-NIC sequelae systematically.

Methods: From January 2025, we prospectively enrolled consecutive adults admitted to NICs (372 h). Standardised assessments were performed during the ICU stay and at 6 months, evaluating physical, cognitive, psychological, and functional domains using validated tools administered in person or by telephone.

Results: A total of 666 patients were enrolled (mean age 58 ± 17 years; 62% male) (Table). Primary diagnoses were traumatic brain injury (25%), subarachnoid haemorrhage (17%), and intracerebral haemorrhage (18%). Median [IQR] admission GCS was 8 [5–11], and mean SAPS II was 39 ± 15. Mechanical ventilation was required in 88% for 8 ± 7 days, and 78% received sedation for 5 ± 4 days. Mean ICU length of stay was 23 ± 4 days, with 14% of mortality. By hospital discharge, 559 (84%) had regained consciousness. At 6 months, overall mortality was 16%. Severe disability remained common: 52 (44%) had GOSE ≤ 3, including 8 (7%) who were in a vegetative state. Ninety-seven/116 (88%) did not regain their previous work.

Conclusions: NIC survivors showed PICS with persistent frailty, functional dependence, and limited return to work at 6 months. The LINK network enabled harmonised multicentre long-term follow-up, revealing unmet needs and ongoing PICS-related impairments. This structured regional model demonstrates feasibility and clinical value, supporting coordinated long-term care and laying the groundwork for targeted rehabilitation and future studies.

P216 PIEZO2 genetic variations are associated with increased severity and mortality of COVID-19

K Li1, Y Ge1, S Geng1, H Hu2, X Yue2, Y Zhu2, Z Li2, Y Zhang2, L Liu1, W Huang1

1Southeast University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Nanjing, China, 2Southeast University, School of Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P216

Introduction: Given that heightened respiratory drive is a key feature of COVID-19, we aim to investigate whether specific genetic variants in PIEZO2—a mechanosensitive ion channel critical for tidal volume regulation—can serve as such biomarkers [1,2].

Methods: This retrospective study of COVID-19 inpatients (December 2022 to February 2023) investigated the associations of PIEZO2 single-nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) with 28-day mortality (primary outcome, analyzed by Cox regression) and disease severity (secondary outcome, defined by the highest level of respiratory support and analyzed by logistic regression) [3,4]. Linkage disequilibrium (LD) analysis (r2 metric) characterized the genetic relationships among PIEZO2 SNPs.

Results: 313 COVID-19 patients were successfully genotyped for four PIEZO2 SNPs and enrolled. LD analysis of four PIEZO2 SNPs revealed two SNP pairs in complete LD: rs73943314/rs9954308 and rs875956/rs2277858 (r2 = 1). When stratified by 28-day survival, the non-survivor group had significantly higher minor allele frequencies of the four PIEZO2 SNPs than the survivor group (p < 0.001). After adjusting for confounding factors, rs875956 (CT + TT vs. CC) and rs2277858 (TC + CC vs. TT) were associated with an increased risk of 28-day mortality (HR = 2.12, 95%CI 1.03–4.37, p = 0.040). In a multivariable analyses, rs73943314 (TC + CC vs. TT) and rs9954308 (TC + CC vs. TT) were independent risk factors for severe COVID-19 (OR 1.94, 95%CI 1.07–3.55, p = 0.030), as were rs875956 (CT + TT vs. CC) and rs2277858 (TC + CC vs. TT) (OR 2.07, 95%CI 1.26–3.40, p = 0.004).

Conclusions: Minor alleles of PIEZO2 SNPs are associated with increased severity and increased 28-day mortality in COVID-19 patients.

P219 Lactylation of S100A9 protein: a new insight into sepsis-associated lung injury

X Zheng1, F Gong2, W Xu2, Y Han2, D Xu2, H Liu2, R Li2, Y Chen2, E Chen2

1Ruijin Hospital, Shanghai Jiao Tong University School Of Medicine, Shanghai, China, 2Ruijin Hospital, Shanghai Jiao Tong University School Of Medicine, Department Of Emergency, Shanghai, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P219

Introduction: Sepsis is a life-threatening systemic inflammatory condition often complicated by acute lung injury (ALI), with limited therapeutic options. The pathophysiology of sepsis-induced organ dysfunction remains poorly understood, highlighting the need for novel insights into disease mechanisms. This study investigates the role of (S100A9 in sepsis-associated ALI, focusing on its post-translational modifications and their implications in inflammation and tissue damage.

Methods: Through lactylation proteomics and immunoprecipitation, we identified that S100A9 undergoes lactylation at K4 and K94. Using BMDM cells and confocal microscopy, we demonstrated S100A9 nuclear translocation and its regulation by lactylation. Subsequent mass spectrometry, CUT&Tag, and RNA sequencing revealed that S100A9 interacts with CEBPB to regulate IL-1β transcriptional activation.

Results: We identified that S100A9 undergoes lactylation at lysine residues K4 and K94. LPS stimulation enhances S100A9’s colocalization with the nucleus and its presence in nuclear fractions. Furthermore, lactylation of S100A9 enhances its nuclear translocation, and through interaction with Cebpb, it regulates the transcriptional activation of IL-1β, thereby mediating inflammatory responses that contribute to sepsis-induced lung injury.

Conclusions: In this study, we revealed the specific mechanism of sepsis-induced lung injury through S100A9 lactylation modification and its interaction with transcription factor Cebpb to regulate pro-inflammatory IL-1β gene transcription. Further research is needed to validate these findings and explore their therapeutic implications, particularly in the context of clinical nutrition and metabolic interventions to improve patient outcomes in sepsis-associated ALI.

P221 The emerging role of a novel circMYO9B-encoded peptide, MYO9B-446AA, in sepsis pathogenesis

Z Xie1, M Xue2, H Wang2, X Wu2, Y Huang2, W Huang2

1Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Nanjing, China, 2Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P221

Introduction: The mechanisms underlying sepsis remain unclear. We investigated a protein-coding circRNA, circMYO9B, and its encoded peptide as potential regulators and biomarkers in sepsis.

Methods: Differentially expressed circRNAs in human peripheral blood were identified by microarray and RNA-seq. Coding potential was assessed using CPC2 and ORFfinder. The circular structure was validated by RNase R digestion and Sanger sequencing. Translational capacity was examined by expressing FLAG/HA-tagged constructs in HEK293T cells followed by Western blotting. circMYO9B expression in clinical samples was quantified by RT-qPCR and correlated with outcomes. Immune-cell subsets from healthy donors were sorted for expression profiling. Subcellular localization of the encoded peptide was analyzed by cytoplasmic-nuclear fractionation. Interacting proteins were identified by IP/MS with subsequent functional enrichment.

Results: Integrating two datasets (sepsis vs. controls and septic shock vs. sepsis) with circBase yielded four candidate circRNAs (|FC|> 1.2, p < 0.05). One was removed for inconsistent ORF predictions. Among the remaining three (CPC2 > 0.7 and ORF > 25 aa), circMYO9B—with the highest coding score—was selected. Its back-splice junction and RNase R resistance were confirmed. Overexpression of tagged circMYO9B produced a peptide of the expected size, designated MYO9B-446aa. circMYO9B levels were elevated in sepsis and associated with higher mortality. Fractionation localized MYO9B-446aa to the cytoplasm. Expression profiling showed circMYO9B is enriched in CD8⁺ T cells. IP/MS identified MYO9B-446aa-binding proteins enriched in proteasome function, complement activation, immunoglobulin responses, and necroptosis—pathways central to CD8⁺ T-cell antigen processing and cytotoxicity.

Conclusions: circMYO9B encodes a novel peptide, MYO9B-446aa, is upregulated in sepsis, and is linked to CD8⁺ T-cell–related immune pathways, suggesting a potential role in sepsis-associated immune dysfunction.

P222 ADAR1 governs sepsis pathogenesis by modulating monocyte senescence

W Huang1, WT Li2, K Fang3

1Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China, 2Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Nanjing, China, 3Key Laboratory of Developmental Genes and Human Disease, School of Life Science and Technology, Southeast University, Nanjing, Jiangsu, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P222

Introduction: Immunosenescence refers to the abnormal activation or dysfunction of the immune system as people age, characterized by excessive expression of proinflammatory cytokines in aged immune cells [1]. However, its specific role and regulatory mechanisms in sepsis remain poorly understood. This study aims to define the clinical impact of cellular senescence in sepsis and to identify the key regulator orchestrating this process.

Methods: We first calculated senescence scores in a large sepsis cohort using an established transcriptomic signature to assess prognostic value, then employed single-cell RNA-seq of patient and healthy donor PBMCs to identify the key senescent cell population, and finally validated the function of the top candidate ADAR1 by mouse model subjected to CLP-induced sepsis.

Results: A high senescence score was associated with an increased risk of 28-day mortality in sepsis patients. scRNA-seq analysis revealed a significant elevation of senescence signatures specifically in monocytes from septic patients compared to healthy controls. Further bioinformatic dissection nominated ADAR1, an RNA-editing enzyme, as the top negative correlate of monocyte senescence. We generated a monocyte-specific Adar1 knockout (Adar1-mKO) mouse model, which exhibited a disrupted monocyte homeostasis with markedly enhanced senescence—mirroring the human phenotype. When challenged with CLP, these Adar1-cko mice suffered significantly higher mortality compared to their wild-type littermates.

Conclusions: Our study identifies ADAR1 as a novel guardian of monocyte homeostasis, revealing the ADAR1-senescence axis as a promising immunomodulatory target.

P223 A procoagulant immature neutrophil state fuels early immunothrombosis in sepsis

WH Hu, SL Liu, QP Peng, WH Huang, XW Wu, YH Huang

Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Department of Critical Care Medicine Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P223

Introduction: Sepsis-induced coagulopathy (SIC) reflects dysregulated immunothrombosis that propagates microvascular thrombosis and organ failure. Neutrophils are implicated, yet the developmental stage and effector programs responsible remain undefined. We posit that expanded immature neutrophils acquire a distinct procoagulant, degranulation-linked phenotype that fuels early microthrombus formation.

Methods: We mapped early pulmonary thrombus formation after cecal ligation and puncture (CLP) using intravital two-photon microscopy and digital pathology, combined with in vivo neutrophil depletion to test causality. Neutrophil states were resolved by single-cell RNA sequencing integrated with high-throughput flow cytometry and examined in parallel in murine models and a sepsis patient cohort.

Results: Intravital imaging at 4 h post-CLP revealed rapid neutrophil recruitment to the pulmonary microvasculature, promoting neutrophil–platelet aggregates and seeding microthrombi. Digital pathology showed a time-dependent increase in microthrombus burden, predominantly in small intrapulmonary vessels, whereas neutrophil depletion markedly reduced pulmonary microthrombi. Single-cell analysis resolved heterogeneity of neutrophil subtypes and revealed a marked expansion of immature populations with a distinct procoagulant, degranulation-associated program; flow cytometry confirmed a progressive increase in Ly6G⁺CD101⁻ immature neutrophils (blood, sham 17.2% vs 4 h post-CLP 80.2%) with a reciprocal loss of mature cells, closely paralleling microthrombosis kinetics. In a sepsis patient cohort, higher proportions of CD10⁻ immature neutrophils were associated with increased SIC scores (r = 0.52, p < 0.0001).

Conclusions: Expanded immature neutrophils represent a dominant effector state underpinning early sepsis immunothrombosis, positioning degranulation as a tractable therapeutic axis. The parallel association with SIC severity in patients provides critical clinical validation.

P225 Expansion and transdifferentiation of CD177⁺ neutrophils promote lung injury in sepsis-induced ARDS

Y Tang, Z Yu, J Zhang, L Jia, J Xie, Y Yang

Zhongda Hospital, Southeast University, Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P225

Introduction: Sepsis is the second leading cause of death worldwide, with approximately 50 million new cases and 11 million deaths each year [1], and the lung is the most vulnerable organ [2]. This study aims to elucidate the mechanisms underlying the pathogenesis of sepsis-induced ARDS.

Methods: We collected paired bronchoalveolar lavage fluid (BALF) and peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) from sepsis patients with or without ARDS, and one postoperative transitional patient, and performed single-cell transcriptomics with plasma proteomics (Figure, panels A-B). Flow cytometry was used to quantify cell subsets, and H&E staining to assess lung injury.

Results: We found that neutrophils were markedly increased in the PBMC of sepsis patients with ARDS, consistent with recent reports [3,4]. Milo analysis identified CD177⁺ neutrophils as the most markedly expanded subset in ARDS patients (Figure, panels C, D). Plasma proteomics demonstrated elevated soluble CD177 levels in patients with ARDS (Figure, panel E). Flow cytometry confirmed an increase of CD177⁺ neutrophils in ARDS patients (Figure, panels F, G). In the CLP mouse model, CD177-neutralizing antibody treatment attenuated sepsis-induced lung injury (Figure, panel H). Mechanistically, CD177⁺ neutrophils transdifferentiate into long-lived reverse transendothelial migration neutrophils that we previously reported when they migrate from the peripheral blood into the lungs (Figure) [5], sustaining proinflammatory cytokine release and aggravating lung injury.

Conclusions: During sepsis, CD177 + neutrophils markedly expand within the PBMC fraction, migrate into the lungs, and transdifferentiate into reverse transendothelial migration neutrophils, ultimately aggravating lung injury.

P226 Cell adhesion molecules contribute to the outcomes of sepsis in diabetes mellitus

Y Qiao1, H Chen2, X Zhou2, H Lu2, K Fang2, W Huang2

1Southeast University, School of Life Science and Technology, Nanjing, China, 2Southeast University, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P226

Introduction: The growing global burden of diabetes mellitus (DM) has been paralleled by a rise in sepsis cases among diabetic patients. This study aimed to explore the effect and mechanisms through which DM modulate the outcome of sepsis.

Methods: C57 mice were used for DM modeling by intraperitoneal injection of streptozotocin (STZ, 60 mg/kg) for 5 days. LPS (2.5/5/15/20 mg/kg) and cecal ligation and puncture (CLP) were used to induce sepsis. Raw gene expression data GSE65682 from 802 subjects (42 healthy controls, 89 sepsis + DM, 301 sepsis-only) were retrieved from the Gene Expression Omnibus (GEO) database. Differentially expressed genes (DEGs) were screened with fold change (FC) ≥ 1.35, followed by pathway enrichment analysis (p < 0.05). 390 sepsis patients were stratified into high- and low-expression groups based on key gene levels to analyze 28-day survival.

Results: Across all LPS doses (2.5–20 mg/kg), diabetic mice exhibited significantly higher mortality than non-diabetic controls. This disparity was most pronounced at low-to-moderate doses (2.5–5 mg/kg), where mortality was dose-dependent in diabetic mice but absent in controls. Consistently, the polymicrobial CLP model demonstrated markedly reduced survival in diabetic mice (20% vs 60% in controls). Bioinformatics analysis of the GEO dataset identified 622 DEGs in sepsis + DM group and 680 DEGs in sepsis-only group, each relative to healthy controls. Both groups showed enrichment in inflammation/immune pathways, while sepsis + DM group was uniquely enriched in the cell adhesion molecule pathway (p = 0.004). Key adhesion gene CD2 were upregulated and expression translated to a significant survival benefit, with high levels predicting improved 28-day survival in both the overall (p = 0.0066) and sepsis + DM cohorts (p = 0.0134).

Conclusions: DM exacerbates sepsis severity, and the cell adhesion molecule pathway, particularly CD2, may play a critical role in this process. This study provides potential target for clinical interventions in diabetic patients with sepsis.

P228 Potential of cGAS-STING pathway as a treatment target in acute lung injury

S Zubyte1, M Fish2, J Rynne1, A Abdalla1, AG Rossi1, K Mackenzie1, M Shankar-Hari1

1The University of Edinburgh, Institute for Regeneration and Repair, Edinburgh, UK, 2Massachusetts General Hospital and Harvard Medical School, Boston, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P228

Introduction: Inflammatory acute lung injury (ALI) in acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) is characterised by diffuse alveolar damage (DAD). The cyclic GMP–AMP synthase (cGAS)–stimulator of interferon genes (STING) pathway is activated by double-stranded DNA and leads to acute inflammation. We hypothesised that DAD in ARDS could be associated with dysregulated activation of the cGAS-STING pathway.

Methods: We assessed evidence for cGAS-STING pathway activation in ARDS at transcriptome, cytokine, histone and cell phenotype levels using (a) blood samples from patients with acute infection-induced ARDS (n = 69) compared to controls (n = 30); (b) lipopolysaccharide-induced ex vivo lung perfusion (LPS-EVLP) injury model (n = 8) and (c) epithelial cell-enriched human lung organoids stimulated with STING agonist diABZI (n = 4).

Results: Compared with controls, ARDS patients had significantly higher expression of CGAS and TBK1 genes, higher interleukins (IL-6, IL-8) and higher citrullinated histone levels found in DNA-enriched NETs (Mann Whitney U test; p < 0.0001). The LPS-EVLP injury model showed a trend towards higher STING expression compared with uninjured lungs, and STING protein expression co-localised with alveolar epithelium, indicating the involvement of STING in lung injury. Further, in the lung organoids stimulated with diABZI, we observed higher IL-6 (paired t-test; p < 0.05) levels and upregulated interferon-stimulated genes, as well as an upregulation of cytoplasmic pattern recognition receptor signalling pathway, supporting our hypothesis.

Conclusions: Our results suggest that upregulation of the cGAS-STING pathway contributes to acute lung inflammation and DAD in ARDS and warrants further investigation as a potential treatment target for ARDS.

P229 The proportion of CD8+ T cell subsets is determined by infection site and correlates with clinical outcomes in the ICU

Y Zhao1, H Chen2, F Xu1

1The First Affiliated Hospital of Chongqing Medical University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Chongqing, China, 2The Second Affiliated Hospital of Chongqing Medical University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Chongqing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P229

Introduction: T cells play a critical role in antifungal immunity across different infection sites. Reduced CD8⁺ T-cell levels have been linked to early mortality in invasive candidiasis and invasive pulmonary aspergillosis in ICU [1,2]. A recently identified antifungal pattern recognition receptor has also been shown to mediate CD8⁺ T-cell immunity in mucosal fungal infections [3]. However, the site-specific characteristics of CD8 + T-cell immunity in fungal infections remain unclear. This study investigates the heterogeneity of T lymphocyte and CD8 + T-cell subset profiles across infection sites and their association with outcomes in ICU patients with fungal infections.

Methods: A cohort of 500 ICU patients with fungal infections was analyzed. Infection sites—blood, respiratory tract, urinary tract, digestive tract, and others (skin/mucosal)—were compared against baseline characteristics using one-way ANOVA with Tukey’s post-hoc testing. Linear regression assessed associations between T lymphocyte levels and disease severity across infection sites.

Results: Patients with fungal infections exhibited significant differences in 28-day survival across infection sites (p < 0.001, Figure, panel A), with the poorest outcomes in blood and respiratory tract cases. T lymphocyte counts (p = 0.004) and CD8⁺ T-cell (p = 0.019) were significantly reduced in blood and respiratory tract cases (Figure, panel B). CD8⁺ T-cell levels correlated with SOFA and APACHE II scores in respiratory tract, and with SOFA scores and duration of mechanical ventilation in blood (Figure, panel C).

Conclusions: T lymphocyte counts were significantly suppressed in patients with fungal infections of blood and respiratory tract, with CD8⁺ T cells identified as the primary differential subset demonstrating significant associations with disease severity and clinical outcomes.

P230 Leptin signaling and adaptive immunity in COVID-19 patients

A Bagul1, M Mura2, J Peltzer2, P Ponsin3, M Pissot3, T Martinez1, J Duranteau1, A Harrois1, N Libert1, A Huertas4

1Paris Saclay University Dynamic Lab, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France, 2Institut De Recherche Biomédical Des Armées, Brétigny Sur Orge, France, 3Service d’AnesthésieRéanimation, Hôpital d’Instruction des Armées Percy, Clamart, France, 4Paris Saclay University Dynamic Lab, Medecine, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France.

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P230

Introduction: During the coronavirus disease (COVID)-19 pandemic, overweight and obesity has emerged as one of the major risk factors for developing severe form of SARS-CoV-2 infection with poor outcome. It is well known that leptin, a cytokine-like hormone derived mainly from adipocytes, regulates a wide range of inflammatory and immune responses, by binding to its receptor ObR, but its role in COVID is still unclear. Hence, we are questioning here whether leptin signaling is involved in immune responses in COVID-19 patients.

Methods: We analyzed immune cells ObR expression in COVID-19 patients admitted in intensive care unit at Percy Hospital (n = 18; #NCT04365166) compared to healthy donors (n = 15). Blood was withdrawn at Day 1 and Day 14 after admission. Peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) were stained with fluorescent antibodies against CD4, CD11b and ObR and analyzed by flow cytometry. Results are expressed as mean ± standard error of the mean (SEM) and with a statistical significance for one-way ANOVA test at p < 0.05.

Results: CD4 + cells number progressively decreased from Day 1 to Day 14 in COVID patients compared to healthy donors, whereas the expression of ObR in CD4 + cells increased overtime in patients compared to controls. At Day 1, 30 ± 6% of CD4 + cells displayed high levels of ObR staining versus 43 ± 8% at Day 14, compared to 11 ± 3% in controls (p < 0.05). On the opposite, CD11b + cells increased overtime in COVID patients compared to controls, but ObR expression decreased in CD11b + cells in patients (7.8 ± 1% at Day 1 and 8.7 ± 2% at Day 14) compared to controls (14 ± 1%, p < 0.05).

Conclusions: ObR is differently expressed in immune cells in COVID-19 and varies overtime depending on cell phenotype. Our results suggest that leptin signaling may be involved in both innate and adaptive immune responses, with cell-type specificity in COVID-19. Further studies are needed to investigate the role of leptin signaling pathway in SARS-CoV-2 infection.

P231 Epithelial–macrophage metabolic crosstalk drives ACSF2 lactylation–mediated ferroptosis in ventilator-induced lung injury

M Ling1, F Yang1, X Xie1, Y Jiang1, H Luo1, J Lin1, E Chang1, L Pan1, L Ye2

1Department of Anesthesiology, Nanning, China, 2Department of Anesthesiology, Guangxi Medical University Cancer Hospital, Nanning, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P231

Introduction: Mechanical ventilation (MV) is a life-saving intervention for acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) patients [1]. However, MV may paradoxically induce or worsen lung injury—ventilator-induced lung injury (VILI) [2]—increasing morbidity and mortality in the ICU. The met.abolic mechanisms driving VILI remain poorly understood. We hypothesized that dysregulated lactate metabolism promotes macrophage ferroptosis through protein lactylation.

Methods: Plasma lactate and PaO₂/FiO₂ were evaluated in VILI patients. A murine VILI model was established. Single-cell transcriptomics and NicheNet ligand–receptor analysis were used to characterize epithelial–macrophage signaling. In vitro studies included co-culture of macrophages with mechanically stretched alveolar epithelial cells or recombinant mucin 1 (MUC1) stimulation. Lactyl-proteomics identified modified proteins. Functional validation involved ACSF2 K510R mutation, SiglecE neutralizing antibody, and ferrostatin-1.

Results: Lactate elevation correlated negatively with oxygenation (r = –0.871). Lactate also accumulated in murine VILI lungs. Macrophages were the predominant lactate-producing population. Stretched epithelial cells released MUC1, activating macrophage SiglecE and downstream Ca2⁺–CaMKII–HIF1α signaling, enhancing glycolysis. Lactyl-proteomics identified ACSF2 lactylation at lysine 510, which increased significantly despite unchanged total ACSF2. ACSF2 K510 lactylation promoted ferroptosis and inflammation. Disruption of MUC1–SiglecE signaling, ACSF2 K510R mutation, or Ferrostatin-1 treatment significantly reduced ferroptosis and attenuated lung injury.

Conclusions: An epithelial–macrophage metabolic communication axis drives ACSF2 lactylation–mediated ferroptosis in VILI (Figure). ACSF2 lactylation represents a promising therapeutic target for critically ill patients.

P232 EMR3 trajectories predict immune recovery and clinical outcomes in sepsis patients

Y Ma1, P Yu2, X Wang3, W Ni2, J Zhang3, J Zhou4

1Sir Run Run Shaw Hospital, School of Medicine, Zhejiang University, Department of Clinical Laboratory, Hangzhou, China, 2Sir Run Run Shaw Hospital, Zhejiang University School of Medicine, Zhejiang, China, 3Sir Run Run Shaw Hospital, School of Medicine, Zhejiang University, Hangzhou, China, 4Sir Run Run Shaw Hospital, Zhejiang University School of Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Zhejiang, China.

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P232

Introduction: Accurate assessment of immune dysfunction is essential for managing sepsis in the ICU. Current biomarkers such as PCT, CRP, and CD64 only partially reflect the complex transition from hyper-inflammation to immune suppression. EMR3 (encoded by ADGRE3), a myeloid-restricted adhesion GPCR expressed on neutrophils, has recently emerged as a potential indicator of immune competence. Its clinical value in sepsis, however, remains unclear. Objective to investigate whether EMR3 expression and its dynamic changes can predict clinical improvement and immune recovery in septic patients.

Methods: In a prospective ICU cohort, 300 adult patients with sepsis underwent serial peripheral-blood sampling at D0, D1, D3, D5, and D7. EMR3 expression on circulating neutrophils was quantified using flow cytometry. Clinical outcomes, including 28-day mortality and organ failure trajectories, were recorded. EMR3 performance was compared against nCD64, CRP, PCT, and SOFA scores. Diagnostic accuracy was evaluated using ROC analysis, focusing on both static values and temporal changes.

Results: EMR3 expression was markedly reduced at sepsis onset and correlated with illness severity (SOFA, p < 0.001). Patients who improved demonstrated a significant rise in EMR3 during the first week, whereas persistently low EMR3 was associated with immune paralysis. The dynamic change D7-D0 achieved the highest prognostic accuracy (AUC 0.87), outperforming PCT, CRP, and nCD64.

Conclusions: Dynamic EMR3 monitoring provides meaningful insight into immune recovery in sepsis and surpasses conventional biomarkers in predicting clinical improvement. EMR3 may support precision immune phenotyping and guide individualized immunomodulatory or antimicrobial strategies in the ICU.

P233 Extracellular vesicle proteomics defines molecular subtypes of sepsis and links metabolic dysregulation to patient outcomes

Y Bi, X Song

Shandong Public Health Clinical Center, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Ji nan, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P233

Introduction: Sepsis is a heterogeneous syndrome characterized by dysregulated host response to infection, leading to high morbidity and mortality [1]. Current clinical classifications fail to capture biological diversity, hindering precision management. Extracellular vesicles (EVs) carry abundant bioactive proteins and may reflect disease-specific molecular alterations [2]. This study aimed to identify molecular subtypes of sepsis and elucidate key proteomic signatures associated with severity and outcomes.

Methods: Serum EVs were isolated from 324 participants, including sepsis-only, sepsis with ARDS, sepsis with AKI, sepsis with both ARDS and AKI, and healthy controls. EVs were characterized by TEM, NTA, and Western blot for CD9, CD81, and TSG101. Proteins were analyzed by LC–MS/MS and processed via Spectronaut and R-based bioinformatics pipelines. Consensus clustering was used for molecular subtyping, and differential expression, KEGG, and SVM analyses were applied to identify metabolic pathways and prognostic markers.

Results: A total of 4,754 EV proteins were identified. CD81, FLOT1, FLOT2, HSP90AA1, and HSP90AB1 increased progressively with sepsis severity. Four molecular subtypes (S1–S4) were defined, with S1 (“Sep-mild”) showing high lymphocyte counts and S4 (“Sep-severe”) displaying elevated procalcitonin and septic shock incidence (Figure). KRT family proteins were enriched in Sep-mild patients, suggesting a protective role. Metabolic pathway proteins were markedly downregulated in severe and non-survivor groups. A panel of eight metabolic proteins achieved an AUC > 0.95 for risk discrimination, validated in an independent cohort.

Conclusions: EV proteomics enabled stratification of sepsis into biologically and clinically distinct subtypes. Metabolic dysregulation emerged as a central determinant of disease severity and survival, highlighting metabolism-targeted intervention as a promising therapeutic avenue for precision sepsis care.

P234 The divergent metabolomic landscape of COVID-19 and community acquired pneumonia

V Rapti1, S Foutadakis2, EJ Giamarellos Bourboulis3, K Dakou2, K Leventogiannis3, G Poulakou1

1National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, 3rd Department of Internal Medicine, Athens, Greece, 2Hellenic Institute for the Study of Sepsis, Athens, Greece, 3National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, 4th Department of Internal Medicine, Athens, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P234

Introduction: The metabolomic profile has the potential to reflect the host status and discriminate between diseases such as COVID-19 and community acquired pneumonia (CAP), offering valuable insights into their unique pathophysiological mechanisms.

Methods: Patient samples were derived from two clinical trials: the SAVE trial (ClinicalTrials.gov identifier: NCT04357366), and the ACCESS trial (ClinicalTrials.gov identifier: NCT04724044). All patients met the Sepsis-3 definitions. Sampling was done before start of the study drug. Untargeted metabolomics experiments were performed using serum subjected to high-performance liquid chromatography coupled with mass spectrometry. The raw metabolomics data were analyzed using the MetaboAnalyst platform.

Results: Samples from 46 patients with CAP and 46 with COVID-19 matched for age, sex and Charlson Comorbidity Index were subjected to untargeted metabolomics experiments. A total of 3,555 metabolites were detected and differential analysis recovered more than 70% of the metabolome as altered between conditions. Metabolite pathway analysis highlighted pathways related to the citric acid cycle and arachidonic metabolism as activated in CAP, while COVID-19 was mainly driven by alterations in amino acid metabolism and metabolites related to mitochondrial function (Figure).

Conclusions: Extensive divergence was observed in the metabolomic landscape of patients with COVID-19 or CAP, underscoring the disease-specific metabolic adaptations associated with viral versus bacterial pneumonia and providing potential targets for diagnostic and therapeutic development.

P237 Pooled purified buffy coats to enhance extracorporeal immune cell therapy: an ex vivo evaluation

S Brabandt1, S Doss2, J Altrichter3, B Heskamp3, C Augustin3, DA Reuter1, JC Schewe1, S Mitzner4, G Klinkmann1

1University Medical Center Rostock, Department of Anesthesiology, Intensive Care Medicine and Pain Therapy, Rostock, Germany, 2Fraunhofer Institute for Cell Therapy and Immunology, Rostock, Germany, 3ARTCLINE GmbH, Rostock, Germany, 4University Medical Center Rostock, Department of Internal Medicine / Clinic for Infectious Diseases, Nephrology, Endocrinology, and Tropical Medicine, Rostock, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P237

Introduction: Buffy coats represent a promising approach for producing granulocyte concentrates for the extracorporeal immune cell therapy ARTICE, enhancing the clinical applicability to counteract sepsis-associated immunoparalysis. Building on a purification process that extends the 24-h shelf life of standard granulocyte preparations to 4 days, the use of buffy coats further improves production feasibility through broad availability. In this ex vivo study, we evaluated the performance of purified granulocyte concentrates from twenty pooled buffy coats (ppGC20) within the ARTICE-system.

Methods: ppGC20 were produced by pooling twenty ABO-identical buffy coats and applying a specialized purification protocol. The ARTICE-system includes a blood circuit and a separate plasma circuit. Before circulation, ppGC20 is introduced into the plasma circuit and retained by a cell filter. Only patient plasma contacts the immune cells before returning to the blood circuit. A plasma pool served as patient simulation. Circulation was maintained for 6 h and viability, functional activity, and metabolic parameters were assessed.

Results: Circulation remained stable over 6 h without technical issues. After 6 h, cell viability and phagocytic activity were maintained at ≥ 80%. Cytokine analyses showed that Eotaxin decreased significantly, while G-CSF, MCP-1, M-CSF, and TNF-a concentration increased significantly. Reduced glucose, elevated lactate, and a corresponding pH decline indicated metabolic activity.

Conclusions: ppGC20 are readily available immune cell preparations with stable functional performance within the ARTICE system. Cytokine shifts demonstrated active immunomodulation under ex vivo conditions. Due to their availability and functional stability, buffy-coat–derived granulocytes hold strong potential for clinical use in the ARTICE-system targeting sepsis-associated immunoparalysis.

P238 Manual lymphatic drainage to improve the outcome of patients after septic shock (MADAME trial): a safety and feasibility pilot randomized controlled trial

R Kula Jr.1, R Kula2, O Jor2, Y Vantuchová3, L Krupová3, J Mutka3, K Liszková2

1Department of Anaesthesiology, Resuscitation and Intensive Medicine, Tomas Bata Regional Hospital, Zlin, Czech Republic, 2Department of Anaesthesiology, Resuscitation and Intensive Medicine, University Hospital Ostrava, Ostrava, Czech Republic, 3Department of Dermatology, University Hospital Ostrava, Ostrava, Czech Republic

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P238

Introduction: Impaired resolution of inflammation after initial treatment of septic shock contributes to persistent organ dysfunction and prolonged ICU stay. Supporting lymphatic drainage may help accelerate recovery of organ function. This pilot randomized trial (NCT05874895) evaluated the feasibility and safety of manual lymphatic drainage (MLD) in the ICU setting.

Methods: Patients with treated septic shock (Sepsis-3), weaned off circulatory support and without SOFA improvement, were randomized to the MLD or the control group (Figure, panel A). MLD was performed daily for five days (30 min with two lymphotherapists or 60 min with one), in the supine position, including the head, neck, thorax, abdomen and extremities. Analysis was done on an intention-to-treat basis. Missing SOFA scores due to death or discharge were imputed using last observation carried forward.

Results: Thirty patients were randomized (15 MLD, 15 control). Baseline characteristics at randomisation (age, SOFA score, procalcitonin, interleukin-6, lactate, infection source, mechanical ventilation) were comparable. In the MLD group, 71 of 75 planned sessions were completed; missed sessions were due to temporary patient absence. No interference with routine nursing care was observed. Blood pressure, heart rate, and SpO₂ remained stable before and after MLD. SOFA trajectories are shown in the Figure, panel B. In the control group, early deaths led to missing SOFA scores (one on day 3 and four on day 5). Delta SOFA (day 0–5), expressed as median (min–max), was − 5.0 (− 10.0 to 1.0) in the MLD group versus − 1.0 (− 8.0 to 4.0) in the control group (p = 0.032). The 28-day mortality was similar between groups (MLD: 5/15 vs control: 7/15, p = 0.710).

Conclusions: MLD appears feasible and safe in patients with septic shock and impaired resolution of organ dysfunction and leads to a significantly greater SOFA score reduction.

P239 Novel subphenotypes of HRV in sepsis and prediction model

FEI Han

Zhongshan Hospital, Fudan University, Department of Critical Care, Shanghai, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P239

Introduction: The autonomic nervous system (ANS) serves as the body’s unconscious control mechanism for vital physiological functions and ensuring homeostasis. The integration of ANS monitoring and targeted therapeutic interventions represents a paradigm shift in sepsis management. The aim of this study was to investigate the correlation between autonomic nervous system (heart rate variability, HRV) subtypes and clinical outcomes in patients with sepsis.

Methods: This retrospective study included 178 sepsis patients. Relevant medical history data and HRV data within 24 h of ICU admission were collected. K-means clustering was used to perform subtype analysis based on HRV data. Clinical outcomes were observed for different subtypes, with primary endpoints being the 28-day mortality rate. We established the prediction model based on the cluster, medical history and disease severity in this population, and evaluated the model using AUC and calibration. Data analysis was conducted using R.

Results: Based on HRV indicators (including time-domain and frequency-domain analyses), K-means clustering divided the 178 sepsis patients into two groups: C1 (144 cases) and C2 (34 cases). Significant statistical differences were found between the two groups for 28-day mortality (41.2% vs. 15.28%, p < 0.001), ICU mortality (38.2% vs. 11.8%, p < 0.001), and MV days (15.32 days vs. 5.56 days, p < 0.001). Kaplan–Meier curves showed significant differences between clusters. In the prediction model, nomogram indicated diabetes, COPD, HRV cluster and APACHE II score showed statistical significance. AUC curve and calibration curve showed the model performed well.

Conclusions: Sepsis patients can be divided into two subtypes based on HRV indicators using K-means clustering. The C2 group had poorer clinical outcomes. The prediction model with the cluster and medical history performed well in sepsis. HRV subtypes may serve as a direction for further clinical trials or future treatments.

P240 Differential resistance and disease tolerance pathway expression in sepsis subtypes and sterile inflammation

R Hoekstra1, A Chowdhury2, M Fish3, J N Kennedy2, C Seymour2, M Shankar-Hari1

1University of Edinburgh, Institute for Regeneration and Repair, Edinburgh, UK, 2University of Pittsburgh, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Pittsburgh, USA, 3Harvard Medical School, Massachusetts General Hospital and Harvard Medical School, Boston, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P240

Introduction: Infections and sterile tissue injury share host response pathways [1] of resistance to eliminate pathogen or damaged tissue (R-programs) and disease tolerance (host adaptations to survive (T-programs). We tested whether SENECA clinical subtypes of sepsis are detectable in sterile inflammation post cardiac surgery and report differences in R- and T-programs between SENECA subtypes.

Methods: Our prospective cohort (n = 97) consisted of adults with critical illness from (a) infection (sepsis) and (b) sterile inflammation post cardiac surgery (post-surgery) [2]. We used the published clinical classifier [3] to assign SENECA subtypes (α, β, γ, and δ). Sample-level enrichment analysis (SLEA) of panleukocyte transcriptome quantified (up or downregulation) of Hallmark pathways mapping to R- and T-programs at individual patient level, with hierarchical clustering for differences between SENECA subtypes.

Results: The α-type was least prevalent (8.2% in sepsis vs 4.1% in post-surgery), γ-type most prevalent (37.1% in sepsis vs 16.5% in post-surgery) and notably δ-type only seen in sepsis (11.3%). SLEA identified two clusters: cluster-2 (59%) with concurrently upregulated and cluster-1 (41%) with downregulated Hallmark pathways mapping to R- and T-programs. Cluster-2 consisted of predominantly sepsis patients (78.9%), while cluster-1 had similar proportions of sepsis and post-surgery patients (55% vs 45%). SENECA subtypes were distributed evenly across the two SLEA clusters, with α-type showing significantly higher expression of R- and T-programs than β, γ, and δ, explaining better outcome in SENECA study.

Conclusions: We report similarities in Hallmark pathways mapping to R- and T-programs between sepsis and sterile inflammation, with differences between SENECA sepsis subtypes, indicating subtype specific mechanisms may exist.

P241 Clustering transcriptomic trajectories in sepsis using dynamical systems cost-fitting landscapes (DSCL)

DA Striegel1, M Fazli1, JG Chenoweth1, D Bishop2, N Adams2, DV Clark1

1Henry M. Jackson Foundation for the Advancement of Military Medicine HJF, ACESO, Hesda, USA, 2Naval Medical Research Command, Naval Medical Research Command, Silver Spring, MD, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P241

Introduction: Sepsis is a life-threatening condition caused by a dysregulated immune response to infection. Significant heterogeneity in its clinical and molecular representation creates challenges for the treatment and prognosis of sepsis. Here, Dynamical Systems Cost-fitting Landscape (DSCL), which uses a ordinary differential equation (ODE) model to characterize the temporal dynamics of gene-pattern interactions was used to cluster transcriptomic trajectories in sepsis revealing multiple common temporal paths.

Methods: Previously, the ACESO program at HJF collected 573 peripheral blood RNA samples from a Ghanaian cohort of 120 suspected sepsis patients and 42 healthy donors. Gene expression data (26,467 genes per sample) was reduced to 30 gene patterns using the Coordinated Gene Association in Pattern Sets (CoGAPS). Here, DSCL was used to characterize the temporal dynamics of gene-pattern interactions, calculate trajectory similarity through cost-fitting landscapes across model parameter space, and cluster these trajectories using the Multi-layer Bundling (MLB) technique.

Results: After applying the DSCL method to the longitudinal gene expression patterns, multiple clusters of trajectories were found. These clusters illustrated multiple failure pathways among non-survivors and heterogeneous recovery paths among survivors. Comparisons with clinical data collected at enrollment time showed significant differences in severity scores and immune system biomarkers across clusters. These results highlight the ability of DSCL to capture biologically and clinically relevant distinctions between clustered trajectory types.

Conclusions: DSCL clustered longitudinal gene expression patterns providing a powerful framework to develop more effective, personalized treatment strategies tailored to individual patient trajectories. This versatile methodology is not limited to sepsis and holds potential to advance the understanding of other complex diseases and dynamic biological processes.

P242 Droplet digital PCR-guided antibiotic use for critically ill patients with bloodstream infections: a retrospective observational study

HY Xiong1, YK Zhu1, KX Wang1, B Wang1, Y Kang2

1West China Hospital, Sichuan University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Chengdu, China, 2West China Hospital, Sichuan University, Chengdu, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P242

Introduction: Bloodstream infections (BSIs) are a significant cause of morbidity and mortality in critically ill patients. Conventional blood cultures (BCs) are limited by low sensitivity and prolonged turnaround time, often necessitating the initiation of broad-spectrum antibiotic use. Droplet digital PCR (ddPCR) offers rapid and high-sensitivity pathogen detection; however, its clinical utility in guiding antimicrobial therapy has not been fully established.

Methods: This retrospective observational study included the adult patients who admitted to the ICU with clinical evidence of BSIs from October 2020 to August 2024. We compared the diagnostic performance and turnaround time of ddPCR against BCs. Additionally, we assessed how ddPCR results affected antimicrobial management, focusing on escalation, de-escalation, or confirmation of empirical therapy. Clinical outcomes, such as 28-day mortality and Sequential Organ Failure Assessment (SOFA) scores were compared.

Results: A total of 201 patients were included in this study. The cumulative mortality rate within 28 days was 40.3%. Compared with BCs, ddPCR showed a significantly higher positive rate (96.0% vs. 27.4%, p < 0.001), detecting 325 pathogens, including multidrug-resistant strains. The turnaround time for ddPCR was 8.57 h shorter than that of BCs (p < 0.0001). Based on ddPCR results, antibiotic therapy was altered in 50.75% of patients (31.34% were escalated, 5.97% were de-escalated, and 13.43% confirmed empiric therapy). No significant differences were observed in 28-day mortality (HR = 1.46; 95%CI, 0.95–2.23; p = 0.072) (Figure) and SOFA scores (p = 0.86) between the groups.

Conclusions: Droplet digital PCR has great potential in the early detection of BSIs. Antibiotic treatment guided by ddPCR had no adverse effect on patient prognosis.

P243 Comparative performance of the T2Bacteria Panel and conventional blood cultures in ICU patients with suspected sepsis

N Kangrga1, D Unic Stojanovic1, I Cirkovic2, I Malesevic1, M Bojic3

1Institute for Cardiovascular Diseases Dedinje, Anesthesia and Intensive Care, Belgrade, Serbia, 2Institute for Microbiology and Immunology, Faculty of Medicine, Belgrade, Serbia, 3Institute for Cardiovascular Diseases Dedinje, Belgrade, Serbia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P243

Introduction: Sepsis remains a leading cause of morbidity and mortality worldwide, where timely and accurate pathogen detection is critical for improving outcomes. Conventional blood cultures are the gold standard but are limited by prolonged turnaround times and suboptimal sensitivity, often delaying targeted therapy [1].

Methods: We performed a retrospective observational study including 30 adult postoperative cardiovascular ICU patients who underwent simultaneous testing with the T2Bacteria Panel and conventional blood cultures. The aim of the study was to evaluate the accuracy of the T2Bacteria panel for the diagnosis of bacterial sepsis and its impact on patient management, in terms of duration of empirical therapy and switch to directed therapy.

Results: T2Bacteria detected all on-panel ESKAPE pathogens with 100% sensitivity and specificity, whereas blood cultures identified 75% of these infections (Table). Discordant findings occurred in 20% of patients. Five off-panel organisms (Burkholderia cepacia and Staphylococcus epidermidis) were isolated only by blood cultures. Antimicrobial therapy was modified after T2 results in 60% of T2-positive patients.

Conclusions: The T2Bacteria Panel provides rapid, highly accurate detection of key bloodstream pathogens and enables earlier optimization of antimicrobial therapy compared with conventional blood cultures. While limited by sample size and single-center design, this study demonstrates that integrating T2Bacteria and T2Resistance into clinical workflows has the potential to improve sepsis management by bridging the critical gap between empirical therapy and definitive microbiological diagnosis.

P244 Multidisciplinary analysis of causes and risk factors of increased mortality in invasive Streptococcus pyogenes infections: a prospective study

J Závora, G Kroneislová, V Adámková

General University Hospital in Prague, Clinical Microbiology and ATB Centre, Prague, Czech Republic

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P244

Introduction: Streptococcus pyogenes (group A streptococcus, GAS) causes a wide spectrum of infections with various outcomes. In recent years, a global rise in the incidence of streptococcal infections – including invasive forms – has been observed. Potential explanations include changes in the pathogen itself (e.g., increased virulence), the possible emergence of the hypervirulent M1UK variant, and broader population-level changes.

Methods: The objective of our ongoing study is to evaluate additional biomarkers (ICIS score, lactate, myoglobin, heparin-binding protein [HBP], vascular endothelial growth factor [VEGF], and MR-proadrenomedullin), together with a detailed assessment of the immunological profiles of affected patients. Using next-generation sequencing (NGS), we aim to identify potential virulence determinants in Streptococcus pyogenes to improve our understanding of factors contributing to the high mortality associated with invasive GAS infections. These parameters will be compared between patients experiencing sepsis episodes and those with non-sepsis infection presentations (e.g., skin and soft tissue infections).

Results: This increasing incidence is also reflected in data from the General University Hospital in Prague. In 2018, 148 unique GAS isolates were recorded; in 2019, 135 isolates; compared with 494 isolates in 2023 and 303 in 2024. Between 2020 and 2025, a concerning global upward trend in mortality associated with invasive GAS infections has also been observed. According to our previous analysis, mortality in our setting in 2016–2019 was 17.7%, whereas in 2020–2024 it increased to 42.6%.

Conclusions: The results of this study are expected to clarify the host and pathogen factors associated with severe disease progression. By integrating biomarker profiling with genomic characterization of circulating strains, we aim to identify indicators that may support earlier risk stratification and targeted clinical management.

P245 A prediction model for causative pathogens in bloodstream infection: development and validation using machine learning

L Liu

Xiangya Hospital, Central South University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Xiangya Hospital, Central South University, China., Changsha, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P245

Introduction: Early prediction of bloodstream infections (BSI) pathogens is important for earlier and appropriate administration of antibiotics [1]. This study aimed to develop a prediction model based on routinely available clinical and laboratory parameters for early identification of causative pathogens in patients with suspected BSI [2].

Methods: Predictors were initially selected from public available medical databases and subsequently calibrated using our database to optimize model parameters. Vital signs at the time of blood culture sampling and laboratory parameters within 24 h before or after sampling were collected from patient medical records. Next-Generation sequencing (NGS) was performed on simultaneously collected blood specimens from a subset of patients. Blood culture results were compared with NGS findings, and a predictive model was subsequently developed based on the analyzed data.

Results: The blood culture-positive (BCP) group and NGS-positive (NGSP) group showed different characteristics. Both groups showed lower lymphocyte count. The BCP group had lower mean arterial pressure and higher neutrophil percentage. The NGSP group exhibited higher levels of C-reactive protein and procalcitonin. The prediction model showed area under receiver-operating characteristics curve of 0.91, which demonstrated good discrimination of BSI. Furthermore, the model had a good performance at the identification of gram-positive bacteria. However, more work are warranted for further pathogen classification, such as gram-negative bacteria or fungi.

Conclusions: We developed a prediction model for BSI based on the daily available parameters that showed significant differences between BSI-positive and BSI-negative patients, which showed a good performance to predict BSI. Additionally, the model could identify certain pathogen of BSI, which would be helpful for early and appropriate antibiotics administration.
