P246 Utility of molecular over traditional diagnostic methods in diagnosis and management of ventilator-associated pneumonia (VAP) caused by gram-negative bacteria in the intensive care unit

A Agrawal1, KP Agrawal1, S Agrawal2, S Prakash1, SSS Siddiqui1, S Misra1

1King George´s Medical University, Critical Care Medicine, Lucknow, India, 2King George´s Medical University, Obstetrics Gynaecology, Lucknow, India

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P246

Introduction: Ventilator-associated pneumonia (VAP) is a major ICU infection, often due to multidrug-resistant Gram-negative bacteria. Culture-based diagnostics are slow, delaying therapy, while molecular methods like real-time PCR enable rapid pathogen and resistance detection, potentially improving outcomes [1,2].

Methods: This 12-month prospective single-centre study in a 36-bed ICU enrolled 184 adults with suspected VAP. Group 1 (n = 92) underwent PCR plus culture, while Group 2 (n = 92) had culture alone. Data included demographics, clinical parameters, turnaround time, microbiology, antibiotic changes, SOFA/CPIS scores, and 28-/90-day mortality.

Results: PCR enabled faster detection (7.9 vs. 72 h), higher yield (98.9% vs. 96.8%), and better resistance gene identification. Despite lower specificity (10%), Group 1 had fewer ventilator days, shorter ICU stay, improved SOFA scores, and reduced 28-day (30.9% vs. 42.5%) and overall mortality (35.1% vs. 52.1%) (Table).

Conclusions: Integrating PCR with standard culture significantly improves early diagnosis and targeted therapy in VAP, leading to better clinical outcomes. Despite limitations in resistance detection specificity, PCR's rapid turnaround and high sensitivity support its role as a valuable adjunct in ICU infection management.

P247 Heteroresistance in Pseudomonas aeruginosa and outcomes of nosocomial pneumonia: a preliminary prospective two-center study

S Nagle1, Q Leduc1, T Billard Pomares2, K Villageois Tran3, AC Maherault4, M Allain4, L Landraux4, JR Zahar5, D Roux6, K Chaibi1

1CHU Avicenne, Intensive Care Unit, Bobigny, France, 2CHU Beaujon, Microbiology Department, Clichy, France, 3CHU Avicenne, Microbiology Department, Bobigny, France, 4CHU Louis Mourier, Microbiology Department, Colombes, France, 5CHU Avicenne, Infection Prevention and Control Team, Bobigny, France, 6CHU Louis Mourier, Intensive Care Unit, Colombes, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P247

Introduction: Nosocomial pneumonia caused by Pseudomonas aeruginosa (PA) is frequent in the ICU and often associated with treatment failure. Heteroresistance (HR) is defined as the presence of a heterogeneous bacterial population with subpopulations exhibiting significantly higher MICs than the main population for the tested antibiotic. This study aimed to determine the prevalence of HR in PA pneumonia and to assess its association with clinical outcomes.

Methods: We conducted a prospective, observational, bicentric, preliminary study including all patients with a first episode of uncomplicated PA nosocomial pneumonia from October 2022 to March 2025. Heteroresistance was assessed using a disk-diffusion antibiogram performed on 10 distinct colonies (Figure); in borderline cases, HR was confirmed when subpopulation MIC was at least eightfold higher than MIC of the main population. We obtained an ethical approval (CLEA-2022–282).The primary composite endpoint (PCE) was treatment failure defined as, death, persistence, or recurrence of pneumonia within 28 days after the diagnosis of PA pneumonia. The association between HR and PCE was assessed using logistic regression adjusted for age and SOFA score at admission. Each component of the PCE was analyzed as a secondary outcome.

Results: Thirty-two patients were included; HR was present in 16/32 (50%). The PCE occurred in 21/32 patients (66%). After adjustment, no significant association was found between HR and the PCE (OR 0.82; 95%CI 0.07–9.96; p = 0.87). Among secondary outcomes, pneumonia persistence at day-28 was associated with HR (OR 7.00; 95%CI 1.18–41.36). No significant association was found with pneumonia recurrence or death, at day-28.

Conclusions: Heteroresistance is frequent in PA nosocomial pneumonia. No association was observed with the primary composite endpoint at day-28, whereas HR was associated with pneumonia persistence. These findings should be interpreted with caution due to the small sample size and the wide confidence intervals.

Figure (abstract P247) Disk diffusion with ceftazidime 10 μg in a clinical isolate of Pseudomonas aeruginosa shows inner-halo colonies within the inhibition zone, suggesting heteroresistance (HR) to ceftazidime. HR was confirmed by comparing the MIC of the inner-halo colonies with the MIC of the main population

P249 Infections related to central venous catheters in patients with hematologic disease before and after changes in institutional hygiene practices: a retrospective cohort study

M Lazarevic Lindblad, E Pålsson, V Lazarevic, O Borgquist, T Kander

Lund University, Department of Clinical Sciences, Lund, Sweden

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P249

Introduction: To improve infection prevention of central venous catheters (CVC) in patients with hematological disease an Anti-Infection Double Lumen Bundle was introduced in December 2020 (implementation date), including replacement of the standard single lumen polyurethane catheter with a double lumen noble-metal alloy–coated CVC and switching to chlorhexidine-impregnated dressings. This study evaluated whether the Anti-Infection Double Lumen Bundle was associated with reduced catheter related infections.

Methods: All non-tunneled CVC insertions with a dwell time was ≥ 24 h, in adults (≥ 18 years), treated for hematologic disease between May 2013 and June 2024 were included. Data were extracted through automated electronic health record queries and manual review. Primary outcomes were suspected catheter-related infection (sCRI) and catheter-related bloodstream infection (CRBSI). Secondary outcomes were catheter tip colonization and infection rates per 1000 catheter-days.

Results: A total of 907 CVC insertions in 690 patients were analyzed (471 before and 436 after the implementation date). No significant differences were observed in sCRI (6.4% vs. 7.6%, p = 0.48), CRBSI (0.4% vs. 0.7%, p = 0.60), or catheter tip colonization (4.7% vs. 4.4%, p = 0.82) before versus after the implementation. Incidence rates per 1000 catheter-days did not differ (sCRI: 1.67 vs. 2.34, p = 0.20 and CRBSI: 0.11 vs. 0.21, p = 0.66). In multivariable analysis, no variables were associated with a higher incidence of sCRI, while antibiotic administration at insertion seemed protective (Table). The variable before vs after the implementation date was not associated with sCRI.

Conclusions: The introduction of the Anti-Infection Double Lumen Bundle did not reduce CVC-related infectious complications in hematology patients. Antibiotic at insertion was independently associated with sCRI. Further studies are needed to clarify optimal CVC type and preventive strategies in this high-risk population.

P251 Impact of colonization with Candidozyma auris on the outcome of ICU patients

L Herbel1, L Magdo2, C Vagner1, M Flonta1, V Briciu2, M Lupse2

1Clinical Hospital of Infectious Diseases, Cluj Napoca, Romania, 2Clinical Hospital of Infectious Diseases, ‘Iuliu Hatieganu’ University of Medicine and Pharmacy, Cluj Napoca, Romania

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P251

Introduction: In ICU populations, Candidozyma auris (CA) infection confers a significant clinical burden, characterized by extended hospitalization and elevated mortality, driven by its multidrug resistance and sustained environmental transmissibility. The objective of the study is to evaluate the impact of colonization and candidemia with CA on clinical outcome of patients in the ICU.

Methods: This retrospective study is based on electronic records of patients admitted during 1st of January 2024 – 30th of September 2025 in the medical ICU that received weekly fungal screening. Patients admitted in the ICU for invasive candidiasis and patients screened for colonization after positive blood cultures were excluded. Risk factors for CA infection were also analyzed. Data was collected and analyzed using jamovi (version 2.3).

Results: From 402 patients admitted in the ICU during the study period 81 (20.14%) were colonized with CA: 54 at admission, 27 during hospitalization—median of 9 days (IQR 7–20). The median age of the cohort was 77 years (IQR 67–82), 75 years (IQR 66.3 – 81) in the colonization at admission group and 82 years (IQR 73– 85.5) in the colonization during ICU stay (p = 0.019). The RR for colonization in patients with previous healthcare contact was 2.41 (95% CI 1.15–5.06, p < 0.001). 9 patients developed CA fungemia at 14.9 days from positive screening (95% CI 3.59 – 26.2). The mortality rate in the cohort was 50%, with 44% in the colonized at admission and 63% in the second group (p = 0.116). The mortality rate in the patients with CA fungemia in the cohort was 100%.

Conclusions: CA colonization and infection represent a key challenge in the ICU setting. Healthcare contact and hospitalization in ICU more than one week represent the most important risk factor for colonization. Patients who became colonized in ICU were older, with prolonged hospitalization, probably due to very strict infection control. Even if colonization had no impact on mortality rate, fungemia significantly decreased the survival.

P252 Comparison of mortality and clinical characteristics in cases of VAP due to monobacterial multidrug-resistant A. baumannii and K. pneumoniae

A Ciginskiene, D Adukauskiene

Lithuanian University of Health Sciences, Medical Academy, Kaunas, Lithuania

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P252

Introduction: The type of causative pathogen in ventilator-associated pneumonia (VAP) due to multidrug-resistant (MDR) bacteria affects patient mortality [1]. This study aimed to compare 3, 7, 14, 30, 40, and 60-day mortality and clinical characteristics in patients with VAP due to monobacterial MDR A. baumannii (AB) vs K. pneumoniae (KP).

Methods: Retrospective data analysis of clinical characteristics and mortality in patients with monobacterial MDR AB and KP strains as pathogens of VAP over a 3-year period was carried out.

Results: Data of 141 monobacterial MDR VAP cases were evaluated: 105 (74.5%) due to AB, and 36 (25.5%) due to KP. The 3, 7, 14, 30, 40, 60 day mortality was 11 (10.5%), 21 (20.0%), 32 (30.5%), 60 (57.1%), 65 (61.9%), 65 (61.9%) in AB VAP cases, and 3 (8.3%), 3 (8.3%), 7 (19.4%), 10 (27.8%), 10 (27.8%), 10 (27.8%), in KP ones, all p < 0.001. Statistically significant differences of clinical characteristics in AB vs KP VAP were found in proportions of male 61 (58.1%) vs 32 (88.9%), p = 0.001, surgical admission 39 (37.1%) vs 25 (69.4%), p = 0.010, unconsciousness on ICU admission 15 (14.3%) vs 13 (36.1%), p = 0.005, use of IV antibiotics within 90 days before VAP onset 104 (99.0%) vs 28 (77.8%), p < 0.001, use of carbapenems 40 (38.1%) vs 3 (8.3%), p = 0.001, antifungals 16 (15.2%) vs 1 (2.8%), p = 0.048, presence of sepsis on VAP onset 93 (88.6%) vs 23 (63.9%), p = 0.001, and need of renal replacement therapy (RRT) during the ICU stay, 39 (37.1%) vs 6 (16.7%), p = 0.023.

Conclusions: All terms of mortality were higher in monobacterial MDR AB VAP cases. Patients with KP cases tended to die earlier (before 30 days). Mail sex, surgical admission, and unconsciousness were found to be associated with monobacterial KP VAP cases. However, in AB ones, there were previous antibacterial treatment, especially with carbapenems and antifungals, also sepsis on VAP onset, and renal failure with RRT.

P253 Secular trends in empiric antimicrobial treatment for severe community-acquired bacterial pneumonia in the United States

M Zilberberg1, M Greenberg2, B Nathanson3, Q Andan4, V Curt2, A Shorr5

1EviMed Research Group, LLC, Health Services Research, Goshen, USA, 2Eagle Pharmaceuticals, Inc., Woddcliff Lake, USA, 3OptiStatim, LLC, Longmeadow, USA, 4EviMed Research Group, LLC, Goshen, USA, 5Washington Hospital Center, Washington, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P253

Introduction: The COVID-19 pandemic precipitated a surge in misuse of broad-spectrum antimicrobials in community-acquired bacterial pneumonia (CABP), accelerating antimicrobial resistance (AMR). As a response, stewardship efforts to limit use of these overly broad antibiotics intensified. It remains unclear if these initiatives have impacted behavior.

Methods: We compared antibiotic prescribing for patients with severe CABP (sCABP) during two time periods, the first covering 2010–2015 [1] and the second 2021–24 (current analysis). Both analyses derived from the Premier Healthcare Database, and both defined sCABP by the IDSA/ATS major criteria, which require advanced respiratory or circulatory support or both. We compared baseline patient characteristics, empiric antibiotic selections, and hospital mortality.

Results: Among patients with CABP, the prevalence of sCABP was 14.1% (n = 21,805) initially and rose to 19.1% (n = 52,618) currently. Current cohort patients were older (mean age + SD 67.6 + 15.1 vs. 69.1 + 13.4 years), less likely male (51.7% vs. 49.7%), and less chronically ill (mean + SD Charlson comorbidity score 4.4 + 2.8 vs. 3.5 + 2.3). We observed a decrease in the frequency of early ICU admission (89.5% to 37.4%) and need for vasopressors (60.3% to 15.8%). The utilization of vancomycin (66.4% to 38.6%) and piperacillin/tazobactam (43.5% to 13.4%) fell substantially. Concomitantly there was an increase in the administration of ceftriaxone (35.9% to 60.3%) and azithromycin (31.9% to 49.4%). Hospital mortality went from 22.0% to 15.0%.

Conclusions: More patients presently receive empiric regimens for sCABP in accordance with both evidence-based guidelines and principles of antibiotic stewardship. The use of narrower spectrum agents indicates continued success of stewardship efforts nationally with the aim of containing the emergence of further AMR. Hospital mortality, though decreased, remains unacceptably high.

P254 Implementation of an antibiotic protocol for VAP prophylaxis in a neuro-ICU

F Daniele1, GM Mazza2, MC Laganà1, S Benedetti1, M Pagani3, A Guglielmi4

1Università di Pavia, Pavia, Italy, 2Fondazione IRCCS Policlinico San Matteo, Anestesia e rianimazione 1, Pavia, Italy, 3Fondazione IRCCS Policlinico San Matteo, Pavia, Italy, 4Alma Mater Ticinensis Foundation of Pavia under the Progetto Professionalità, Pavia, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P254

Introduction: The role of short-term antibiotic administration in Ventilator Associated Pneumonia (VAP) prophylaxis remains debated. Recently the PROPHY-VAP1 study has shown promising results by implementing a single dose of prophylactic ceftriaxone [1]. The aim of this study is to investigate if the routine implementation of such measure modifies clinical practice in patients with acute brain injury, in a tertiary hospital care of a high-income country.

Methods: This is a retrospective single centre observational study comparing two cohorts of patients with acute brain injury admitted to the ICU before (BC) and after (AC) the implementation of routine prophylactic ceftriaxone after intubation. We measured adherence to new protocol, VAP incidence, and antibiotic consumption as total days of antibiotic therapy (DOTs).

Results: We included in the analysis 124 patients (53 in the BC group, 71 in the AC group). No differences in terms of sex, age or severity were found between the cohorts, while rate of previous hospitalization higher in the AC group (29.6% vs 11.3%, p = 0.015). Adherence to new protocol was low (33%). VAP rates did not differ between the two cohorts, and the same was true for DOTs (10.63 ± 14.23 vs 13.85 ± 16.78, p = 0.25). We performed a secondary analysis inside the AC group comparing patients who actually received ceftriaxone vs those who didn’t, but the previous results were confirmed.

Conclusions: The implementation of protocolized prophylaxis with ceftriaxone did not reduce the total consumption of antibiotics. We hypothesize that such result was due to low adherence to the protocol, but further investigation is needed.

P256 Influenza A-associated pulmonary aspergillosis in patients admitted to the intensive care unit in China: a retrospective cohort study

M Xue1, Z Zhou1, R Zheng2, Y Yang1, H Qiu1, J Xie1

1Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China, 2Jiangsu Province Hospital, Nanjing, China, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P256

Introduction: Invasive pulmonary aspergillosis (IPA) is a life-threatening complication increasingly recognized in critically ill patients with influenza, a condition known as influenza-associated pulmonary aspergillosis (IAPA). However, large-scale data on the incidence, risk factors, and impact of IAPA specifically in Influenza A patients from China are limited.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective, multicenter cohort study across 20 intensive care units (ICUs) in China. The study included adult patients with severe Influenza A pneumonia admitted between November 1, 2024, and February 28, 2025. Patients were categorized into IPA and non-IPA groups based on the FUNDICU diagnostic criteria. Demographics, clinical characteristics, and outcomes were compared. Multivariate logistic regression and Cox regression analyses were used to identify independent risk factors.

Results: Of 526 patients with severe Influenza A, 179 (34.0%) were diagnosed with IPA. Compared to non-IPA patients, those with IPA were younger (69 vs. 72 years, p = 0.017), had higher APACHE II scores (22 vs. 19, p = 0.007), and had a higher prevalence of diabetes (48% vs. 36%, p = 0.017). IPA was independently associated with diabetes (aOR 1.86, 95% CI 1.25–2.79), age ≥ 75 years (aOR 1.80, 95% CI 1.20–2.73), continuous renal replacement therapy (aOR 1.95, 95% CI 1.18–3.22), and corticosteroid use during the ICU stay (aOR 1.61, 95% CI 1.09–2.39). The 60-day mortality was significantly higher in the IPA group (47.5% vs. 32.9%, p = 0.001). Hematologic disorders (aHR 4.61, 95% CI 1.43–14.84) and elevated C-reactive protein levels (aHR 1.01 per unit increase, 95% CI 1.00–1.01) were independent risk factors for IPA mortality (Figure).

Conclusions: IPA is a frequent and fatal complication in critically ill patients with Influenza A in China, occurring in over one-third of ICU admissions. Key risk factors include diabetes, advanced age, organ support, and corticosteroid use. Increased awareness of risk factors and early mycological screening are crucial.

P257 Financial benefit with clarithromycin treatment in hospital stay: an analysis of the ACCESS randomized clinical trial

V Rapti1, E Tasouli2, A Kotsaki3, G Niotis4, C Loutos5, E Christaki6, EJ Giamarellos-Bourboulis3, G Poulakou1

1National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, 3rd Department of Internal Medicine, Athens, Greece, 2Thriasio General Hospital, 1st Department of Internal Medicine, Elefsis, Greece, 3National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, 4th Department of Internal Medicine, Athens, Greece, 4National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, 2nd Department of Propedeutic Medicine, Athens, Greece, 5Hellenic Institute for the Study of Sepsis, Athens, Greece, 6University of Ioannina, 2nd Department of Internal Medicine, Ioannina, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P257

Introduction: In the ACCESS randomized controlled trial (NCT04724044) addition of clarithromycin to standard-of-care (SoC) antibiotics provided substantial clinical benefit in adult patients hospitalized for community-acquired pneumonia (CAP), including early clinical responses and decrease of the need for mechanical ventilation and transfer to the Intensive Care Unit (ICU) [1]. We investigated the impact of clarithromycin treatment on the total cost of hospital stay.

Methods: Patients with CAP, total SOFA score 2 or more and blood procalcitonin 0.25 ng/ml or more were randomized to seven days of treatment with SoC and placebo or SoC and clarithromycin. The overall cost of hospital stay was explored using three approaches: a) the cumulative daily cost for 90 days; b) differences in per diem costs between groups; and c) the total cost of ICU stay.

Results: Substantial decrease of the cumulative cost was apparent as early as day 3 (Figure). Per-diem costs of hospital stay were 27.22 Euros less in the clarithromycin group. The mean (SE) cost of ICU stay was €931.2 (250.1) in the placebo arm and €191.8 (78.5) in the clarithromycin arm (p: 0.005).

Conclusions: The addition of clarithromycin to SoC antibiotics substantially decreased the cost of management of hospitalized patients with CAP in the ACCESS trial. Reduction in ICU-related costs in the clarithromycin group was a major contributor to the overall savings. The ACCESS trial was funded by Abbott Products Operations which had no role in study design, data collection, data analysis, or data interpretation.

P258 Reproducing sepsis endotypes of interferon-gamma and immunoparalysis in animal models

P Koufargyris, E Karagianni, K Leventogiannis, EJ Giamarellos-Bourboulis.

National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, 4th Department of Internal Medicine, Athens, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P258

Introduction: Functional classification recognizes two major sepsis endotypes: sepsis-induced immunoparalysis (SII) and IFNγ-driven sepsis (IDS) [1]. Given the lack of established preclinical models, we aimed to develop two mice models of SII and IDS.

Methods: 88 C57BL/6 mice (both sexes, < 16 weeks) were divided into 5 groups: A) intravenous challenge (IV) with phosphate buffered saline (PBS) followed by sham abdominal operation after five days (Sham), B) IV challenge with lipopolysaccharide (LPS) followed by Sham, C) IV PBS followed by 50% cecal ligation and puncture (CLP), D) IV LPS followed by CLP and E) IV challenge by Escherichia coli followed by CLP. Mice challenged by E.coli were resuscitated with antibiotics. Mice were sacrificed 48 h after surgery. Splenocytes and bone marrow cells were isolated and stimulated with LPS or heat-killed Candida albicans for the production of tumour necrosis factor-alpha (TNFα) and CXCL9.

Results: Splenocytes of the E.coli + CLP group were exhausted for TNFα production. LPS + CLP group exhibited most of CXCL9 in plasma and from bone marrow cells (Figure).

Conclusions: The combination of pre-treatment with E.coli followed by CLP can be used to model SII, while LPS + CLP can be used to model IDS.

P259 Prospective validation of sepsis endotype classification: results from the EMBRACE trial

A Alevizou1, A Efthymiou2, A Lavrentieva3, E Antoniadou4, G Dimopoulos5, D Liatsi6, E Mouloudi7, S Sympardi8, A Ioakeimidou9, EJ Giamarellos-Bourboulis1

1National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, 4th Department of Internal Medicine, Athens, Greece, 2AHEPA Thessaloniki General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Thessaloniki, Greece, 3G. Papanikolaou Thessaloniki General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Thessaloniki, Greece, 4G. Gennimatas Thessaloniki General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Thessaloniki, Greece, 5National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, Intensive Care Unit, Alexandra Athens General Hospital, Athens, Greece, 6Papageorgiou Thessaloniki General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Thessaloniki, Greece, 7Ippokrateion Thessaloniki General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Thessaloniki, Greece, 8Thriasion Elefsis General Hospital, 1st Department of Internal Medicine, Elefsis, Greece, 9Asklipeion Voula General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Voula, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P259

Introduction: A recent retrospective analysis of 5,503 septic patients across three countries identified four endotypes: macrophage activation-like syndrome (MALS); interferon-gamma driven sepsis (IDS); sepsis induced immunoparalysis (SII) and adaptive endotype [1]. EMBRACE (NCT06694701) is an ongoing, multicentre, phase 2a, double-blind, randomised controlled trial investigating whether treatment with emapalumab improves outcomes for patients with interferon-gamma (IFNγ)-driven sepsis (IDS). Analysis of patients screened for eligibility provides prospective validation of the four endotypes.

Methods: Between March and October 2025, 374 patients who met the Sepsis-3 criteria were screened for eligibility. Blood samples were analyzed by flow cytometry for human leukocyte antigen-DR (HLA-DR) receptors expressed on CD45/CD14-monocytes, and by enzyme immunosorbent assay for ferritin, interferon-gamma (IFNγ) and CXCL9. The following definitions were applied: macrophage activation-like syndrome (MALS) as ferritin > 4,420 ng/mL; interferon-gamma driven sepsis (IDS) as the combination of ≥ 8,000 HLA-DR receptors with detectable IFN-γ and CXCL9 > 2,200 pg/mL; sepsis induced immunoparalysis (SII) as < 8,000 HLA-DR receptors; and the adaptive endotype as ≥ 8,000 HLA-DR receptors with detectable IFN-γ and CXCL9 < 2,200 pg/mL.

Results: The distribution across the four sepsis endotypes is presented in the Figure: MALS, n = 14 (3.7%); IDS, n = 74 (19.8%); SII, n = 154 (41.2%); adaptive endotype, n = 132 (35.3%). Among patients with SII, 14 patients (3.7%) exhibited elevated CXCL9. Classification within 6–12 h of blood sample collection was achievable, demonstrating the feasibility of real-time endotype determination and supporting the potential for timely, targeted therapeutic interventions.

Conclusions: The described frequency of sepsis endotypes [1] was prospectively validated and confirmed using the screening data from the EMBRACE trial.

Giamarellos-Bourboulis EJ et al. eBioMedicine 2024;109:105414

P260 Integrating inflammatory and endothelial biomarkers in machine learning models for predicting sepsis development in critically ill patients

I Papapanagiotou1, AG Vassiliou2, V Giannopoulou3, A Karalis1, Z Mastora4, S Kokkoris3, S Golemati3, O Kampouropoulou3, CS Vrettou3, I Dimopoulou3

1IB Hellas, Athens, Greece, 2National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, 1st Department of Critical Care Medicine Pulmonary Services, GP Livanos and M Simou Laboratories, Athens, Greece, 3National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, Athens, Greece, 4General Hospital of Thoracic Diseases Sotiria, Athens, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P260

Introduction: Sepsis mortality remains high despite advances in research and clinical care, making accurate prediction crucial. Using machine learning (ML) methods, we developed models that, apart from vital signs and routine blood parameters, incorporate inflammatory and endothelial markers and compared their performance against traditional clinical scoring systems.

Methods: Our retrospective dataset contained 480 patients hospitalized in the intensive care unit (ICU) of Evangelismos Hospital. The patients were non-septic on admission, while 43 developed sepsis during ICU stay. Interleukin (IL)-6 and soluble endothelial protein C receptor (sEPCR) were measured by ELISA. Single time points upon ICU admission (first 24-h) were recorded and used as input for the ML models. Missing value imputation was handled natively by the models. Recursive feature elimination was used to select the final set of 37 features. The ML algorithms Random Forest (RF), XGBoost (XGB), LightGBM (LGBM), and Ensemble (RF, XGB, LGBM) were used. SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) was used to explain the model output.

Results: Our best performing model in numerical metrics was the Ensemble model, achieving 91.48% AUC (85.80–96.46, 95% CIs). The top 10 ranked features in Gini feature importance rankings were IL-6, procalcitonin, sEPCR, lymphocytes (%), total bilirubin, albumin, C-reactive protein, hemoglobin, platelet count, and white blood cell count. Our model significantly outperformed traditional scoring systems such as SOFA and APACHE II scores (p < 0.05) (Figure).

Conclusions: Our ML models integrating inflammatory and endothelial biomarkers demonstrated promising discrimination and higher numerical metrics than traditional clinical scoring systems for sepsis prediction. However, their clinical utility requires further validation through prospective, multicenter studies with larger datasets.

P261 Reinforcement learning for treatment decision-making in sepsis: a scoping review

ZY Tang1, PL Sun1, RT Liang2, SQ Tong1, Y Yang1, MC Yang3, H Chen1

1Southeast University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China, 2Chengdu University of Technology, College of Computer Science and Cyber Security, Chengdu, China, 3The University of Hong Kong, Li Ka Shing Faculty of Medicine, Hong Kong, SAR China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P261

Introduction: Sepsis management involves sequential decisions that align with reinforcement learning (RL), yet current sepsis RL applications remain exploratory with methodological gaps. This scoping review characterises model construction, clinical tasks, and key limitations in the existing literature.

Methods: We systematically searched six databases and two preprint servers through 4 July 2025 and screened all records against pre-specified inclusion and exclusion criteria. The project was prospectively registered on OSF.

Results: Of the 1,515 records identified, 72 studies were included. Most focused on optimising vasopressor therapy and fluid resuscitation using retrospective ICU data (Table). Designs varied widely in states, actions, objectives, algorithms, and policy evaluation, with limited work on interpretability, generalisability, and safety.

Conclusions: RL is a promising tool for sepsis decision support, but evidence is early and heterogeneous, and key gaps and future directions are summarised in the Table.

P262 A digital twin model uncovers treatable traits and enable personalized treatment suggestions

L Mellhammar1, AM Scott2, E Malmström2, AG Gustafsson2, A Bakochi3, M Tirthankar2, L Thelaus2, F Kahn2, J Malmström2, A Linder2

1Faculty of Medicine, Lund University, Department of Clinical Sciences, Lund, Sweden, 2Faculty of Medicine, Lund University, Lund, Sweden, 3Swedish National Infrastructure for Biological Mass Spectrometry, Lund, Sweden

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P262

Introduction: We have constructed a digital twin model based on clinical and proteomic data for sepsis stratification in patients with suspected sepsis using machine-learning. As patients with similar clinical features are adjacent in the digital twin model, we here explore molecular trajectories through the digital twin model to suggest therapeutically actionable pathways.

Methods: Patients in a testing cohort with suspected sepsis (n = 680) were split into a low-risk and a high-risk group depending on their estimated rate of developing sepsis using the digital twin model. The mean importance of the 10 most important proteins associated with predicting sepsis were identified. A pathway for individual patients in a validation cohort with suspected sepsis (n = 253) was extracted from an area in the digital twin model with poor outcome (i.e. where the neighbors have persistent organ dysfunction) to an area of good outcome (i.e. where the neighbors have low persistent organ dysfunction). The shortest path from the poor outcome to the good outcome areas were identified and its corresponding personalized molecular profile trajectories, based on the 10 proteins in the model (Figure).

Results: Most proteins were broadly associated with persistent organ dysfunction whereas some proteins displayed a more variable response. These proteins could act as direct targets for treatment or as proxy proteins that implicate their associated biological pathways as targets. Examples in our model are that lowering von Willebrand factor (VWF) and FGL1 levels are linked to better predicted outcomes. FGL1 is an immune checkpoint ligand for LAG-3 that drives T-cell dysfunction. Excess VWF with reduced ADAMTS13 activity promotes thrombosis and multi-organ dysfunction. Targeting these trajectories may benefit high-VWF or FGL 1 patients.

Conclusions: These findings demonstrate that molecular digital twin models can identify potential therapeutically actionable pathways associated with persistent organ damage for certain patients at the time-of-admission.

Figure (abstract P262) A selected representation of an extracted pathway of a new patient

A selected representation of an extracted pathway of a new patient

P265 Procalcitonin and C-reactive protein and their correlation as predictive markers of positive cultures in neurocritical patients during the orotracheal intubation phase in an intensive care unit of a hospital in Northeast Mexico

LGD Gaucin Delgado, MRR Rodriguez Reyna, L Velasco Morales, GA Aguirre Gomez, OI Aguilera Olvera, JA Villalobos Silva

Hospital Regional de Alta Especialidad Ciudad Victoria, Tamaulipas, ICU, Ciudad Victoria, Mexico

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P265

Introduction: In neurocritical patients undergoing orotracheal intubation, the main complication is ventilator-associated pneumonia. This condition increases morbidity and mortality; therefore, a retrospective study was conducted to evaluate the correlation between C-reactive protein (CRP) and procalcitonin—our most accessible acute-phase reactants—and the development of infectious agents identified through bronchial secretion or sputum cultures.

Methods: A retrospective descriptive study was performed using clinical records from 34 orotracheally intubated patients during the 2023–2024 period. Demographic, laboratory, and microbiological data were collected and analyzed using descriptive statistics.

Results: Of the 34 patients included, 18 had positive bronchial cultures. Those with positive cultures had a mean CRP of 91.15 mg/dL, whereas the 16 patients with negative cultures had a mean CRP of 86.09 mg/dL (Figure). Procalcitonin levels averaged 18.78 ng/mL in patients with positive cultures versus 20.09 ng/mL in those with negative cultures. These findings indicate no correlation of these markers and the presence of a positive culture. No meaningful differences were observed between marker levels and culture positivity.

Conclusions: No correlation was identified between procalcitonin, C-reactive protein, and the presence of positive cultures.

P266 Validating pancreatic stone protein for early sepsis detection and outcome prediction in community acquired infections: evidence from a tertiary medical center

C Michailides1, M Lagadinou1, T Paraskevas2, K Papantoniou3, EK Velissari1, E Georgopoulou1, N Polychronopoulos1, E Kechagia1, D Velissaris1, M Marangos1

1University Hospital of Patras, Internal Medicine, Patras, Greece, 2University Hospital of Patras, Nephrology, Patras, Greece, 3University Hospital of Patras, Gastroenterology, Patras, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P266

Introduction: Evaluation of pancreatic stone protein (PSP) is effective and possibly inferior to other commonly used biomarkers in predicting unfavorable outcomes in multiple infections and sepsis. We aim to validate PSP in patients with community acquired infections to establish its role in point-of-care setting.

Methods: Adult patients consecutively admitted to the Emergency Department (ED) of a tertiary medical center, with the diagnosis of intra-abdominal infection (IAI), urinary tract infection (UTI) or lower respiratory tract infection (LRTI) who met the inclusion criteria were enrolled. PSP was measured in whole blood, within one hour since admission, by spectrophotometry using abioSCOPE device. Statistical analysis was performed, and a cut-off value for PSP to predict the composite outcome of sepsis, readmission, antibiotic treatment escalation and need for invasive treatment was estimated. Patients were followed for 28 days to document their outcomes.

Results: A total of 101 patients were included. 52.5% had LRTI, 36.6% had UTI and 18.8% had IAI, 12.9% > 1 infection site. Our primary outcome met statistical significance, as PSP predicted the composite outcome of sepsis, readmission, antibiotic treatment escalation and need of invasive treatment with an area under curve (AUC) = 0.844 (95% CI 0.767–0.920), in the optimal cut-off of 48.5 ng/dL (Figure). PSP predicted sepsis with an AUC = 0.892 (95% CI 0.826–0.956) and was also an independent risk factor for sepsis and mortality after age adjustment. PSP outscored other sepsis biomarkers for sepsis and the composite outcome. It was also correlated with SOFA day 1, SOFA peak and qSOFA independently of renal function.

Conclusions: PSP is a valuable biomarker that can rule out sepsis and identify low-risk patients, giving insights regarding their clinical management. It outperforms other sepsis biomarkers and relates with frequently used severity assessment scores, such as SOFA. In the ED, PSP can identify infected patients at high risk for sepsis despite low qSOFA scores.

P267 Heparin binding protein in sepsis. A comprehensive overview of pathophysiology, clinical usage and utility as biomarker

C Michailides, F Tasouli, E Georgopoulou, C Chatzigrigoriadis, N Polychronopoulos, E Kechagia, EK Velissari, M Lagadinou, D Velissaris

University Hospital of Patras, Internal Medicine, Patras, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P267

Introduction: Heparin binding protein (HBP) is an enzymatically inactive protein of the serine protease family which dramatizes important role in host response to stress, especially infection and sepsis. It is produced by activated neutrophils due to a variety of stimuli and is part of the immune response that leads to macrophage, lymphocytes and neutrophils activation and monocytes adhesion (Figure). Its most common repository is the azurophilic granules of the neutrophils.

Methods: A PubMed search was conducted between November 2024 and February 2025 using the following terms: ((heparin binding protein OR HBP) and (sepsis OR pathophysiology OR prognosis OR diagnosis)). All types of studies were potentially eligible for inclusion in this review. We excluded expert opinions, studies referring to child population and animal studies.

Results: HBP has been studied as a biomarker for several infections, including central nervous system infection, respiratory tract infection and urinary tract infection and in several settings, including Emergency Department and Intensive Care Unit, with promising results. As a biomarker for infection and sepsis, HBP has been compared to other commonly used biomarkers such as neutrophil to lymphocyte ratio, white blood count, C-reactive protein and procalcitonin, with at least comparable performance. Its sharp increase is promising for the early detection of sepsis.

Conclusions: Heparin binding protein is a promising biomarker for sepsis and infection because of the ability to differentiate inflammatory conditions from infections and bacterial from non-bacterial causes of infection. The sepsis-related organ damage, as it is represented by Sequential Organ Failure Assessment score can also be reflected by the proportional to the score, HBP increase. Thus, it could serve as an early detector of sepsis and stratify patients causatively and according to their organ dysfunction risk. Consequently, HBP could be a helpful and promising biomarker for sepsis and infection.

P269 Prognostic performance of IL-6 and STREM-1 compared with conventional markers in adults with sepsis and septic shock

L Ratiani1, N Gvajaia1, G Janigashvili2, L Gabunia2

1The First University Clinic of Tbilisi State Medical University, Department of Anesthesiology and Reanimatology, Tbilisi, Georgia, 2The First University Clinic of Tbilisi State Medical University, Department of Clinical Pharmacology, Tbilisi, Georgia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P269

Introduction: This study evaluated whether interleukin-6 (IL-6) and soluble triggering receptor expressed on myeloid cells-1 (sTREM-1) provide superior prognostic information compared with lactate, C-reactive protein (CRP), and leukocyte count in adults with sepsis or septic shock.

Methods: A prospective cohort of 60 adults admitted with sepsis or septic shock to the First University Clinic, Tbilisi (October 2022–August 2023), was studied. Blood samples were taken on admission and days 1, 2, 3, and 5. IL-6, sTREM-1, and CRP were assessed by electrochemiluminescence; lactate and leukocytes by routine assays. Group differences were analysed using the Kruskal–Wallis test.

Results: Mortality was 76% in septic shock and 38% in sepsis (χ2 = 4.43, p = 0.035) (Figure). Non-survivors had persistently elevated lactate, while survivors showed declines. Baseline lactate and leukocyte count differentiated selected groups (e.g., shock deaths vs shock survivors: lactate p = 0.002; leukocytes p = 0.006), whereas CRP showed no discriminatory value. IL-6 and sTREM-1 levels were higher in non-survivors and decreased in survivors. Within septic shock, both biomarkers distinguished survivors from non-survivors (sTREM-1: H = 8.464, p = 0.003; IL-6: H = 5.377, p = 0.020). sTREM-1 also separated shock deaths from sepsis with recovery (H = 11.097, p = 0.001).

Conclusions: Serial IL-6 and sTREM-1 outperform CRP and leukocyte count and complement lactate for early risk stratification in sepsis, particularly in septic shock. Their integration into routine assessment may improve identification of high-risk patients and guide timely management.

P270 A novel molecular phenotype based on longitudinal changes in serum Il-6 levels in acute inflammatory diseases

H Matsumoto, Y Mitsuyama, T Ebihara, J Oda

The Department of Traumatology and Acute Critical Medicine, The University of Osaka, Suita, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P270

Introduction: Interleukin-6 (IL-6) is a central cytokine that orchestrates systemic inflammatory responses and immune–metabolic networks during acute inflammation. However, in critical care, no cross-disease molecular phenotyping approach has been established based on temporal changes in serum IL-6 levels. Although IL-6 contributes to endothelial injury, hypercoagulability, multiorgan failure, and mortality, phenotypes that capture its longitudinal behavior remain poorly characterized. The aim of study is to define novel phenotypes based on longitudinal changes in serum IL-6 levels and to clarify their association with clinical characteristics and patient outcomes.

Methods: This prospective observational study enrolled patients with acute inflammatory conditions transported to the Osaka University Critical Care Center. Eligible conditions included burns, heat stroke, trauma, intoxication, cardiac and neurological emergencies, infections, and post-resuscitation encephalopathy. Serum IL-6 levels were measured on Day 1 and on Days 3–8 after ICU admission. For each patient, the slope of IL-6 change over time was calculated and categorized as decreasing (Class 1, slope < 0) or non-decreasing (Class 2, slope ≥ 0). Survival was analyzed using the Kaplan–Meier method.

Results: Across all acute inflammatory etiologies, serum IL-6 levels were markedly elevated, and patients were stratified based on temporal IL-6 dynamics. Class 1 included a higher proportion of infectious cases and more frequent use of mechanical ventilation. Class 2 patients were younger than those in Class 1. Kaplan–Meier analysis demonstrated a trend toward poorer survival in Class 2, despite relatively lower initial IL-6 levels, suggesting the presence of a persistent inflammatory state that cannot be explained by baseline IL-6 levels alone.

Conclusions: Phenotyping based on longitudinal changes in serum IL-6 levels identified novel molecular clusters shared across diverse acute inflammatory diseases.

P271 Viscoelastic coagulation monitor as a point-of-care test for early sepsis detection

P Soulioti1, AE Rekoumi1, G Damoraki1, K Leventogiannis1, E Polyzogopoulou2, J Parissis2, EJ Giamarellos-Bourboulis1

1National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, 4th Department of Internal Medicine, Athens, Greece, 2National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, Emergency Department, Athens, Greece

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P271

Introduction: Sepsis is often associated with clinical or subclinical coagulopathy. Viscoelastic Coagulation Monitoring (VCM) captures 8 coagulation variables in one hour and it is studied as a point-of-care (POC) tool of early sepsis detection in the emergency department (ED).

Methods: 147 adults admitted at the ED with suspicion of infection were prospectively enrolled. A blood draw via peripheral venipuncture was obtained upon admission and on three consecutive days for patients remaining hospitalized; coagulation tests and inflammatory mediators were measured. Sepsis classification was done by Sepsis-3.

Results: Clotting time was significantly (p: 0.007) lower in sepsis (median: 322.0, 95% CI: 252.0–391.5) versus non-sepsis patients (Median: 370.5, 95% CI: 296.50–426.2; p: 0.007). The area under the curve of ROC for sepsis diagnosis was 0.649. One cut-off of CT less than 290 s was identified for sepsis diagnosis (negative predictive value 79.2%). In the multivariate analysis, CT was an independent variable of sepsis (Table). Out of range VCM measurements accumulated over time for both groups of comparison, indicating persistence and progression over time.

Conclusions: VCM can be a one POC tool for sepsis detection at the ED.

P272 Multiclass classification of sepsis using subtype ensembles

LN Ward1, J Kennedy1, CCH Chang2, DC Angus1, L Tang2, CW Seymour1

1University of Pittsburgh, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Pittsburgh, USA, 2University of Pittsburgh, Department of Biostatistics, Pittsburgh, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P272

Introduction: Multiple approaches identify sepsis subtypes, yet they may not agree and have significant overlap. Rather than choosing a single approach, multiclass classification creates ensembles of subtypes, yet little is known about their epidemiology and outcomes.

Methods: We performed a secondary analysis of a multicenter prospective cohort study of critically ill adults with sepsis in the MARS cohort, identified with Sepsis-2 or Sepsis-3 criteria upon ICU admission [1]. Clinical, biomarker, and transcriptomic data were used to assign each patient to four subtyping approaches – SENECA, MARS, ARDS subphenotypes, and SRS [1–4]. A portion of patients were further labeled by Dysregulated Immune Profile (DIP) [5]. Ensembles (n = 64) were created using membership from four labels, illustrated with heatmaps, and compared across 28-day in-hospital mortality.

Results: Among 522 patients, the mean age was 61 ± 15 years, 57% were men, and mean SOFA score was 7.3 ± 3.96. There were 42 ensembles identified out of 64 possible. The 4 ensembles with the most patients were γ-Mars3-Hypoinflammatory-SRS2 (12%), γ-Mars2-Hyperinflammatory-SRS1 (9.2%), δ-Mars2-Hyperinflammatory-SRS1 (6.3%), and γ-Mars1-Hypoinflammatory-SRS2 (5.8%). Among these 4 ensembles, there were significant differences in SOFA and APACHE scores and presence of septic shock. Of ensembles with 10 or more patients, the range of 28-day mortality was 0 to 52%, with the δ-Mars1-Hyperinflammatory-SRS1 ensemble the greatest (52% (95% CI: 31%, 73%)) (Figure).

Conclusions: In a prospective cohort study, patients can be feasibly grouped into sepsis subtype ensembles with clinical, biomarker, and transcriptomic data, suggesting potential for further study of mechanistic targets and ensemble-level treatment effects.

P273 Protein quantification platforms for use in critical care precision medicine trials: a scoping review

R McMullan on behalf of the Queen’s University Belfast, Critical Care and Respiratory Research Group1, MR Smit2, V Scaravilli3, J Millar4, J Kataoka5, LM Hays6, K Reddy1

1Wellcome-Wolfson Institute for Experimental Medicine, Queen’s University Belfast, Northern Ireland, UK, 2Department of Intensive Care, Amsterdam UMC, University of Amsterdam, Netherlands, 3Dipartimento di Scienze della Salute, Università degli Studi di Milano Bicocca, Monza, Italy, 4University of Edinburgh Medical School, Edinburgh, Scotland, UK, 5Department of Pulmonary and Critical Care Medicine, Tokyo Bay Urayasu Ichikawa Medical Center, Urayasu, Japan, 6University College Dublin Clinical Research Centre, St. Vincent's University Hospital, Dublin, Ireland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P273

Introduction: Critical care research is undergoing a paradigm shift with the identification of treatment responsive phenotypes across clinical syndromes such as ARDS and sepsis. Phenotype identification has driven the development of classifiers that use panels of cytokines and endothelial injury markers to allocate patients to precision medicine strata. This scoping review summarises available automated protein quantification devices to support the design of biomarker stratified clinical trials.

Methods: We conducted a scoping review (protocol: 10.17605/OSF.IO/FU546). Searches of MEDLINE, Embase and the Espacenet patent database were conducted to 4 April 2025. We included automated devices that quantitatively measured human blood proteins and excluded proof-of-concept, semi-quantitative, microbiological and animal studies.

Results: We included 612 records describing 163 automated devices from 60 manufacturers (Figure). Among multiplex platforms, ELLA (Bio-Techne, Minneapolis, USA) and Simoa HD-X (Quanterix, Billerica, USA) were most frequently reported, with smaller numbers using AFIAS devices (Boditech Med Inc., Chuncheon, South Korea) and Randox Evidence analysers (Randox Laboratories, Crumlin, Northern Ireland). Cytokines were reported in 52 records, with IL-6 being the most commonly reported (38 records), however endothelial injury markers (e.g. angiopoietin-1, angiopoietin-2, VCAM-1 and ICAM-1) were quantified in only four records.

Conclusions: Many automated platforms already exist that can quantify cytokines required for biologically informed phenotyping in critical care. Leveraging existing multiplex cartridges and prioritising key endothelial injury biomarkers on these platforms could markedly accelerate translation of ARDS and sepsis phenotypes into precision medicine trials.

P274 Machine-learning algorithm identifies STREM1 has a key biomarker for outcome prediction in a mixed-ICU population

C de Roquetaillade

APHP, Paris, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P274

Introduction: Prognostic assessment in the intensive care unit (ICU) typically relies on severity scores or single biomarkers. Machine learning (ML) may refine risk stratification by integrating multiple biomarkers and clinical variables. To compare the predictive value of inflammatory biomarkers individually and combined using ML for 90-day mortality. Secondary objective was prediction of major adverse kidney events (MAKE).

Methods: Post-hoc analysis of the FROG-ICU cohort including invasively ventilated or vasopressor-dependent patients. Fourteen plasma biomarkers were measured. ML models included LASSO and Random Forest (RF). Variable importance was assessed by mean decrease in accuracy. External validation used the MARS sepsis cohort.

Results: Among 2,061 FROG-ICU patients, 33.2% died by day-90. ML models combining biomarkers and clinical variables performed well for mortality prediction (AUC 0.73 LASSO; 0.74 RF) and outperformed SAPS II (AUC 0.64). sTREM-1 was the top predictor in variable-importance analyses and performed similarly to ML models when used alone (AUC 0.72). For MAKE, sTREM-1 also showed high performance (AUC 0.78) comparable to ML (AUC 0.80–0.84). Results were confirmed in the external MARS cohort (Figure).

Conclusions: ML analysis identified sTREM-1 as a key prognostic biomarker across heterogeneous ICU populations, predicting both mortality and kidney-related outcomes. These findings support further investigation of TREM-1-targeted therapeutic strategies.

P275 Predictive value of serum sPD-L1 combined with disease severity scores for 28-day mortality in sepsis

C Wang, X Song

Shandong Public Health Clinical Center, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Ji nan, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P275

Introduction: Sepsis is a life-threatening syndrome of organ dysfunction resulting from a dysregulated host immune response to infection. Early identification of high-risk patients is essential to improve outcomes, yet no validated method accurately predicts sepsis prognosis. This study aimed to develop an optimized prognostic model by integrating the immune biomarker soluble programmed death-ligand 1 (sPD-L1) with conventional severity scores.

Methods: A retrospective analysis was conducted in the Department of Critical Care Medicine, Shandong Provincial Hospital, from June 2022 to December 2023. A total of 164 septic patients were enrolled, and their clinical data and serum samples were analyzed. The prognostic value of sPD-L1 alone and in combination with established scoring systems—Acute Physiology and Chronic Health Evaluation II (APACHE II), Sequential Organ Failure Assessment (SOFA), and Sepsis Severity Score (SSS)—was evaluated using receiver operating characteristic (ROC) analysis to predict 28-day mortality.

Results: Among the traditional scores, SOFA showed the best predictive power for 28-day mortality (AUC = 0.799), followed by SSS (AUC = 0.739) and APACHE II (AUC = 0.701) (Table). Serum sPD-L1 also exhibited moderate prognostic value (AUC = 0.712; optimal cutoff = 48.99 pg/mL). Incorporating sPD-L1 into each scoring system improved predictive accuracy, with the sPD-L1 + SOFA model performing best (AUC = 0.846; sensitivity = 83.3%; specificity = 73.5%).

Conclusions: sPD-L1 is significantly associated with 28-day mortality in sepsis and serves as an independent prognostic marker. By complementing the SOFA score’s limited ability to capture immune dysfunction, sPD-L1 enhances risk stratification and outcome prediction. The combined sPD-L1 + SOFA model provides a more accurate and immunologically informed approach for early prognosis in septic patients.

Among all models, the sPD-L1 + SOFA combination achieved the best predictive performance, underscoring the complementary role of immune markers in refining outcome assessment

P276 Evaluation of calprotectin as a proxy biomarker for sepsis immune dysfunction classification and 28-day mortality

A Alevizou1, I Alevroudis2, Z Aidoni3, S Avgeri4, I Giagtzoglou5, T Agapitou6, C Charatsis7, R Van Amstel8, S Weis9, MG Netea10

1Hellenic Institute for the Study of Sepsis, Athens, Greece, 2Ippokrateion Thessaloniki General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Thessaloniki, Greece, 3AHEPA Thessaloniki General Hospital, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Thessaloniki, Greece, 4Sotiria Athens General Hospital, Polyvalent Intensive Care Unit, Athens, Greece, 5Asklipeion Voula General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Voula, Greece, 6Sotiria Athens General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit of Center for Respiratory Failure, Athens, Greece, 7Theageneio Thessaloniki General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Thessaloniki, Greece, 8Amsterdam Medical University Center, Department of Intensive Care Medicine, Amsterdam, Netherlands, 9Jena University Hospital, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Care, Jena, Germany, 10Radboud University Medical Center, Department of Internal Medicine and Radboud Center for Infectious Diseases, Nijmegen, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P276

Introduction: The ImmunoSep trial (NCT04990232) classified Sepsis-3 patients using blood ferritin and expression of HLA-DR on monocytes. Ferritin > 4,420 ng/mL classified macrophage activation-like syndrome (MALS); the combination of ferritin ≤ 4,420 ng/mL and HLA-DR < 5,000 sepsis-induced immunoparalysis (SII); and the remaining patients were labeled as unclassified for immune state [1]. Calprotectin, predominantly produced by neutrophils and monocytes, contributes to both pro-inflammatory responses and progression toward SII. The added value in outcome prediction was studied.

Methods: In this exploratory post-hoc analysis, plasma calprotectin (S100A8/A9) was measured in 559 of 672 screened patients, using the GCAL® Calprotectin Reagent Kit. Enrolled patients received treatment with precision immunotherapy (anakinra or recombinant human IFN-gamma) or placebo for 15 days. The primary endpoint was the improvement of organ function by day 9 using the SOFA score; 28-day mortality was a secondary endpoint.

Results: Among these 559 patients, 40 were classified as MALS, 230 as SII and 289 were unclassified. Median calprotectin was 7.37, 6.20 and 5.03 mg/l respectively (p = 0.016 MALS vs unclassified; and p = 0.002 SSI vs unclassified). The Youden index of one receiver operator characteristics curve analysis to discriminate between MALS/SSI and unclassified was 7.75 mg/l. Among patients randomized to treatment, 94 had calprotectin ≥ 7.75 mg/L. In this population, precision immunotherapy provided better odds for survival (Figure).

Conclusions: Findings from the ImmunoSep randomized controlled trial suggest that calprotectin may further select patients with MALS or SII who are most likely to benefit from immunotherapy in terms of survival.

P277 The effect of statin on sepsis mortality: a systematic review and meta-analysis

T Wang, JT Bao, X Zhou, MF Wu, Y Yang, F Peng

Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P277

Introduction: Sepsis remains a major global health problem with high mortality and limited effective adjunctive therapies [1]. Statins have anti-inflammatory, immunomodulatory, and endothelial-stabilizing effects that may provide benefit, but previous randomized clinical trials (RCTs) show inconsistent results. We performed a systematic review and meta-analysis to evaluate the association between statin therapy and mortality in critically ill patients with sepsis.

Methods: Following PRISMA guidelines, we searched PubMed, Embase, Web of Science, and the Cochrane Library through October 14, 2025. RCTs comparing statins with placebo in adult sepsis patients and reporting mortality outcomes were included. Risk ratios (RRs) with 95% CIs were pooled using inverse variance–weighted models. Trial Sequential Analysis (TSA) assessed robustness and evidence sufficiency. Prespecified subgroup analyses were conducted by statin type and enrollment source.

Results: Ten RCTs (n = 1063) were included (Figure). Six trials (n = 613) reported short-term mortality, with statins significantly reducing risk (RR 0.73; 95% CI 0.57–0.92; I2 = 3%). TSA supported this as a reliable finding. Four trials (n = 557) reported long-term mortality, which also favored statins (RR 0.77; 95% CI 0.61–0.90; I2 = 0%), but TSA indicated insufficient cumulative evidence. Subgroup analysis suggested statin-specific effects, with simvastatin showing a stronger mortality reduction than atorvastatin.

Conclusions: Statin therapy was associated with a modest and TSA-supported reduction in short-term mortality in sepsis, whereas evidence for long-term benefit remains inconclusive. Given limited sample sizes and trial variability, these findings should be interpreted cautiously. Larger, high-quality RCTs are needed before statins can be recommended as routine adjunctive therapy for sepsis.

P278 Xuebijing injection ameliorates cytokine storm by regulating monocyte function via the ACTN4/STAT1 pathway in sepsis

D Hu1, Y Zhu1, L Liu1, S Chen1, H Xue1, Y Wang2, S Liu1

1Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Trauma Center, Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Nanjing, China, 2The First People´s Hospital of Lianyungang, Lianyungang, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P278

Introduction: Excessive production triggers a cytokine storm, leading to tissue injury, multiple organ dysfunction, and poor clinical outcomes in sepsis patients. Monocytes play a pivotal role in host defense by secreting a diverse array of cytokines that initiate and regulate inflammatory processes. Previous studies demonstrated that Xuebijing injection (XBJ) reduced 28-day mortality compared with placebo and exerts therapeutic effects on by regulating interferon (IFN) signaling in monocytes. Nevertheless, its specific molecular targets and underlying mechanisms remain unknow.

Methods: THP-1 cells were stimulated with LPS to establish an inflammatory model. Four groups were designed: PBS + Veh (vehicle), PBS + XBJ, LPS + Veh, and LPS + XBJ. IFNs, interferon-stimulated genes (ISGs) and inflammatory cytokines were quantified via RT-qPCR and ELISA. Drug affinity responsive target stability (DARTS) identified direct protein targets of XBJ. AlphaFold 3 predicted the target protein structure, followed by virtual screening and molecular docking to pinpoint key bioactive components. WB and Co-IP validated target expression and pathway interactions.

Results: Compared with the PBS + Veh, the LPS + Veh displayed remarkably upregulated expression of IFNs, ISGs and pro-inflammatory cytokines, while XBJ treatment in the LPS + XBJ markedly reversed these upregulatory effects. DARTS analysis identified α-actinin-4 (ACTN4) as a direct target of XBJ. Molecular docking results revealed strong binding affinities between ACTN4 and two key components: Miltionone II and stigmasterol. WB assays demonstrated that LPS stimulation downregulated ACTN4 expression and upregulated STAT1 expression, whereas XBJ treatment reversed these LPS-induced changes. Additionally, Co-IP assays confirmed the interaction between STAT1 and ACTN4 proteins.

Conclusions: XBJ mitigates sepsis-associated cytokine storm by modulating the ACTN4/STAT1 pathway in monocytes. ACTN4 may be a direct target, with miltionone II and stigmasterol as potential key active constituents.

P279 Heterogeneous treatment effect of xuebijing injection on 28-day mortality in mechanically ventilated patients

S Chen1, L Liu1, H Xue1, D Hu1, Y Zhu1, Y Yang1, H Qiu1, S Liu2

1Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Trauma Center,, Nanjing, China, 2Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P279

Introduction: Sepsis remains a leading cause of critical illness and death in intensive care units (ICU), particularly among patients requiring mechanical ventilation (MV). Xuebijing (XBJ) injection, a botanical formulation, has shown survival benefits in sepsis, but its heterogeneous treatment effects (HTE) in MV patients remains unclear.

Methods: A post hoc secondary analysis of multi-center EXIT-SEP randomized controlled trial was conducted, which enrolled adults with sepsis from 45 ICUs in [removed], involving 946 MV patients (463 XBJ, 483 placebo). HTE was assessed through subgroup analysis, causal forest and Bayesian regression to identify patient characteristics predictive of treatment benefit.

Results: XBJ significantly reduced 28-day mortality (26.1% vs. 32.9%; HR: 0.79, 95%CI: 0.65–0.96; p = 0.02), with the greatest benefit observed in males, non-smokers, and patients normal BMI. Causal forest modeling revealed considerable variability in treatment effects, with the highest benefit observed in patients with lower lactate, higher PaO2/FiO2 ratios, and preserved organ function. Patients in the highest benefit tertile (Q3) had markedly lower mortality with XBJ (1.5% vs. 9.0%; ARR: 7.5%, p = 0.04), while the lowest tertile (Q1) showed no benefit (p = 0.15).

Conclusions: XBJ reduced 28-day mortality in MV patients with sepsis, but treatment effects varied considerably across subgroups. Precision approaches may help identify patients most likely to benefit, supporting individualized sepsis management in the ICU.

P280 Heterogeneous treatment effects of xuebijing injection in patients with sepsis-induced coagulopathy

XR Lou, N Shi, RX Yu, H Chen, Y Yang, YZ Huang, SQ Liu, JF Xie, XJ Wu, HB Qiu

Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P280

Introduction: Patients with sepsis-induced coagulopathy (SIC) have a high mortality risk. Although randomized trials suggest that xuebijing injection (XBJ) can improve outcomes in sepsis, it is unclear whether SIC patients derive similar benefit. This study aims to determine the efficacy of XBJ in patients with SIC and to evaluate how the treatment effect is modified by heterogeneity of treatment effects (HTE) across individuals.

Methods: This exploratory analysis of the EXIT-SEP trial compared XBJ with placebo in sepsis patients from 45 intensive care units across China, enrolled between October 2017 and June 2019. A total of 546 patients who met the SIC diagnostic criteria were included. The primary outcome was 28 mortality. HTE was evaluated using conventional subgroup analysis, risk-based analysis, and effect-based analysis.

Results: A total of 546 patients were included (279 in the placebo group and 267 in the XBJ group), and baseline demographic characteristics were similar between the two groups. There was no statistically significant difference in 28-day mortality between the groups (placebo: 29.7%, XBJ: 22.8%; P = 0.083). Conventional subgroup analyses suggested that the effect of XBJ on mortality differed according to septic shock status (yes vs. no; posterior probability of a difference in OR: 97.8%) and SIC score (4–5 vs. 6; posterior probability of a difference in OR: 99.3%). In the risk-based analysis, patients at the lowest risk of death were most likely to benefit from XBJ (posterior probability of OR < 1: 99.4%) (Figure). In the effect-based analysis, patients with younger age, better coagulation function, and lower proportions of shock and mechanical ventilation at baseline were more likely to benefit from XBJ treatment.

Conclusions: In patients with SIC, the effect of XBJ appeared heterogeneous, with greater benefit among those who were less severely ill at baseline. These findings highlight the importance of accounting for HTE when designing and analyzing randomized controlled trials in SIC.

P281 Dexamethasone reprograms immune signatures in severe COVID-19: a multi-algorithm feature-selection analysis

C Grimm1, L Saager1, K Meissner1, S Pöhlmann2, A Eichmann3, O Moerer1, M Osuchowksi3, T Skirecki4, B Scicluna5, M Winkler1

1University Medical Center Göttingen, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Göttingen, Germany, 2Infection Biology Unit, German Primate Center – Leibniz Institute for Primate Research, Göttingen, Germany, 3Ludwig Boltzmann Institute for Traumatology The Research Center in Cooperation with AUVA, Vienna, Austria, 4Laboratory of Flow Cytometry, Centre of Postgraduate Medical Education, Warsaw, Poland, 5Department of Applied Biomedical Science, Faculty of Health Sciences, Mater Dei Hospital, University of Malta, Msida, Malta

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P281

Introduction: Despite its proven survival benefit [1], how dexamethasone (DEX) modulates peripheral immunity in severe COVID-19 remains insufficiently understood.

Methods: We conducted an observational ICU cohort study at the University Medical Centre Göttingen(03/2020–03/2021; ethics #24/4/19Ü) including 45 adults with severe COVID-19 (DEX n = 18; no DEXn = 27). Evolving guidelines enabled comparison of patients receiving DEX (6 mg/day, ≤ 10 days) with earlier untreated patients. Serial blood and PBMC samples underwent broad immune profiling covering T-cell activation/adhesion/exhaustion markers, B-cell naïve/memory subsets, plasmablasts, and monocyte subsets (CD163, CD38, HLA-DR, GILZ). Endothelial/coagulation-related extracellular vesicles and cytokines/soluble proteins were quantified. Five feature-selection algorithms (random forest, LASSO, sPLS-DA, logistic regression, Boruta) identified treatment-associated markers; variables selected by ≥ 3 methods defined consensus features. UMAP was used for visualization.

Results: Eight immune parameters consistently distinguished DEX-treated from untreated patients (Figure). Non-classical monocytes showed increased CD163 and reduced HLA-DR; intermediate monocytes were decreased. T-cell activation/adhesion markers (LFA-1 on CD4⁺, CD49d on CD8⁺) and CD3⁺ T-cell proportions were lower under DEX. CD24⁺ IgD⁻IgM⁻ B cells were expanded. Tissue-factor–positive CMG⁺ endothelial/coagulation events were markedly reduced. UMAP embedding showed clear separation of treatment groups.

Conclusions: Multi-algorithm feature selection revealed a distinct DEX-associated immune signature insevere COVID-19, characterized by anti-inflammatory monocyte polarization, reduced T-cell activation, expansion of CD24⁺ IgD⁻ IgM⁻ B cells, and dampened endothelial/coagulation activity. These findings provide mechanistic insight into corticosteroid responsiveness and may inform immune-guided treatment strategies.

P282 Trimodulin (polyvalent IgM, IgA, IgG solution) modulates Th17 cell populations

C Baumann, M Visser, F Bohländer

Biotest AG, Dreieich, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P282

Introduction: CD4⁺ T helper 17 (Th17) cells are critical mediators of host defense and inflammation. Their dysregulation and excessive IL-17A secretion have been linked to the pathogenesis observed in sepsis and severe community-acquired pneumonia (sCAP). Targeted modulation of Th17 cells therefore represents a promising therapeutic approach. The present study investigated the immunomodulatory effects of trimodulin a polyvalent immunoglobulin preparation comprising ~ 23% IgM, ~ 21% IgA and ~ 56% IgG (Biotest AG) on Th17 cells in vitro.

Methods: Naïve CD4⁺ T cells were isolated from healthy donor blood, differentiated into Th17 cells (co-stimulation with anti-CD3/CD28 and cytokines TGF-β1/IL-6), and treated with trimodulin at concentrations of 1 g/L or 7.5 g/L for four days. Th17 differentiation and proliferation were assessed by flow cytometry using lineage-specific surface markers (CD4, CD25 and CD196) and the transcription factor RORγt. Cytokine concentrations (TNF, IL-2, IFN-γ, IL-10, IL-6, IL-4, TGF-β and IL-17A) were quantified in culture supernatants using a cytometric bead array and ELISA.

Results: High-dose trimodulin (7.5 g/L) treatment led to a significant reduction in Th17 cell frequency and proliferation compared with untreated controls. Cytokine analysis confirmed a marked decrease in secretion of pro-inflammatory mediators (TNF, IL-2, IFN-γ) as well as IL-10. While the formulation buffer displayed some dose-dependent inhibitory effects, trimodulin exhibited an additional suppressive effect on Th17 cells beyond the buffer control.

Conclusions: These data demonstrate that the IgM, IgA, IgG solution trimodulin effectively attenuates Th17 cell differentiation, proliferation, and cytokine secretion in vitro (Figure). The findings suggest that trimodulin may contribute to restoring immune balance in hyperinflammatory conditions and support its potential as an immunomodulatory therapy for severe inflammatory diseases.

Trimodulin showed stronger suppression than the buffer control

P283 Trimodulin in severe community-acquired pneumonia: design and statistical concept of the randomized phase III ESsCAPE trial

R Ferrer1, C De la Fuente Martos2, V Šrámek3, V Fraipont4, SM Lobo5, P Biston6, D Wyncoll7, A Frenkel8, C Schmidt9, CC Heinz9

1Intensive Care Department, Vall d’Hebron Hospital Universitari. SODIR Research Group, Vall d’Hebron Institut de Recerca VHIR. Department of Medicine, Universitat Autònoma de Barcelona, Barcelona, Spain, 2University Hospital Reina Sofia, Cordoba Investigation Medical Institute IMIBIC, Spanish Ciberinfect Group, Córdoba, Spain, 3Department Anesthesia and Intensive Care, St. Anne´s University Hospital, Brno, Czech Republic, 4Intensive Care Unit, Citadelle Hospital, Liège, Belgium, 5Intensive Care Division, Hospital de Base and FAMERP, São José do Rio Preto, Brazil, 6CHU CharleroiChimay, Charleroi, Belgium, 7Dept of Critical Care, Guy´s St Thomas´ NHS Trust, London, UK, 8General Intensive Care Unit, Soroka University Medical Center, and The Faculty of Health Sciences, Ben Gurion University of the Negev, Beer Sheva, Israel, 9Corporate Clinical Research Development, Clinical Strategy Development, Biotest AG, Dreieich, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P283

Introduction: Severe community-acquired pneumonia (sCAP) remains a life-threatening condition associated with high mortality. Trimodulin is a polyvalent immunoglobulin preparation enriched with IgM and IgA in addition to IgG, supporting a wide array of anti-pathogen and immune modulating activities in the host. In the phase II CIGMA trial, trimodulin significantly reduced mortality in a subset of patients with sCAP on invasive mechanical ventilation (IMV) and with inflammation.

Methods: The ESsCAPE trial is a confirmatory, prospective, randomized (1:1), placebo-controlled, double-blind, multi-center, multi-national, phase III trial evaluating the efficacy and safety of trimodulin as adjunctive treatment to standard of care in patients with sCAP requiring IMV. Eligible patients have a C-reactive protein level of ≥ 70 mg/L. Early after starting IMV (< 24 h), patients receive daily infusions of 3.8 mL/kg/day for five consecutive days. Follow-up assessments are conducted until day 29 and on day 91 to evaluate short- and long-term outcomes (Figure). The primary efficacy endpoint is the 28-day all-cause mortality rate, evaluated in a predefined IgM-low subset of patients (IgM ≤ 0.8 g/L at baseline), as well as in the overall population. A flexible-adaptive design will be applied: Approximately 590 patients are expected to be required to demonstrate superiority of trimodulin over placebo. An interim analysis is planned after enrolling about 50% of patients to guide potential sample size adaptation. Further secondary endpoints address safety, the pharmacokinetics of trimodulin, and pharmacodynamic responses modulated by trimodulin. Analyses will be conducted on an intention-to-treat basis.

Results: Currently, the trial is actively enrolling patients.

Conclusions: Trimodulin may offer clinical benefit in critically ill patients with sCAP and systemic inflammation. The confirmatory ESsCAPE trial is expected to provide valuable data on its efficacy and safety in this patient population.

P284 Immunomodulatory therapy is associated with improved organ function in sepsis: a systematic review and meta-analysis

J Hollosi, C Turan, A Walter, P Bakos, L Zubek, C Alkan, K Koch, Z Molnar, P Hegyi

Semmelweis University, Centre for Translational Medicine, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P284

Introduction: Sepsis remains a leading cause of mortality in ICUs, hence adjunctive immunomodulatory therapies such as intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG), plasmapheresis, hemadsorption, or polymyxin B adsorption have been proposed, but their effects remain uncertain. We compared these therapies in sepsis with a network meta-analysis.

Methods: We systematically searched 3 major databases on October 30, 2024. Only randomized controlled trials (RCTs) of adult septic patients receiving immunomodulatory therapies reporting changes in organ dysfunction scores and mortality rates were included. Comparisons were performed using a frequentist network meta-analysis approach, applying a random effects model to account for the considerable between-study heterogeneity observed among the included studies. All analyses were conducted using the netmeta (v2.9–0) and dmetar (v0.1.0) packages in R (v4.4.2). Results were visualized via forest plot and surface under the cumulative ranking curve (SUCRA) plots.

Results: 70 RCTs met criteria. Polymyxin B showed significant improvement in SOFA score compared to standard therapy (mean difference -2.06 points, 95% CI: -3.17 to -0.90), while other therapies showed a tendency towards a lower score without reaching statistical significance. In addition, plasmapheresis showed a significant odds ratio of 0.48 (95% CI: 0.28 to 0.83) in 28-day mortality, while other therapies showed a tendency toward improved mortality odds as well (Figure).

Conclusions: Adjunctive immunomodulatory therapies may improve sepsis outcomes. Further trials are needed to clarify optimal patient selection and treatment strategies, but clinicians may consider these therapies alongside standard care.

P285 Do hemoperfusion systems deliver what they claim? An in vitro evaluation targeting inflammatory mediators

S Harm, C Schildböck, J Hartmann

University for Continuing Education Krems, Department for Biomedical Research, Krems, Austria

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P285

Introduction: Systemic inflammation and endotoxemia drive organ dysfunction in critical illness. Hemoperfusion devices are used to reduce circulating inflammatory mediators. We hypothesized that commonly used adsorbers differ markedly in their ability to remove inflammatory cytokines and endotoxin from human whole blood.

Methods: Three adsorbers with different marketed claims were tested: CytoSorb300 (cytokine removal; LOT 111424B) and Jafron HA380 (cytokine removal; LOT 2402280101) and Efferon LPS (cytokine + endotoxin removal; LOT 25-LEU001). Fresh heparinized whole blood was spiked with LPS (10 EU/mL) and incubated for 6 h at 37 °C to induce inflammation; IL-10 and PCT were added exogenously. Batch experiments (n = 6) ran for 2 h with an adsorber-to-blood ratio of 1:10, dynamic experiments for 6 h in a miniaturized extracorporeal circuit. Pre- and post-treatment levels of IL-6, IL-1β, IL-10, IL-18, TNF-α, PCT and LPS were measured using multiplex ELISA and an adapted limulus amebocyte lysate assay [1].

Results: None of the devices removed endotoxin. All systems reduced PCT. CytoSorb300 achieved the strongest cytokine reduction across IL-6, IL-10, TNF-α, IL-18 and IL-1β (Figure). Jafron HA380 showed only modest cytokine decreases. In contrast, the Efferon LPS adsorber, despite being marketed for LPS and cytokine removal, caused a marked increase in IL-1β and IL-18 in both models – indicative of NLRP3 inflammasome activation – and failed to remove endotoxin.

Conclusions: In our in-vitro whole blood setup, CytoSorb300 showed the most effective reduction of inflammatory mediators, Jafron HA380 demonstrated limited efficacy, and the Efferon LPS cartridge removed neither cytokines nor endotoxin while increasing inflammasome-related cytokines. These findings reveal substantial performance differences between hemoperfusion systems and support the need for rigorous preclinical testing prior to clinical application.

Acknowledgement: This study was funded by the government of lower Austria with the project ID #WST3-F-5030664/030-2022 (biomimetic surfaces) and Cytosorbents Europe GmbH.
