P286 Effect of acute blood purification using a novel blood purification column in critically ill ICU patients: a single-center retrospective study—the REMCYTO study

T Horiuchi, K Sato, T Taniguchi

Kanazawa University, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Kanazawa, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P286

Introduction: Critically ill patients in the intensive care unit (ICU) often require continuous kidney replacement therapy (CKRT). The blood purification column Remcyto™ developed by our group in 2023 showed favorable cytokine adsorption and biocompatibility in vitro [1]. This study aimed to evaluate its clinical utility.

Methods: We retrospectively included adult ICU patients between February 2024 and October 2025 who underwent CKRT for acute kidney injury (AKI) based on the KDIGO classification. Day 0 was defined as the start of CKRT, and data were collected through Day 4. Blood samples were analyzed for blood gas, complete blood count, and biochemistry including IL-6, lactate, and procalcitonin (PCT). SOFA scores and catecholamine index (CAI) were also calculated. The primary endpoints were changes over time in SOFA score, IL-6, lactate, CAI, and PCT from Day 0 to Day 4. The secondary endpoint was ICU length of stay. Continuous variables are presented as median (interquartile range). Changes over time in primary endpoints were evaluated using a linear mixed-effects model with Day as a fixed effect and patient ID as a random effect.

Results: Seventy patients (39 males, 31 females; median age 68.5 years) were included. Median ICU stay was 7 days. All primary endpoint measures showed significant decreases from Day 0 to Day 4:SOFA score decreased from 12 (10–16) to 11 (9–14), IL-6 from 1,786 (120–21,802) to 93 (28–227) pg/mL, lactate from 2.0 (1.1–4.2) to 1.3 (0.9–1.6) mmol/L, CAI from 23.3 (4.8–34.1) to 0.9 (0–3.5), and PCT from 21.0 (1.8–60.4) to 6.6 (0.9–20.5) ng/mL (Figure).

Conclusions: In AKI patients treated with CKRT using the novel Remcyto™ column, inflammatory markers and hemodynamic indices improved over time. This device may be useful in the management of critically ill patients, and further validation in comparative and multicenter prospective studies is warranted.

P287 Detection of Oxiris membrane saturation based on endotoxin and cytokines adsorption kinetics in critically ill septic patients: protocol for the OxiCLEAR (Oxiris cytokines and endotoxin adsorption rate) study

D Smirnova1, M Broman2, G Villa3, O Sabelnikovs4

1Paulswe Stradins Clinical University Hospital, Department of Anesthesiology and Reanimatology, Riga, Latvia, 2Skåne University Hospital Perioperative and Intensive Care, Lund, Sweden, 3Department of Anesthesia and Intensive Care, Section of Oncological Anesthesia and Intensive Care, Careggi University Hospital, Florence, Italy, 4Riga Stradins University, Department of Anesthesiology, Intensive Care and Clinical Simulations, Riga, Latvia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P287

Introduction: Septic shock involves a dysregulated host response with excessive cytokines release, endotoxin burden, and progressive multi-organ dysfunction. The Oxiris membrane provides combined renal support and adsorptive removal of cytokines and endotoxin; however, the time-dependent decline in adsorptive capacity and optimal timing for membrane exchange remain unclear. We hypothesize that the Oxiris membrane exhibits progressive saturation of binding sites, reducing clearance efficacy. The OxiCLEAR study aims to characterize the adsorption kinetics of endotoxin and cytokines and identify the saturation point of the Oxiris membrane during continuous veno-venous hemofiltration (CVVH) in critically ill septic patients.

Methods: This single-centre prospective observational study will include adult patients with sepsis-associated acute kidney injury requiring standardized CVVH with Oxiris for ≥ 24 h. Endotoxin and cytokines concentrations will be measured in pre-filter, post-filter, and effluent samples at baseline, 1 h, 3 h, 6 h, 12 h, and 24 h using the same membrane set [Figure]. Endotoxins will be quantified by the LSBio Gram-Negative Endotoxin Competitive ELISA and cytokines by the Randox Evidence Investigator Cytokine and Growth Factors Array. Adsorptive efficiency will be calculated from inlet–outlet concentration gradients normalized to plasma flow, while effluent analyses will determine convective clearance. Hemodynamic parameters, organ dysfunction indices, and inflammatory markers will be recorded. Ethical approval: Riga Stradiņš University Ethics Committee (2-PĒK-4/429/2022).

Results: Not applicable.

Conclusions: This study will define the time-based profile of Oxiris adsorption and identify the point of functional saturation, establishing the effective adsorption window. The OxiCLEAR study is expected to provide evidence to optimize the timing of membrane replacement and enable more precise, targeted use of adsorptive renal support therapy in septic shock.Figure (abstract P287) Sampling scheme showing inlet (pre-filter), outlet (post-filter), and effluent sampling sites for evaluating endotoxin and cytokines adsorption at 0 (baseline), 1, 3, 6, 12, and 24 h during CVVH with the Oxiris set

Sampling scheme showing inlet (pre-filter), outlet (post-filter), and effluent sampling sites for evaluating endotoxin and cytokines adsorption at 0 (baseline), 1, 3, 6, 12, and 24 h during CVVH with the Oxiris set

P288 The use of Oxiris in the ICU: a Delphi consensus of recommendations

J Kurz1, G Villa2, N Van Regenmoertel3, M Bell4, A Mingoia5, F Ramasco6, T Rimmelé7, D Smirnova8, M Broman9

1Vantive Health, Medical Director, München, Germany, 2Department of Health Sciences, University of Florence, Italy; Department of Anesthesia and Intensive Care, Careggi Hospital, Italy, Florence, Italy, 3University Hospital, Department of Perioperative Medicine and Intensive Care PMI, Karolinska University Hospital, Stockholm, Sweden, 4 Karolinska Institutet, Department of Perioperative Medicine and Intensive Care PMI, Karolinska University Hospital, Stockholm, Sweden, 5 Centre Hospitalier, Critical Care Unit, Centre Hospitalier Wallonie Picardie, Tournai, Belgium, 6Hospital Universitario de La Princessa, Universidad Autónoma de Madrid UAM, Madrid, Spain, 7Université de Nice, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Nice University Hospital, Nice, France, 8University Hospital, Department of Anesthesiology, Intensive Care and Clinical Simulations, Riga Stradiņš University; Department of Anesthesiology and Reanimatology, Pauls Stradiņš Clinical University Hospital, Riga, Latvia, 9 Skane University Hospital, Perioperative and Intensive Care, Lund, Sweden

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P288

Introduction: The Acute Dialysis Quality Initiative (ADQI) has provided guidance on the management of acute kidney injury (AKI), including extracorporeal blood purification (EBP) techniques [1]. Recent studies have suggested EBP connected to a cardiopulmonary bypass (CPB) circuit results in a significant reduction in cardiac surgery-associated AKI [2]. Oxiris is an EBP device that provides three distinct mechanisms of purification, diffusion, convection and adsorption of cytokines and endotoxins, simultaneously. To provide guidance on the optimal use of Oxiris for critically ill patients, fourteen intensivists convened to reach a consensus on prescription, patient selection and monitoring of Oxiris treatment.

Methods: A consensus meeting was held in Rome on 20th November 2024, chaired by a non-voting clinical moderator with experience in blood purification and critical care. The overall process followed a modified Delphi methodology, which included a literature analysis, an online pre-meeting survey, an in-person consensus meeting, and an online post-meeting survey.

Results: Consensus was reached for multiple statements. Experts agreed that patients who have conditions characterized by dysregulated systemic inflammation and/or endotoxemia may benefit from treatment with Oxiris. There was a majority agreement that several clinical parameters should be evaluated in conjunction to indicate treatment effectiveness. Moreover, experts agreed that hemodynamic improvement is an adequate surrogate for treatment effectiveness.

Conclusions: This consensus process provided practical guidance on patient selection, treatment timing, prescription strategy and monitoring. Consensus was reached on its usefulness in sepsis and other conditions of acute systemic inflammation.

P289 Adjunctive cytokine hemoadsorption in toxic shock syndrome: a case series

A Burov1, M Maslennikova2, S Vladimirov2, A Bryleva2, N Matushkov2

1Kommunarka Medical Center, Intensive Care, Moscow, Russian Federation, 2Kommunarka Medical Center, Moscow, Russian Federation

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P289

Introduction: Toxic shock syndrome (TSS) carries high mortality despite antimicrobials and organ support. As additional therapeutic option for patients who do not respond to standard of care, use of inflammatory mediator adsorption may be considered. We describe our experience with adjunctive hemoadsorption in patients with TSS.

Methods: In this case series, we included seven consecutive ICU patients with TSS requiring ≥ 0.3 µg/kg/min norepinephrine despite standard care: four with necrotizing fasciitis, two with postpartum endometritis, and one with community-acquired pneumonia. In all patients, Streptococcus pyogenes or Streptococcus pneumoniae were identified as the pathogen. Each patient received blood purification approach, which included CRRT with an enhanced-permeability membrane (EMIC2) and an inflammatory mediator adsorber (CytoSorb) for 1–2 sessions. We report baseline characteristics and the evolution of the Vasoactive-Inotropic Score (VIS), serum lactate, interleukin-6 (IL-6), procalcitonin (PCT), and SOFA score over 72 h.

Results: Patients were severely ill at hemoadsorption initiation (median SOFA 16 [IQR 14–18], median norepinephrine 1.2 µg/kg/min [0.95–1.3]). Within 72 h, we observed a reduction in median SOFA score (from 16 to 6), VIS (from 120 to 35), lactate (from 3.4 to 1.2 mmol/L), PCT (from 52.6 to 18.65 ng/mL) and within 48 h- reduction in median IL-6 (from 3450 to 236 pg/mL). Shock was reversed (vasopressor-free for > 24h) in 6/7 patients. Six patients survived to ICU discharge, and one died from refractory multiorgan failure.

Conclusions: In this case series, adjunctive inflammatory mediators hemoadsorption was integrated into CRRT for severe TSS. Clinical improvement, including hemodynamic stabilization and MOF severity reduction, was observed following its application. These preliminary findings support the feasibility of this approach and justify further investigation in controlled trials.

P290 Metaanalysis of extracorporeal cytokine removal therapies in COVID-19

AM Kersten1, K Thiele2, A Krannich3, M Dreher4, N Marx1

1University Hospital Aachen, Department of Cardiology, Pulmonology, Angiology and Critical Care, Aachen, Germany, 2University Hospital Aachen, Department of Cardiology, Angiology and Critical Care, Aachen, Germany, 3BioStats GmbH, Nauen, Germany, 4University Hospital Aachen, Department of Pneumology and Critical Care, Aachen, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P290

Introduction: COVID-19 showed dysregulated host immune response and severe inflammation and endothelial injury. Extracorporeal blood purification techniques (CytoSorb) are adjunctive therapies to mitigate hyperinflammation. Clinical data supporting efficacy are sparse and inconsistent. This meta-analysis sought to evaluate outcomes in controlled and single-arm studies of extracorporeal cytokine removal use in critically ill COVID-19 patients.

Methods: We performed a comprehensive systematic literature search (PubMed, Cytosorbents database) to identify trials reporting mortality in critically ill COVID-19 treated with CytoSorb. We sratified into controlled (comparator arm) and single-arm trials (CT, ST). The longest reported mortality endpoint was analyzed, if it assessed mortality during COVID-19-related critical illness. When mortality timing was not stated, endpoint classification Referenced to the primary data source. Pooled OR for mortality were calculated for CT and were estimated for ST using random-effects models to account for between-study heterogeneity.

Results: We included 11 CT (3 randomized, 1 prospective, 7 retrospective;n = 507 (Figure, panel A). Pooled OR favored CytoSorb (OR 0.74; 95% CI: 0.27–1.98) with a large CI not reaching statistical significance. In 16 ST (n = 368, Figure, panel B) the random effects pooled mortality rate was 38% (95% CI: 0.29 – 0.47). This is lower than comparable reported mortality rates: ECMO 42–53%; CRRT without ECMO 44–53%. Previously reported concerns regarding potential harm by CytoSorb were not substantiated in this more comprehensive synthesis.

Conclusions: CytoSorb was associated with trends to lower mortality compared to benchmark outcomes. No signals of harm were detected. There was a high degree of heterogeneity (significant heterogeneity test and high I2). Use of ECMO and CRRT varied greatly between studies. The results justify continued differentiated evaluation of CytoSorb in severe hyperinflammatory conditions, both within and beyond the context of COVID-19.Figure (abstract P290) Analysis of A) controlled and b) single arm trials

Analysis of A) controlled and b) single arm trials

Introduction: Hemoperfusion therapy (HP) has been used for cytokine/endotoxin removal to improve the disturbed immune homeostasis in septic shock and severe ARDS [1]. P292 Early physiologic response to Jafron® hemoadsorption in refractory shock: a retrospective ICU cohort

CRC Cerca, MC Costa, I Quinteiro, A Balbino, L Santos, A Azevedo, AF Monteiro, AL Valente, L Bento

Unidade Local de Saúde do Hospital de São José Serviço de Medicina Intensiva Unidade de Urgência Médica, Lisboa, Portugal

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P292

Introduction: Hemoadsorption is increasingly used as adjunctive therapy in refractory shock to reduce cytokine-driven inflammation. Evidence suggests likely upsides in septic and hemorrhagic shock, mitigating ischemic-reperfusion injury, though clinical outcomes remain inconsistent. This study evaluates physiologic effects, safety, and short-term outcomes following hemoadsorption in critically ill patients.

Methods: We performed a single-center retrospective analysis of 15 ICU patients with refractory shock treated with Jafron® cartridges. Demographics, severity, vasopressor requirements, inflammatory biomarkers (CRP, PCT, IL-6), microbiology, and complications were recorded. Changes in SOFA, norepinephrine (NE) dose, lactate and biomarkers at 24 h were analyzed using paired non-parametric tests. ICU and 28-day mortality were assessed.

Results: Fifteen patients were included; 8 with septic shock, 6 with hemorrhagic shock and 1 with drug intoxication; 67% were male. Severity was high (mean SAPS II 63; mean SOFA 14). All required mechanical ventilation, with a median PaO₂/FiO₂ of 160. At 24 h, the SOFA score decreased from 14 to 12 (p < 0.01). Median norepinephrine dose fell from 1.2 ± 0.83 mcg/kg/h to 0.4 ± 0.34 mcg/kg/h (p < 0.01) and lactate declined from 2.5 ± 4.40 mmol/L to 1.7 ± 2.07 mmol/L (p < 0.01). PCT and IL-6 showed marked reductions, while CRP trended downward. Complications were limited to circuit issues. ICU mortality was 40%, and 28-day mortality 47%, consistent with expected severity. Survivors started hemoadsorption earlier (within 1 day) than non-survivors (within 3 days).

Conclusions: Hemoadsorption was associated with early improvement in organ dysfunction, hemodynamics, and inflammatory burden, with a good safety profile. Although findings support potential immunomodulatory benefit, the small cohort and retrospective design limit conclusions. Larger controlled studies are needed to define optimal timing, patient selection, and clinical impact.

P294 Hemoadsorption in critical care: real world outcomes from the international COSMOS registry

R Ferrer1, T Kirschning2, M Unglaube3, N Malewicz Oeck4, J Kreutz5, M Tholl6, B Tyczynski7, D Henzler8, T Klaus9, FS Taccone10

1Vall d'Hebron University Hospital, Shock, Organ Dysfunction and Resuscitation Research Group SODIR, Barcelona, Spain, 2Heart and Diabetes Center NRW, Bad Oeynhausen, Germany, 3Helios Dr. Horst Schmidt Klinik Wiesbaden, Wiesbaden, Germany, 4BG University Hospital Bergmannsheil, Bochum, Germany, 5University Hospital, Philipps University of Marburg, Marburg, Germany, 6Klinikum Oldenburg AoeR, Oldenburg, Germany, 7University Clinic Essen, Essen, Germany, 8Ruhr University Bochum, Hospital Herford, Herford, Germany, 9CytoSorbents Europe GmbH, Critical Care, Berlin, Germany, 10Hôpital Universitaire de Bruxelles HUB, Université Libre de Bruxelles ULB, Brussels, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P294

Introduction: COSMOS is a prospective, international registry (NCT05146336) capturing utilization patterns and clinical outcomes with CytoSorb® (CS) use in critical care patients.

Methods: Clinical parameters were assessed 24 h before and after CS therapy with a 90-day follow-up. Investigators reported device-related adverse events. Analyses followed a predefined statistical plan, using descriptive statistics and paired tests. Only patients with complete datasets were included.

Results: This analysis included 306 patients (30% female, mean age 58 ± 17) from 23 sites in 6 countries. Main indications were septic shock (46.9%), cardiogenic shock (13.4%), rhabdomyolysis (12.3%), liver failure (11.4%), and ARDS (6.0%). Overall SOFA score improved from 12 [9, 15] to 11 [8, 14] (p < 0.0001). ICU mortality was 32.7%, which was lower than predicted by risk scores. At 90 days survival rate was 56.5% with 90.1% being dialysis-free. No serious adverse device effects or device deficiencies were reported.

Conclusions: CS hemoadsorption as adjunct therapy in critical care patients is generally safe and helps achieve important treatment goals including reduction in vasopressors, improved oxygenation and fluid balance. P295 Combined double plasma molecular adsorption system (DPMAS) with sequential low-dose plasma exchange (LPE) and high volume plasma exchange (HPV) in acute-on-chronic liver failure: a prospective cohort study

V Xuan Toan1, D Ngoc Son2, B Van Cuong2, D Quoc Tuan2, N Cong Tan2, B Thi Huong Giang2, P The Thach2

1Bach Mai Hospital, ICU, HaNoi, Viet Nam, 2Bach Mai Hospital, HaNoi, Viet Nam

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P295

Introduction: ACLF causes multiple organ failure and high short-term mortality. DPMAS clears protein-bound toxins, while LPE/HVP replenishes plasma components. Their sequential use may enhance detoxification and improve short-term outcomes. This study compares DPMAS + LPE with HVP in ACLF patients.

Methods: A study at the Center for Critical Care Medicine, Bach Mai Hospital enrolled 54 ACLF patients (DPMAS + LPE: 32; HVP: 22) per APASL 2023 criteria. DPMAS used HA330-II and BS330 cartridges in series, followed by low-dose PEX (1.0–1.5 L). Clinical, biochemical, and inflammatory markers were assessed before and after each session. Primary outcomes were changes in ammonia, total/direct bilirubin; secondary outcomes were 30-day survival and clinical recovery.

Results: End of each session, the DPMAS + LPE group showed significantly greater reductions in total bilirubin (− 139.6 ± 86.2 vs − 88.0 ± 109.5 µmol/L, p < 0.01) and direct bilirubin (− 94.4 ± 66.1 vs − 59.6 ± 79.3 µmol/L, p < 0.01) compared with the HVP group, indicating superior detoxification efficacy (Figure). Ammonia levels decreased modestly in both groups without statistical significance (p = 0.999). At 30 days, survival rates were 87.5% in the DPMAS + LPE group and 81.8% in the HVP group (p = 0.564). The overall clinical success rate (complete or partial recovery) was higher in DPMAS + LPE (40.6%) than in HVP (27.3%), though not statistically significant (p = 0.389).

Conclusions: Combined DPMAS + LPE significantly reduced bilirubin and improved short-term biochemical recovery more than HVP, indicating superior clearance of protein-bound toxins. Its better clinical recovery trend suggests value as a bridge to hepatic regeneration, while similar 30-day survival shows both methods remain viable bridging options before liver transplantation.Figure (abstract P295) The Figure depicts the changes in ammonia, total bilirubin, and direct bilirubin from baseline (T₀) to immediately post-treatment (T₂), comparing the DPMAS + LPE group with the HVP group. P296 From liver transplantation to ischaemic cholangiopathy

M Pinheiro1, C Pratas1, N Germano1, É Mateus2, H Pinto Marques3

1Unidade Local de Saúde de São José Hospital Curry Cabral, Unidade de Cuidados Intensivos Polivalente, Lisboa, Portugal, 2Unidade Local de Saúde de São José Hospital Curry Cabral, Unidade de Transplantes, Lisboa, Portugal, 3Unidade Local de Saúde de São José Hospital Curry Cabral, Serviço de Cirurgia Hepatobiliopancreática, Lisboa, Portugal

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P296

Introduction: Liver transplantation (LT) is the gold standard treatment for end-stage hepatic failure, with an excellent survival rate lately. However, post-operative biliary complications are a frequent cause for patient morbidity and mortality. Ischaemic cholangiopathy (IC) is characterized by multiple diffuse non-anastomotic strictures affecting the graft biliary system in the absence of hepatic artery thrombosis or stenosis.

Methods: This study aims to explore potential risk factors for the development of IC after LT. We conducted a retrospective analysis of ICU admissions following LT from January 1, 2018, to December 31, 2019. Exclusion criteria were death or re-LT within 30 days after the initial LT. Data were extracted from hospital records, and statistical analysis was performed using SPSS v27.

Results: Of the 231 LTs performed in our center, 197 were included in this study, with 69.5% being men and a mean age of 53,3 years. Nine were excluded due to re-LT, and 25 due to death within 30 days after LT. LTs were mostly due to viral and/or alcoholic cirrhosis, with a mean MELD score of 16.4 and with 26.4% being urgent. Mean donor age was 62,3 years and mean cold ischemia time was 7 h. A total of 17 (8.6%) patients developed IC, 70% of them in the first 6 months. The variables that showed statistical correlation (p < 0.05) with the development of IC were the following: recipients’ age at the time of the LT, total days in the ICU, the occurrence of hepatic thrombosis, the number of red blood cell transfusions and maximum lactate measurement in the first 24 h post-LT. IC was associated (p < 0.05) with higher rate of re-LT (64.7% vs 3.9%) and shorter graft survival time (33.4 vs 81.7 months).

Conclusions: Our findings reflect the role of ischemic injury in the development of IC. However, other risk factors have been recognized in the literature. Due to the multifactorial nature of IC and the sample size of our study, further studies are warranted.

P297 Serial spleen stiffness measurement as a novel bedside prognostic biomarker in critically ill patients with cirrhosis

E Von Maldeghem, P Kalus, M Nanos, S Rusch, P Mester, V Pavel, M Müller-Schilling, STS Schmid

University Hospital Regensburg, Department of Internal Medicine I, Regensburg, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P297

Introduction: Non-invasive elastography techniques are widely used to assess liver fibrosis and portal hypertension. While liver stiffness measurement is well established, spleen stiffness has emerged as a promising surrogate marker of portal hypertension. Evidence in critically ill patients—especially in those with cirrhosis or acute-on-chronic liver failure (ACLF)—remains limited. This study evaluated the feasibility of serial spleen stiffness assessment using acoustic radiation force impulse (ARFI) elastography in the intensive care unit (ICU) and explored its prognostic relevance.

Methods: In this prospective study, 44 ICU patients with chronic liver disease underwent ARFI-based spleen stiffness measurements every 48 h. Demographic data, organ support therapies and clinical outcomes were systematically collected.

Results: Serial ARFI measurements of spleen stiffness were feasible in all included patients. Higher spleen stiffness values showed a significant association with the requirement for renal replacement therapy (p < 0.001) and invasive mechanical ventilation (p < 0.001). Spleen stiffness demonstrated an inverse correlation with ventilatory pressure parameters (p = 0.017). No correlation was observed with age, BMI or vasopressor support.

Conclusions: Serial spleen stiffness measurement using ARFI elastography is feasible, reproducible and robust in critically ill patients with cirrhosis. Beyond its role as a marker of portal hypertension, spleen stiffness reflects extrahepatic organ dysfunction and correlates with the development of organ failure. Dynamic spleen stiffness monitoring may therefore serve as a novel, non-invasive bedside biomarker to improve risk stratification and guide clinical decision-making in intensive care medicine.

P298 Preoperative and perioperative factors associated with ICU stay in cytoreductive surgery with intraperitoneal chemotherapy: secondary analysis with 2025

G Madrid1, D Diaz1, O Amaya1, A Romero1, A Ojeda1, J Gonzales2, DM Abril2

1Hospital Universitario Fundacion Santa Fe de Bogota, Anestesiologia, Bogotá, Colombia, 2Universidad de los Andes, Bogotá, Colombia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P298

Introduction: Cytoreductive surgery with HIPEC requires substantial postoperative critical care resources. In 2023, our group reported that anesthetic technique, intraoperative bleeding, and extubation site were significant determinants of ICU length of stay (LOS) in this population. With the expansion of the institutional registry through 2025, we conducted a secondary analysis to validate and strengthen these findings using a larger cohort.

Methods: This study expanded the original cohort (n = 216, 2007–2022) to include 245 patients operated between 2023 and 2025, applying the same inclusion and exclusion criteria. ICU LOS was reexamined in relation to key perioperative variables: anesthesia technique, surgical and anesthetic duration, transfusion requirements, colloid administration, intraoperative blood loss, and extubation site. Statistical analyses included Mann–Whitney U, chi-square tests, and multivariable regression models.

Results: The enlarged dataset confirmed all previously reported associations. Total intravenous anesthesia (TIVA) remained independently associated with shorter ICU LOS (median 3 vs 4 days; p = 0.0001), and extubation in the operating room continued to demonstrate a protective effect (2 vs 3 days; p = 0.000). Surgical time, anesthetic duration, blood loss, and red blood cell transfusion volume were again positively correlated with longer ICU stay (all p < 0.001). Colloid selection, particularly albumin or Voluven, was consistently associated with prolonged ICU LOS. No new confounders emerged after increasing the cohort size.

Conclusions: This secondary analysis, incorporating additional patients and expanded follow-up, reinforces the robustness of our earlier findings. TIVA and early extubation continue to appear as modifiable perioperative factors associated with reduced ICU burden in HIPEC patients. The reproducibility of these associations across independent cohort expansions highlights their clinical relevance and supports their integration into perioperative management strategies.

P299 Prognostic scores for short- and long-term mortality in acute-on-chronic liver failure in intensive care

P Baptista, M Ferreira, T Cardoso, GC Campello

Unidade Local de Saúde Tâmega e Sousa, Serviço de Medicina Intensiva, Guilhufe, Portugal

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P299

Introduction: Acute-on-chronic liver failure (ACLF) is a distinct entity from hepatic decompensation and often requires intensive care unit (ICU) admission. It affects 31% of patients with acute cirrhosis decompensation and carries higher mortality (34% versus 1.9%) [1,2]. Prognostic scores are essential to guide initiation and continuation of intensive care treatment in ACLF. This study describes ACLF episodes admitted to a secondary care ICU and evaluates general and specific prognostic scores for short- and long-term mortality.

Methods: Retrospective cohort of all ACLF cases admitted to the ICU of a secondary care hospital between January 2022 and December 2023. Discriminative performance of the Child–Pugh (CP) score, Model for End-Stage Liver Disease Sodium (MELD NA) score, Acute Physiology and Chronic Health Evaluation (APACHE II) score, Simplified Acute Physiology (SAPS II) score, and Chronic Liver Failure Consortium (CLIF-C) ACLF score was assessed using receiver operating characteristic (ROC) analysis.

Results: Among 80 patients with acute cirrhosis decompensation, 25% (n = 20) had ACLF. Their mean age ± SD was 55 ± 11 years, 75% were male, and 25% (n = 5) were referred for liver transplantation. At admission, 25% (n = 5) had grade 1, 35% (n = 7) grade 2, and 40% (n = 8) grade 3 ACLF. CP classes were 20% (n = 4) A, 20% (n = 4) B, and 60% (n = 12) C. Median (IQR) scores were: MELD NA 31 (22–36), SAPS II 45 (33–67), APACHE II 24 (19–24) and CLIF-C ACLF 52 (49–59). Mortality in ICU, in hospital, at 28 days and at 1-year was 30% (n = 6), 50% (n = 10), 55% (n = 11) and 65% (n = 13), respectively. The overall performance of the scores is summarized in the Table.

Conclusions: In this cohort, SAPS II best predicted ICU mortality, while CLIF-C ACLF was superior for hospital and long-term mortality. The specific scores like CP and MELD-NA, calculated in the first 24 h of admission, were not useful for this particular propose.Table (abstract P299) Performance of prognostic scores

P306 Urinary albumin-to-creatinine ratio for prediction of acute kidney injury: a systematic review and meta-analysis

N Kitisin1, A Serpa Neto2, N Raykateeraroj1, J Ismail2

1Faculty of Medicine, Siriraj Hospital, Mahidol University, Department of Anesthesiology, Bangkok, Thailand, 2Department of Intensive Care, Austin Hospital, Heidelberg, Victoria, Australia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P306

Introduction: The urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR) is a well-established marker of chronic kidney disease, but its utility for predicting acute kidney injury (AKI) in hospitalized adults remains uncertain. We evaluated UACR performance for predicting AKI incidence and progression.

Methods: We performed a PRISMA-compliant systematic review and meta-analysis [1] (PROSPERO CRD420250648041) of studies in adults (≥ 18 years) assessing UACR for AKI incidence or progression. Ovid MEDLINE, Embase and CENTRAL were searched to February 2025. Random-effects models pooled odds ratios (ORs), sensitivity, specificity and constructed a summary receiver operating characteristic (sROC) curve.

Results: Sixteen studies were included (13 AKI incidence; 3 AKI progression), enrolling 10,438 and 1,596 patients, respectively, across settings including cardiac surgery, sepsis, COVID-19, acute myocardial infarction and cirrhosis. Elevated UACR was associated with increased risk of AKI development (8 studies; pooled OR 1.45, 95% CI 1.10–1.90). Pooled sensitivity and specificity for AKI prediction were 0.71 (95% CI 0.59–0.80) and 0.67 (95% CI 0.56–0.76), with sROC area under the curve 0.74 (95% CI 0.64–0.84), indicating moderate diagnostic accuracy (Figure). In cardiac surgery patients, elevated UACR consistently predicted AKI (3 studies; pooled OR 1.56, 95% CI 1.14–2.15). For AKI progression, elevated UACR strongly predicted worsening stage (3 studies; pooled OR 3.76, 95% CI 2.59–5.45).

Conclusions: Elevated UACR is significantly associated with both development and progression of AKI in hospitalized adults and shows moderate diagnostic accuracy, particularly in cardiac surgery. Given its low cost, non-invasiveness and wide availability, UACR is a promising candidate for early AKI risk stratification; standardized thresholds and further validation are needed before routine implementation.Figure (abstract P306) Each numbered circle represents an individual study included in the meta-analysis. The red diamond marks the summary operating point, reflecting pooled sensitivity and specificity estimates. The solid black curve denotes the fitted sROC curve. The shaded blue ellipse illustrates the 95% confidence region, while the dashed blue contour indicates the 95% prediction region. The overall area under the curve (AUC) was 0.739 (95% CI: 0.641–0.837), indicating moderate diagnostic performance of UACR for predicting AKI. Pooled sensitivity and specificity were 0.707 (95% CI: 0.589–0.803) and 0.668 (95% CI: 0.561–0.760), respectively

P308 Hydrogen alleviates sepsis-associated acute kidney injury by enhancing mitophagy to inhibit STING-dependent cellular senescence

S Pei1, K Xie2

1Tianjin Medical University General Hospital, Tianjin, China, 2Tianjin Medical University General Hospital, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Tianjin, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P308

Introduction: Specific therapies for sepsis-associated acute kidney injury (SA-AKI) are limited. While cellular senescence contributes to its pathology, its initiating mechanisms remain poorly defined. We hypothesized that hydrogen (H₂) confers protection in SA-AKI by alleviating cellular senescence through modulation of the STING pathway.

Methods: We employed a murine cecal ligation and puncture (CLP) model and LPS-stimulated human renal tubular (HK-2) cells. Senescence was evaluated via SA-β-Gal staining, p16/p53 expression, EdU incorporation, and SASP. In vivo outcomes included survival and renal function. STING, identified through transcriptomics, was validated in SA-AKI patient serum. Functional studies used STING agonist (diABZI), knockout mice, siRNA, and the mitophagy inhibitor 3-MA. MtDNA cytosolic release, cGAS-STING-NF-κB signaling, and mitophagy were analyzed.

Results: SA-AKI models exhibited a pronounced senescent phenotype. H₂ inhalation significantly improved survival, restored renal function, and attenuated senescence and tissue injury. STING was upregulated in SA-AKI and suppressed by H₂. Crucially, STING activation abolished H₂’s benefits, whereas STING deficiency reproduced its anti-senescent effects. We demonstrated that LPS-induced mitochondrial damage provoked mtDNA release, activating the cGAS-STING-NF-κB cascade. H₂ augmented mitophagy, which cleared damaged mitochondria and cytosolic mtDNA. All protective effects of H₂ were reversed by 3-MA.

Conclusions: This study defines a novel pathogenic pathway in SA-AKI where mitochondrial damagepromotes STING-driven cellular senescence. Hydrogen protects by enhancing mitophagy to interrupt this pathway (Figure), positioning mitophagy augmentation as a compelling therapeutic strategy.Figure (abstract P308) The schematic illustrates the proposed mechanism without (Left Panel) and with (Right Panel) hydrogen (H₂) treatment

The schematic illustrates the proposed mechanism without (Left Panel) and with (Right Panel) hydrogen (H₂) treatment

P310 Risk factors and outcomes of postoperative acute kidney injury in acute type A aortic dissection surgery

N Raykateeraroj1, K Raksamanee1, H Mayingo1, L Weinberg2

1Faculty of Medicine Siriraj Hospital, Mahidol University, Department of Anesthesiology, Bangkok, Thailand, 2Austin Hospital, Department of Anesthesiology, Heidelberg, Victoria, Australia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P310

Introduction: This study aimed to determine the incidence, independent risk factors, and clinical outcomes of postoperative acute kidney injury (AKI) in patients undergoing surgery for acute type A aortic dissection (ATAAD), a complication associated with high mortality [1–3].

Methods: We conducted a retrospective cohort study of adult patients undergoing ATAAD repair between 2017 and 2023. Patients with end-stage renal disease were excluded. AKI was defined by Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO) criteria. Multivariable logistic regression identified independent risk factors.

Results: Of 246 patients, 99 (40.2%) developed postoperative AKI. Multivariable analysis identified pre-existing chronic kidney disease (CKD) and preoperative vasoactive-inotropic score (VIS) as independent predictors (Table). Patients with AKI had significantly higher rates of in-hospital mortality (18.2% vs 3.4%; p < 0.001) and 30-day mortality (15.2% vs 2.7%; p < 0.001). The AKI group also experienced prolonged mechanical ventilation (median 76 vs 41 h; p < 0.001) and longer intensive care unit (ICU) length of stay (median 7 vs 3 days; p < 0.001).

Conclusions: Postoperative AKI is common in ATAAD surgery and significantly impacts survival and resource utilization. Preoperative renal dysfunction and hemodynamic instability requiring inotropes are key predictors.

P311 Hemoadsorption therapy in rhabdomyolysis: report of the international, prospective COSMOS registry

J Kreutz1, C Scharf2, B Tyczynski3, R Ferrer4, M Unglaube5, D Henzler6, P Hohlstein7, N Malewicz Oeck8, T Klaus9, FS Taccone10

1University Hospital, Philipps University of Marburg, Department of Cardiology, Angiology, and Intensive Care Medicine, Marburg, Germany, 2LMU, Department of Anesthesiology, Munich, Germany, 3University Clinic Essen, Department of Medical Intensive Care, Essen, Germany, 4 Vall d´Hebron University Hospital, Shock, Organ Dysfunction and Resuscitation Research Group SODIR, Intensive Care Department, Barcelona, Spain, 5Helios Dr. Horst Schmidt Klinik Wiesbaden, Department of Intensive Care, Wiesbaden, Germany, 6Ruhr University Bochum, Klinikum Herford, Department of Anesthesiology, Surgical Intensive Care, Emergency and Pain Medicine, Herford, Germany, 7RWTH University Hospital Aachen, Department for Gastroenterology, abolic Disorders and Intensive Care Medicine Department of Medicine III, Aachen, Germany, 8BG University Hospital Bergmannsheil, Medical Faculty of Ruhr University Bochum, Department of Anesthesiology, Surgical Intensive Care, Emergency and Pain Medicine, Bochum, Germany, 9CytoSorbents Europe GmbH, Critical Care, Berlin, Germany, 10Hôpital Universitaire de Bruxelles HUB, Université Libre de Bruxelles ULB, Department of Intensive Care, Brussels, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P311

Introduction: Rhabdomyolysis with markedly elevated myoglobin is associated with acute kidney injury (AKI) and mortality. CytoSorb (CS) hemoadsorption has been used to remove myoglobin, but evidence remains limited. This study evaluated whether CS could reduce circulating myoglobin and therefore help to mitigate renal complications.

Methods: The COSMOS Registry (NCT05146336) is a prospective, international observational study assessing CS hemoadsorption in critically ill patients. Data were collected 24 h before and after CS therapy, at ICU and hospital discharge, 90 days post-treatment. Changes in biomarkers were analysed with Wilcoxon signed-rank test. Results are reported as median [Q1, Q3] or mean ± SD. Only patients with fully monitored data were included in the analysis.

Results: Forty-five patients (mean age 56 ± 20 years; 29% female; 11% trauma-induced) with rhabdomyolysis from 14 centers in 4 countries were included. Median APACHE II was 24 [18, 30]; 33% had pre-existing renal dysfunction (KDIGO 3 [1.5, 3.5]). CS was integrated into renal replacement therapy (RRT) in 88.6% and used stand-alone in 11.4%. Treatment significantly reduced median myoglobin (from 8,798 [4,232, 30,000] to 2,959 [774, 6,735] µg/L; p < 0.0001), with the percentage of critically elevated levels (> 10,000 µg/L) decreasing from 48.3% to 10.3% (p < 0.0001, Figure). CK (9,826 [2,390, 31,273] to 4,130 [1,046, 8,764] IU/L; p < 0.0001) and creatinine (2.3 [1.5, 3.6] to 1.8 [1.1, 2.8] mg/dL; p = 0.0004) also decreased. Median ICU stay was 15 [9, 26] days and RRT duration 10 [4, 12] days. 32 (71.1%) out of 45 patients survived from ICU stay. The percentage of patients remaining dialysis dependent declined from 25% at ICU discharge to 8% at 90-days. No serious device-related adverse events occurred.

Conclusions: CS hemoadsorption is associated with significant myoglobin reduction and may help to improve renal function in rhabdomyolysis. These real-world results require confirmation in controlled studies.Figure (abstract P311) Patient-level, pre- to post-CS myoglobin level

Patient-level, pre- to post-CS myoglobin level

P312 Development of a prediction score for continuous kidney replacement therapy-related hypothermia

P Chummano1, T Thongkam1, N Chansatchanali1, Y Sonprasom1, K Trongtrakul2, W Sirikul3

1Chiang Mai University, Faculty of Medicine, Chiang Mai, Thailand, 2Chiang Mai University, Department of Internal Medicine, Faculty of Medicine, Chiang Mai, Thailand, 3Chiang Mai University, Department of Community Medicine, Faculty of Medicine, Chiang Mai, Thailand

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P312

Introduction: Hypothermia is a common complication during continuous kidney replacement therapy (CKRT) and is associated with an increased mortality in critically ill patients. Due to the lack of practical prediction tools, this study aimed to develop CKRT-related hypothermia prediction score to facilitate early identification and guide clinical management.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective cohort study of critically ill patients who commenced CKRT in the 11-bed Medical Intensive Care Unit at the Faculty of Medicine, Chiang Mai University, between February 2018 and August 2024. Hypothermia was defined as a body temperature below 35 °C within the first 48 h of CKRT initiation. Potential predictors were identified using multivariable logistic regression analysis. Model calibration was assessed using the Hosmer–Lemeshow (HL) test and internal validation was performed using a 1,000-replication bootstrapping method.

Results: Among 214 patients (mean age 65 ± 17 years, 38.3% female, and APACHE II 28 ± 6), 51.9% (111 cases) experienced CKRT-related hypothermia. The predictors included in the final model and their assigned scores were: respiratory failure at ICU admission (+ 13 points), APACHE II score (1.5 points per point), pre-CKRT body temperature (-1 point per 0.1 °C increased), serum creatinine (-2 points per mg/dL increase), and level of total bilirubin (+ 1 point per mg/dL increase) (Table). The total score demonstrated good discrimination, with a C-statistic of 0.78; 95% CI 0.72–0.84. Model calibration (p = 0.47) indicated accepTable fit, and internal validation confirmed stable performance, with a C-statistic of 0.78; 95% CI, 0.71–0.84, p < 0.001.

Conclusions: Our prediction score demonstrated good discriminative performance in identifying patients with high risk for CKRT-related hypothermia in the derivation cohort. However, external validation is necessary to confirm its effectiveness and generalizability in clinical practice.Table (abstract P312) Predictive factors and scoring for hypothermia risk during continuous kidney replacement therapy (CKRT)

P313 Citrate anticoagulation in continuous renal replacement and effect on calcium balance: a single centre retrospective analysis

M Barker1, B Sanderson2, L Tovey2, M Khosravi2

1Guys and St Thomas NHS Foundation Trust, Pharmacy/Critical Care, London, UK, 2Guys and St Thomas NHS Foundation Trust, Critical Care, London, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P313

Introduction: There is limited information about total body calcium balance in patients receiving prolonged continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT) with citrate anticoagulation. A small cohort study suggests there may be a deficit in total calcium over time, risking demineralisation and fractures [1]. This may be heralded by a reduction in rate of calcium compared to citrate infusion over time.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective chart review to determine the effect of CRRT duration on the citrate/calcium infusion rates, ratios, and association with select patient characteristics (Table). Calcium and citrate rates and patient data were extracted from our clinical information system for patients who received citrate CRRT between Jan 2018 to 2023. Calcium/citrate ratios were calculated and a mixed linear regression model used to determine the effects of time on the ratio of citrate to calcium and association with the other selected demographics.

Results: 2,207 patients where found to have received citrate CRRT. Patients received citrate CRRT for a median of 5 days (IQR 3,11). Other demographics are shown in the Table. The fixed effect of time on Citrate/calcium infusion ratio was estimated to be an increase of 0.5 (95%CI: 0.4–0.7) every 28 days (p < 0.001). No association was found with any of the selected patient demographics, except ionised calcium.

Conclusions: There was a statistically significant increase in citrate/calcium ratio over time. The magnitude of this 0.5 increase over a short number of days is small and unlikely to be clinically significant for the majority of patients. Patients on citrate CRRT for several months however may be at risk, so further studies in this cohort are required.

P314 A post-market clinical performance and safety evaluation of GamCath High-Flow Dolphin Protect catheter

J Echeverri1, W Dabrowski2, M Winiarz3, M Szukala2, M Peters4, K Harenski5, T Kudelka4, M Zammit4, B Keller4

1Vantive U.S Healthcare LLC, Global Medical Affairs, Deerfield, USA, 2First Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Therapy Medical University of Lublin, Lublin, Poland, 3Independent Public Health Care Unit, Leczna, Poland, 4Vantive U.S Healthcare LLC, Deerfield, USA, 5Vantive Health Germany GmbH, Unterschleißheim, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P314

Introduction: Catheters are essential in acute therapy, but their safety profile is often underestimated. Catheter-related complications can lead to reduced catheter survival, which may compromise patient safety and reduce therapy efficacy. This study evaluated the real-world safety and performance of Gamcath Highflow Dolphin Protect Catheters; a type of coated catheter designed to prolong catheter survival and reduce the risk of infection.

Methods: A retrospective review of medical records was conducted for adult patients who received the Gamcath Dolphin Catheter for extracorporeal blood purification from 2018 to 2024 in 2 intensive care units in Poland. The catheters were inserted through jugular or femoral veins. Data on catheter survival, removal reasons, and patient outcomes were collected. Catheter survival was assessed using time-to-event analysis; removal due to complications was considered an event, while other outcomes were censored.

Results: Records of 75 patients were analysed; most were male (76.0%), with a mean age of 63.7 years. Primary diagnoses were acute renal failure (n = 33) or acute kidney injury (n = 30). Overall, 130 catheters were placed, of which only 9 (6.9%) were removed due to an event (Table). Notably, 12 catheters remained in place for at least 25 days, and 5 remained in place for at least 30 days. As fewer than 50% of catheters were removed due to an event, the median survival time was not reached. Thirty-three (44.0%) patients experienced 37 adverse events (21.8 events per 1000 catheter days), 36 of which were serious (21.3 events per 1000 catheter days) while 4 (infection, sepsis, and product issue) were related to the study device. Thirty patients (40.0%) died with the catheter in-place; none of these deaths were study device related.

Conclusions: These results support the performance and safety of Gamcath Highflow Dolphin Protect catheters, showing a low rate of complication-related removals and prolonged catheter survival over time.Table (abstract P314) Reported outcomes

P315 A computational approach for early detection of citrate accumulation in continuous kidney replacement therapy

MM Müller1, D Hansel2, J Bachmann3, J Bartussek3, O Sazpinar3, R Pohlmeier2, S David3

1University Hospital Zürich, Institute of Intensive Care Medicine, Zürich, Switzerland, 2Fresenius Medical Care Deutschland GmbH, Bad Homburg, Germany, 3University Hospital Zürich, Zürich, Switzerland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P315

Introduction: The aim of this study was to: (1) determine the incidence of citrate accumulation (CA) during CKRT with regional citrate anticoagulation (RCA); (2) evaluate its association with clinical outcomes; and (3) assess whether a computational algorithm developed to predict bicarbonate levels during CKRT can identify patients at risk of CA using the difference between predicted and measured bicarbonate levels (ΔHCO₃⁻).

Methods: This retrospective study included critically ill patients at the University Hospital Zurich (USZ) receiving CKRT. CA was assessed using three definitions: (1) the local USZ standard of an albumin-corrected total-to-ionised calcium ratio (T/iCa) ≥ 2.5; (2) the Meier-Kriesche definition of a T/iCa > 2.5 without albumin correction; and (3) the Khadzhynov criteria combining T/iCa without albumin correction with hypocalcaemia and metabolic acidosis. The predictive performance of ΔHCO₃⁻ for CA was evaluated using AUROC.

Results: Among 1,077 included patients, 153 (14%), 36 (3%), and 12 (1%) fulfilled the criteria for CA according to the USZ, Meier-Kriesche, and Khadzhynov criteria, respectively (Figure, panel A). CA occurred after a median of 8 [IQR 5–16], 9 [IQR 5–28], and 6 h [IQR 4–15] from CKRT initiation, according to the respective definitions. The corresponding ICU mortality rates were 65%, 83%, and 100%, compared with 39% in the overall cohort. After multivariable adjustment, all three definitions remained independently associated with ICU mortality. ΔHCO₃⁻ showed the highest discriminative ability for CA 4 h after CKRT initiation, with AUROC values of 0.72 (USZ), 0.83 (Meier-Kriesche), and 0.97 (Khadzhynov). Optimal ΔHCO₃⁻ thresholds were 2.06 (USZ), 2.83 (Meier–Kriesche), and 4.86 mmol/L (Figure, panel B).

Conclusions: The incidence of CA varies substantially by definition, is associated with adverse outcomes, and can be detected early using a bicarbonate prediction algorithm. Prospective validation is required to confirm the algorithm’s accuracy in clinical practice.Figure (abstract P315) (A) Incidence proportion of citrate accumulation (CA) according to the three investigated definitions: the University Hospital Zürich definition, the Meier-Kriesche definition, and the Khadzhynov definitions. (B) Area under the ROC curve (AUROC) of the bicarbonate-prediction algorithm for detecting CA based on the same three definitions, using the difference between predicted and true measured bicarbonate levels (ΔHCO₃⁻)

Hospital de Vila Franca de Xira, ICU, Vila Franca de Xira, Portugal

Introduction: Acute kidney injury requiring renal replacement therapy (RRT) significantly affects outcomes in critically ill patients. Early identification of patients who will need RRT may improve prognosis. We developed a predictive model and a bedside nomogram using early ICU variables to estimate the probability of RRT.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective study of ICU patients admitted between 2015 and 2023. Clinical and laboratory parameters collected in the first 48 h were analysed. A multivariate logistic regression analysis was used to select six independent predictors: creatinine, serum potassium, arterial pH, urine output, base excess (BE), and SAPS II. Model performance was assessed using ROC analysis and optimal threshold determination.

Results: In this study, 5830 patients admitted to a general ICU were enrolled (56.8% male; mean age 65.5 ± 16.7 years). Creatinine was the strongest predictor of RRT, followed by potassium and pH; urine output also showed a relevant impact, while base excess and SAPS II had moderate effects. The model demonstrated excellent discrimination (AUC 0.900, 95% CI 0.892–0.908), good calibration, yielding a sensitivity of 84.7% and a specificity of 83.2%. A nomogram based on the six predictors was generated for bedside application [1] (Figure).

Conclusions: Early ICU data can accurately predict the need for RRT [2]. P317 A transparent machine learning model for predicting CRRT requirement in patients with sepsis-associated acute kidney injury: a multi-center retrospective study

F Mingxing1, Y Tang2

1Hebei Medical University Third Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, China, 2Hebei Medical University Third Hospital, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P317

Introduction: Sepsis-associated acute kidney injury (SA-AKI) has high mortality, and identifying patients requiring continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT) remains challenging. This study developed and validated an interpretable machine learning (ML) model to predict CRRT need in SA-AKI.

Methods: Adult patients meeting Sepsis-3 and KDIGO-AKI criteria were extracted from MIMIC-IV (n = 5,568) and eICU-CRD (n = 4,825). Twenty-nine early variables were used to train eight ML models. Performance was assessed by AUC and F1-score. SHAP was applied for interpretability. External validation and a simplified nomogram were included.

Results: Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA) performed best (AUC 0.933). A reduced 7-feature model (creatinine, SOFA, AKI stage, lactate, hematocrit, urine output, SpO₂) preserved high accuracy and achieved an AUC of 0.833 externally. SHAP identified creatinine and lactate as major contributors. Using a 28.06% cutoff, subgroup analysis showed high mortality in true positives (56.5%) and notably elevated mortality in false positives (45.7%), indicating a high-risk phenotype missed by current criteria (Figure). A nomogram and online calculator were created.

Conclusions: We developed a robust and interpretable ML model for early CRRT prediction in SA-AKI. The model generalized well across centers and identified clinically vulnerable subgroups, supporting more timely and precise renal support.Figure (abstract P317) 28-day mortality across true negative (TN), false positive (FP), true positive (TP), false negative (FN) groups

P318 Impact of sex on renal replacement therapy initiation, a multicenter observational study

I Lemagnen1, B Trachsel2, B Brunoni1, T Kelevina1, A Schneider1

1Centre Hospitalier Universitaire Vaudois CHUV, Adult Intensive Care Unit, Lausanne, Switzerland, 2Center for Primary Care and Public Health Unisanté, Department of Epidemiology and Health Systems, Quantitative Research, Lausanne, Switzerland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P318

Introduction: The impact of sex on the decision to initiate renal replacement therapy (RRT) in critically ill patients with acute kidney injury (AKI) remains unclear.

Methods: We studied patients admitted to the intensive care unit (ICU) for > 6h in two cohorts Laus’AKI (Lausanne, 2010–2020, derivation) and MIMIC-IV (Boston, 2008–2019, validation). We excluded those with chronic dialysis, kidney transplant or intoxication. We collected demographics, comorbidities, SAPS II score, laboratory values, AKI stage and criteria for RRT initiation (based on potassium, pH, fluid balance diagnosis and PaO₂/FiO₂) within 24 h of ICU admission. We considered acute kidney disease (AKD), dialysis dependence, and mortality as outcomes. We assessed the influence of sex on RRT initiation, adjusted for age, objective dialysis criteria, KDIGO stages, selected SAPS II subcomponents, and admission diagnosis. Outcomes were analyzed using a propensity score for RRT receipt. Analyses were repeated in patients with stage ≥ 2 AKI and after stratification for age.

Results: We analyzed 14,980 patients (1,040 dialyzed: 719 men (69%), 321 women (31%)) from the Laus’AKI cohort and 48,362 patients (1,123 dialyzed: 676 men (60%), 447 women (40%)) from the MIMIC-IV. In both cohorts, male sex was associated with higher odds of RRT (OR Laus’AKI 1.20, [95% CI 1.01–1.43] and MIMIC-IV 1.29, [95%CI 1.10–1.51]). Sensitivity analyses confirmed our main results. No statistically significant differences were observed between sexes in patients undergoing RRT with regards to AKD occurrence or dialysis dependence. Furthermore, receipt of RRT did not influence the association between sex and mortality (p-value for interaction > 0.05 in the validation cohort).

Conclusions: Male ICU patients are more likely to receive RRT than females, however this appears not to impact their outcomes.

P319 NRS 2002 predictive validity in critically ill patients who receive renal replacement therapy

NG Russo1, NL Mattos2, E Colonnezi2, A Yamaguti3, LU Taniguchi2

1Hospital Beneficente de Senhoras SírioLibanês, Nutrition / Intensive Care Unit, São Paulo, Brazil, 2Hospital Beneficente de Senhoras SírioLibanês, Intensive Care Unit, São Paulo, Brazil, 3Hospital Beneficente de Senhoras SírioLibanês, Nutrition, São Paulo, Brazil

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P319

Introduction: Nutritional risk screening 2002 (NRS 2002) is a common screening tool of malnutrition in intensive care units (ICU), but its accuracy to predict mortality might be influenced by the necessity of invasive support. Since renal replacement therapy (RRT) is frequently used to support ICU patients, we aimed to describe the ability of NRS 2002 to predict hospital mortality in this subgroup.

Methods: We performed a retrospective cohort study of all adult (> 18 years) patients admitted to an ICU of Hospital Sírio-Libanês (São Paulo, Brazil) from 2022 to 2024. NRS 2002 was calculated in the first 24 h of ICU admission. Discrimination and predictive ability of this tool for hospital mortality were evaluated with area under ROC curves and logistic regression, adjusting for age, sex, SOFA score, status performance, Charlson Comorbidity Index, and type of ICU admission. Test for interaction between NRS 2002 ≥ 3 and RRT (RRT group vs. no-RRT group) was performed in the logistic regression.

Results: The cohort included 4,809 patients (315 received RRT). Prevalence of NRS 2002 ≥ 3was 45.1% (95% confidence interval [CI] 43.7%–46.5%), higher in the RRT group (68.6%,95% CI 63.1%–73.7%) compared with the no-RRT group (43.5%, 95% CI 42.0%–44.9%, p < 0.001). Predictive validity of NRS 2002 for hospital mortality was heterogeneous according to RRT (p value for heterogeneity = 0.007): odds ratio 2.21 (95% CI 1.68–2.92, p < 0.001) for no-RRT group, and odds ratio 0.94 (95% CI 0.53–1.65, p = 0.83) for RRT group. ROC curves were different according to RRT subgroups (p < 0.001): 0.74 (95% CI 0.72–0.75) for no-RRT group, and 0.52 (95% CI 0.46–0.57) for RRT group. Sensitive analysis of NRS 2002 predictive validities for hospital mortality according to mechanical ventilation (p = 0.59) and vasopressors(p = 0.65) did not demonstrate heterogeneity for these subgroups.

Conclusions: NRS 2002 did not demonstrate predictive validity for hospital mortality in our cohort of patients who received RRT.

P321 Comparison of lactate clearance between continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT) modalities

F Magon, F Mani, B Brunoni, M Caramella, F Zadek, T Langer

Università Statale di MilanoBicocca, Anesthesia and Critical Care, Niguarda Hospital, Milan, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P321

Introduction: Lactate reduction is a key target within the resuscitation bundles recommended by the Surviving Sepsis Campaign [1]. However, little is known about lactate clearance during continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT) and the specific contribution of veno-venous hemofiltration (CVVH) and continuous veno-venous hemodialysis (CVVHD). To address these gaps, we conducted a prospective observational study to compare lactate mass clearance in patients treated with CVVH or CVVHD.

Methods: We performed a single-center, prospective observational study including adult oligo-anuric ICU patients with a clinical indication for CRRT. Blood gas analyses were obtained before and after the filter and in the ultrafiltrate/dialysate. Lactate content in plasma water and effluent was calculated as previously described [2].

Results: Forty-six patients were enrolled: 24 treated with CVVH and 22 with CVVHD. Baseline lactate concentrations were similar between groups (1.6 [1.1–3.5] mmol/L for CVVH vs 2.0 [1.2–3.9] mmol/L for CVVHD, p = 0.76). Lactate ultrafiltrate/dialysate rates were similar during CVVH and CVVHD (0.08 [0.05–0.15] and 0.08 [0.06–0.17] mmol/min, respectively, p = 0.58) (Figure, panel a). The relationship between the inlet lactate load and the corresponding lactate mass clearance was assessed. Linear regression revealed a markedly steeper slope for CVVH compared with CVVHD, indicating substantially higher efficiency of CVVH in removing lactate for any given inlet load (p < 0.0001) (Figure, panel b).

Conclusions: CVVH and CVVHD provide similar absolute lactate effluent removal. However, when assessed using a mass-balance approach, CVVH shows significantly greater lactate clearance efficiency for a given inlet load. These findings suggest that the choice of CRRT modality may influence lactate handling in critically ill patients.Figure (abstract P321) The box plot on the left shows comparable effluent lactate flow rates between CVVH and CVVHD. The scatter plot on the right displays the relationship between lactate inlet load (Q_lac in) and lactate mass clearance (Q_lac eff), with linear regression demonstrating a significant difference in slope between the two modalities

P322 Hypokalemia with diluted regional citrate anticoagulation during continuous renal replacement therapy in critically ill patients

F Mani, F Magon, B Brunoni, M Caramella, F Zadek, T Langer

University of MilanBicocca, Department of Medicine and Surgery, Monza, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P322

Introduction: Acute kidney injury (AKI) affects up to two-thirds of critically ill patients, and about 20% of them require continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT) [1, 2]. Diluted regional citrate anticoagulation (dRCA) is widely used to maintain circuit patency and reduce bleeding risk [3]. However, because dRCA is potassium-free and infused in substantial volumes, it may alter potassium (K +) balance. This study evaluated the effect of dRCA on serum potassium concentration [K +] in different CRRT modalities.

Methods: We analyzed both in-vitro and in-vivo (ST150 filter, Baxter) different CRRT modalities performed with dRCA (Regiocit, Baxter) combined with 4 mmol/L K + containing dialysate/replacement fluids (Biphozyl or Phoxilium, Baxter). K + concentration was measured using a blood gas analyzer, in-vitro at baseline and after reaching a stable value, i.e., after 210 min, while in-vivo at start and after 72 h of treatment.

Results: Regardless of the technique employed, in-vitro experiments performed with dRCA (n = 12) resulted in significant hypokalemia. In contrast, when systemic heparin anticoagulation was used instead of dRCA (n = 8), [K +] matched those of the dialysate or replacement solution (Figure). In the in-vivo experiments, using exclusively dRCA, patients treated with CVVH (n = 24) or CVVHD n = 11) had [K +] at 72 h of 4.0 ± 0.3 mEq/L (p < 0.01) and 3.9 ± 0.4 mEq/L (p < 0.01), respectively. In both modalities, patients required intravenous K + supplementation during the 72 h (159 ± 104 mEq CVVH vs. 96 ± 51 mEq CVVHD, p = 0.07).

Conclusions: CRRT performed with dRCA requires substantial intravenous K + supplementation to avoid hypokalemia.Figure (abstract P322) In-vitro experiments representing both baseline and potassium concentration reached at equilibrium (data are expressed as mean ± SD). Red dots represent experiments performed with dRCA, while white dots represent experiments performed using heparin

In-vitro experiments representing both baseline and potassium concentration reached at equilibrium (data are expressed as mean ± SD). Red dots represent experiments performed with dRCA, while white dots represent experiments performed using heparin

P325 Evaluation of the furosemide stress test to predict successful liberation from renal replacement therapy in critically ill patients

T Soccorsi1, B Trächsel2, I Lemagnen3, C Monard4, A Schneider5

1Centre Hospitalier Universitaire Vaudois CHUV, Service de Médecine Intensive Adulte, Lausanne, Switzerland, 2Department of Epidemiology and Health Systems, Center for Primary Care and Public Health Unisanté, Lausanne, Switzerland, 3Faculty of Biology and Medicine, University of Lausanne, Lausanne, Switzerland, 4Hôpital Edouard Herriot, Hospices Civils de Lyon, Lyon, France, 5Centre Hospitalier Universitaire Vaudois CHUV, Lausanne, Switzerland

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P325

Introduction: Furosemide is commonly administered to assess patients’ potential for RRT liberation; however, the standardized furosemide stress test (FST [1]) was not formally evaluated. We aimed to determine FST’s ability to predict RRT liberation within 72 h.

Methods: This single-center, prospective, interventional study included critically ill adult patients undergoing RRT for whom a discontinuation attempt was decided by the physician in charge. FST (1 or 1.5, if exposed within 7 days, mg/kg of iv furosemide) was performed within 12 h of RRT liberation. We collected patients’ baseline characteristics, urine output (UO) and urine levels of urea and creatinine at the time of FST. Our primary outcome was the successful liberation from RRT within 72 h of FST. We computed FST’s sensitivity, specificity, positive and negative predictive values (PPV, NPV) as well as its area under the curve (AUC) for RRT liberation. Secondary outcomes were successful RRT liberation at ICU and hospital discharge as well as adverse events (AE) associated with FST.

Results: Among the 52 patients included in the study, FST was positive (UO ≥ 200 mL within 2 h of FST) in 12 (23.1%). Compared to those with a negative FST, these patients had similar baseline characteristics, but higher UO prior to RRT liberation (26 vs 235 mL/day, p < 0.001) and higher urinary urea level at the time of FST (47.1 vs 124.5 mmol/l, p < 0.001). FST’s performance for the primary outcome was: sensitivity 0.69, specificity 0.95, PPV 0.81, NPV 0.90 with a resulting AUC of 0.97. Its performance was lower to predict RRT liberation at ICU (AUC 0.67) and hospital discharge (AUC 0.58). There was no significant AE associated with FST.

Conclusions: FST has excellent performance to predict RRT liberation within 72 h with a high specificity and NPV.

Reference Chawla LS et al. Crit Care 2013;17:R207

P326 Effect of renal function on acidemia caused by hyperlactatemia in sepsis: interim analysis of an observational study

S Maitra, EE Ernest, H Mehrotra, S Bhattacharjee, DK Baidya

All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Anaesthesiology, Pain Medicine Critical Care, New Delhi, India

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P326

Introduction: Renal response to compensatory decrease in PaCO2 in response to metabolic acidosis is controversial. Preclinical data demonstrated that change in plasma bicarbonate in metabolic disorder is not contributed by the primary disorder itself, rather a maladaptive renal response. A previous study reported that acidosis from hyperlactatemia was modulated by renal dysfunction [1]. We hypothesized that acidosis caused by hyperlactatemia in sepsis patients is multifactorial and alone renal function cannot explain it.

Methods: Data of n = 38 patients with sepsis from perforation peritonitis with hyperlactatemia (arterial lactate > = 2 mEq/L) were analysed in this interim analysis. Effect of serum lactate level on standardized base deficit (sBD) was evaluated by Spearman correlation. Indirect effect was serum lactate on sBD was evaluated by generalized linear model (GLM) mediation analysis.

Results: Median (IQR) arterial lactate was 3.35 (2.60- 4.75) mEq/L and sBD was 9.5 (6.2- 13.7) mEq/L. There was a statistically significant correlation between sBD and arterial lactate (rho = 0.58, p < 0.001). However, there was no indirect effect of arterial lactate on sBD mediated by serum creatinine (beta = -0.009, p = 0.86). No indirect effect was observed with other buffers such as albumin (p = 0.26) or haemoglobin (p = 0.32). Multiple linear regression reported that serum lactate was an independent predictor of sBD even after adjustment for other buffers (t = 2.79, p = 0.009).

Conclusions: Our preliminary analysis suggests that acidosis caused by hyperlactatemia was not modulated by serum creatinine level. This study also suggests that acidosis multifactorial and lactate is one of the direct contributors to it. However, as this is an interim analysis from a small cohort, validation in the larger cohort is required.

P327 Effect of free water-based intervention on serum sodium and clinical outcomes in critically ill patients with moderate to severe hypernatremia: a target trial emulation

A Caroli, A Chaba, J Nübel

Austin Hospital, Department of Intensive Care, Melbourne, Australia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P327

Introduction: This study aimed to evaluate whether a free water deficit-based intervention (FWBI) accelerates hypernatremia correction and improves clinical outcomes in ICU patients. Moderate to severe hypernatremia is frequent among critically ill patients and is associated with poor outcomes, and the optimal correction strategy and its impact on clinically relevant endpoints remain unclear.

Methods: We conducted a target trial emulation using the MIMIC-IV database, including adult patients with moderate to severe hypernatremia (serum sodium ≥ 150 mmol/L). The intervention consisted of administering a volume of hypotonic fluids at least equal to the calculated free water deficit in the first 24 h. Patients were assigned to the FWBI or usual care group using cloning, censoring, and weighting to reduce immortal time and confounding biases. The primary outcome was resolution of hypernatremia within 24 h. Secondary outcomes included serum sodium change, ICU/hospital mortality, and ICU/hospital length of stay. Robustness was assessed using targeted minimum-loss based estimation and overlap propensity weighting.

Results: 3,035 patients were eligible for the target trial. FWBI significantly increased the rate of hypernatremia resolution at 24 h (51% vs. 19%; HR 3.24, 95%CI 2.63–3.83) (Figure). Serum sodium decreased more rapidly in the FWBI group (–3.3 mmol/L vs. control; 95%CI –3.96 to –2.70). Mortality did not differ significantly between groups, but FWBI was associated with significantly shorter ICU and hospital stay. The benefit was greatest in patients with ICU-acquired hypernatremia and under invasive mechanical ventilation.

Conclusions: A physiologically guided free water deficit-based intervention accelerates hypernatremia correction and reduces ICU and hospital stay without evidence of harm. These findings justify a randomised controlled trial to determine the impact of this strategy on patient-centred outcomes.Figure (abstract P327) Forest plots of primary, secondary and subgroup results. Estimates were estimated under three causal-inference frameworks: cloning-censoring-weighting, targeted minimum-loss based estimation, and overlap weighting. All analyses emulated a predefined target trial protocol with consistent eligibility, treatment strategies, and follow-up definitions, and were adjusted for baseline confounders using stabilized weights specific to each method

P328 Association of early magnesium sulfate use with 28-day mortality for sepsis patients: a target trial emulation

X Luo, FR Lin, T Huang, JZ Zhang, AQ Lao, ZF Yang

Sun Yatsen Memorial Hospital, Sun Yatsen University, Intensive Care Unit, Guangzhou, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P328

Introduction: Magnesium supplementation is commonly administered in critically ill patients for a variety of indications. However, the effect of early magnesium sulfate to improve sepsis outcomes remains controversial. We aim to emulate a target trial evaluating the effect of early magnesium supplementation on 28-day mortality.

Methods: This target trial emulation study utilized data from the Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care-IV version 3.1 (MIMIC-IV v3.1) database. The study included adult patients diagnosed with sepsis who received magnesium sulfate supplementation within the first 24 h of intensive care unit (ICU) admission. The intervention was defined as early magnesium sulfate administration following sepsis onset, and the primary outcome was 28-day all-cause mortality. The primary analysis was conducted using the parametric g-formula. Sensitivity analysis was performed to evaluate the impact of delayed magnesium sulfate use on 28-day mortality, confirming the robustness of the primary findings. Subgroup analyses were carried out based on age, sex, Sequential Organ Failure Assessment (SOFA) score, Charlson Comorbidity Index, and magnesium status to examine potential variations across different patient populations.

Results: A total of 21,272 patients met the inclusion criteria, of whom 10,739 (50.5%) received magnesium sulfate. The estimated 28-day mortality was 14.23% (95% CI, 12.36% to 16.47%) in the early magnesium sulfate group and 34.00% (95% confidence interval [CI], 31.98% to 36.33%) in the no magnesium sulfate group, yielding a relative risk of 0.42 (95% CI, 0.39% to 0.44%) (Figure). Lower mortality was consistently observed across all evaluated subgroups, regardless of baseline serum magnesium status.

Conclusions: Early magnesium sulfate use was associated with significantly lower mortality among critically ill patients with sepsis. These findings should inform the design of future randomized controlled trials evaluating early magnesium sulfate supplementation in sepsis.Figure (abstract P328) Detailed process and results of early magnesium sulfate use with sepsis patients: a target trial emulation
