P330 Management of hypocalcemia in adult critically ill patients: an international survey

M Tujunen1, H Kiiski1, P Sivapalan2, A Granholm2, MP Wise3, KL Ellekjær2, TS Meyhoff2, A Perner2, MH Møller2, J Hästbacka1

1Tampere University Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Tampere, Finland, 2Rigshospitalet, Copenhagen University Hospital, Department of Intensive Care, Copenhagen, Denmark, 3University Hospital of Wales, General Adult Intensive Care Unit, Cardiff, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P330

Introduction: Hypocalcemia is common in critically ill patients and associated with adverse outcomes, but its optimal management is uncertain. We examined views about management of hypocalcemia and need for interventional trials among international intensivists. We hypothesized substantial practice variation.

Methods: We conducted an international online survey in the Collaboration for research in Intensive Care (CRIC) network using REDCap. The survey was distributed in fall 2025 across 17 countries and included 21 questions on ionized calcium (iCa) monitoring, treatment practices, indications, and views on a future RCT.

Results: We received 964 responses giving a response rate of 32%. Most respondents worked in mixed ICUs (77%) and 64% were intensive care specialists. 75% of respondents reported measuring iCa levels several times a day. Hypocalcemia was reported most often corrected “frequently” (39%), typically at an iCa threshold < 1.0 mmol/L (46%), and using bolus administration (60%), while 15% of respondents reported correcting it rarely (Figure). Expectations regarding benefits varied. The most common mandatory indication was impaired coagulation and active bleeding (79%) and the most frequently reported contraindication was tumor lysis syndrome (39%). Most respondents would randomize patients in an RCT comparing calcium correction with no correction (91%) or different thresholds (92%), and rated both designs as clinically important (median 6, IQR 5–7 on a scale from 1 to 9). “Any adult general ICU patient” was considered an eligible population by 57% of the respondents. Most respondents (85%) anticipated no major barriers to such a trial.

Conclusions: Most intensivists reported measuring iCa frequently in adult ICU patients, yet preferences for hypocalcemia correction in clinical practice varied considerably. Despite mixed expectations regarding benefit, there was strong support for a future RCT to evaluate management of hypocalcemia in ICU patients.Figure (abstract P330) Reported thresholds and frequency of calcium supplementation

Reported thresholds and frequency of calcium supplementation

P334 Hypophosphatemia as a biomarker of metabolic unreadiness for enhanced nutrition in the PICU: a secondary analysis of the PEPaNIC RCT

N Umman Serin1, I Vanhorebeek1, F Güiza1, L Mebis1, S Verbruggen2, K Joosten2, G Van den Berghe1, J Gunst1

1KU Leuven, Clinical Division and Laboratory of Intensive Care Medicine, Department of Cellular and Molecular Medicine, Leuven, Belgium, 2Erasmus Medical Center, Sophia Children’s Hospital, Division of Pediatric Intensive Care Unit, Department of Neonatal and Pediatric ICU, Rotterdam, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P334

Introduction: The PEPaNIC RCT showed that early supplementation of insufficient enteral nutrition by parenteral nutrition (early-PN) worsened outcome of critically ill children as compared with omitting PN for 1 week (late-PN). The best timing to initiate nutritional support in the pediatric intensive care unit (PICU) remains unclear. In adults, declining phosphate levels may identify patients who are particularly harmed by early-PN. We assessed whether early hypophosphatemia (hypoP) in critically ill children may indicate metabolic intolerance to nutrition.

Methods: This PEPaNIC secondary analysis investigated whether development of hypoP statistically interacts with the randomized intervention for its impact on outcome (new infections, duration of PICU dependency, 90-day mortality, and duration of mechanical ventilation and hospital stay), adjusting for baseline risk factors. Subsequently, the impact of early-PN vs late-PN in patients with and without early hypoP was assessed. Analyses were performed for phosphate abnormalities on PICU day 1 and 2, with and without imputing missing phosphate data.

Results: Of patients with available phosphate measurements, 19.9% and 27.5% developed hypoP on day 1 and 2, respectively. Day 1 hypoP did not interact with the randomized intervention for any outcome. On day 2, hypoP interacted with randomization for risk of new infection (p = 0.03), likelihood of earlier live PICU discharge (p = 0.03) and time to live weaning from mechanical ventilation (p = 0.02). Harm by early-PN was more pronounced in patients with hypoP than in those without. After imputing missing phosphate data an additional interaction was found for 90-day mortality (p = 0.03) revealing higher mortality with early-PN in patients with hypoP.

Conclusions: Development of hypoP may identify critically ill children who are particularly harmed by early-PN. This opens perspectives for its use as biomarker of unreadiness for enhanced nutrition, which requires further investigation.

P337 Restricted caloric strategy during refeeding hypophosphatemia and its impact on mortality in the ICU

A Chaba1, K White2

1Pitié Salpêtrière Assistance Publique des Hopitaux de Paris, Intensive Care Unit, Paris, France, 2Princess Alexandra Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Woolloongabba, Queensland, Australia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P337

Introduction: Refeeding hypophosphatemia is a common complication in critically ill patients and has been independently associated with worse outcomes. Whether a restrictive caloric strategy in this setting improves survival remains uncertain.

Methods: We conducted a target trial emulation using routinely collected data from six intensive care units in Queensland, Australia. Adult patients who developed moderate to severe hypophosphatemia (decrease in serum phosphate > 0.16 mmol/L and a serum phosphate level less than 0.65 mmol/L) within 24 h of initiating artificial nutrition were eligible. Patients were assigned to a restrictive caloric strategy (decreased caloric intake on the day hypophosphatemia occurred) or usual care (increased caloric intake permitted). The primary outcome was 30-day hospital mortality. Cloning, censoring, and weighting were used to minimize immortal time bias, and robustness was assessed with targeted minimum-loss based estimation and overlap propensity score weighting.

Results: Among 1,304 eligible patients. The mean age was 55 ± 17 years, 59% were male, and the mean APACHE II score was 20 ± 7. At 24 h, caloric intake decreased by 5.4 kcal per kilogram per day in the intervention group and increased by 12 kcal per kilogram per day in the control group. Thirty-day hospital mortality was 9% with the restrictive strategy and 18% with usual care (RR, 0.47; 95% CI, 0.32 to 0.69) (Figure). Restrictive feeding was also associated with lower ICU mortality, reduced persistent critical illness, and shorter ICU and hospital stays. Phosphate trajectories were similar between groups.

Conclusions: In this target trial emulation, a restrictive caloric strategy in patients with refeeding hypophosphatemia was associated with lower mortality and improved clinical outcomes, independent of phosphate correction. These findings support further randomized controlled trials to confirm the role of restrictive feeding in this high-risk ICU population.Figure (abstract P337) Estimated 30-day hospital mortality risk under the restricted caloric treatment strategy versus usual care

Estimated 30-day hospital mortality risk under the restricted caloric treatment strategy versus usual care

P339 Reducing the rate of enteral feeding interruption of patients on mechanical ventilation in intensive care unit by a Lean Six-Sigma approach

X Nie1, X Li1, X Zheng1, X Liu1, J Jin1, Wm Chan2

1The University of Hong Kong Shenzhen Hospital, Department of Adult Intensive Care, Shenzhen, China, 2LKS Faculty of Medicine, The University of Hong Kong, Critical Care Medicine Unit, Hong Kong, SAR China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P339

Introduction: Interruption of enteral feeding is common for patients on mechanical ventilation [1].

Methods: A Lean Six-Sigma approach was used to define the problem, identify the root causes, and design an approach to minimize interruption. These include a pragmatic protocol to streamline enteral tube feeding, an evidence based approach of feeding tubes care, together with adopting a portable direct vision naso-jejunal (PDV-NJ) tube insertion system. The PDV-NJ tube insertion system is a novel device with a video camera at the tip of the naso-jejunal tube (NJT). Insertion was done at bedside by ICU nurses. Successful placement was confirmed by visualizing important landmarks until jejunal villi were identified (Figure). Patients at high risk of failed naso-gastric feeding were also identified and early switch to NJT feeding was arranged. The aim is to achieve enteral feeding targets within 48 h of ICU admission. The Enteral Feeding Interruption (EFI) Rate was defined as the number of patients having interruption for over 60 min per day relative to the total number of ventilated patients on enteral feeding.

Results: The programme was conducted from Aug 24 to May 25, with 98% of ICU Nurses became familiar with the care of NJT. ICU staff satisfaction rate with the PDV-NJ system was 93%. A total of 268 PDV-NJ tubes were inserted. Success rate of post-pyloric placement at first attempt was 90.74%. The mean insertion time was 18.6 min (SD 8.67 min). Using the period from Oct 23 to Jul 24 as baseline, EFI rate dropped from 58.45% to 19.48%. There was no unplanned NJT removal due to blockage.

Conclusions: A protocol using the Lean Six-Sigma approach can effectively reduce feeding interruptions for patients on mechanical ventilation.

P341 Oxygen consumption and muscular evaluation of standardized ICU rehabilitation exercises in healthy population

A Bisman1, G Cuvelier1, S Leteneur2, S Mashayekhi3, M Cotils4, L Dujardin1, B Delhaye1, C Bousseau1, F Duprez5, CE Hickmann5

1Laboratoire de l´Effort et du Mouvement, HEPH Condorcet, Tournai, Belgium, 2LAMIH, Université Polytechnique Hauts de France UPHF, LAMIH, Valenciennes, France, 3Service des Soins Intensifs, Centre Hospitalier EpiCURA, Hornu, Belgium, 4Service de Pneumologie, Centre Hospitalier EpiCURA, Hornu, Belgium, 5Unité de Recherche et d’Innovation Condor EpiCURA URICE, Soins Intensifs, Hornu, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P341

Introduction: The objective of this preliminary study was to explore the feasibility of measuring oxygen consumption (VO2) coupled with muscle recruitment assessment during a standardized exercise program in healthy subjects. This methodology is intended to be transposed and compared to critical and post-intensive care unit (ICU) patients. Early physical activity is considered a feasible and safe approach to prevent or treat neuromuscular complications in ICU, but current "early mobilization" lacks standardization in intensity and duration [1].

Methods: Twenty healthy adults (13 women, 7 men; mean age ± SD: 32 ± 8 years) were included. The protocol consisted of 10 standardized exercises: five for the upper limbs (UL), from 1 to 5 and five for the lower limbs (LL), from 6 to 10. Each exercise lasted three minutes, followed by two minutes of rest. Continuous VO2 was measured using indirect calorimetry (QNR-G + , Cosmed©) and expressed as a percentage of the strict rest value. Surface electromyography (sEMG) was recorded on the biceps brachii (BB), rectus femoris (DF), and tibialis anterior (TA), expressed as a percentage of maximal voluntary contraction.

Results: Increases in were observed during all UL exercises, ranging from to (mean ± SD: 14 ± 18% to 117 ± 41% (p < 0.001)). Similar increases were observed during LL exercises, ranging from to (27 ± 23% to 174 ± 64% (p < 0.001)). The most significant muscle recruitment was observed for the BB during UL exercises 1, 2, 4, and 5; for the DF during LL exercises 6, 7, 8, 9, and 10; and for the TA during exercises 6 and 9.

Conclusions: These preliminary data demonstrate the potential of indirect calorimetry to quantify the intensity of exercises administered to patients in rehabilitation and suggest the feasibility of segmental analysis via sEMG.

P342 The effect of augmented protein intake on nitrogen balance: a nested cohort of the TARGET Protein trial

AC Chaba

Pitié Salpêtrière Assistance Publique des Hopitaux de Paris, Intensive Care Unit, Paris, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P342

Introduction: The impact of augmenting enteral protein intake on nitrogen balance and urea generation in critically ill patients is poorly understood. We aimed to investigate the impact of augmenting protein intake on nitrogen balance and urea generation in a nested cohort within the TARGET Protein trial.

Methods: We conducted a single-center study within a cluster randomized cross-over trial. Consecutive patients were enrolled during either the standard protein intake period (n = 52) or the augmented protein intake period (n = 55). Nitrogen balance, its components, and plasma urea concentrations were compared between groups. Multivariable analysis identified factors independently associated with nitrogen balance.

Results: From day 1 to day 7 the mean protein intake achieved was 0.84 ± 0.73 g/kg per day in the control group versus 1.35 ± 0.94 g/kg per day in the augmented protein group. Patients receiving augmented protein achieved a less negative nitrogen balance than those receiving usual protein (mean difference + 5 g/day; 95% CI, 0.4 to 10; p = 0.033), driven by greater nitrogen intake (mean difference + 7 g/day; 95% CI, 5 to 10; p < 0.001). Independent predictors of nitrogen balance were ideal body weight (–0.18 g per kg; 95% CI, –0.35 to –0.01; p = 0.040), acute kidney injury at ICU admission (+ 4.7 g; 95% CI, 0.10 to 9.3; p = 0.046), and allocation to augmented protein therapy (+ 5.1 g; 95% CI, 0.43 to 9.7; p = 0.033). From day 1 to day 7, plasma urea concentrations were greater increased more in the augmented protein group (median 14 vs. 11 mmol/L) (Figure), with greater urea nitrogen accumulation (+ 3 g; 95% CI, 1.0 to 6.0; p = 0.035). Approximately 60% of the additional positive nitrogen balance was accounted for by urea generation.

Conclusions: Augmented protein intake increased nitrogen balance in critically ill patients but also urea production and plasma urea concentration, suggesting limited usefulness.Figure (abstract P342) Blood urea evolution after enteral feeding start

P343 Early protein goal intake and mortality in septic patients

L Velasco, OI Aguilera Olvera, JA Villalobos Silva, GA Aguirre Gomez, M Rodriguez Reyna, LC Gaucin Delgado

High Specialty Regional Hospital of Victoria, Intensive Care Unit, Ciudad Victoria, Mexico

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P343

Introduction: Current guidelines recommend achieving full protein goal intake past 72 h in critically ill patient [1]. Aim to evaluate early protein goal intake in septic shock patients and mortality.

Methods: This retrospective study included septic ICU patients in a tertiary center in Mexico, excluding those under 18 years and pregnant women. Patients were grouped according to whether they reached 1.3 g/kg/day of protein within 48 h. Numerical and categorical data were summarized using means (SD) and percentages, and associations were assessed with odds ratios and the chi-square test (p < 0.05).

Results: We analyzed 38 patients, 63% female, with a mean age of 50 years. Type 2 diabetes (42%) and hypertension (50%) were the most common comorbidities, and abdominal infections were the main source (45%). Mean APACHE II and SOFA scores were 15.9 and 8.3. Septic shock occurred in 79%, and 76% had positive cultures. Early nutrition was provided in 95% of cases, mainly enteral, with 71% experiencing minor gastrointestinal complications. Mortality was 26%, and 29% required renal replacement therapy. Nutrition began on day 1, and protein goals were met by day 1.7. Early protein goal achievement was not associated with mortality (OR 1.97; p = 0.41) (Figure).

Conclusions: In this small retrospective study, early protein intake (< 48 h) was not associated with mortality. Although gastrointestinal symptoms were common, no complications were attributable to early nutrition. Further studies are needed to clarify the role of early protein intake in critically ill patients.Figure (abstract P343) Relationship between protein intake, survival, and mortality

P344 Impact of impaired intestinal absorption in mechanically ventilated patients on feeding efficiency—preliminary data from the ATTAINMENT study

C Cobilinschi1, AC Andrei1, A Dumitriu1, R Darie1, R Tincu1, I Marandiuc2, B Velescu3, E Moisa4, L Mirea1

1Clinical Emergency Hospital of Bucharest, Anaesthesia and Intensive Care, Bucharest, Romania, 2Cantacuzino National Institute for Medical Military Research and Development, Toxicology Department, Bucharest, Romania, 3Carol Davila University of Medicine and Pharmacy, Anaesthesia and Intensive Care, Bucharest, Romania, 4Elias Clinical Emergency Hospital, Anaesthesia and Intensive Care, Bucharest, Romania

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P344

Introduction: Prolonged negative energy balance in the ICU is an independent mortality risk factor. Gastrointestinal(GI) dysfunction is common and may limit feeding efficiency, but the true rate of malabsorption in mechanically ventilated ICU patients remains uncertain. This study investigates whether tailored nutritional support enables adequate energy and protein delivery in ICU patients with documented GI dysfunction (NCT06510348).

Methods: This randomized trial includes mechanically ventilated patients with controlled shock. Eligibility requires GI dysfunction, defined by increased gastric residual volume on ultrasound and impaired paracetamol absorption after a 15 mg/kg test dose. Patients are randomized to enteral nutrition (EN) or supplemental parenteral nutrition (SPN).In the EN group, feeding is advanced to 75% of target by day 5 and to full intake thereafter. In the SPN group, SPN is initiated to reach 75% of target by day 5,with trophic EN providing the remaining 25%. Primary outcomes are cumulated energy and protein balances on days 10 and 15.Two-way ANOVA was used to assess differences in daily energy and protein intake per kg of actual body weight.

Results: Out of 1,128 patients admitted during 2025, 327 required mechanical ventilation for more than 72 h. Among these 327 patients, 85 fulfilled the predefined inclusion criteria, and 31 of them (36%) had confirmed malabsorption. The SPN group achieved higher energy delivery on days 5 and 6, while from day 7 onward both groups reached similar energy intake. Protein intake was consistently higher in the SPN group, however the recommended 1.2 g/kg/day target was reached only on day 10.

Conclusions: In this cohort of ICU patients with GI dysfunction, SPN allowed earlier achievement of energy and protein targets without evidence of overfeeding. The study also provides prospective comparative data on ultrasound-estimated gastric residual volume and the paracetamol absorption test, showing that the two methods offer complementary information for identifying impaired GI function.

P345 Resting energy expenditure assessment in critically ill patients: direct vs. predictive measurement on the 3rd day of hospitalization

C Ferreira1, B Rachão2, M Santos Silva3, F Cardoso3, C Teixeira3

1ULS São João, Serviço de Medicina Intensiva, Porto, Portugal, 2ULS São João, Porto, Portugal, 3ULS São João, Unidade Local de Saúde São João, Porto, Portugal

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P345

Introduction: Accurate determination of the energy requirements (ER) of critically ill patient (CIP) is essential to ensure adequate nutritional support [1]. Indirect calorimetry (IC) is the reference method, but its clinical use is limited and often replaced by predictive equations (PE) or international society recommendations (ISR) [2]. The reliability of these approaches throughout hospitalization remains poorly explored [3]. This study aimed to assess resting energy expenditure (REE) by IC on the 3rd day of hospitalization and compare it with values estimated by predictive equations (PE) and international society recommendations (ISR).

Methods: Cross-sectional observational study with 30 patients admitted to the Intensive Care Medicine Department of the Local Health Unit of São João. ER were assessed by IC, PEs (Harris–Benedict, Penn State, Ireton Jones, Swinamer and Faisy) and ISRs (ESPEN, ASPEN and BRASPEN). Non-parametric statistical tests were applied (p < 0.05).

Results: The sample included 63.3% men, with a mean age of 62 ± 15 years (23–86), mean stay of 23 days and mortality 20%. Overweigh/obesity prevalence was 53.3%. Median REE by IC was 17 kcal/kg (P25 = 14, P75 = 20). All PEs and ISRs showed significant positive correlations with IC (p < 0.01), with Harris–Benedict and Swinamer showing the highest correlation coefficients (rs = 0.778). BRASPEN was the closest to IC (18 kcal/kg, p = 0.314). ASPEN underestimated ER with a median of 14 kcal/kg, p < 0.001. All remaining PEs and ISRs significantly overestimated ER (p < 0.001). Men had higher IC values (p = 0.001). Age correlated negatively with IC (p = 0.011). Median exclusive intake was 11 kcal/kg/day, significantly lower than IC (p = 0.011).

Conclusions: Important discrepancies were found between PEs/ISRs and IC, reinforcing the value of this gold-standard method and suggesting early underfeeding.

P346 Interpersonal and communication skills of the intensive care unit dietitian in facilitating personalized nutritional care

L De Hart1, E Dom1, C Verhelst1, L Leemans1, R Blaauw2, E De Waele1

1UZBrussel, Clinical Nutrition and Dietetics, Brussel, Belgium, 2Stellenbosch University, Division of Human Nutrition, Faculty of Medicine and Health Sciences, Cape Town, South Africa

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P346

Introduction: The role of the intensive care unit (ICU) dietitian (RD) has expanded to include active participation in multidisciplinary decision-making. Effective interpersonal and communication skills support collaboration with the multi-professional team (MPT) and may improve consistent implementation of nutritional protocols and individualized nutrition care plans. Existing literature suggests that interprofessional communication is a crucial factor in overcoming barriers to adequate nutritional support in the ICU [1–3].

Methods: We conducted a descriptive review of the literature to identify interpersonal and communication skills relevant to the ICU RD.

Results: Key interpersonal and communication skills identified include clear and respectful verbal communication, clarification of nutrition-related orders, conflict resolution, advocacy for timely nutrition delivery, culturally appropriate communication. Non-verbal skills include active listening, showing empathy, effective problem-solving and a professional personal appearance. These skills support effective collaboration with ICU physicians, nursing staff, speech therapists, logistics department and foodservice teams, as well as other allied health professionals involved in nutrition delivery.

Conclusions: The ICU RD´s interpersonal and communication skills are important facilitators in supporting the MPT’s adherence to nutrition protocols as well as an individualized nutrition plan and ensuring continuity of nutritional care for critically ill patients.

P348 Metabolomic endotyping of an international observational sepsis cohort

J Brandsma1, JG Chenoweth2, JW Thompson3, G Oduro4, T Vantha5, N Adams6, EL Tsalik3, CW Woods3, DV Clark2

1Henry M. Jackson Foundation, Austere Environments Consortium for Enhanced Sepsis Outcomes, Hesda, USA, 2Henry M. Jackson Foundation, Hesda, USA, 3Duke University, School of Medicine, Durham, NC, USA, 4Komfo Anokye Teaching Hospital, Kumasi, Ghana, 5Takeo Provincial Referral Hospital, Takeo, Cambodia, 6Infectious Diseases Directorate, Naval Medical Research Command, Silver Spring, MD, USA

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P348

Introduction: Expeditious and accurate information is critical for clinical decision-making in sepsis and can be enhanced using host-response biomarkers indicative of sepsis subtype and prognosis. Our research consortium has previously identified clinically relevant sepsis endotypes based on gene and inflammatory protein expression levels, and we now expand upon those results by integrating metabolomics data from matched samples.

Methods: Sepsis patients were recruited at clinical sites in Cambodia, Ghana and the USA (n = 540), and concentrations of 150 endogenous blood plasma metabolites were quantified using the Biocrates AbsoluteIDQ p180 protocol. Patients were assigned to one of five previously identified sepsis gene expression endotypes, and metabolite profiles were compared between groups, as well as between 28-day survivors and non-survivors.

Results: Distinct clusters of metabolites were associated with each of the sepsis endotypes, and could be linked to significant differences in disease prognosis and clinical presentation. Patients with a low-risk endotype had a plasma lipidome closest to that of non-diseased adults. In contrast, patients with a high-risk endotype showed significantly elevated short-chain acylcarnitine and decreased sphingomyelin levels, as well as a highly skewed lyso-to-diacylphosphatidylcholine ratio. Furthermore, sepsis non-survivors showed more pronounced deviations and endotype-specific differences in metabolite expression levels than sepsis survivors.

Conclusions: Sepsis patients with different disease endotypes differ markedly in the composition of their circulating metabolome. These results provide evidence for distinct pathways of host metabolic dysregulation and organ disfunction, and warrant further study into immunometabolic regulation of sepsis.

P349 Admission plasma lactate-to-glucose ratio predicts 90-day mortality but not metformin response in type 2 diabetic patients with sepsis

SJ Noorloos1, J Minturn1, IE Saraiva1, D Payen2, H Gómez1

1University of Pittsburgh, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Pittsburgh, USA, 2Université Paris Cité Sorbonne, Paris, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P349

Introduction: A high lactate to glucose (L/G) ratio may reflect the presence of metabolic reprogramming away from oxidative phosphorylation, a potential mechanism of sepsis-induced organ dysfunction. In this study we tested the hypotheses that (1) a high L/G ratio is associated with worst clinical outcomes in patients with type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (DM2) and sepsis, and (2) a high L/G ratio can identify patients that will have the highest impact of metformin on mortality.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective cohort study of critically ill DM2 patients with sepsis admitted to one of the 16 University of Pittsburgh Medical Center (UPMC) hospitals, with available ICU admission plasma lactate and glucose. Associations between L/G ratio and 90-day mortality were examined using Kaplan–Meier curves and Cox-proportional hazards modeling, while stage 2–3 AKI was investigated using multivariable logistic regression models. We classified patients into low or high L/G ratio based on the Youden index and investigated the association of L/G ratio with outcome and impact of the use of metformin on 90-day mortality.

Results: Among 14,847 patients, 4,309 had an available L/G ratio, of whom 168 received metformin. The L/G ratio was associated with 90-day mortality (HR 1.49, 95%CI 1.38–1.61, p < 0.001), but not with the development of stage 2–3 AKI (OR 1.14, 95%CI 0.8–1.9). The Youden threshold (0.168) classified 2,402 patients as low and 1,907 as high L/G ratio. A high ratio was associated with increased 90-day mortality (OR 1.38, 95%CI 1.23–1.54, p < 0.001), but not with stage 2–3 AKI compared to low L/G ratio (OR 1.13, 95%CI 0.93–1.38). Metformin exposure was associated with lower 90-day mortality in both groups (Figure).

Conclusions: A high ICU admission L/G ratio is associated with higher 90-day mortality in septic DM2 patients. While metformin was associated with improved survival overall, the L/G ratio did not predict which patients would respond to therapy.

P350 Metabolic reprogramming of glycolysis in ECMO patients: identification of prognostic biomarkers through transcriptomic and machine learning approaches

S Chen, L Liu, H Xue, D Hu, Y Zhu, J Wu, H Fu, Y Yang, H Qiu, S Liu

Zhongda Hospital, School of Medicine, Southeast University, Jiangsu Provincial Key Laboratory of Critical Care Medicine, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Trauma Center, Nanjing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P350

Introduction: Extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO) is a critical therapy for patients with severe cardiopulmonary failure, but outcomes are variable and reliable prognostic biomarkers are lacking. Glycolysis plays a key role in cellular metabolism and stress responses. This study explores the differential expression and prognostic potential of glycolysis-related genes in ECMO patients.

Methods: Transcriptomic data from ECMO patients were analyzed to identify differentially expressed glycolysis-related genes between success and failure groups. Bioinformatics analyses included volcano plots, chromosomal distribution, heatmaps, and correlation networks. Functional implications were assessed using Gene Ontology (GO), KEGG, and Gene Set Enrichment Analysis (GSEA). Machine learning methods such as LASSO regression and SVM-RFE were used to identify key prognostic biomarkers, and predictive performance was evaluated through ROC curves, calibration plots, and decision curve analysis (DCA).

Results: Multiple glycolysis-related genes were differentially expressed between ECMO success and failure groups. Enrichment analysis revealed their involvement in metabolic reprogramming, apoptosis, inflammation, and hypoxia. Core genes like ALDOB, FKBP4, IGFBP3, and ALDH3A1 were identified as robust prognostic biomarkers. ROC analysis demonstrated high discriminatory ability (AUC > 0.95 in both training and validation sets). A combined risk model improved predictive accuracy, confirmed by DCA and calibration analyses.

Conclusions: Glycolysis-related gene signatures are closely associated with ECMO outcomes. These biomarkers and the predictive model provide insights into metabolic regulation during ECMO and may serve as tools for risk stratification and personalized management of critically ill patients.

P351 U-shaped association between stress hyperglycemia ratio and mortality in AHRF: validation through consensus clustering and supervised machine learning models

F Mingxing1, Z Qi2, Z Linru2

1Hebei Medical University Third Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, China, 2Hebei Medical University Third Hospital, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P351

Introduction: Acute hypoxemic respiratory failure (AHRF), a life-threatening condition in critically ill patients, correlates with poor clinical outcomes. Although stress hyperglycemia ratio(SHR) is implicated in critical illness severity, its prognostic role in AHRF remains undefined. This study investigates the association between SHR and mortality risk in AHRF patients.

Methods: We retrospectively analyzed 2514 AHRF patients from the MIMIC-IV database. Restricted cubic splines identified optimal SHR thresholds. Kaplan–Meier survival analysis and Cox proportional hazards models evaluated 28-day mortality. Machine learning techniques (Boruta feature selection, CoxBoost + SuperPC modeling) assessed SHR’s predictive utility. Consensus clustering stratified patients into phenotypes based on clinical profiles.

Results: Higher SHR was associated with progressively increased 28-day, in-hospital, and overall mortality (Figure). Patients in the highest SHR quartile had significantly greater 28-day mortality (HR 3.89, 95% CI 2.81–5.40). Restricted cubic splines demonstrated a U-shaped relationship, with an inflection at 0.929. SHR was the most important predictor in Boruta analysis. The CoxBoost + SuperPC model showed the highest discriminative performance (C-index 0.836). Consensus clustering revealed three phenotypes: Phenotype I (metabolically stable, mild inflammation; mortality 2.7%), Phenotype II (moderate inflammation with metabolic imbalance; mortality 24%), and Phenotype III (hyperinflammation with metabolic dysfunction and hypoxia; mortality 30%). SHR ≥ 0.93 was most strongly associated with mortality in Phenotype II.

Conclusions: A U-shaped association between SHR and 28-day mortality in AHRF, with the highest quartile SHR linked to a 3.89-fold mortality risk. Consensus clustering identified three phenotypes, where Phenotype-III(high inflammation-metabolic dysfunction) exhibited the highest mortality, and SHR specifically stratified mortality risk in Phenotype-II.

P352 Acute and long-term impact of tight versus liberal blood glucose control in corticosteroid-treated critically ill patients

E Vanderwegen1, I Vanhorebeek1, F Güiza2, DD Benoit3, E Hoste3, A Wilmer4, B Stessel5, J Dubois5, G Van den Berghe2, J Gunst2

1KU Leuven, Laboratory of Intensive Care Medicine, Leuven, Belgium, 2KU Leuven, Clinical Division and Laboratory of Intensive Care Medicine, Leuven, Belgium, 3UGent, Department of Intensive Care Medicine, Gent, Belgium, 4KU Leuven, Medical and Laboratory Intensive Care Unit, Leuven, Belgium, 5Jessa Hospital, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Hasselt, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P352

Introduction: Iatrogenic severe hyperglycemia by early parenteral nutrition (PN) was shown harmful in critical illness. We hypothesized that corticosteroids in ICU are another iatrogenic cause of severe hyperglycemia, increasing vulnerability for its acute and long-term harmful effects.

Methods: The hypothesis was tested in a secondary analysis of the multicenter TGC-Fast trial, in which patients not receiving early PN were randomized to tight (TGC) or liberal (LGC) glucose control [1]. We assessed whether baseline corticosteroids, defined as systemic corticosteroids initiated on ICU d-1 or 2, statistically interacted with randomization for impact on acute and 2-year outcomes via multivariable regression. The effect of TGC vs LGC was reported for patients with and without baseline corticosteroids, unadjusted and adjusted for baseline risk factors. p < 0.05 and interaction-p < 0.1 were considered significant.

Results: Of 9230 randomized patients, 2293 (24.8%) received baseline corticosteroids. Patients with baseline corticosteroids had higher blood glucose concentrations and insulin needs than patients without corticosteroids. There was significant interaction between baseline corticosteroids and randomization to TGC vs LGC on occurrence of in-ICU delirium, reduced from 33.1% to 27.5% in the first week (p = 0.005) with TGC for patients on corticosteroids only. At 2-year follow-up, significant interaction was present for mortality and mental health outcomes. For patients on corticosteroids only, TGC reduced 2-year mortality from 32.8% to 28.7% (p = 0.03), improved SF-36 mental component scores from 44.5 ± 12.8 to 46.0 ± 12.8 (p = 0.02), and improved subscores for vitality (p = 0.03) and mental health (p = 0.03). Similar results were obtained after adjustment for baseline risk factors.

Conclusions: Corticosteroids in ICU increased the severity of hyperglycemia and TGC in corticosteroid-treated patients reduced in-ICU delirium and improved long-term survival and mental health outcomes.

P353 Intraoperative glycaemic variability and postoperative infections after elective and urgent coronary artery surgery: a Netherlands heart registration analysis

G Mondelli1, TC Rettig2, S Houterman3, PG Noordzij1

1St. Antonius Hospital, Anaesthesiology, Intensive Care and Pain Medicine, Utrecht, Netherlands, 2Amphia Hospital, Department of Anaesthesiology and Intensive Care, Breda, Netherlands, 3Netherlands Heart Registration, Utrecht, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P353

Introduction: This study evaluates whether intraoperative glycaemic variability (IGV) is associated with postoperative infections in a large national cardiac surgery registry, and explores whether this relationship differs in patients with a history of diabetes. IGV may be associated with adverse events after cardiac surgery, including postoperative infections. Pre-existing diabetes may increase the extent of IGV, but most previous studies are small or single centre cohorts.

Methods: A nationwide cohort study that included all adult patients undergoing isolated CABG from the Netherlands Heart Registration between January 2019—June 2024. IGV was defined as the difference between the highest and the lowest intraoperative serum glucose concentration. Multivariable logistic regression analysis adjusted for confounders was performed to investigate the association with postoperative infections after 30 days, including pneumonia, deep sternal wound infection, urinary tract infection and wound infection of the graft harvest site. An interaction term between IGV and diabetes was then added to the model to explore whether the relationship is affected by diabetes status.

Results: A total of 10,308 patients were included. The median IGV was 1.20 (IQR 0.30–2.50) mmol/L and 28.7% had diabetes. 341 patients (3.30%) developed a postoperative infection. Higher IGV was associated with an increased odds of 30-day postoperative infection (adjusted OR 1.08; 95% CI 1.03–1.15; p < 0.01). The interaction term was not significant (p = 0.08), indicating no strong evidence for a differential effect by diabetes status.

Conclusions: Intraoperative GV shows a modest association with postoperative infections in adults undergoing elective and urgent isolated CABG. We found no clear evidence that this association differs in patients with diabetes. These results provide a foundation for further research on IGV, an easily modifiable factor, and its relationship to postoperative infections.

P354 Experience in a Mexican population with high-dose insulin infusion therapy in severe diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) compared with standard therapy

YL Medina Osorio, E Mendoza Arteaga, S Maldonado Valencia, L Godinez Monroy, J Villalobos Silva

Public Health, Norberto Treviño Hospital, ICU, Ciudad Victoria, Mexico

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P354

Introduction: DKA is a severe condition often requiring ICU care. Standard treatment uses crystalloids and insulin 0.1 U/kg/h, but higher doses may be needed in severe or shock cases to hasten ketone clearance, correct acidosis, and improve early metabolic recovery [1].

Methods: Retrospective review (2023–2025) identified 73 adults with severe DKA. Patients were grouped by 24-h insulin dose: high-dose ICU (≥ 0.10 U/kg/h) vs conventional IMCU (0.05–0.10). Demographic, metabolic data, insulin use, fluids, and outcomes were compared with t-test and Chi-square.

Results: A total of 250 records were reviewed; 73 patients (29%) with severe DKA were included. They were divided into two groups: ICU (intensive unit) and IMCU (intermediate unit). Acute kidney injury occurred in 34% of patients, and overall mortality was 7%.

Conclusions: In this Mexican cohort of severe DKA, ICU patients treated with high-dose insulin arrived with more profound acidosis, yet both groups achieved similar metabolic correction at discharge. IMCU patients required greater fluid resuscitation. These results suggest high-dose insulin is safe and effective when early intensive management is applied.

P355 Two types, one protocol: diabetic ketoacidosis management outcomes compared

I Orleane1, L Tutane2, I Konrade1

1Riga East University Hospital, Riga, Latvia, 2Riga Stradins University, Riga, Latvia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P355

Introduction: Standard diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) protocols were developed mainly for type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1DM), with no separate treatment strategies for T1DM vs type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM) and no individualized insulin or potassium adjustment despite potassium’s link to glycaemic changes.With rising DKA cases in T2DM [1], it is unclear whether the same approach is equally effective for both. We aimed to compare DKA treatment response between T1DM and T2DM regarding insulin needs, metabolic correction, and treatment-related complications.

Methods: A retrospective analysis included 142 DKA patients treated in the Intensive Care Unit at Riga East University hospital. Serum pH, potassium and glucose levels were recorded over 24h period as well as given insulin infusion rate. Linear mixed models were used to analyse repeated measures.

Results: Of 142 patients, 53.5% had T1DM and 46.5% T2DM. Insulin infusion rates in the first 24 h were similar (2.1 vs 2.3 U/h), declined over time (p < 0.001), and did not differ by type (p = 0.62). Total insulin requirement was slightly higher in T2DM (55.7 vs 50.3 U, p = 0.12). Glucose decreased significantly (p < 0.001) with consistently higher levels in T2DM (p = 0.003) (Figure). Time to glucose normalisation was longer in T2DM (10.1 vs 8.6 h, p = 0.002) and varied by DKA cause (p < 0.001), being longest in poor adherence and new-onset diabetes. Hypoglycaemia occurred in 9.2% and hypokalaemia in 33.1% without type differences. T1DM patients had lower baseline pH (7.02 vs 7.07, p = 0.046), but pH recovery was similar (p = 0.11).

Conclusions: Both diabetes types responded similarly to standard DKA treatment, with comparable insulin needs and metabolic recovery. Glucose normalized more slowly in T2DM, especially in cases linked to poor adherence or new-onset diabetes. Overall, the protocol is effective for both types, though some patients may benefit from more individualized adjustment.

Ata F et al. BMC Endocr Disord 2023;3:193

Ata F et al. BMC Endocr Disord 2023;3:193

P356 Metformin as a protective factor of mortality in type 2 diabetic patients admitted to the ICU for septic shock: a multicentre retrospective cohort study. The SEPSMET study

V Glinez1, P Martel2, A Harrois1, J Duranteau1, A Rouquette3, M Werner1

1Hôpital Bicêtre, Anesthésie Réanimation Chirurgicale, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France, 2Hôpital Bicêtre, Unité de Recherche Clinique, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France, 3Hôpital Bicêtre, Service de Santé Publique et Epidémiologie, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P356

Introduction: Septic shock is a leading cause of death worldwide and a major concern in type 2 diabetic patients, who are particularly prone to infections. Metformin, the first-line treatment for type 2 diabetes, has pleiotropic effects that may benefit these patients in the context of septic shock. The primary objective was to assess the effect of metformin exposure on 30-day mortality in type 2 diabetic patients admitted to the intensive care unit (ICU) for septic shock.

Methods: This multicentric retrospective cohort study used data from the Clinical Data Warehouse of Greater Paris University Hospitals. Type 2 diabetic patients admitted to the ICU for septic shock between 2017 and 2022 were included and classified according to prior metformin exposure. A propensity score was generated from variables associated with both metformin exposure and 30-day mortality. A doubly robust approach, combining regression adjustment and inverse probability of treatment weighting, was used to estimate the effect of metformin on 30-day mortality.

Results: Among the 2740 included patients, 55% had prior metformin exposure (M +) and 45% received another antidiabetic treatment (M-). The cohort was predominantly male (69.5%), with a mean age of 68 ± 11 years; 72.9% had chronic hypertension, and 31.0% had chronic renal failure. Pulmonary infections were the most frequent source (47.3%). Baseline arterial lactate averaged 4.8 ± 4.7 mmol/L in the M + group vs. 4.4 ± 4.3 mmol/L in the M- group. Overall, 76.0% required mechanical ventilation and all patients received catecholamine vasopressors. Thirty-day mortality was 38.7% overall: 35.9% in the M + group and 42.1% in the M- group. After adjustment, metformin exposure was significantly associated with lower mortality (OR 0.75; 95% CI 0.65–0.89) (Figure). Length of stay, mechanical ventilation rates, and acute kidney injury incidence were comparable between groups.

Conclusions: Prior metformin exposure in type 2 diabetic patients with septic shock was associated with reduced 30-day mortality.

P357 Prognostic impact of serum vitamin E levels on functional outcomes in traumatic brain injury with intracranial lesions

SH Lee, YJ Seong, EJ Jung, SJ Ryu, YS Cho

Chonnam National University Hospital, Emergency Medicine, Gwangju, South Korea

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P357

Introduction: Traumatic brain injury (TBI) remains a significant global health concern and is frequently accompanied by long-term neurological impairment. Although vitamin E is known for its antioxidative properties and potential neuroprotective effects, its role in predicting functional outcomes after TBI has not been well established. This study investigated whether serum vitamin E levels are associated with disability outcomes in patients with intracranial injuries.

Methods: A multicentre, prospective cohort study was conducted at five university hospitals from 2018 to 2020. Adult patients presenting to the emergency department with traumatic intracranial hemorrhage or diffuse axonal injury confirmed on radiological imaging were enrolled. Serum vitamin E concentrations (mg/dL) were grouped into four categories: low (0.0–5.4), low-normal (5.5–10.9), high-normal (11.0–16.9), and high (≥ 17.0). Multilevel logistic regression models were used to estimate adjusted odds ratios (AORs) for poor functional outcomes—defined as Glasgow Outcome Scale (GOS) scores 1–4—at 1 and 6 months.

Results: A total of 550 eligible patients were included. The median serum vitamin E level was 10.0 mg/dL (IQR 8.0–12.3). Disability was observed in 37.1% of patients at 1 month and 36.1% at 6 months. Compared with the high-normal group, the low vitamin E group had significantly higher risks of disability at both 1 month (AOR 3.66; 95% CI 1.62–8.27) and 6 months (AOR 2.60; 95% CI 1.15–5.85). The low-normal group also showed a modest but significant increase in disability risk at 1 month (AOR 1.63; 95% CI 1.08–2.48) and 6 months (AOR 1.60; 95% CI 1.04–2.43).

Conclusions: Lower serum vitamin E levels at presentation were associated with worse functional outcomes at 1 and 6 months in adults with intracranial traumatic brain injury. These findings suggest that vitamin E status may serve as an accessible prognostic biomarker for neurological recovery following TBI.

P358 Mechanisms of road traffic injury among severely injured patients in France, 2010–2024: a nationwide cohort study from the Traumabase registry

M Plaud1, S Archer2, A Harrois1, S Novelli2, M Werner1

1Hôpital Bicêtre, AnesthésieRéanimation Chirurgicale, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France, 2Hôpital Bicêtre, Santé Publique et Epidémiologie, Le Kremlin-Bicêtre, France

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P358

Introduction: Road traffic accidents (RTAs) remain a major cause of severe injury and death, with emerging mobility modes such as e-scooters and bicycles reshaping trauma epidemiology. We aimed to characterize injury patterns, care pathways, and outcomes across mechanisms of injury in France.

Methods: We analyzed data from the French Traumabase® registry including patients involved in RTAs from 2010 to 2024. Patients were categorized by mechanism of injury: motor vehicle, motorized two-wheel, bicycle, e-scooter, and pedestrian. Injury severity, management, and outcomes were compared across groups using multivariate Cox regression for 30-day intensive care unit (ICU) mortality.

Results: A total of 27,280 patients were analyzed, with a median age of 34 years (IQR 24–51) and 77% (21,046) being male. Traumatic brain injury (TBI) occurred in 45% (12,040) of patients, including severe TBI in 32% (3,838). Cyclists, e-scooter users, and pedestrians showed the highest TBI rates. Severe bleeding occurred in 6.9% (1,793), and 67% (18,005) sustained severe trauma. Surgery was performed in 52% of patients (13,116) within 30 days, predominantly orthopedic procedures (72%). 30-day ICU mortality was 7.8% (2,069), highest among pedestrians (15.7%) and lowest among motorized two-wheel vehicles users (5%) (Figure). In multivariate analysis, pedestrian mechanism, age ≥ 45, severe TBI, severe bleeding, and ISS > 16 independently predicted 30-day mortality.

Conclusions: Following RTA, injury patterns and outcomes differed markedly by mechanism. Pedestrians faced the highest mortality, while emerging micromobility users showed increasing neurotrauma burden. These findings call for mechanism-specific prevention strategies and continued adaptation of trauma systems to evolving mobility behaviours.

P360 Bacterial contamination and reprocessing efficacy of ultrasound probes in the emergency department: a prospective observational and randomized controlled study

J Sasaki1, M Kodama2, T Yamanaka2, R Yamamoto2

1Keio University School of Medicine, Emergency and Critical Care Medicine, Tokyo, Japan, 2Keio University School of Medicine, Tokyo, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P360

Introduction: Ultrasound imaging is indispensable in emergency departments (ED) owing to its rapidity, non-invasiveness, and cost-effectiveness; nevertheless, bacterial contamination of ultrasound probes, including multidrug-resistant organisms, poses a substantial risk of cross-infection. This study sought to elucidate the prevalence of bacterial contamination on ultrasound probes within the ED and to evaluate the effectiveness of a novel reprocessing device in mitigating this contamination.

Methods: A two-phase investigation was conducted in the ED. The first phase entailed a prospective observational study (April—July 2023) assessing bacterial contamination on patient-used probes via quantitative and qualitative swab cultures. Variables recorded included probe type, reprocessing method, use of probe covers, and presence of blood contamination. The second phase comprised a randomized controlled trial (August—December 2024) comparing standard reprocessing (ethanol wipes) with high-level disinfection (HLD) via an automated device (trophon®2) in alternate two-week intervals. The primary endpoint was the rate of probe contamination.

Results: During the observational phase, 75 of 101 probes (75%) exhibited bacterial contamination (median 10 CFU/swab). Predominant organisms included methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus epidermidis (MRSE, 18%), Staphylococcus aureus (SA, 18%), and coagulase-negative Staphylococci (CNS, 16%). Reprocessing method was the sole significant factor influencing colony counts; ethanol wipes were associated with reduced contamination (p = 0.04). The RCT phase demonstrated a zero-contamination rate in the trophon®2 group (n = 109) compared to 37.3% (47/126) in controls (p < 0.001) (Table).

Conclusions: Ultrasound probes in the ED are frequently subject to considerable bacterial contamination, including resistant strains; however, rigorous reprocessing using the trophon®2 device effectively eradicates contamination and may significantly attenuate cross-infection risk.

P361 Macro-microcirculatory decoupling in septic shock patients admitted to the emergency department

L Trovato1, M Bellan1, X Monnet2, A Friuli1, C Rosset1, M Beltrame1, F Gavelli3

1Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Emergency Department, Novara, Italy, 2Université Paris-Saclay, Service de Médecine Intensive-Réanimation, Le Kremlin-Bicetre, France, 3Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Department of Translational Medicine, Novara, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P361

Introduction: In septic shock patients the effects of fluid resuscitation are typically measured through macrocirculatory parameters, such as cardiac output. However, whether this adequately reflects tissue perfusion is still debated. Hence, we aimed to assess the concordance between aortic velocity–time integral (VTI) and capillary refill time (CRT) after fluid resuscitation in early septic shock, to identify potential macro-microcirculatory decoupling.

Methods: In septic shock patients admitted to the emergency department VTI and CRT were measured before and after each 500-mL fluid bolus administered by the treating physician. A VTI increase ≥ 15% defined macrocirculatory responders (RMACRO) whereas a CRT decrease ≥ 1 s defined microcirculatory responders (Rmicro). Concordance between continuous changes was assessed through the concordance correlation coefficient (CCC) and agreement between responder classifications was evaluated using Cohen’s kappa.

Results: Sixty-seven fluid boluses were administered to 44 patients (39% male). Median age was 77 [69–85] years, baseline lactate 3 [2–4] mmol/L and baseline CRT was 4 [3–5] seconds; 28-day mortality was 18%. Twenty-six boluses resulted in RMACRO with a VTI change of 24 [18–27] %, and 27 in Rmicro with a CRT change of -1 [-2 to -1] seconds. Overall, VTI changes did not correlate with CRT changes, with a CCC of -0.180 (95% IC -0.329 to -0.023) (Figure). When the agreement between RMACRO and Rmicro was evaluated, Cohen’s kappa was 0.32 (95% IC 0.09–0.56).

Conclusions: In septic shock patients at emergency department admission, fluid resuscitation demonstrated a decoupling between macro- and microcirculatory responses. These findings support a dual assessment of macrocirculation and microcirculation when evaluating the effects of fluid therapy.

P362 Inferior vena cava collapsibility does not identify hydrostatic pulmonary oedema in dyspneic patients at emergency department presentation

E Fabbro1, M Bellan1, X Monnet2, M Crevola1, F Chiarini1, F Rastello1, F Gavelli3

1Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Emergency Department, Novara, Italy, 2Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Service de Médecine Intensive-Réanimation, Novara, Italy, 3Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Department of Translational Medicine, Novara, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P362

Introduction: Inferior vena cava (IVC) respiratory variation is often used as an indicator of volume overload, to detect venous congestion in patients with acute dyspnea presenting to the Emergency Department (ED). However, whether IVC collapsibility is reliable in detecting hydrostatic pulmonary oedema (HPO) is still debated.

Methods: In this prospective observational study, we enrolled patients presenting to the ED for dyspnea and acute respiratory failure. The IVC diameter was measured both at end-inspiration (IVCINSP) and expiration (IVCEXP) to calculate the IVC collapsibility index (ΔIVC) ((IVCEXP − IVCINSP)/IVCEXP × 100). Measurements were obtained at presentation (T0) and after 1 h (T1). The area under the receiver operating characteristic (AUROC) analysis was used to evaluate the ability of ΔIVC to detect HPO.

Results: We enrolled 51 patients with acute respiratory failure (26 HPO, 25 non-HPO). Thirty-four were female and the median age was 81 [74–89] years. Pro-BNP was 9556 [2315–19804] ng/L in HPO patients and 516 [270–1555] ng/L in non-HPO. IVCEXP was significantly higher in HPO than non-HPO (21.0[18.0–23.0] mm vs. 15 [13.6–19.0] mm, respectively; p = 0.001), as well as IVCINSP (18.0 [11.3–20.7]mm vs.9.1[7.1–13.8] mm, respectively; p = 0.002). ΔIVC at T0 was not different between HPO and non-HPO patients (16.7 [4.8–38.9]% vs.40 [17.8–52.8]%, respectively; p = 0.052). Conversely, ΔIVC at T1 was significantly different between the two groups (17.4 [8.3–27.5]% for HPO vs. 49.2 [19.1–56.7]% for non-HPO; p = 0.002). ΔIVC performed poorly in detecting HPO at T0 with an AUROC of 0.657 [95% IC 0.512–0.783] and a cut-off of 38.9% (Figure). ΔIVC at T1 showed an AUROC of 0.748 (0.607–0.859) and a cut-off of 32.2% which was not significantly different from the one of T0 (p = 0.389 for AUROC comparison).

Conclusions: In patients presenting to the ED for acute respiratory failure ΔIVC demonstrates poor accuracy for identifying HPO. Therefore, its use for this purpose in this setting is not recommended.

Figure (abstract P362) Area under the receiver operating curve for the inferior vena cava collapsibility index to detect hydrostatic pulmonary oedema at ED presentation

Area under the receiver operating curve for the inferior vena cava collapsibility index to detect hydrostatic pulmonary oedema at ED presentation

P363 Haemoglobin and protein changes detect hydrostatic pulmonary oedema in dyspneic patients at the emergency department

M Crevola1, M Bellan1, X Monnet2, E Fabbro1, E Viganò1, F Rastello1, M Pirisi3, JL Teboul2, F Gavelli4

1Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Emergency Department, Novara, Italy, 2Université Paris-Saclay, Service de Médecine Intensive-Réanimation, Le Kremlin-Bicetre, France, 3Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Internal Medicine, Novara, Italy, 4Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Department of Translational Medicine, Novara, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P363

Introduction: In hydrostatic pulmonary oedema (HPO) the transudation of protein-free fluid from blood vessels to the pulmonary interstitium has been shown to determine a variation in haemoglobin (ΔHb) and plasma protein concentration (ΔProt). In this study we aim at investigating whether such changes can be used to detect HPO in dyspnoeic patients presenting to the emergency department (ED).

Methods: In this prospective observational study, we enrolled patients admitted to the ED for dyspnoea and acute respiratory failure. Vital signs and blood samples were collected at baseline (T0) and after one hour (T1), along with clinical, radiological and demographic data. Patients were divided into HPO and non-HPO according to clinical, radiological and pro-BNP results, and the ability of ΔHb and ΔProt between T1 and T0 to detect HPO was evaluated.

Results: Fifty-four dyspnoeic patients (19 males; age 83[73–89] years) were enrolled, of whom 27 had HPO. Pro-BNP was 9556[2315–19804]ng/L in HPO and 546[239–1220]ng/L in non-HPO. Baseline Hb did not differ between HPO and non-HPO patients (11.6[10.1–13.2]g/dL vs. 12.5[10.3–13.8]g/dL; p = 0.368), whereas at T1 it was lower in the HPO group (11.2[9.7–12.7]g/dL vs. 12.8[10.9.14.0]g/dL; p = 0.044). ΔHb was significantly higher in HPO patients (-5.3[-6.2 to -3.6]% vs. 1.4[-2.8 to 3.4]%, respectively; p < 0.001) (Figure). ΔHb detected HPO with an area under the receiver operating characteristics (AUROC) of 0.847[0.723–0.930] and a cut-off of -3.8%. Baseline plasma protein levels were similar between HPO and non-HPO (69.2[66.8–73.0]g/L vs. 69.2[63.0–72.2]g/dL; p = 0.337). At T1 protein levels remained comparable (66.8 [63.7–70.9]g/L vs. 68.6 [63.1–72.8]g/dL, respectively; p = 0.429). However, ΔProt was significantly higher in HPO patients (-5.3[-6.2 to -3.6]% vs. 1.4[-2.8 to 3.4]%, respectively; p < 0.001), detecting HPO with an AUROC of 0.843[0.713–0.931] and a cut-off of -4.1%.

Conclusions: Both ΔHb and ΔProt detect HPO in patients admitted to the ED for dyspnoea and acute respiratory failure.

Percentage haemoglobin changes in HPO and non-HPO patients

P364 Prognosis of cardiogenic acute pulmonary edema in the emergency department: value of the emergency heart failure mortality risk grade score

Z Youssef, W Demni, Y Walha, D Hamdi, I Aloui, W Bahria, N Nouira

Monji Slim Hospital, Emergency Department, Tunis, Tunisia

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P364

Introduction: The Emergency Heart Failure Mortality Risk Grade (EHMRG) was proposed to predict mortality in patients with acute heart failure. Our Study aimed to evaluate the prognostic value of the EHMRG score in terms of seven days, one month, and six months mortality in patients presenting with cardiogenic acute pulmonary edema(APE) and to identify predictive factors of 7-day mortality in these patients.

Methods: We conducted a prospective, analytical study in the emergency department of Mongi Slim Hospital in Tunisia, over an 18-month period. Patients admitted for APE were included. The EHMRG score was calculated at admission. The primary endpoint was 7-day mortality. We assessed predictors of 7-day mortality and evaluated the prognostic value of the EHMRG score for 7-day, 1-month, and 6-month mortality.

Results: A total of 224 patients were included. The mean age was 71 ± 11 years. The gender ratio was 1.07. The main comorbidities were hypertension (73.3%), diabetes (48.7%) and coronary artery disease (34.8%). The median EHMRG score was 44[-125–351]. According to the EHMRG score, patients were classified as "very high risk" and "high risk" in 44.1% and 22.8% of cases, respectively. Cumulative mortality at day seven, one month, and six months was 17%, 22% and 28%, respectively. The areas under the ROC curves for EHMRG score regarding mortality at day 7 were respectively 0.84 (p < 10–3), 0.87 (p < 10–3) and 0.85 (p < 10–3). A cut-off value of 50.2 was identified for 7-day mortality (Se = 87%, Sp = 71%, PPV = 38%, NPV = 96%). In multivariate analysis, independent predictors of 7-day mortality were: history of chronic kidney disease (p = 0.02), prior hospitalization for APE (p = 0.02), signs of respiratory distress (p < 10–3), and EHMRG score ≥ 50.2 (p = 0.001).

Conclusions: The EHMRG score, easy to calculate in the emergency setting, helps to predict short and medium-term mortality in patients with cardiogenic APE, thereby encouraging clinicians to optimize management to improve patient outcomes.

P365 FEDU – REMAP: frequent emergency department users – re-entry and mortality predictors

A Rubinetti1, F Gavelli2, M Ratti3, F Balzano1, M Bellan1

1Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Emergency Department, Novara, Italy, 2Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Department of Translational Medicine, Novara, Italy, 3Università degli Studi del Piemonte Orientale, Medical Executive Office, “Maggiore della carità” Teaching Hospital, Novara, Italy

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P365

Introduction: Frequent emergency department users (FEDU) pose a challenge for the Emergency Physician as repeated visits may mask evolving or missed life-threatening conditions. This study aimed to identify predictors of re-entry and 90-day mortality in patients with at least 3 ED visits for the same complaint within one year.

Methods: In this retrospective single-centre study we collected demographic, past medical, laboratory and radiological data of patients with ≥ 3 ED visits for the same reason over one year. Patients were grouped by presenting complaint, and for each category we assessed predictors of re-entry or mortality within 90 days.

Results: Out of 87,087 ED admissions of two years (2023–2024) in a tertiary teaching hospital, 1891 FEDU patients were identified (44.6% women, mean age 60 ± 23 years). Main complaints were mostly abdominal (20.3%), neurological (14.7%) and cardiovascular (8.9%) symptoms. Most of them were discharged at home (60.5%), 26% were admitted to an hospital ward and 9.1% left before a full medical evaluation. Overall, 864 patients had a 90-day event: 101 (11.7%) deaths and 763 (88.3%) readmissions. In abdominal FEDU, performing liver function blood tests during previous ED visits or routine blood tests during the 3rd visit were protective for 90-day events (HR 0.51[0.35–0.73] and HR 0.60[0.45–0.81] respectively; p < 0.001). In neurological FEDU, performing blood tests, brain CT scan or neurological consult before the 3rd access reduced the risks of events (HR 0.39 [0.23–0.66], HR 0.59 [0.42–0.83] and HR 0.52 [0.35–0.77], respectively; p < 0.001), but a neurological consult during the 3rd visit, did not (HR 0.68 [0.46–1.01]; p = 0.067). Notably, in cardiovascular FEDU troponin testing or cardiological consults both before and during the 3rd ED visit did not reduce events (p > 0.05) (Figure).

Conclusions: These preliminary data identify potential predictors of adverse outcomes among FEDU. Integrating them into clinical practice may support earlier recognition and management of high-risk patients.

P366 Intensive care admissions for deliberate self-poisoning a retrospective cohort review

J Thomson1, E Smith1, CA McKenzie2

1University Hospital Southampton, Pharmacy, Southampton, UK, 2NIHR BRC Southampton, Perioperative and Critical Care, NIHR BRC Southampton, Perioperative and Critical Care, Southampton, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P366

Introduction: Deliberate self-poisoning (DSP) leads to 160,000 hospital admissions annually in the UK. A proportion require admission to the intensive care unit (ICU), for all poisonings between 3–10% [1]. The INTOXICATE study collected international data on poisoned patients, although lacked specific data on DSP [2]. This aim of our evaluation was to describe demographics and outcomes for ICU admissions in Southampton due to DSP.

Methods: This retrospective cohort review was registered as a service evaluation; ethics approval was waived. ICU adult admissions over 5 years coded as to “intentional self-poisoning” were identified via multiprofessional notes, categorised and reported in MS Excel, focus on demographics, need for ventilation, ICU mortality and causative agent.

Results: 94 patients (52% female) were “intentional self-poisoning” ICU admissions. Median age was 44 (17–87). 5 of 94 died, 9 were discharged home and 80 transferred to general wards. 69% were intubated on ICU admission, with 4% intubated after ICU admission. 37% required vasopressor support and 5% renal replacement therapy (RRT). Most DSP were mixed, 64 (67%) were mixed ingestions followed by amitriptyline. Most admissions, (73%) were less than 3 days duration. Previous mental health, neurodiversity and psychiatric follow up were poorly documented.

Conclusions: In our cohort age and gender were similar to INTOXICATE (52% vs 53%, 44 vs 41 respectively). Our largest group were mixed ingestions (67% vs 50%), followed by amitriptyline. More patients in our cohort were intubated, received vasoactive agents but fewer required RRT. Limitations of our review include missed patients due to coding, limited analysis and follow up. Our initial capture accounted for < 1% of total ICU admissions. Future work will focus, wider data capture, on previous mental health conditions, neurodiversity, psychiatric history and ICU medication reconciliation for this vulnerable group.

P367 Significance of substance use disorder (SUD) in ICU

MJ Jacka1, P Thompson2, T Hewer3, V Lau2, O Rewa2, P Brindley2, K Macala2, D Kutsogiannis2

1University of Alberta, Anesthesiology and Critical Care, Edmonton, Canada, 2University of Alberta, Critical Care, Edmonton, Canada, 3University of Alberta, Critical Care Research, Edmonton, Canada

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P367

Introduction: SUD-related ICU admissions increased from ~ 10% in the 1990’s to ~ 30% in 2025 [1]. The characteristics of patients with SUD as an admission or contributory diagnosis remain unclear. We studied factors and outcomes associated with survival at a major Canadian urban hospital (Table).

Methods: The Royal Alexandra Hospital is an 869-bed urban hospital with 29 med-surg-trauma ICU beds in Edmonton AB Canada. We conducted a cohort study of all SUD-related admissions to ICU (DSM5 criteria) from June 1 2024 to May 31 2025. We reported data as absolute numbers and compared these using student's t-test or Chi-squared testing as appropriate.

Results: Of 1385 patients, 426 (31%) had SUD as a 1o or 2o diagnosis. Survivors and non-survivors were not differentiated on the basis of age (44.7 vs 43.9) nor gender (male/female 73.1/26.6 vs 72.7/28). APACHE was higher among non-survivors (26.9/17.7, p < 0.001) but LOS was briefer (6.9 vs 4.8 days, p = 0.002). Fewer survivors were homeless (44% vs 57.8%, p = 0.01). More survivors had chronic pain (13.6 vs 4.7%, p = 0.006). Fewer survivors had previous overdose (22.1 vs 37.5%, p = 0.002) or substance-induced psychosis (6.1 vs 11.7%, p = 0.07). Of 128 non-survivors, 84 (66%) died from hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy, virtually all having presented after cardiac arrest. Of these, 29 (35%) met clinical criteria for neurologic determination of death and 24 (29%) went to organ donation.

Conclusions: SUD accounted for or contributed to 31% of ICU admissions, representing a major increase over time. Non-survival was associated with homelessness, previous overdose, substance-induced psychosis, and opioid use. No association was seen with stimulant or alcohol use. We found no association with gender, race, or age. Two-thirds of SUD-related deaths were due to hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy, most occurring after cardiac arrest associated with overdose, and many of these going to eventual organ donation. Further study of factors and outcomes is warranted.

Baldwin WA et al. Chest 1993;103:21–25

P368 Variability, influencing factors, and clinician stress in ICU admission decision-making: the ICUPASS study

M De Silva Morgan, A Binuraj, O Reeve Chen, C Chacko, T Veenith

Royal Wolverhampton Trust, Intensive Care, Wolverhampton, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P368

Introduction: ICU admission decisions are complex and vary widely between clinicians [1]. This study quantified variability in admission decisions, identified influencing factors, and measured associated stress using standardised clinical vignettes.

Methods: A cross-sectional survey of ICU clinicians across the West Midlands, UK captured ratings of ten vignettes on likelihood of admission, decision-making stress, and admission likelihood with unlimited resources (10-point Likert scales) (Figure). Reasons for non-admission were recorded. Clinicians were grouped as decision-makers (DMs: consultants/senior registrars) or non-decision-makers (NDMs: nurses, junior doctors, ACCPs). Data were summarised using median (IQR) and compared with Mann–Whitney U, Friedman, and chi-square tests.

Results: Eighty-four clinicians (47 DMs; 37 NDMs) completed all vignette ratings. Admission likelihood showed marked heterogeneity (median 5, IQR 6). Overall likelihood did not differ between groups, though DMs identified more “clear reject” cases. NDMs reported higher stress than DMs (median 5 vs 4, p < 0.001). Ambiguous cases generated the most variability and stress. Admission likelihood decreased when resources were unlimited (median 3 vs 5, p < 0.001). “Futility” was the most common reason for non-admission (32.3%).

Conclusions: ICU admission decisions show substantial inter-clinician variability and significant stress, especially among NDMs and in ambiguous cases. Structured decision-support tools may help improve consistency and reduce psychological burden on ICU staff.

P369 Association between ICU admission time and 24-h mortality: a single-center retrospective observational study

K Shirakawa1, Y Katsura2, H Watanabe1, R Seo1

1Kobe City Medical Center General Hospital, Department of Emergency Medicine, Kobe, Hyogo, Japan, 2Kobe City Medical Center General Hospital, Department of Anesthesiology, Kobe, Hyogo, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P369

Introduction: ICUs admit patients 24/7. Nighttime admissions have been linked to higher mortality [1], but not in intensivist-staffed settings [2]. Physician handovers may worsen outcomes [3]. Examining admission time–outcome associations is important for resource allocation, yet evidence remains limited. We investigated whether ICU admission time is associated with 24-h mortality in a 24/7 intensivist-staffed ICU, hypothesizing higher risk at night and around handovers.

Methods: This single-center retrospective observational study used data from the Emergency-ICU of Kobe City Medical Center General Hospital registered in Japanese Intensive care PAtient Database (JIPAD). All patients admitted Apr 2017–Jul 2025 were included. Admission hour was modeled as a continuous predictor in logistic regression with restricted cubic splines (ref 14:00). Covariates: age, sex, APACHE II, mechanical ventilation, comorbidities (respiratory failure, metastatic cancer, heart failure, liver failure, cirrhosis, dialysis, immunosuppression, lymphoma, acute myeloid leukemia, multiple myeloma), and admission diagnosis category. 95% CIs were obtained by parametric bootstrap. Sensitivity analyses used hour categories. Admission-time distribution was shown with a violin plot.

Results: Among 5,765 patients, 204 (3.5%) died within 24 h. Admissions clustered at 15:00–20:00. Adjusted risk increased around handovers: at approximately 07:00, OR 1.92 (95% CI 1.23–2.89); at approximately 16:00, OR 1.16 (0.91–1.45). Nighttime (00:00–07:00) showed no significant association, OR 1.25 (0.79–1.94) (Figure). Sensitivity analyses were consistent.

Conclusions: ICU admission time was associated with 24-h mortality. While nighttime admission itself was not linked to a significant increase in risk, admissions during and around handover periods were associated with higher mortality.

P370 Delayed admissions to critical care in a tertiary centre and its complex web of causes

A Naito, A Jervis, S Kent, J Fideles, E Madine, V Metaxa

King´s College Hospital NHS Foundation Trust, Critical Care Department, London, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P370

Introduction: Delayed ICU admissions are associated with increased risk of mortality, higher cost [1], and is a quality indicator. They are defined as > 4 h from decision-to-admit (DTA) time [2]. The Intensive Care National Audit and Research Centre (ICNARC) reports show a significantly higher proportion of delayed admissions at our hospital compared to similar units. Our aim was to ascertain incidence and causes of delayed ICU admissions.

Methods: Single UK tertiary centre retrospective and prospective observational study. Data were collected from a) electronic records and ICNARC reports of adults admitted to ICU 01/10/2024–31/03/2025; b) contemporaneous electronic forms completed by members of the critical care outreach team (CCOT) and ICU bed managers at the time of admission. Planned admissions and patients with no DTA times were excluded.

Results: We identified 597 patients. Median age was 60 years; 36% were female. 19% were delayed admissions with a median 5.2 h. Non-delayed admissions had a median 1.7 h (p < 0.001). There were no significant differences in demographics, ICU length of stay and mortality between the two groups. 17% of delayed admissions had > 1 cause of delay. Capacity, requiring a procedure/imaging, and cleaning/porter delays were the commonest cited reasons. ICU factors affected 13% of delayed admissions (Figure).

Conclusions: Our ICU admissions are delayed mainly due to pre-ICU and hospital factors. Contrary to previous literature, delayed admissions were not associated with increased ICU length of stay or mortality, which could be in part due to our robust CCOT and ability to provide critical care treatment outside ICU. 42% of patients did not have a reason documented for their delayed admission. Next steps are to improve DTA documentation and focus on a detailed qualitative analysis of the reasons for delayed admissions.

P371 Beyond NEWS: external validation of machine learning early warning scores for clinical deterioration

T Bakkes1, J Van Lieshout2, I Paulussen3, A De Bie Dekker3, S Turco2, M Mischi2, U Kaymak2, A Bouwman3

1Eindhoven University of Technology, Electrical Engineering, Eindhoven, Netherlands, 2Eindhoven University of Technology, Eindhoven, Netherlands, 3Catharina Hospital Eindhoven, Eindhoven, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P371

Introduction: Early warning scores (EWS) help monitor hospitalized patients, but conventional systems like the National Early Warning Score (NEWS) focus on current status and are less suited for predicting future complications. Machine learning-based models such as the Advance Alert Monitor (AAM) and Deep Early Warning System (DEWS) aim to improve predictive accuracy [1, 2]. However, external validation and direct comparisons remain limited. We externally validated AAM and DEWS in a large independent cohort and compared their performance against NEWS and each other.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective study using electronic health record data from Catharina Hospital, Eindhoven, the Netherlands, collected between December 2018 and January 2023. The primary composite outcome was clinical deterioration, defined as in-hospital mortality, unplanned ICU admission, or acute surgery. The AAM and DEWS models were each optimized on a training-validation set and evaluated on an independent test set. Performance was compared to the National Early Warning Score (NEWS).

Results: Both data-driven models outperformed the conventional NEWS in predicting clinical deterioration. The AUROC was 81.7% for AAM, 80.0% for DEWS, and 72.8% for NEWS (Figure). In terms of precision-recall performance, DEWS achieved the highest AUPRC at 37.9%, followed by AAM at 35.6%, and NEWS at 26.4%, indicating superior early detection capability for machine learning-based scores.

Conclusions: Data-driven early warning scores demonstrated superior predictive performance compared to the conventional NEWS for identifying patients at risk of clinical deterioration. Both AAM and DEWS achieved higher discrimination and precision. These findings support the potential of machine learning-based models for improving early detection and guiding timely interventions in hospitalized patients.
