P373 The Dutch ICUdata warehouse: standardised and scalable infrastructure for observational ICU data

OV Sigling, AR Jagesar, LA Biesheuvel, M Otten, M Hoogendoorn, PWG Elbers

Amsterdam UMC, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P373

Introduction: Single-hospital observational datasets generalise poorly due to patient and practice variations, and their size restricts prediction model complexity, but combining hospital datasets creates legal, technical and interoperability challenges. We present the Dutch ICUdata warehouse, a multi-centre, multi-EHR standardised data warehouse that addresses these challenges, allowing participating hospitals access to a large, diverse and scalable dataset for machine learning and AI research (Figure).

Methods: A data-use agreement developed with legal and privacy experts governs participation, supported by a participant council ensuring federated oversight. Hospitals deliver raw tabular data from all historical adult ICU admissions, extracted from Epic, HiX or Metavision. An automated, EHR-specific data pipeline transforms the data into the Observational Medical Outcomes Partnership Common Data Model (OMOP-CDM), a widely used healthcare database standard. Mapping of all source clinical concepts to OMOP Standard vocabularies is done by domain experts with assistance from a large language model, resulting in a high-quality, clinically comprehensive dataset suitable for a wide range of research questions. Data from the National Intensive Care Evaluation registry has been linked for validation and outcome enrichment.

Results: Currently, 12 hospitals have signed the data-use agreement of which 8 have delivered data, resulting in a database of 117,430 patients and 130,472 admissions, with up to 20 years of data per hospital. It contains data from both academic and general hospitals of different sizes from across the Netherlands. The automatic pipeline allows rapid scaling with the addition of new hospitals.

Conclusions: ICUdata provides a standardised, growing resource for Dutch ICU research that overcomes key barriers to multi-centre collaboration, demonstrating that national-scale ICU data standardisation is feasible. Future developments will focus on expanding hospital participation across the Netherlands.

P374 AmsterdamUMCdb Van Gogh OMOP: the largest openly accessible European ICU database

OV Sigling, LA Biesheuvel, PJ Thoral, M Otten, A Jagesar, MC Reuland, P Hilders, PWG Elbers

Amsterdam UMC, Amsterdam, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P374

Introduction: AmsterdamUMCdb 1.0, released in 2020 as the first openly accessible European ICU database, consisted solely of data from one campus of the now merged Amsterdam University Medical Center [1]. The forthcoming AmsterdamUMCdb Van Gogh OMOP combines data from both campuses and incorporates the Epic EHR, substantially enlarging the cohort and broadening temporal coverage. The dataset has been transformed into the Observational Medical Outcomes Partnership Common Data Model (OMOP-CDM), making it fully FAIR (Findable, Accessible, Interoperable, Re-usable). Its public release is planned before ISICEM 2026.

Methods: An automatic data pipeline transforms all clinical, device and laboratory tables from both Metavision and Epic to the OMOP-CDM. Mapping of all source clinical concepts to OMOP Standard vocabularies was done by domain experts with assistance from a large language model, resulting in a high-quality, clinically comprehensive dataset. We applied the same risk-based de-identification framework validated for v1.0. Data from the National Intensive Care Evaluation registry has been linked for validation and enrichment of diagnoses and outcomes.

Results: AmsterdamUMCdb Van Gogh OMOP currently comprises 65,110 patients with 78,750 ICU admissions occurring between 2003 and 2024, representing a more than threefold increase in size. The dataset contains more than 2,000 clinical concepts, including high-frequency laboratory measurements, vital signs, and medication administrations.

Conclusions: AmsterdamUMCdb Van Gogh OMOP will become the largest openly accessible high-resolution ICU database to date. It integrates data from three different EHR systems into a single GDPR-compliant and OMOP-mapped dataset. By including both campuses of Amsterdam UMC, it significantly increases cohort size and heterogeneity. The resource will enable reproducible machine learning research, cross-centre benchmarking, and external validation of clinical AI tools.

P376 External validation of preoperative risk models for adverse outcomes after major non-cardiac surgery in frail patients

EM Groenewegen

St Antonius Ziekenhuis, Anesthesiology, Nieuwegein, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P376

Introduction: Effective risk stratification before major surgery can support clinical decision-making in frail patients. However, preoperative risk models have been derived nor validated in this population. This study aimed to externally validate the predictive performance of established preoperative risk models in frail patients.

Methods: A retrospective cohort study in frail patients who underwent elective major non-cardiac surgery between November 2015 and February 2025. All patients had a comprehensive frailty assessment and multidisciplinary team evaluation prior to surgery. Risk scores were calculated for five established models: SORT, POSPOM, SURPAS, RQI and ACS-NSQIP. Outcomes of interest were 120-day mortality, major complications and non-home discharge. Model performance was evaluated with discrimination and calibration. Models with acceptable performance were then evaluated to determine whether adding frailty characteristics enhanced risk prediction.

Results: Among 910 included patients, 66 died (7.3%), 189(20.8%) had a severe complication and 128(14.1%) were not discharged home. Predictive performance for mortality was not acceptable for SORT, POSPOM, SURPAS and RQI, indicating that these models are clinically unsuitable in frail patients. For ACS-NSQIP, discrimination was moderate for mortality (AUROC 0.64 (95%CI 57–0.70), 0.66 (0.61–0.79) for major complications and 0.63(0.58–0.67) for non-home discharge. Calibration demonstrated moderate agreement between predicted and observed outcomes. Adding frailty characteristics to ACS-NSQIP improved model fit, but did not enhance predictive performance.

Conclusions: Most commonly used risk models cannot be used for preoperative risk stratification in frail patients. ACS-NSQIP showed moderate discrimination and calibration, indicating limited clinical potential.

P377 Variables used in the decision-making process for patient transitions within the postoperative setting: an interview study

E Lemmens, M Afonso, M Mischi, I Paulussen, RA Bouwman, S Turco, A Akkermans

Catharina Ziekenhuis, Anesthesiology, Eindhoven, Netherlands

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P377

Introduction: The use of machine learning in the decision-making process for patient transitions offers great potential to relieve the workload within healthcare. To build a model that can advise in patient transitions, it is crucial to first identify which variables should be considered for incorporation. Therefore, this study aimed to identify variables used in the decision-making process during patient transitions postoperatively.

Methods: In this interview study, physicians and nurses on surgical wards of the Catharina Hospital Eindhoven, the Netherlands were contacted between October and November 2025. In a concurrent nested mixed-method design a Likert scale questionnaire was used to verify 9 open-ended questions. Member checks were performed to verify correct interpretation of answers. Interviews were audio taped and transcribed afterwards. Descriptive and thematic analyses were performed. The need for informed consent was waived.

Results: 15 participants were interviewed (11 (8 SD) years of experience, including 4 specialists, 9 (specialized) nurses and 2 physician assistants). Activity of daily living (73%), intake (60%), mobilization (60%), pain (40%) and vital signs (40%) were valued most in the decision to discharge a patient. The variable vital signs (100%) was the most important when considering transfer to the ICU followed by occupation of available nurses (60%) (Figure).

Conclusions: Vital signs, activity of daily living, intake, mobilization and pain are the most important variables for healthcare professionals guiding patient transitions in a surgical ward. These variables should be considered for inclusion in future models aiming to predict within-hospital patient transitions.

P378 Physiological and anatomical severity indices predict early adverse outcomes in critically ill polytrauma patients

B Queiroz, I Pinto, R Duarte, F Bessa, JM Ribeiro

Unidade Local de Saúde Santa Maria, Intensive Care Unit, Lisboa, Portugal

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P378

Introduction: Multisystemic trauma remains a major cause of complications and mortality. Epidemiological data collecting seems essential to increase efficiency of care pathways. Timely identification of high-risk polytrauma patients is crucial to optimize early interventions and resource allocation, making reliable severity prediction scores determinant for rapid triage and clinical decision-making.

Methods: Retrospective data from 378 polytrauma admissions to a tertiary ICU were collected across a four-year period. Statistics analysis, including multivariable logistic regression, was used to assess predictors of mortality, ICU length of stay (LoS) and complications.

Results: Patients were predominantly male (81.7%), 56.5% were younger than 55 years. Mean Injury Severity Score (ISS) was 37, Revised Trauma Score (RTS) 6.5 and TRISS-predicted survival 61.6%. Pre-ICU surgery occurred in 60%, mainly damage-control procedures. Orthopaedic surgery was the most frequent (36.3%), followed by neurosurgery (18.4%). Major complications included rhabdomyolysis (44.9%), coagulopathy (37.4%) and haemorrhagic shock (24.8%). ISS correlated with ICU-LoS (ρ = 0.31; p < 0.001) and with 24h mortality (r = 0.25; p < 0.001), while RTS and TRISS showed inverse correlations. TRISS was associated with worse early outcomes (r = -0.28, p < 0.001). SAPS II predicted 24h mortality (OR 1.06; p = 0.008). ISS independently predicted major complications (OR 1.02; p = 0.012) and transfusion use (OR 1.03; p < 0.001). Trauma type and surgical timing were not associated with mortality. SAPS II, SOFA and ISS showed good discrimination for hospital mortality (AUC 0.81, 0.78 and 0.74).

Conclusions: Physiological and anatomical severity scores (particularly SAPS II, ISS and RTS) are strong predictors of early mortality, complications and transfusion requirements in polytrauma patients. Their systematic application can improve early risk stratification and clinical prioritisation, supporting structured multidisciplinary management to reduce morbidity and shorten ICU stay.

P379 Predictive value of the reverse shock index multiplied by Glasgow Coma Scale score for mortality in ICU patients with heatstroke: a multicenter retrospective study

L Shi1, Z Zhang1, M He1, S Chen1, R Wang2, X Zhang1

1West China Hospital, Sichuan University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Chengdu, China, 2The First Medical Centre, Chinese PLA General Hospital, Department of Neurosurgery, Beijing, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P379

Introduction: To evaluate and compare the predictive value of the reverse shock index multiplied by Glasgow Coma Scale score (rSIG) with the shock index (SI), Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS), and quick Sequential Organ Failure Assessment (qSOFA) score for short-term mortality in intensive care unit (ICU) patients with heatstroke.

Methods: This multicenter retrospective study enrolled 671 heatstroke patients admitted to ICUs across 83 hospitals in southwest China. The predictive performance was compared using receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curves. Independent risk factors were identified via univariate and multivariate logistic regression, and a nomogram prediction model incorporating rSIG and laboratory markers was developed and validated.

Results: Among the cohort, 120 patients (17.88%) died. ROC analysis demonstrated that rSIG had the highest predictive AUC of 0.739 (95% CI: 0.687–0.782), outperforming SI (0.688), GCS (0.703), and qSOFA (0.613) (Figure). Multivariate analysis identified prothrombin time (PT), creatinine (Cr), lactate (Lac), and rSIG as independent predictors. The nomogram integrating these four factors showed good discrimination, with an AUC of 0.80 (95% CI: 0.77–0.84). Subgroup analysis confirmed the consistent predictive value of rSIG.

Conclusions: The rSIG is a simple and effective early screening tool for rapid risk stratification in ICU patients with heatstroke. The predictive model combining rSIG with PT, Cr, and Lac further enhances prognostic accuracy, offering significant clinical utility.

P381 Prediction of readmission to ICU based on Japanese Intensive Care Patient Database by backward-elimination logistic regression with robust train/test pre-processing

A Ishii1, E Hashiba2, Y Sasaki3, H Endo4, K Noto1, J Saito1

1Hirosaki University Graduate School of Medicine, Department of Anesthesiology, Hirosaki, Japan, 2Hirosaki University Hospital, Department of Intensive Care, Hirosaki, Japan, 3Hirosaki University Hospital, Medical Informatics, Hirosaki, Japan, 4Graduate School of Medicine, The University of Tokyo, Department of Healthcare Quality Assessment, Tokyo, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P381

Introduction: Readmission to ICU (Re-ICU) during the same hospitalization causes health economic problems. There are no good predictive models for emergency Re-ICU in Japan. As nationwide ICU database, Japanese Intensive Care Patient Database (JIPAD) is now available, this study aimed to establish a logistic regression model to predict Re-ICU using a machine learning method.

Methods: The JIPAD records between 2018 and 2023 from 95 ICUs were used in this study. We identified single ICU admission (S-ICU) records without death or hospital transfer at ICU discharge and the first ICU admission records of emergency Re-ICU patients during same hospitalization. We investigated those patient’s characteristics and divided the dataset into 70% for predicting model training and 30% for the external model validation. Chi-square test, multivariable logistic regression analysis and a backward stepwise approach using the Python software with the scikit-learn library were employed to analyze. In order to establish the best prediction model 81 features like age, diagnosis, type of admission, patient's severity scores, chronic diseases, therapies in ICU and so on were included into the models.

Results: Among 325,844 records we identified 6,567 the first ICU admission record of emergency Re-ICU patients and 212,698 records of S-ICU patients. The hospital mortality rates of the emergency Re-ICU and S-ICU were 27.2% and 7.7%, respectively. We created the best prediction model using the training dataset with 42 features, which maximum AUROC was 0.73 (Figure). The AUROC of the model for the external validation was 0.72, suggesting appropriateness of the model. The positive features to the model were hematologic disease, digestive surgery, non-operative admission, emergency operation, SAPSII score and so on and the negative features were admission from emergency department, ICU admission after post resuscitation and so on.

Conclusions: Based on JIPAD, a good predictive model for emergency ICU readmission was established and validated.

The selection of covariates for removal at each step was determined by maximizing the mean validation area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) obtained through 10 iterations of threefold cross)-validation

P383 The immunoglobulin-to-total protein ratio identifies distinct immune phenotypes and predicts outcomes in critically ill patients

YK Zhu, HY Xiong, B Wang, Y Kang

West China Hospital, Sichuan University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Chengdu, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P383

Introduction: The immune status of critically ill patients is a key determinant of prognosis, yet conventional measures of circulating immunoglobulins are confounded by both hemodilution and protein redistribution. The immunoglobulin-to-total protein (Ig/TP) ratio may provide a more accurate reflection of humoral immune competence than current measures. This study evaluated the prognostic value of the Ig/TP ratio among ICU patients.

Methods: Adult patients from the MIMIC-IV (v3.1) database with immunoglobulin and total protein data within 72 h of ICU admission available were included. Cox proportional hazards models were used to assess the predictors of 28- and 180-day mortality, followed by restricted cubic spline (RCS) analyses to characterize the nonlinear relationships between Ig/TP and outcomes. Subgroup analyses evaluated the consistency of associations across clinical strata, and random forest models were used to compare the predictive performance of different Ig/TP combinations.

Results: Among 645 patients, both IgA/TP and IgG/TP were significantly associated with mortality (Figure), whereas IgM/TP was not predictive. IgA/TP was associated with both 28-day and 180-day mortality: patients with either low or high ratios had a significantly greater risk of death than those in the intermediate range did. In contrast, the IgG/TP ratio had a weaker prognostic effect, affecting mainly long-term outcomes. The combined IgA/TP + IgG/TP model achieved the highest discrimination for both 28-day (AUC 0.825) and 180-day mortality (AUC 0.837), outperforming models based on absolute immunoglobulin concentrations.

Conclusions: The Ig/TP ratio, particularly the IgA/TP ratio, reliably stratifies immune phenotypes and predicts both short- and long-term mortality in critically ill patients. Combining IgA/TP and IgG/TP data increases prognostic accuracy. These findings may help explain the variable efficacy of IVIG and support Ig/TP-based profiling as a tool for personalized immunotherapy in the ICU.

P384 Revisiting organ failure in sepsis: incorporating immunologic markers into the SOFA concept in severe COVID-19

K Mattern1, J Grundmann1, O Moerer1, MS Winkler1, A Rau1, L Saager1, J Schanz2, B Tampe3, J Wieditz4, C Grimm1

1University Medical Center Göttingen, Department of Anesthesiology and Intensive Care Medicine, Göttingen, Germany, 2University Medical Center Göttingen, Department of Hematology and Oncology, Göttingen, Germany, 3University Medical Center Göttingen, Department of Nephrology and Rheumatology, Göttingen, Germany, 4University Medical Center Göttingen, Department of Medical Statistics, Göttingen, Germany

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P384

Introduction: COVID-19 represents a form of viral sepsis. Sepsis is diagnosed based on the presence of a pathogen and organ dysfunction, which is commonly assessed using the Sequential Organ Failure Assessment (SOFA) score [1]. However, the SOFA score does not include the underlying immune dysregulation that constitutes the central hallmark of sepsis. This study aimed to integrate laboratory and immunological parameters into an extended scoring concept (“iSOFA”) and to evaluate their prognostic relevance for survival in patients with severe COVID-19.

Methods: We retrospectively analyzed 207 ICU-treated patients with confirmed severe COVID-19. Conventional SOFA parameters were combined with immunological markers: CRP, D-dimers, ferritin, fibrinogen, IL-6, IL-2R, lactate, procalcitonin, leukocytes, and FACS-based lymphocyte subsets (CD3⁺, CD4⁺, CD8⁺, CD19⁺, CD3⁺/56⁺/16⁺). After multiple imputation (Rubin’s rules), stepwise forward logistic regression was applied, including SOFA in all models. Final analysis was performed on patients with complete data.

Results: Median age was 61 (50–71) years; the median SOFA score was 3 (2–4); 72% of patients were intubated and 37% received ECMO therapy. Overall mortality was 38%. In multivariable analysis, low CD19⁺ B-cells, CD4⁺ T cells, NK cells and CD8⁺ T cells, as well as elevated lactate levels were independently associated with mortality (Table). Area under ROC could be increased from 69.7%, 95%-CI [58.9–80.4] for a SOFA-only model to 89.2%, 95%-CI [82.7–95.6] for the “iSOFA” model.

Conclusions: Integrating immunological markers into the SOFA framework enhances risk stratification in COVID-19-associated sepsis. The iSOFA score represents a biologically informed extension of organ failure assessment, supporting a more holistic view of sepsis pathophysiology.

P385 SOFA 2.0 is superior to six scoring systems for predicting ICU mortality in sepsis patients: a multicenter retrospective data

X Luo, FR Lin, T Huang, JZ Zhang, AQ Lao, ZF Yang

Sun Yatsen Memorial Hospital, Sun Yatsen University, Intersive Care Unit, Guangzhou, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P385

Introduction: Sepsis remains a life-threatening condition necessitating early identification of high-risk patients. Since conventional scoring systems show declining predictive performance over time, we evaluated the newly introduced SOFA 2.0 score against established prognostic models for predicting ICU mortality.

Methods: Patient data were extracted from the Sun Yat-sen Memorial Hospital (SYSMH), Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care IV version 3.1 (MIMIC-IV v3.1) and eICU Collaborative Research Database (eICU-CRD) (Figure). We calculated the sequential organ failure assessment (SOFA) 2.0 score based on its latest definition and compared against six assessment models (SOFA 1.0, Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS), systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS), Oxford Acute Severity of Illness Score (OASIS), Charlson comorbidity index (Charlson). Discrimination, calibration, and clinical utility were evaluated using area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC), Youden index, decision curve analysis (DCA), calibration curves and multiple subgroup analyses.

Results: There were 871, 19028, and 7405 patients in the SYSMH, MIMIC-IV and eICU-CRD cohorts, respectively. In the internal database, the discrimination for ICU mortality with the SOFA 2.0 model was superior to the six traditional score of illness models, including SOFA, GCS, SIRS, OASIS, Charlson and APACHE II. Furthermore, SOFA 2.0 showed the largest reasonable risk threshold probability range, highest net benefit and Youden index, compared to traditional scoring systems models. Subgroup analysis and external cohorts yielded results consistent with the overall findings. Finally, an exploratory analysis confirmed that predictive model was improved when lactate was incorporated into the SOFA 2.0 score.

Conclusions: SOFA 2.0 demonstrated superior predictive capability of ICU mortality compared to traditional six models. We strongly recommend the early application of the SOFA 2.0 score in the intensive care unit.

P386 Prognostic value of the SOFA-2 score in emergency department patients with suspected sepsis: a multicenter prospective study

XX Xu1, YH Xu1, Q Zhong1, LT Jin2, RH Lin1, SW Jin3, ZH Zhang4, YP Jiang1

1Taizhou Hospital of Zhejiang Province, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Taizhou, China, 2Taizhou Central Hospital Taizhou University Hospital, Department of Emergency Medicine, Taizhou, China, 3The Second Affiliated Hospital and Yuying Children´s Hospital of Wenzhou Medical University, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Wenzhou, China, 4Sir Run Run Shaw Hospital, Department of Emergency Medicine, Taizhou, China

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P386

Introduction: Sepsis is a life-threatening condition in the emergency department (ED), making early and accurate assessment crucial. Although the updated SOFA-2 score performs well in the ICU, its value for ED patients with suspected sepsis is not well established [1].

Methods: We conducted a multicenter, prospective cohort study of patients with suspected sepsis admitted from the ED between May 2022 and August 2025. Clinical data and 28-day outcomes were recorded. We analyzed and compared the predictive value of SOFA-2, SOFA-1, qSOFA, and NEWS for 28-day mortality.

Results: Among 1,056 enrolled patients, the 28-day mortality rate was 15.34% (163/1056). The SOFA-2 score provided the best discrimination for mortality (AUROC: 0.744; 95% CI: 0.700–0.788) (Figure), significantly outperforming the other scores. Using an optimal cutoff of 7.5, SOFA-2 achieved a high negative predictive value of 91.2%. After multivariate adjustment, each 1-point increase in SOFA-2 was independently associated with an 18% higher risk of death (OR = 1.18, 95% CI: 1.13–1.24, p < 0.001).

Conclusions: The SOFA-2 score shows superior predictive accuracy for 28-day mortality in ED patients with suspected sepsis compared to other common scores. It is an effective tool for early risk stratification and can help guide clinical management.

P387 Change of mean SOFA score to surrogate final outcome: a secondary analysis of the ImmunoSep trial

F Bloos1, K Leventogiannis2, C Nakou3, P Amoiridou4, A Flevari5, A Kosmidou6, G Nikolaidis7, A De Nooijer8, M Netea9, EJ Giamarellos Bourboulis2

1Jena University Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Jena, Germany, 2National and Kapodistrian University of Athens, 4th Department of Internal Medicine, Athens, Greece, 3Ippokrateion General Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Thessaloniki, Greece, 4AHEPA University Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Thessaloniki, Greece, 5Sotiria Hospital, Polyvalent Intensive Care Unit, Athens, Greece, 6Sotiria Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Athens, Greece, 7Asklipieion Hospital, Intensive Care Unit, Voula, Greece, 8Radboud University Medical Center, Intensive Care Unit, Nijmegen, Netherlands, 9Radboud University Medical Center, Department of Internal Medicine, Nijmegen, Netherlands.

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P387

Introduction: The primary endpoint of the ImmunoSep trial (NCT04990232) was the attainment of ≥ 1.4 points decrease of mean sequential organ failure assessment (SOFA) score by day 9 [1]. Selection of the primary endpoint was based on previous data from the VISEP [2] and MAXSEP [3] randomized clinical trials, which indicated that reduction of the mean SOFA by 1.4 points is associated with lower 28-day mortality among patients with severe sepsis. In this secondary analysis of the ImmunoSep trial, we assessed whether this primary endpoint could function as surrogate for 28-day mortality.

Methods: The primary efficacy endpoint of the ImmunoSep trial was calculated by subtracting the mean value of SOFA scores between days 2 and 9 from baseline score of day 1. Participants achieving a reduction of ≥ 1.4 points were classified as achievers, all others as non-achievers. For this secondary analysis, 28-day mortality was compared between achievers and non-achievers. Comparison was conducted using Fisher’s exact test; odds ratio (OR) and 95% confidence interval (CI) were calculated according to Mantel and Haenszel.

Results: A total of 276 participants were analysed in the primary population analysis, all of whom were included in this secondary analysis. Eight of 72 achievers (11.1%) died by day 28 compared to 121 of 204 non-achievers (59.3%) of the primary endpoint (Figure).

Conclusions: Decrease of mean SOFA score of at least 1.4-points effectively served as surrogate for 28-day mortality in the ImmunoSep trial. Further studies are necessary to validate whether this surrogate endpoint can reliably replace 28-day mortality in future pilot clinical trials.

P389 SAPS 3 evaluation in two high dependency units – performance analysis and prognostic value

P Bonazza1, E Sellin Bredberg2, M Nilsson3

1Karolinska University Hospital, Acute and Reparative Medicine, Stockholm, Sweden, 2Karolinska Institutet, Stockholm, Sweden, 3Karolinska Institutet, Department of Medicine, Solna, Stockholm, Sweden

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P389

Introduction: High dependency units (HDUs) care for patients with acute or impending organ dysfunction who require close monitoring but not full intensive care. As ICU capacity remains limited, HDUs play an increasingly important role, yet validated prognostic tools tailored to this setting are scarce. The Simplified Acute Physiology Score 3 (SAPS-3) is widely used in ICUs, but its performance in Swedish HDUs is unknown. Since the Swedish Intensive Care Registry (SIR) recalibrated SAPS-3 for Swedish ICUs [1], the model may better reflect mortality risk in Swedish critical-care environments, making evaluation in HDUs relevant. Our aim was to assess the ability of SAPS-3 to predict 30-day mortality in patients admitted to two HDUs at a tertiary university hospital.

Methods: Retrospective cohort study including 265 adults admitted to two medical HDUs at Karolinska University Hospital in 2024. SAPS-3 was calculated from admission data. Discrimination was evaluated using AUC, calibration using SMR, and multivariable logistic regression assessed the independent association between SAPS-3 and mortality.

Results: Mean SAPS-3 was 53.0 ± 14.0. Observed 30-day mortality was 18.1% vs. a predicted 15.9% (SMR 1.14). SAPS-3 demonstrated strong discrimination (AUC 0.854, 95% CI 0.802–0.906). Each one-point increase in SAPS-3 corresponded to a 10% increase in mortality risk (OR 1.10, p < 0.001). A “No ICU” treatment-restriction order was also associated with higher mortality (OR 3.86, p < 0.01).

Conclusions: SAPS-3 showed good predictive performance for HDU patients, comparable to results from ICU cohorts. The acceptable calibration may partly reflect the SIR recalibration of SAPS-3 for Swedish practice. These findings support SAPS-3 as a useful risk-stratification tool in HDUs. Larger multicentre studies are needed to confirm generalizability and evaluate whether further calibration is required.

P390 Centralized management panel for stratifying the severity of bronchiolitis and sepsis in a pediatric ICU

N Arguedas1, A Felipe1, A Solé1, E Esteban1, I Jordán1, R Casadevall2, J Gibert3, F García3, O Rodríguez1, FJ Cambra1

1Hospital Sant Joan de Déu, Pediatric Intensive Care Unit PICU, Esplugues de Llobregat, Spain, 2Hospital Sant Joan de Déu, Healthcare Management Support Unit, Esplugues de Llobregat, Spain, 3Hospital Sant Joan de Déu, Data and Digital Strategy Unit, Esplugues de Llobregat, Spain

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P390

Introduction: In pediatric intensive care units (PICU), bronchiolitis and sepsis are relevant due to their high incidence and the difficulty of predicting their clinical course. At Sant Joan de Déu Hospital, have been developed and validated two severity scoring systems: BROSJOD for bronchiolitis and PESERS for sepsis [1, 2]. Both scores are reliable tools for diagnosis and severity classification.

Methods: To implement centralized management, patient data streams from hospital information systems were integrated through queries that enabled near real-time visualization. Risk scores are generated automatically and updated every 5 min through algorithms that track key clinical metrics. The system uses the Denodo platform to create and refresh dynamic views, which are then displayed in Tableau, giving clinicians a clear and actionable overview of patient status and evolving risk. This dashboard is embedded within a centralized monitoring framework, both in the PICU and in the Clinical Command Center [3].

Results: A data ecosystem was created, resulting in a centralized management dashboard with real-time updates (Figure).

Conclusions: The dashboard aims to provide early warnings of patient deterioration and to support clinical decision-making by offering an integrated, continuously updated view of bronchiolitis severity and the early diagnosis and stratification of sepsis, ultimately improving care quality and outcomes for critically ill children.

P391 Enhancing ICU information delivery: integrating analog with digital approaches

L Lim1, C Kang2, H Lee1, HG Ryu2, SY Oh2

1Seoul National University Hospital, Department of Anesthesiology and Pain Medicine, Seoul, South Korea, 2Seoul National University Hospital, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Seoul, South Korea

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P391

Introduction: Caregivers of critically ill patients often experience significant distress, partly due to insufficient or poorly accessible information. While digital information delivery such as web-based mobile video has become standard, particularly after COVID-19, concerns exist about accessibility and the digital divide. We hypothesized that supplementing mobile-based video education with a physical leaflet would enhance caregiver satisfaction with ICU information.

Methods: We conducted a single-center, pre–post implementation study in a surgical intensive care unit (ICU). During the pre-implementation phase (July–August 2023), caregivers received a mobile video explaining the ICU environment, visiting policies, and care processes. During the post-implementation phase (September 2023–February 2024), a physical leaflet summarizing the same key information was provided in addition to the video. To evaluate information satisfaction, caregivers completed System Usability Scale (SUS) at discharge. The primary outcome was SUS total score; secondary outcomes included usability, learnability, and perception of appropriateness and timeliness.

Results: Among 143 caregivers (video only: n = 60; video + leaflet: n = 83), baseline characteristics were similar between groups. SUS total scores were higher when the leaflet was added to the video (77.5 [67.5–86.2]) compared with video alone (65.0 [57.5–80.8]), as were usability (78.1 vs. 65.6) and learnability (75.0 vs. 62.5), as shown in the Table. A greater proportion of caregivers agreed that the information provided was appropriate and timeliness ratings.

Conclusions: Supplementing mobile-based video with a physical leaflet significantly improved caregiver satisfaction with ICU information compared with video alone. Integrating analog and digital approaches may better address diverse caregiver needs and enhance communication quality in critical care settings.

P393 Improving continuity of care: audit of discharge summaries and follow-up for bisoprolol and amiodarone started in ICU

S Harvey, A Krishan, M Rich, J Small, U Watson, B.J Philips

University Hospitals Sussex NHS Foundation Trust, Intensive Care Unit, Brighton, UK

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P393

Introduction: Medications are often started in Intensive Care to manage rapidly evolving complications of critical illness. Bisoprolol and amiodarone are commonly used for arrhythmias, yet continuation after ICU is not always necessary and may cause harm [1]. Both require clear documentation and planned follow-up. We examined why these medications were initiated, the quality of discharge documentation, and whether follow-up was adequate.

Methods: We audited ICU patients discharged between Oct 2024 and Oct 2025 with new bisoprolol or amiodarone prescriptions. ICU and hospital discharge summaries were reviewed for documentation of the medication, indication, and planned follow-up. Documentation was compared with NICE guidelines [2]. Patients who died in ICU or lacked accessible documents were excluded.

Results: 83 patients (26 women, mean age 66) were discharged with new bisoprolol prescriptions; discharge documentation is summarised below (Figure). 34/83 ICU discharge summaries did not highlight the new medication and indication, and only 5/83 ICU discharge summaries documented ideal discontinuation plans on ward stepdown. 3 patients (1 woman, mean age 80) were discharged on newly initiated amiodarone. 2/3 ICU discharge summaries did not highlight the new medication and indication; follow-up plans were absent. Only one patient continued amiodarone after hospital discharge, with appropriate review arranged.

Conclusions: The audit highlights major gaps in documenting ICU-initiated medications and planning follow-up. In response, we are delivering targeted education and updating the discharge summary template to prompt clearer recording. Improved collaboration with the ICU rehabilitation team may further support safer transitions of care and improved long-term medication management.

P394 The impact of ICU follow-up: identifying ongoing needs six months after discharge

I Quinteiro1, C Cerca1, M Cardoso Costa2, A Balbino1, AF Monteiro1, S Galhano1, A Valente Santos1, L Bento1

1Unidade Local de Saúde de São José, Unidade de Urgência Médica, Lisboa, Portugal, 2Unidade Local de Saúde de São José, CRECMO, Lisboa, Portugal

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P394

Introduction: Follow-up after ICU discharge enables early detection of post-intensive care syndrome (PICS) and timely referral to specialists when long-term problems are identified. This study aims to assess the effectiveness of a structured ICU follow-up program in detecting ongoing health concerns and facilitating access to multidisciplinary care.

Methods: We performed a retrospective analysis of patients attending the ICU follow-up clinic of a tertiary hospital between January and December 2024. Appointments occurred 6 months after ICU discharge, either in person or via videocall. Patients with an ICU stay shorter than 48 h were excluded. Data on physical, cognitive, and psychological outcomes were collected using validated questionnaires and clinical assessments.

Results: Among 855 ICU admissions, 399 patients met eligibility criteria; 364 had stays under 48 h, and 92 died in the ICU. Of eligible patients, 107 (29.3%) attended the appointment—64 men (59.8%) and 43 women (40.2%), with a mean age of 53.0 ± 17.5 years, and an average ICU stay of 11.0 ± 9.3 days. Six months after ICU discharge, patients scored 92.7 ± 19.0 on the Barthel Index, 70.3 ± 20.0 on the EQ-5D, 4.5 ± 4.8 on the HADS, and 10.4 ± 11.4 on the PTSS-10. Thirty-seven patients (34.6%) were referred to additional specialties due to symptoms identified during screening or missed follow-up, most commonly Psychology and Pulmonology.

Conclusions: In our cohort, ICU follow-up had a significant impact on long-term care, serving as a safety net for complex patients by addressing all dimensions of PICS and facilitating specialist referrals. In 35% of patients, follow-up resulted in referral to an appropriate specialty for further assessment. These results led to restructuring follow-up pathways for selected patient groups in our centre.

P395 A qualitative analysis of coping types among ICU survivors with chronic intensive care related pain

G Laporte, BA EbobAnya, C Gélinas

McGill University, Ingram School of Nursing, Montreal, Canada

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P395

Introduction: In Canada, one in three intensive care (ICU) survivors develops chronic pain [1] that can interfere with daily functioning and affect quality of life [2]. In ICU survivors, the role of coping in the progression and management of chronic ICU-related pain (CIRP) remains poorly understood. Our study aimed to propose a typology of coping types based on qualitative interviews.

Methods: According to the qualitative typology method developed by Gerhardt and Weber [3], we established a typology of coping styles based on interviews conducted with ICU survivors who experienced pain > 3/10 three months after discharge. Interview questions focused on the challenges, preferences, and perceived effectiveness of the coping strategies they used. We wrote individual case summaries and regrouped ICU survivors who adopted a similar coping style. We drafted a general description of each case grouping, which was identified by a representative label.

Results: We conducted interviews with 23 ICU survivors over 24 months. Participants were mostly men (n = 15), aged 25 to 85 years old (mean = 56). They had spent between 3 and 65 days in the ICU (mean = 12). Most had been admitted for surgical reasons (n = 14), and experienced pain ranging from 3 to 8 (mean = 6) at 3 months post-ICU. Our typology uncovered four coping types among these ICU survivors, based on two main traits: independence and proactivity. Most of our participants (n = 11) were categorized as social-proactive or independent-proactive (n = 4) types. The remaining participants were classified as independent-detached (n = 5) or social-detached (n = 3) types.

Conclusions: Our results indicate ICU survivors can show different coping styles when faced with chronic pain. Future studies should explore the relationship between coping types and health outcomes post-ICU.

P396 The impact of social connectivity on ICU survivor and family recovery

S Tang1, JY Kung2, V Kalinin1, S Tymchuk3, L Carayannopoulos4, ME Wilcox1

1University of Alberta, Department of Critical Care Medicine, Edmonton, Canada, 2University of Alberta, Geoffrey Robyn Sperber Health Sciences Library, Edmonton, Canada, 3University of Alberta, Department of Psychiatry, Edmonton, Canada, 4McMaster University, Department of Medicine, Hamilton, Canada

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P396

Introduction: This study investigates the effects of social connectivity on ICU survivors and their families. Social support has positive impacts on health outcomes in many populations. With rising rates of ICU survivorship, optimization of follow-up care is increasingly important. ICU survivors and their families often experience enduring symptoms impacting physical, psychological and cognitive aspects of their lives [1]. This is described as post-intensive care syndrome (PICS) or post-intensive care syndrome-family (PICS-F).

Methods: The PRISMA-ScR [2] and Joanna Briggs Institute [3] guidelines were used to structure this scoping review. We searched MEDLINE, EMBASE, CINAHL, and PsychInfo, grey literature, and the first 200 Google Scholar results. Our population of interest was adult ICU survivors and their families or caregivers. We included all research except reviews and case reports. Abstracts and full texts were assessed by two independent reviewers, and qualitative data was collected and analyzed.

Results: Our analysis included 37 full texts. ICU survivors required 6–12 months of recovery to regain physical function, measured by SF-36 scores, walk tests and spirometry. Those with more social supports reported subjective improved physical recovery. ICU families and caregivers experienced fewer depressive symptoms and improved quality of life with social support, but anxiety was often found to be unrelated to social supports.

Conclusions: Social connectivity can provide benefit in the recovery of physical function, mental health symptoms, and overall quality of life in ICU survivors and their families. Further research will be required to elucidate the most effective ways to improve social connectivity and recovery, and to quantify the impact of organized support groups.

P397 Association of frailty identified by a population-level frailty index and long-term ICU patient outcomes

Q Li1, D Nassim1, H Dahel2, D Williamson3, S Chagnon4, JP Bluteau4, J Rinfret5, J Beaucage-Charron5, JP Emond6, HT Wang7

1University of Montreal, Faculty of Medicine, Montreal, Canada, 2University of Montreal, Faculty of Pharmacy, Montreal, Canada, 3Centre Intégré Universitaire de Santé et de Services Sociaux du Norddel´Île de Montréal, SacréCoeur Hospital, Montreal, Canada, 4Centre Intégré Universitaire de Santé et de Services Sociaux de l´Estdel´Île de Montréal, Montreal, Canada, 5Maisonneuve-Rosemont Hospital, Department of Pharmacy, Montreal, Canada, 6University of Laval, Division of Geriatric Medicine, Quebec, Canada, 7Centre Hospitalier de l´Université de Montréal, Division of Critical Care, Montreal, Canada

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P397

Introduction: Frailty is a multidimensional state of reduced physiological reserve associated with increased vulnerability to acute stressors, including critical illness. Frail ICU patients experience more adverse outcomes [1], but few studies have examined their long-term outcomes. Our objective was to assess the association between frailty and long-term ICU outcomes, including 1-year mortality and health resource use, using a Frailty Index (FI) derived from medico-administrative data.

Methods: We identified adults admitted between 2015–2023 to the ICUs of two hospitals in Quebec (Canada). Clinical information was extracted from medico-administrative databases (hospital discharge summaries, home care records, in-hospital medication data). A 30-item Frailty Index (FI) was created across comorbidity, cognitive, functional, psychosocial, and socioeconomic domains [2]. Frailty was defined as FI ≥ 0.20 [2]. Outcomes at 3 months and 1 year were compared using descriptive analyses and multivariable logistic regression adjusted for age, sex, dialysis, mechanical ventilation, admission year, and hospital site.

Results: Among 10,857 patients, 23% were frail and 8871 patients survived initial hospitalization. Frailty was associated with higher 3-month mortality (11.5% vs 4.7%, p < 0.001) and 1-year mortality (26.7% vs 10.4%, p < 0.001) (Table). Frail patients had more 1-year hospitalizations (45.9% vs 23.1%, p < 0.001) and ED visits (43.1% vs 30%, p = 0.050). Frailty strongly predicted 1-year mortality (aOR 2.51, 95% CI 2.18–2.88). Mechanical ventilation (aOR 1.43, 95% CI 1.23–1.65) and dialysis and (aOR 1.75, 95% CI 1.33–2.29) also increased risk.

Conclusions: Our 30-item Frailty Index independently predicted 1-year mortality and was associated with greater post-ICU healthcare use.

P398 The importance of frailty, and anticipating post intensive care syndrome

LG Élo1, O Szucs2, E Benkovics1, Z Molnár1, G Élö1, L Zubek1

1Semmelweis University, Department of Intensive Therapy, Budapest, Hungary, 2Semmelweis University, Department of Surgery, Transplantation and Gastroenterology, Budapest, Hungary

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P398

Introduction: We have conducted a single-centre prospective observational study at the Semmelweis University Department of Intensive Therapy. We focused on post-intensive care syndrome (PICS), quality of life, and their correlation with frailty and acute illness severity at admission.

Methods: We conducted our study on 245 patients at two ICUs in our department, with diverse aetiologies and pre-hospitalisation histories. Exclusion criteria were death or discharge before 24 h of ICU care. At admission, we recorded the Clinical Frailty Scale (CFS), APACHE II, and SAPS II scores. At 3 months after discharge, EuroQoL 5D5L was collected via telephone interview among 103 of 112 survivors. At 12 months, EQ-5D, SF-36 QoL survey, MMS, and Beck Depression Inventory were collected during a personal interview, with 45 respondents out of 97 survivors.

Results: Compared with validated population studies of Hungary [1], our patients have shown below-average scores in General Health, Physical Role Impairment, and Mental Health. We observed above-average scores in Bodily Pain, Social Functioning, Vitality and Emotional Role Impairment dimensions. The average EQ5D index was 0.92 at 3 months and 0.84 at 12 months. MMS test showed no cognitive impairment. U tests suggested a strong correlation between Physical Functioning and CFS, General Health and APACHE/SAPS-II. Pain, Social Function, Emotional Role Impairment and EQ5D correlated with all three. ROC analysis has shown AUC > 0.7 for CFS and Physical functioning, as well as the one-year EQ5D index.

Conclusions: Contrary to the established literature [2], we have observed that our patients' long-term QoL is not significantly worse than that of the general population and that there is a strong statistical correlation between frailty and acute illness severity at admission and eventual QoL. We have failed to show a significant predictive value for any of the parameters monitored at admission, for eventual QoL.

P399 Quality of dying and death in a Korean surgical ICU: a comparative analysis of physician and nurse perceptions

YCJ Jeong1, HL Lee2

1Seoul National University Hospital, Critical Care Medicine, Seoul, South Korea, 2Seoul National University Hospital, Anesthesiology and Pain medicine, Seoul, South Korea

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P399

Introduction: Given the differences in intensive care unit (ICU) settings, few studies have looked at how medical staff view death in surgical ICUs. This research aims to address this gap by studying how medical personnel, mainly in surgical ICUs, view and understand death. These insights are crucial for raising the quality of care for patients at the end-of-life.

Methods: A preliminary prospective study was carried out between June 2023 to October 2025. Physicians and nursing staff directly involved in the care of patients who had passed away were invited to complete the Quality of Dying and Death (QODD) survey within two days following the patient’s demise. The 20-item QODD instrument yields a total score from 0 to 100, with higher scores indicating a better end-of-life experience.

Results: Analysis of 143 questionnaires from 58 ICU staff (22 doctors, 36 nurses) caring for 49 deceased patients showed doctors reported higher QODD scores than nurses (23.8 ± 9.4 vs. 19.4 ± 9.3; p = 0.007). Doctors significantly experienced more death than nurses (r = -0.48, p < 0.01) but received less education about death (r = 0.59, p < 0.01). Discussions about patient wishes and applying appropriate sedation correlated positively with better death quality, respectively (r = 0.78, p < 0.01; r = 0.65, p = 0.01). Additional findings revealed that despite advanced care planning and end-of-life decision documentation (85.7%), 16.3% received cardiopulmonary resuscitation within 24 h before death. Despite low prevalence on use of sedatives and analgesics (53.1% and 65.3%), low GCS seem to give the impression of appropriate sedation.

Conclusions: In the present study, physicians and nurses in surgical ICUs reported substantially lower quality of dying and death scores for their patients compared with those typically observed in medical ICUs. Our findings underscore the necessity of more consistent advance care planning to elevate the standard of end-of-life care for patients and their healthcare providers.

P400 Postoperative persistent inflammation, immunosuppression, and catabolism syndrome (PIICS): incidence and outcomes after major surgery

K Sasaki1, K Tojo2, T Mihara1

1Department of Health Data Science, Graduate School of Data Science, Yokohama City University, Yokohama, Japan, 2Department of Anesthesiology, Yokohama City University School of Medicine, Yokohama, Japan

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P400

Introduction: Persistent Inflammation, Immunosuppression, and Catabolism Syndrome (PIICS) has been described as a chronic critical condition characterized by sustained inflammatory and immune dysregulation [1]. Although PIICS has primarily been studied in critically ill patients, major surgery also imposes substantial physiological stress, yet whether PIICS occurs in surgical patients and affects their outcomes remains unknown. We therefore aimed to determine the incidence, clinical characteristics, and mortality impact of PIICS after major surgery.

Methods: We conducted a retrospective observational study including 18,137 adults undergoing major surgery lasting > 2 h. PIICS was defined around postoperative day 14 when all of the following criteria were met: C-reactive protein > 3.0 mg/dL, albumin < 3.0 g/dL, and lymphocyte count < 800/μL [1]. We compared in-hospital mortality and clinical characteristics between PIICS and non-PIICS groups and estimated the adjusted association between PIICS and mortality using multivariable logistic regression.

Results: PIICS occurred in 3.2% of surgical patients. Compared with non-PIICS patients, those with PIICS were older, more often male, and had lower BMI, longer operative duration, and greater blood loss. In-hospital mortality was markedly higher in the PIICS group (6.6% vs. 0.2%). After adjustment for demographic and clinical covariates, PIICS remained strongly associated with in-hospital mortality (adjusted odds ratio 18.8, 95% CI 10.7–33.5, p < 0.001).

Conclusions: PIICS was strongly associated with in-hospital mortality in surgical patients. These findings suggest that PIICS represents a clinically important postoperative phenotype, highlighting the need to elucidate its underlying immune–metabolic mechanisms and to develop approaches for early recognition and prevention.

P401 Functional reserve in sepsis survivorship: a missing construct in critical care recovery

SW Radies1, S Bradwell2, J Gelinas2, G Nonis1, N Eves3, J Boyd4, G Koelwyn2

1University of British Columbia, Medicine, Vancouver, Canada, 2Centre for Heart Lung Innovation, Exercise Physiology, Vancouver, Canada, 3University of British Columbia, School of Health and Exercise Sciences, Vancouver, Canada, 4University of British Columbia, Critical Care Medicine, Vancouver, Canada

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P401

Introduction: There is a growing call to action concerning post-sepsis morbidity. To date, impairments in physical functioning following sepsis have been conceptualized in terms of reduced functional capacity. This construct misses a crucial point: not only is maximal capacity decreased, resting demands also increase. The overall effect is a loss of functional reserve needed to perform basic activities and adapt to rehabilitation stimuli. We contend that functional reserve is a novel framework to guide optimized interventions and improve patient outcomes following sepsis.

Methods: We conducted a targeted synthesis of literature describing energetic disturbances and recovery trajectories in sepsis, including studies of indirect calorimetry, cardiopulmonary exercise testing (CPET), organ-specific dysfunction, and ICU and post-ICU rehabilitation interventions.

Results: Sepsis triggers a precipitous loss of functional reserve, driven by its pathology, treatment effects, and host risk factors (Figure). Following sepsis, functional capacity is 30–50% below age-matched norms, persisting up to 5 years post diagnosis [1, 2]. Further, resting energetic demands increase through ICU stay and remain elevated post-discharge [3]. As a result, the metabolic cost of basic activities may become unmanageable for patients.

Conclusions: The prognostic significance of functional reserve dynamics is currently underexplored in sepsis survivorship. Although functional capacity is the gold-standard outcome in rehabilitation protocols, it may be an inappropriate initial target in the context of profoundly reduced functional reserve. We recommend rehabilitation protocols that decrease the demand of sub-maximal exercise, widen the adaptive window, and incrementally introduce system stress in a patient-specific manner.

P402 Association between abnormal DNA methylation and altered transcriptome in muscle five years after critical illness

C Uzun Ayar1, F Güiza2, I Derese1, G Van den Berghe2, I Vanhorebeek1

1KU Leuven, Laboratory of Intensive Care Medicine, Leuven, Belgium, 2KU Leuven, Clinical Division and Laboratory of Intensive Care Medicine, Leuven, Belgium

Critical Care 2026, 30 (Suppl 1): P402

Introduction: Critically ill patients admitted to the intensive care unit (ICU) suffer from persisting muscle weakness for years. We documented altered RNA expression in muscle 5-years post-ICU, indicating impaired mitochondrial function, disrupted lipid metabolism, and fibrosis, which associated with lower long-term muscle strength [1]. We hypothesized that abnormal DNA methylation may provide a biological basis for this long-term altered muscle transcriptome and weakness.

Methods: Genome-wide DNA methylation was analyzed in muscle of 118 former ICU patients and 30 controls. Differentially methylated positions (DMPs) in former ICU patients versus controls were identified, followed by pathway overrepresentation of affected RNAs. DMP methylations were correlated with RNA expressions. Risk factors for abnormal DNA methylation were evaluated with multivariable linear regression.

Results: Former ICU patients showed 7379 DMPs (average methylation difference 2.6% ranging up to 24.9%), associated with 1334 unique genes, enriched for muscle contraction, vascular development, cell differentiation and signal transduction. DMPs correlated more strongly with differentially expressed RNAs (DERNAs) than with non-DERNAs (18.1% vs 1.7% correlations with |rho|> 0.3). Such correlations were more abundant among DERNAs associated with reduced muscle strength vs those not associated (24.4% vs 12.5%), also within the previously identified disrupted pathways (mitochondrial function 23.3% vs 10.9%, lipid metabolism 15.9% vs 7.2%, fibrosis 44.3% vs 5.8%). Several potentially avoidable in-ICU treatments were associated with more abnormal DNA methylation, most notably glucocorticoids, benzodiazepines and early parenteral nutrition.

Conclusions: Abnormal DNA methylation in muscle 5 years post-ICU, partly attributable to possibly avoidable risk factors, associated with abnormal RNA expression related to lower long-term muscle strength.
